# PEG-LEG GREG — BOOK III — ACT I: THE WORKING COMPANY

**Manuscript-only NotebookLM source. Chapters 181–219.**

---

# CHAPTER 181

## THE CALLER

Lyssa was gone before I woke.

There was blue thread on the table.

Not all of it. One loose piece, maybe the length of my hand, had escaped
whatever bundle she was carrying and caught beneath the cup.

I picked it up.

It was blue.

I had no further useful observations.

I left it under the cup.

Outside, Carrow had already decided to be loud. A cart wheel complained
somewhere toward the market. Somebody was selling hot cakes badly enough
that I could hear him from the end of the lane. Two women were arguing
over a basket. A boy ran past with three prepared lamp wicks bundled
under one arm and nearly hit me.

He apologized without slowing.

East Market Hall's charcoal sign was gone.

Mostly.

GREG remained as a gray stain beside the latch.

I rubbed it once with my thumb.

"Don't start."

Rinna was inside the door.

"I wasn't."

"You were touching it."

"That's not starting."

"Good. We need you."

That was rarely good.

"What am I?"

"Calling second and third."

"What happened to whoever calls second and third?"

"Not here."

"Who?"

"Jori."

"Do I know Jori?"

"No."

"Then how do I know his job?"

"You don't."

She handed me a folded page.

I looked at it.

There were lines.

There were marks beside the lines.

There were names I knew and names I didn't.

"Rinna."

"Yes."

"I don't know this."

"I know."

"That's comforting."

"You're not calling first."

"Why?"

"Because first is complicated."

"Also comforting."

She pointed toward the stage.

"Pell will show you second. Veya knows third. Do not invent anything."

"I don't invent things."

Rinna looked at me.

"At work."

She left.

Pell was under the stage.

I knew because his voice came through the ghost tube.

"Greg."

I looked toward the dark corner of the hall.

"Under here."

I looked down.

There was a square opening beside the stage steps.

"Why are you under there?"

"Tube."

"Why?"

"Ghost."

"I understand the nouns."

His head appeared.

"We moved the outlet."

"Why?"

"People could see it from the left benches."

"I thought they couldn't."

"They could."

"Did someone complain?"

"No. Nessa sat there."

That was worse.

He disappeared again.

I lowered myself onto the stage edge and then down one step at a time.
Crutches were useless for the first part. Pell took one without asking
and leaned it against a beam.

The space beneath the stage was high enough for Pell to crouch.

It was not high enough for me to do anything gracefully.

Dust stuck to my palms.

"Second piece," he said.

"What is second?"

"The Dead Brother."

"That's cheerful."

"Ghost is the brother."

"Who is dead."

"Usually."

"Usually?"

"Don't worry about it."

I worried about it.

Pell showed me the page.

There were four marks that mattered.

Curtain open.

Lamp lower.

Ghost.

Door.

"That's it?"

"That's it."

"Why does Rinna have a whole page?"

"Other people."

"Ah."

He tapped ghost.

"When Nessa says, 'I heard him on the stair,' you point at me."

"Why?"

"So I talk."

"You need me to point at you?"

"I can't hear her well from here."

"Then how do you know when to talk normally?"

"Someone points."

"Jori."

"Usually."

"Who isn't here."

"Yes."

I looked at the tube.

"So my job is pointing."

"Second piece."

"Excellent."

"And curtain."

"Less excellent."

Pell grinned.

We climbed out.

The curtain was a problem.

It was not heavy. It was awkward.

The pull ran along the wall beside the stage, then around a wooden wheel
overhead. Jori apparently stood there, watched the stage, watched the
side door, listened for Nessa, and somehow did not let actors walk into
the curtain.

I tried once.

I let the curtain out too quickly.

It slapped the stage.

Everyone stopped.

Nessa looked up from the coat she was pinning.

"No."

"I noticed."

"Again."

Pell reset it.

I tried again.

Too slow.

The curtain crawled.

Sellen, waiting behind it, said, "I will die before the scene begins."

"You're not in this one."

"I'll die anyway."

"Quiet," Nessa said.

We tried again.

This time the curtain opened at a speed nobody commented on.

Apparently that was success.

My right shoulder already hurt.

"You pull with both hands?" I asked.

Pell said, "Jori does."

"I don't have both hands if I'm standing."

"You can sit."

There was a low box beside the wall.

I sat.

That changed the angle enough that I could brace my good foot and pull
without fighting the crutches.

"Better," I said.

Pell shrugged.

Nobody congratulated me for solving sitting down.

That was also good.

The third piece was The Petition.

Veya took over.

"You know your own entrance."

"Yes."

"You're not in it tonight."

I stared at her.

"What?"

"You're calling."

"But I'm Third Man."

"Not tonight."

"Why?"

"Because Jori isn't here."

"Can I be both?"

"No."

"I could."

"No."

"I know the scene."

"Good. Then you know when Sellen enters."

I looked at the page.

There was a mark beside my old line.

NEIGHBOR.

"You want me to call Sellen."

"I want you to point at Sellen."

"Why is all theatre pointing?"

"Because if you shout his name the audience hears you."

That was fair.

Sellen was across the backstage passage putting on the neighbor coat.

He looked at me.

I pointed.

"Not now."

"I was practicing."

"Don't."

Veya took the page from me and drew a small line.

"Watch me. When I put the petition down, point to the reed. After the
reed, Sellen. Do not anticipate."

"I don't anticipate."

Sellen laughed.

"Nobody asked you."

"You're not in the scene."

"I know."

That hurt more than it should have.

Before we opened the doors, Nessa made me walk the backstage route once
without the page.

Not for education. She needed to know whether I could get from the
curtain box to the side gap quickly enough without blocking the costume
change.

I could, barely.

The narrowest place was between a scenery brace and a basket of folded
cloth. My left crutch fit. My right one caught.

"Move the basket," I said.

"No."

"Why?"

"Quick change."

"Move the brace."

"No."

"Why?"

"Wall."

I looked at the passage.

"So I don't fit."

"You fit differently."

That was not inspiring. It was also true.

I turned sideways, planted both crutches ahead, and moved through in two
short hops.

Nessa watched.

"Do that."

"I hate that."

"Do it anyway."

So I did.

By the third trip my palm was sore where the grip rubbed. I wrapped a
strip of scrap cloth around it and kept going.

The hall filled around us.

Nessa had the wrapped parcel from yesterday open on a table. It
contained three narrow rolls of pale cloth and a packet of black hooks.

That was all.

No mystery.

She was arguing with the paint-wrist woman about whether the hooks were
too bright.

Teren had won something from the baker.

I could tell because there were trays of small rolls in the back room.

"Four bookings?" I asked.

"Three evenings and an afternoon," he said.

"That's four."

He pointed at me.

"Don't."

I took a roll.

He slapped my hand.

"After."

"You're cruel."

"Work."

At the front door, a woman asked me whether the ghost was real.

I had been sent to help for ten minutes while Rinna found change.

I looked at her.

Her daughter was holding her hand.

Behind them, three men were arguing about whether two copper was too
much.

Yesterday I would have said Pell.

Today I said, "You'll hear him."

The girl looked delighted.

The woman bought two tickets.

I felt guilty for almost half a second.

Then one of the men asked, "Is it frightening?"

"Not very."

Rinna, behind me, kicked my crutch.

"Ow."

"Sell."

"It's not frightening."

"People like frightened."

"Do you want me to lie?"

"No. Stop finishing every answer."

The men bought tickets anyway.

One said, "If it's bad, I'm blaming you."

"That's fair."

Rinna sighed.

Someone behind us said, "Move the ticket box with magic."

I turned.

The young actor from yesterday was carrying two lamp stands.

"No."

"Bad magic."

"Yes."

He kept walking.

That was the entire consequence of my possible breakthrough.

The first piece began.

I was not calling it.

Jori's replacement for first was a woman named Hara who had apparently
done the job before. She stood where I would stand later, barely moved,
and somehow knew what everyone needed.

I watched her instead of the play.

She did not look at the page much.

That was annoying.

At the end, she handed me the cord.

"Don't wrap it around your wrist."

"Why?"

She showed me a pale scar across hers.

"Ah."

Then she left to change costume.

I did not ask why the caller was also acting.

Apparently everyone was everything.

Second piece.

The Dead Brother.

I sat on the low box.

Crutches leaned within reach.

Page on my knee.

Pell was under the stage.

Nessa was onstage in a dark dress I had seen hanging from a nail that
morning.

The room quieted.

Curtain.

I pulled.

Good enough.

Lamp.

The lamp boy lowered the left lamp after I pointed.

Too far.

Nessa's face nearly vanished.

I pointed up.

He stared at me.

I pointed up harder.

He raised it.

Nessa kept speaking.

Nobody died.

Not yet.

A chair crossed.

Door closed.

Nessa stopped beside the painted stair.

"I heard him on the stair."

I pointed at Pell.

Nothing.

I pointed again.

Nothing.

My stomach dropped.

Then I realized I was pointing at the floor.

Pell was under the stage.

He could not see through wood.

There was a narrow gap beside the curtain.

That was where Jori pointed.

I leaned.

Too far.

My crutch tip slid into the edge of the curtain.

The fabric twitched.

Nessa looked toward the dark stair.

Not at me.

I got the crutch free.

I shoved my hand into the gap and pointed down.

The ghost spoke.

"Nessa."

I knew it was Pell.

I knew exactly where he was.

I knew the leather tube ran beneath my feet and ended behind the dark
flat beside the stair.

My eyes went to the stair anyway.

Nessa froze.

Not before.

After.

The room froze with her.

"You're dead," she whispered.

Pell answered through the tube.

"I know."

Someone in the audience made a small sound.

Not a scream.

More like they had forgotten to breathe and remembered badly.

Then I remembered the door.

I pointed.

The door operator did not move.

I pointed again.

Wrong person.

The operator was on the other side.

I had been told that.

I had also forgotten it.

I reached across and tapped the wooden frame twice.

That was apparently not the cue.

The door stayed open.

Nessa crossed toward it.

She was supposed to close it on the next line.

It was already open too wide.

For one terrible second, I could see the whole mechanism behind the
painted stair from where I sat.

The audience could not.

Probably.

Nessa reached the door, caught it herself, and slammed it.

The sound was better than the planned one.

The audience jumped.

Pell whispered through the ghost tube.

"Good."

I wanted to kill him.

The rest of the piece worked.

When the curtain closed, Nessa came offstage and walked directly to me.

"Wrong side."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"Now."

"Good."

She kept walking.

That was my review.

Pell crawled out from under the stage.

"You pointed at the floor."

"You were under the floor."

"Yes."

"So it was reasonable."

"No."

"I hate theatre."

"No, you don't."

"Everyone keeps saying that."

"Because you keep lying."

Third piece.

The Petition.

I was still annoyed that I was not in it.

The new Third Man was one of the actors I had seen arguing about a coat
yesterday. His name was Tovin.

He had my goat.

Not physically.

The goat remained theoretical.

I sat with the page.

Veya began.

First petitioner.

Drainage.

Second petitioner.

Pig.

Then Veya put the petition down.

I pointed at the reed.

The reed sounded.

I pointed at Tovin.

He entered.

"My neighbor's goat has eaten half my fence and all my patience."

Wrong.

He had reversed it.

It was still a sentence.

Sellen looked at me from the wing.

I almost laughed.

Tovin continued.

Sellen entered on neighbor anyway.

The scene worked.

Different.

Not broken.

I had spent two days learning exactly how the cue fit and now somebody
else had said it wrong and the building had not fallen down.

I was offended.

Sellen did not use the father line.

He used another line instead.

The audience laughed.

Less.

Then Veya got a bigger laugh with a look.

I sat behind the curtain and pointed when I was supposed to point.

At some point I realized I was enjoying myself.

That seemed unfair.

After the third piece, my shoulder hurt enough that I stopped pretending
it didn't.

I leaned both crutches against the wall and rubbed it.

Davin appeared beside me.

I had not seen him all day.

"Pulling?"

"Curtain."

He looked at the cord.

"Bad angle."

"I sat."

"Better."

"Thank you."

He touched the wooden wheel overhead, frowned, and walked away.

"Are you fixing it?"

"Tomorrow."

"What's wrong with it?"

"Worn."

"Was it worn yesterday?"

"Yes."

"Why tomorrow?"

He looked back.

"Because today is today."

Then he disappeared toward the rear yard.

I accepted this.

Cleanup took longer because someone had spilled lamp oil near the prop
shelf.

Nothing caught fire.

That was the good news.

The bad news was that Nessa made everyone stop carrying cloth through
the area until it was sanded and wiped.

The rolls came down.

The hooks went back in the parcel.

The fake loaf went somewhere I did not see.

Rinna paid me one copper.

"Caller."

"I was bad."

"You were adequate."

"I missed the ghost."

"It arrived."

"I missed the door."

"It closed."

"Nessa closed it."

"Yes."

"That means I was bad."

"That means the show happened."

She put the copper in my hand.

"Jori back tomorrow?"

"Maybe."

I stared at her.

She smiled.

"Don't."

Pell had not rewritten the sign.

Instead, somebody had drawn a tiny piece of paper beside the gray GREG
stain.

It had little lines around it like it was flying.

I left it.

Outside, Carrow was cooling.

I found Lyssa two streets from home carrying a bundle of blue thread.

Not one bundle.

Six.

They were looped over her arm.

"You carried blue thread."

"I told you."

"I didn't understand the scale."

"Move."

I moved.

She shifted two bundles onto me.

"I have crutches."

"You have an arm."

"Pell said that about a tube."

"Who?"

"Nobody important."

We walked home.

"What did you do?"

"Called."

"Called what?"

"Ghost."

She stopped.

"You summoned a ghost?"

"No."

"Good."

"It was Pell."

"I don't know who that is."

"Exactly."

She started walking again.

I carried blue thread.

At home there was a note under the door.

Guild paper.

I knew before I opened it.

Lyssa set down her bundles.

"Paper?"

"Different paper."

I unfolded it.

Hessa's handwriting.

TOMORROW. AFTER MIDDAY.

Below that:

NEW FINS.

Then:

DO NOT BRING PELL.

I stared.

Lyssa leaned over my shoulder.

"Who is Pell?"

"I may have mentioned him."

"To Hessa?"

"Possibly."

"Why?"

"I don't remember."

She took the note.

"She knows you."

"Unfortunately."

Lyssa handed it back.

I put it beside my question paper.

One movement.

Not repeated.

One fin easier.

Tomorrow, new fins.

My shoulder hurt.

There was blue thread on the table.

Somewhere at East Market Hall, Pell was probably still under something.

I had spent the day making a ghost arrive late, failing to close a door,
and watching another man say my line wrong.

Tomorrow Hessa wanted me to try not to move paper incorrectly.

Both sounded like work.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 182

## THE SUBJECT

Davin had the curtain wheel on the floor.

I stopped in the East Market Hall doorway.

The wheel looked smaller there.

Most things did when somebody took them apart.

Davin sat on an overturned bucket with the wooden wheel between his
knees, working a narrow metal pin out of its center. The curtain cord
hung uselessly from above. A second man I did not know was on a ladder,
scraping old wax from the bracket.

"Today is tomorrow," I said.

Davin looked at me.

"Yes."

"Good."

He went back to the pin.

That concluded our conversation.

Jori was back.

I knew because a thin man with a crooked nose was standing at my low box
with the cue page folded in half.

"Are you Jori?"

"Yes."

"I did your job."

"I heard."

"I was adequate."

He looked at the curtain cord hanging from the ceiling.

"Apparently."

Then he walked away.

I had expected to feel replaced.

Instead I felt relieved.

Rinna caught me before I could steal one of the baker's rolls.

"Third Man tonight."

"My goat?"

"Your goat."

"Tovin said it wrong."

"He says most things wrong."

"Why did you let him have it?"

"You were busy."

That was also apparently the entire theory of casting.

I ate the roll.

Rinna saw me.

She did not stop me.

That worried me more than being caught.

"Guild after midday," I said.

"I know."

"How?"

"You told Teren."

"I did?"

"You tell people things."

I thought of Hessa's note.

DO NOT BRING PELL.

"I should work on that."

"Yes."

She pointed toward a stack of chairs.

I moved chairs until noon.

Lyssa did not appear.

Neither did Pell, which made keeping him away from Hessa considerably
easier.

After midday I went to the Guild.

Hessa was not there.

Her room was open, but a gray-coated man had three wooden boxes on her
table and was comparing seals against a page. I recognized none of the
marks. He did not look at me.

I sat outside.

People went past.

A woman carrying a tray of small prepared glass beads stopped at the
room, saw the boxes, swore quietly, and kept going. Two Guild workers
argued at the end of the corridor about whether something belonged in
south storage or had only been found there. One of them said, "If it was
logged east, it stays east until someone changes the page."

The other said, "The crate is south."

"Then the crate is wrong."

I liked him.

Hessa arrived carrying six paper fins in one hand and looking irritated.

"You're here."

"You told me to be."

"Good."

She looked into her room.

The gray-coated man looked up.

"Five minutes."

"You said that ten minutes ago."

"I wasn't here ten minutes ago."

He considered this.

"Five minutes."

Hessa sat beside me.

I looked at the fins.

They were all the same pale paper.

"New fins."

"Yes."

"Did you make them?"

"No."

"Who did?"

"Someone who folds paper better than you."

"That could be anyone."

"Yes."

She had not brought a book.

She had not brought my old notes.

For five minutes she sat beside me and watched Guild workers go past.

I wanted to ask about the boxes.

I did not.

At six minutes, Hessa stood.

The gray-coated man came out carrying two boxes. He left the third on
her table.

Hessa stopped him.

"No."

He looked at the box.

"I need the table."

He took it.

Hessa went inside.

I followed.

"Do not touch anything."

"I know."

"You touched the glass last time."

"After."

"You touched it."

I sat.

The wooden frame from our last test was gone.

The glass shield remained.

So did the washers.

Hessa brought out a different base. It was a flat board with two shallow
grooves cut parallel into the surface. Each groove had three ink marks
beside it.

"New?"

"Mostly."

"Mostly?"

"The board is old."

"From what?"

"Not relevant."

That meant I would never know.

She set the six fins in a row.

They were small folded pieces of paper, each with a flat foot and an
upright face. The folds looked sharper than the ones we had used before.

Hessa picked up the first.

"Look."

"I am."

"The base."

I looked.

"Flat."

She set it down.

Second.

"Flat."

Third.

"Flat."

By the sixth I understood the activity.

"Flat."

"Good."

"Was that a test?"

"No."

"Good."

She took a narrow piece of wood and pressed it along the fold of each
fin.

"Same edge," she said. "Same paper. Same fold."

"Same person?"

"Yes."

"Who?"

"No."

I leaned back.

Hessa placed two fins into the grooves. One in each.

She slid the glass shield over them.

"Nothing yet."

"I know."

She checked the table with the same small wooden wedges she had used
before. One leg got a thin sliver beneath it.

Then she tapped the table.

Both fins stayed still.

She walked around it.

Both stayed still.

She opened the glass, switched the fins, closed it, and tapped again.

Nothing.

"You're making this very boring."

"That is the intention."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"Usually."

She looked at me.

"Why did you write not to bring Pell?"

"Who?"

I stared at her.

Hessa's mouth moved.

Barely.

"You remembered."

"Unfortunately."

"Why?"

"Because you described a man under a stage speaking through leather."

"That is Pell."

"I gathered."

"He would have liked this."

"No."

"He likes garbage."

"No."

"Fair."

Hessa sat opposite me.

"Hands."

I put my hands on the table.

She checked them.

Then my wrist.

Then the old familiar questions.

Pain.

Numbness.

Tingling.

Heat.

Anything unusual since the last session.

No.

No.

No.

No.

No.

"Draw."

I did.

The mana came easier than it had before the road, though I no longer
trusted easy as a useful word. It was familiar. That was different.

I held the minimal draw where she had taught me to hold it.

Hessa watched.

"Stop."

I stopped.

"Again."

I looked at her.

"Again?"

"Baseline."

I drew again.

The second was smaller.

Cleaner.

The old unevenness along the wrist edge was there. Not stronger. Not
gone.

Hessa touched two fingers lightly against the inside of my wrist.

"Stop."

I stopped.

She wrote.

"Two?"

"Two."

"So twenty-four."

"Yes."

I had been keeping count.

Apparently so had she.

"Shape."

"No paper?"

"Not yet."

I shaped the draw without trying to send anything anywhere.

The asymmetry came back almost immediately.

It felt like leaning toward a door that was not there.

I corrected too hard.

Hessa said, "No."

"I know."

"Then stop."

I stopped.

She waited.

"Again."

The second shaping was better.

Not good.

Better.

I held it for three breaths.

"Stop."

I stopped.

Hessa wrote again.

"Twenty."

"Yes."

I flexed my hand.

"So now paper."

"Now paper."

She opened a small cloth bag.

Inside were six little wooden counters.

Three had black dots.

Three were blank.

"What are those?"

"Not magic."

"I assumed."

"You shouldn't."

She shook two counters into her closed hand.

"Choose."

"Why?"

"Choose."

"Left."

She opened her left hand.

Black dot.

She placed the black counter beside the left groove.

"Target?"

"Yes."

"You could have just said left."

"Then you would know I chose left."

"I know now."

"Yes."

I hated this.

She closed the glass.

"One attempt."

"Only one?"

"One, then we inspect."

I settled.

The room was quiet except for voices somewhere in the corridor and the
scratch of somebody dragging something heavy across stone.

I drew.

Not much.

I shaped.

The target fin was on the left.

I tried not to look at its edge.

That was impossible.

I looked at its edge.

I wanted it to move.

I wanted it so badly that I could feel myself beginning to turn wanting
into evidence.

I stopped thinking about the fin.

That did not help.

I shaped toward the space in front of it.

The familiar wrist-edge tendency pulled sideways.

I corrected.

Too much.

Back.

Smaller.

The fin moved.

I stopped breathing.

It had moved.

Not far.

Less than last time, maybe.

Forward.

Across the first ink mark.

The right fin had not moved.

"It moved."

"Stop."

I stopped.

Hessa did not look at me.

She looked at the table.

Then at my hands.

Then at the glass.

Then at the fin.

She walked around the table.

I waited.

She opened the shield.

"Don't touch."

"I wasn't."

She checked the target base.

Then the control.

Then the groove.

Then the ink mark.

She switched the fins.

The moved fin went to the right.

The control went left.

Then she shook the counters again.

"Choose."

"Right."

Blank.

She put the blank counter beside the right groove.

"Target?"

"Right."

"Again."

"Isn't that the same fin?"

"Yes."

"So if it moves again, we know the fin is easier."

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because that is not the only explanation."

"Right."

I hated her.

I drew.

The second attempt felt worse immediately.

Not painful.

Crowded.

The shape would not sit where I wanted it.

I tried to force it narrower.

Hessa said, "Don't chase."

I loosened it.

The right fin did not move.

The left fin did not move.

I held.

Nothing.

"Stop."

I stopped.

Hessa wrote.

"That doesn't help."

"It does."

"How?"

"It didn't move."

"That's not helpful."

"It is information."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"No."

She let me rest.

I rubbed my palm.

The cloth I had wrapped around the crutch grip had left a faint line
across the skin.

Hessa noticed.

"What's that?"

"Curtain."

She looked at me.

"That answer is not useful."

"I pulled a curtain."

"Better."

"I called a ghost."

"No."

"Fine."

She waited until I stopped smiling.

Then she changed both fins.

Two unused ones.

She placed them in the grooves.

She checked the bases.

Glass down.

Counters.

"Choose."

"Left."

Blank.

Target right.

I did not like not choosing the target.

That was probably why she was doing it.

Third attempt.

I drew.

The shape came cleaner.

Not easy.

Cleaner.

I found the wrist edge, let it be there, and worked around it instead of
trying to erase it.

Hessa did not speak.

The right fin stood beyond the glass.

I shaped toward it.

Nothing.

I kept the shape small.

Nothing.

Then the fin gave a tiny jump.

Not forward.

Sideways.

Its upright face twisted a fraction toward the groove wall.

I stared.

The left fin did not move.

Hessa said, "Stop."

I stopped.

She checked everything again.

"That wasn't forward."

"No."

"Does it count?"

"As movement."

"Caused by me?"

She looked at me.

"Wait."

I waited.

She removed the right fin and set it on the table.

It stood.

She tapped the board.

It trembled but did not twist.

She replaced it.

Then she switched the two fins.

"Again?"

"No."

"What?"

"Rest."

"I can do another."

"I know."

"Then why rest?"

"Because your hand is shaking."

I looked.

It was.

Barely.

Still enough.

I put it in my lap.

Hessa wrote.

I tried to read upside down.

She covered the page with her hand.

"Hostile."

"Rest."

We waited.

The room did not become dramatic.

Someone outside laughed.

A cart rattled in the street.

Hessa got up, went to the shelf, and came back with water.

I drank.

"How many external attempts is that?"

"Three today."

"So eight."

"Yes."

"And two movements."

"Today."

"Three total."

"Observed movements during external attempts, yes."

"That sounds good."

"It sounds like three movements."

"You're impossible."

"Good."

After several minutes she put the fins back.

Not the same pair.

She chose two others.

This time she put the counters in her hands before I could say anything.

"Choose."

"Right."

Black.

Target right.

"Last."

"Only?"

"Last."

I settled again.

My shoulder hurt from yesterday.

My palm hurt.

My wrist did not.

That mattered more.

I drew.

The mana sat where it sat.

I shaped.

The right fin waited.

I did not imagine the audience.

I did not imagine Pell.

I did not imagine a ghost.

I tried to make one stupid piece of paper move a stupidly small distance
while one woman watched and refused to be impressed.

The fin moved forward.

Not much.

Enough to cross the first ink mark.

I let the shape go immediately.

"Stop."

"I stopped."

"Hands away."

I pulled them back.

Hessa checked.

Glass.

Table.

Fin.

Control.

Groove.

Base.

She switched the fins.

She checked the board.

She looked at the wedges.

Then she sat.

I waited.

She wrote for a long time.

"Well?"

"No."

"No what?"

"Do not make me answer while I am writing."

I waited.

She finished.

Then she turned the page toward me.

There were four lines for today.

First: left target, forward movement.

Second: right target, none.

Third: right target, lateral movement.

Fourth: right target, forward movement.

Below that she had written:

THREE DIFFERENT FINS MOVED ACROSS TWO POSITIONS.

I read it twice.

"Three?"

"The first, the third, the fourth."

"The second was the same fin as first."

"Yes. It did not move."

"So three different fins moved."

"Yes."

"While I was shaping."

"Yes."

"Targets."

"Yes."

"Controls didn't."

"No visible control movement."

I looked at her.

"Did I move paper?"

Hessa leaned back.

I could see her deciding how much she hated the question.

"Under this setup," she said, "during supervised deliberate shaping
toward a designated paper target, we have now observed small movement of
multiple target fins without visible movement of the paired controls."

I waited.

"That is a terrible sentence."

"It is an accurate sentence."

"Did I do it?"

"We have removed the easiest explanation from last time."

"That isn't what I asked."

"I know."

"Hessa."

She looked at the page.

Then at the fins.

Then at me.

"Yes."

Nothing happened.

No light.

No sound.

No pressure.

No revelation.

I sat there.

"Yes?"

"Within the limits I just stated."

"I moved paper."

"You produced a repeatable external response under this apparatus."

"Paper."

"Do not make me regret this."

I smiled.

I could not help it.

Hessa pointed at me.

"No."

"I'm not doing anything."

"You are expanding the claim in your head."

"I am not."

"You are."

I was.

I stopped.

Mostly.

"What does it mean?"

"That we have a result."

"What can I do?"

"This."

She pointed at the paper.

"That's pathetic."

"Yes."

I laughed.

She did not.

Then she almost did.

I saw it.

"Can we try something heavier?"

"No."

"Farther?"

"No."

"Different direction?"

"Not today."

"Tomorrow?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Because you have done two minimal draws, two deliberate shaping
baselines, and four external attempts."

I counted.

"Twenty-four draws. Twenty shaping. Nine external."

"Yes."

"Nine?"

"Four today."

I had forgotten the last one while celebrating it.

Hessa folded the page.

"You are done."

"But now we know."

"Now we know one thing."

"One good thing."

"One small thing."

"Same."

"No."

I looked at the fins.

"What next?"

"Direction."

"Forward?"

"Whether forward is yours or the apparatus."

That took some of the smile out of me.

"How?"

"Different orientation. Different target direction. Same fins. Not
today."

"Because hand shaking."

"Among other things."

She gathered the counters.

"What other things?"

"You have somewhere to be."

I looked toward the window.

The light had shifted.

"Shit."

"Yes."

"Rinna."

"Probably."

I grabbed my crutches.

Hessa said, "Greg."

I stopped.

"Restrictions remain."

"I know."

"Say them."

"No independent draw. No shaping. No testing. No trying paper at home.
No trying anything else because paper worked."

"Good."

"It worked."

"Leave."

I left.

I was halfway down the corridor when I went back.

Hessa looked up.

"What?"

"Can I tell Lyssa I moved paper?"

Hessa stared at me.

"Leave."

I took that as yes.

At East Market Hall, Davin had the curtain wheel back up.

Jori was calling.

Nobody cared that I had returned from the Guild.

Rinna pointed at the coat rack.

"Third Man."

I put on the coat.

Sellen was already waiting.

"You're late."

"I moved paper."

He looked at me.

"Good."

"With magic."

"Better."

"Hessa says under a controlled setup during supervised deliberate
shaping toward a designated paper target, we observed small movement of
multiple target fins without visible movement of paired controls."

Sellen stared.

"Did you move paper?"

"Yes."

"Why didn't you say that?"

I hated everyone.

The Petition began.

I waited for my entrance.

Veya put down the petition.

The reed sounded.

Jori pointed at me.

I went.

"My neighbor's goat has eaten all my patience and half my fence."

Sellen came in exactly where he was supposed to.

The audience laughed.

Not huge.

Enough.

I did not add anything.

Afterward, Rinna paid me one copper.

"Pell says congratulations."

"Where is Pell?"

"Under the stage."

"Of course."

I found Lyssa after the show at home with the blue thread spread into
smaller bundles.

She looked up.

"Paper?"

"Yes."

"Moved?"

"Yes."

"On purpose?"

"Yes."

"More than once?"

I put my crutches against the wall.

"Yes."

Lyssa sat back.

"Oh."

That was better than cheering.

I sat opposite her.

"Hessa said yes."

"Actually yes?"

"With a terrible sentence around it."

"That sounds like Hessa."

"I have a result."

"What can you do?"

"Move very small pieces of paper a very small amount under a glass cover
while Hessa watches."

Lyssa considered this.

"Useful."

"You're mocking me."

"A little."

"I did magic."

"You've been doing magic."

"I did magic outside me."

She stopped sorting thread.

That was the part that reached her.

"Ah."

"Yes."

She looked at my hand.

"Does it hurt?"

"No."

"Good."

Then she picked up the thread again.

"What happens next?"

"Different direction."

"Why?"

"To see if I moved it or the table likes forward."

Lyssa nodded as if this were reasonable.

It was.

I put the copper from the show on the table.

One copper.

One result.

Six bundles of blue thread.

Lyssa had apparently spent the day turning six into twelve.

Tomorrow the hall had another show.

Hessa did not want me tomorrow.

That felt rude now.

I wanted to move something else.

I wanted to move the cup.

The thread.

The copper.

Anything.

I did not.

I sat on my hands for a moment instead.

Lyssa noticed.

"Good."

"Don't."

She smiled.

The paper had moved.

This time, maybe had become yes.

It was a very small yes.

It was enough.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 183

## THE WORKER

I woke up looking at the corner of a piece of paper.

It was sticking out from under Lyssa's book.

I stared at it.

The paper did not move.

That was because I was not doing anything.

I knew this.

I continued staring at it.

Lyssa came in carrying a cup and stopped.

"No."

"I am not."

"You are looking at it."

"Looking is allowed."

"Is it?"

"Yes."

"Did Hessa say?"

I looked at Lyssa.

She drank from the cup.

"Hostile."

"You moved paper yesterday."

"Yes."

"Go to work."

She had already dressed. The blue thread from last night was gone except
for three short lengths on the table, all tied around small folded
scraps of cloth. I did not know what the scraps meant.

"Where is the thread?"

"Mostly not here."

"I can see that."

"Good."

"Where is it?"

"With people who needed blue thread."

That seemed intentionally unhelpful.

Lyssa picked up two of the folded scraps.

"Marra?"

"Some."

"Jessa?"

"One."

"Who are the others?"

She kissed my forehead.

"People."

Then she left.

I sat for another minute.

The paper corner was still there.

So was the cup.

So was one copper from last night.

The copper seemed heavier than it had before.

Not actually heavier.

I knew that too.

I put my hands on my knees, stood, got my crutches, and went to work.

At East Market Hall, Pell was waiting for me beside the repaired latch.

That was suspicious.

"You did magic."

"Yes."

He grinned.

It was the first time I had been able to answer that without immediately
correcting the sentence.

"Do it."

"No."

His grin vanished.

"Bad magic."

"Yes."

"What can you move?"

"Paper."

He picked up a torn corner of an old handbill from the floor and held it
out.

"Move this."

"No."

"You just said paper."

"Under glass."

Pell looked at the handbill.

"Put glass over it."

"No."

"Why?"

"Hessa."

"She isn't here."

"That is the problem."

Pell thought about this for perhaps half a second.

"Bad magic."

"I already agreed."

He dropped the handbill.

"Can you move a feather?"

"I don't know."

"A string?"

"I don't know."

"A fake petition?"

"I don't know."

"Things?"

"No."

"You can move paper but not things."

"Paper is a thing."

"Then you can move things."

"No."

Pell looked genuinely offended by my categories.

I understood Hessa a little better.

Nessa came through carrying a painted wooden bracket.

"Pell."

"Yes?"

"Tube."

He left immediately.

Apparently I ranked below tube.

Rinna arrived with a small wooden box under one arm and a folded sheet
in her hand.

"Afternoon."

"I know what time it is."

"Show."

"Oh."

The baker's afternoon.

I had forgotten.

Rinna handed me the sheet.

It was not a cue page.

It was a bill.

THE LITTLE KING.

THE MILLER'S SON.

THE RED DOOR.

Under that, in smaller writing:

SHORT SONG IF CHILDREN BEHAVE.

I looked at her.

"Do children behave?"

"No."

"Then why write it?"

"Hope."

Teren came out from behind the stage carrying a length of rope.

"Greg."

"Yes?"

"Can you do the paper thing on cue?"

"No."

"Can you do it here?"

"No."

"Can you do it without the Guild woman?"

"No."

"Can you move anything larger than paper?"

"No."

He nodded once.

"Good."

"Good?"

"Then it isn't my problem."

He handed me the rope.

"Doorframe."

That was how my first established external magical effect entered the
theatre.

I carried rope.

The red doorframe had split at the lower joint.

It was not the same painted doorway from The Widow's Room. This one was
narrower, red on the audience side, bare wood on the back, and built to
stand by itself with two feet that stuck out behind it.

One foot had cracked.

The frame leaned.

Nessa was kneeling beside it with the broken foot in her lap.

"Don't lift it."

"I was going to look."

"Then look lower."

I sat on the floor.

The crack ran through one of the old peg holes.

"Can Davin fix it?"

"Yes."

"Where is Davin?"

"Not here."

"Can you fix it?"

"Enough."

She took the rope from me.

"What do you need me to do?"

"Hold that."

She pointed at the upright.

I held it.

For the next fifteen minutes, my contribution to theatre consisted of
keeping a fake red door vertical while Nessa bound a replacement block
against its foot.

My shoulder still hurt from the curtain work. Holding the frame was not
heavy, but it was awkward. I shifted my grip twice.

"Stop moving."

"My arm hurts."

"Then sit closer."

I moved closer.

That helped.

Nobody congratulated me.

A girl carrying a basket of costume belts stepped over my good leg
without apologizing.

Sellen came past wearing a green coat and stopped.

"I heard you can move objects with magic."

"No."

"Pell says."

"Pell says I moved paper."

"He said objects."

"He's wrong."

Sellen considered the doorframe I was holding.

"Can you move that?"

"No."

"Then that's disappointing."

"Thank you."

He left.

Nessa pulled the rope tighter.

"You told Pell?"

"He already knew."

"How?"

"Rinna."

Nessa looked toward the front.

"Rinna."

"What?"

"Nothing."

The repaired block held when she let go.

She stood.

"Now lift."

I lifted my side.

A man I did not know took the other.

We carried the doorframe six paces to its mark.

The new curtain wheel worked.

I noticed because Jori pulled the curtain while we were setting the
door, and it opened without the old scrape and hitch.

That was all.

Davin had fixed it.

The curtain worked.

Nobody mentioned it.

The afternoon audience began arriving before we were ready.

They were louder than the evening people.

Children came in pairs and groups, some with adults and some with older
siblings who had apparently been told to keep them alive until supper.
Market workers arrived still wearing aprons. One baker came with flour
up both forearms.

Rinna put me at the door because she had not learned.

"Two copper full bill," I said. "One for children."

A woman with three children pointed at the painted sign.

"Magic?"

I looked.

Someone had added a small star beside THE LITTLE KING.

"I don't know why that's there."

The oldest child looked at me.

"Are you the magician?"

"No."

"Pell says you are."

Of course he did.

"I moved paper."

The child waited.

"With magic," I added.

"Can you make something disappear?"

"Yes."

His face changed.

I took the handbill from his hand, put it behind my back, and handed it
to Rinna.

The child stared at me.

Rinna stared at me.

"That's not magic," he said.

"No."

He seemed disappointed.

His mother laughed and paid four copper for all of them.

Rinna waited until they were inside.

"That was terrible."

"They bought tickets."

"Do it again."

"I hate this job."

"No, you don't."

I hated when people knew that.

The afternoon problem was not the children.

It was the lamp.

The Little King used a small prepared lamp with a pale blue glass cap.
Not the same blue-lamp piece I had hung before. This one was supposed to
brighten briefly when the king opened a wooden box.

It did not.

The first test gave a weak gray flicker.

The second gave nothing.

Pell found me.

"Magic."

"No."

"Fix."

"No."

"Bad."

"Find Nessa."

Nessa was already there.

She took the lamp from the boy holding it, checked the worked line under
the cap, then called for Rinna.

Rinna touched the prepared strip with one finger.

Nothing.

"Spent," she said.

"Can it be charged?" I asked.

"Yes."

"Now?"

"No."

Pell looked at me.

I pointed at Rinna.

"See?"

"Your magic is still bad."

"My magic has nothing to do with this."

Nessa removed the blue cap.

"Plain lamp."

Pell said, "But the king's box."

"Plain lamp."

"What about the flash?"

"No flash."

"What if Greg moves paper?"

Nessa looked at him.

Pell left.

The piece ran with a plain lamp.

The king opened the box.

Nothing brightened.

A little girl in the front row still gasped because the actor looked
into the box as if he had seen something wonderful.

I was carrying a stool behind the side cloth and almost stopped to
watch.

Nessa tapped the stool.

"Move."

I moved.

The Miller's Son was next.

I had one line.

Not Third Man.

Not Shopkeeper.

Not anything I had performed before.

I was a clerk.

Teren had given it to me twenty minutes before the audience came in.

My entire job was to enter with a folded page, tell the miller's son
that the north wheel belonged to the widow until the debt was paid, hand
him the page, and leave.

"Which widow?" I had asked.

"The widow."

"From the other piece?"

"No."

"Does she have a name?"

"No."

"Why does she own a wheel?"

"Debt."

That had been enough for me.

I liked the clerk.

He knew what he was doing.

I entered.

The miller's son was played by Hara.

She looked younger than she had yesterday because someone had put her
hair loose and given her a badly fitted brown coat.

I knew she was Hara.

She looked seventeen.

That was annoying.

I gave her the page.

"The north wheel belongs to the widow until your father's debt is paid."

Hara stared at me.

I waited.

She was supposed to say, "My father is dead."

She did not.

A child coughed.

Hara looked down at the page.

Then back at me.

"My father is dead."

Too late.

I had already started turning.

I stopped.

My crutch squeaked on the floor.

A boy laughed.

I turned back.

"That does make collection difficult."

The audience laughed.

Hara's eyes changed.

Not angry.

Interested.

"Will you tell the widow?"

"No."

Bigger laugh.

That was not the scene.

I knew it immediately.

I had done it again.

Hara folded the page.

"Then I'll tell her myself."

That got us back.

I left.

Backstage, Teren was waiting.

"It worked," I said before he could speak.

"For you."

I closed my mouth.

He had not been there for the last version of this conversation, but
apparently theatre had one answer.

Hara came off two minutes later.

"You left."

"You forgot your line."

"I remembered it."

"After I left."

"You were supposed to wait."

"I did wait."

"You turned."

"Because you didn't say anything."

"That was my silence."

"It was a long silence."

"It was my silence."

Teren pointed between us.

"Both."

Hara frowned.

"Both what?"

"Both wrong."

Then he walked away.

I looked at Hara.

She looked at me.

"Your father is dead," I said.

"I know."

We both started laughing.

The Red Door used the repaired frame.

That was its entire reason for being called The Red Door as far as I
could tell.

A woman came home.

The door was locked.

Her brother was inside.

He would not open it.

They argued through an empty frame that had no wall attached to it.

From backstage, it looked ridiculous.

From the side gap, I could see the brother standing three feet behind
the red frame with nothing between him and his sister.

From the audience, the frame and a strip of painted cloth made a house.

The repair held.

Halfway through, one of the children yelled, "Go around!"

The woman onstage stopped.

The brother stopped.

The audience laughed.

She looked at the painted wall.

Then at the child.

"I've tried."

The child accepted this.

The play continued.

The short song happened because the children had not behaved and Teren
apparently wanted them tired.

Afterward, the hall looked like a meal had exploded.

Cups under benches.

A heel of bread behind the third row.

One child's scarf tied around the leg of a chair.

A wooden horse that belonged to nobody in the company.

Rinna held it up.

"Whose?"

Three children shouted, "Mine."

She put it on the ticket table.

"Fight later."

We cleaned.

Pell found the old magic sign stain and added a second tiny paper beside
the first.

This one had legs.

"Why?"

"It can move."

"Paper does not have legs."

"Yours does."

"No."

He added a hat.

"Stop improving it."

"Magician."

"I'm not a magician."

"You did magic."

"Yes."

"You moved a thing."

"No."

He put down the charcoal.

"You moved paper."

"Yes."

"Paper is a thing."

I looked at him.

He smiled.

I did not have Hessa's patience.

Rinna paid me one copper for the afternoon clerk role and crew work.

"One?"

"One."

"I also did door."

"You sold tickets."

"I repaired scenery."

"Nessa repaired scenery."

"I held scenery."

"One."

I took it.

"Fair."

"Yes."

"Are we paid tonight too?"

"If you work tonight."

"What am I?"

"Third Man."

"My goat."

"Your goat."

That made two shows in one day.

The afternoon crowd left.

The hall did not empty completely.

A few company people lay down backstage. Hara ate half an onion with
bread. Sellen disappeared. Jori sat on the low box with his cue page
over his face.

I went outside.

Carrow was still doing everything else.

A fish seller poured meltwater from a shallow cooling tray into the
gutter. A delivery boy pushed a handcart too fast around a corner and
lost three cabbages. Two women argued over a bolt of yellow cloth. A
prepared bakery stone showed a dull orange line beneath a pan of rolls.

Nobody knew I had moved paper.

That was good.

I bought one roll.

The baker did not ask me to move it.

Also good.

When I returned, Teren had changed the evening bill.

The Petition was gone.

Third Man was gone with it.

I stared at the board.

"What happened to my goat?"

Teren did not look up.

"Tomorrow."

"You told me tonight."

"I was wrong."

"What am I doing?"

"House."

"Door?"

"House."

"What does that mean?"

"Seats. Late people. Don't let anyone through the left passage during
the second piece."

"Why?"

"Costume change."

I had become less important in under three minutes.

I worked the house.

It was worse than acting.

People wanted aisle seats after buying center tickets. A man arrived
during the first piece and whispered that he had paid yesterday. A woman
insisted the left passage was where she had entered last week.

"No."

"Yes."

"Not tonight."

"Why?"

"Costume change."

"Whose?"

"I don't know."

That was the correct amount of information.

She went around.

During the second piece, I stood at the back wall and watched a scene I
had never seen before. Sellen played a physician. Veya played a woman
pretending not to know him.

I did not know why.

It was good.

Nobody needed me.

Then a bench leg shifted.

I caught the end before it tipped.

The man sitting on it looked at me.

"Thank you."

"Don't move."

He froze.

I found a folded scrap of wood, shoved it under the short leg, and let
go.

The bench stayed.

The scene continued.

That was my largest practical accomplishment of the day.

No magic.

After the evening show, Rinna gave me another copper.

"House."

"Bench."

"House."

"I saved four people."

"You stopped a bench wobbling."

"It was going."

"Two copper total today."

I put both coins in my pocket.

Pell came past carrying the ghost tube.

"Can you move coins?"

"No."

"Bad magic."

"Go away."

He did.

Lyssa was not home when I got there.

There was a note on the table.

BACK LATE. EAT.

Under it sat half a loaf and a small bowl of beans.

I ate.

Then I found paper.

Not the paper I had stared at that morning.

A different piece.

I wrote:

FORWARD?

Then underneath:

MY FORWARD OR BOARD FORWARD?

I looked at it.

That was ugly enough to be useful.

I did not draw.

I did not shape.

I did not put anything under glass.

I folded the paper and set it beneath Hessa's old note.

Then I put the two copper from the day beside yesterday's copper.

Three.

The coins did not move.

Neither did the paper.

Lyssa came in while I was washing the bowl.

She carried no blue thread.

"Where is it?" I asked.

"Where is what?"

"The thread."

"Gone."

"All of it?"

"Most."

"What happened?"

"People took it."

"Who?"

Lyssa looked at me.

"Still people."

I dried the bowl.

"I worked two shows."

"Good."

"I was a clerk."

"Good."

"I moved no paper."

"Very good."

"I held a door."

"That sounds more useful."

"It was."

She took off her shoes.

"How was the magic?"

"I told you. No paper."

"I mean yesterday."

"Oh."

She smiled.

I leaned against the table.

"It is still good."

"Good."

"Everyone keeps asking what I can do."

"What can you do?"

"Move paper."

"Then tell them."

"I do."

"And?"

"They ask what else."

"What else?"

"Nothing."

Lyssa nodded.

There was no cruelty in it.

That made it worse.

I looked at the folded question.

Tomorrow Hessa did not want me.

Tomorrow the hall did.

I had one small piece of real magic and no use for it.

I had a repaired curtain, a red door that stood if nobody kicked it, two
copper from a day of work, and a question about which direction paper
preferred.

That seemed like enough things to own at once.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 184

## THE RUNNER

Lyssa asked me for a favor before I had finished chewing.

That should have warned me.

"You're going east?"

"Eventually."

"Good."

She put a parcel on the table.

It was wrapped in gray cloth and tied twice with blue thread.

I looked at it.

"No."

Lyssa stopped.

"What?"

"I am not moving it."

"I want you to carry it."

"Oh."

She stared at me.

"Your life has become strange."

"Recently."

The parcel was not heavy. It was about the size of two folded shirts and
soft enough that I could wedge it under my arm.

"Where?"

"Marra's."

"I know Marra's."

"Do you?"

"Green door."

"Good."

"Not Sen's."

"Very good."

"I made that mistake once."

"You nearly made that mistake once."

"That still counts as learning."

Lyssa tore bread in half.

"Give it to Marra. Tell her the left side is marked. Do not untie it."

"Why would I untie it?"

"I know you."

"That is not an answer."

"It has worked so far."

I took the parcel.

"Anything else?"

"Yes. If Jessa is there, tell her I said the narrow one, not the wide
one."

"The narrow what?"

"She'll know."

"I don't."

"You don't need to."

This was apparently what trust looked like.

I finished breakfast with the parcel against my leg.

My right palm was still tender. The scrap cloth I had wrapped around the
crutch grip after calling the show had shifted overnight and bunched
under my hand. I retied it.

Lyssa watched.

"You should replace that."

"It works."

"It is dirty."

"Those are different questions."

"You've been spending too much time with Hessa."

I smiled before I meant to.

Lyssa noticed.

"Still pleased?"

"Yes."

"You moved paper."

"Yes."

"A very small amount."

"Yes."

"Under glass."

"Yes."

"While supervised."

"Yes."

"Good."

"Why are you doing this?"

"Because you're smiling at bread."

I stopped smiling.

Mostly.

Lyssa picked up her bag.

"I'll be west until midday. Maybe longer."

"People?"

"People."

"Blue thread?"

"Not today."

"What today?"

She kissed me.

"Work."

Then she left.

I had the parcel, my crutches, three copper on the table, a theatre call
before midday, and no reason to go near the Guild.

It seemed manageable.

That was my second warning.

Marra's was not on the way to East Market Hall.

It was only a few streets wrong.

A few streets on crutches was still a few streets.

I went north first, cut across the cloth lane, and found the green door
open.

Marra was inside with one sleeve pinned around a wooden arm and a mouth
full of pins.

I held up the parcel.

"Lyssa."

Marra pointed at a table.

I put it there.

"The left side is marked."

Marra removed two pins from her mouth.

"I know."

"Good."

"Did she tell you that?"

"Yes."

"Then why did you tell me?"

"Because she told me to."

Marra looked at me for a moment.

"Useful."

"Thank you."

"Not you."

She cut the blue thread.

I watched the knot fall loose.

Inside was dark cloth.

Not the same dark cloth from Lyssa's table, unless all dark cloth looked
like dark cloth to me, which seemed possible.

Marra unfolded one edge.

A row of tiny chalk marks ran along it.

"Jessa here?"

"No."

"I have a message."

"Then she's not here."

"I understand the concept."

Marra put two pins back in her mouth.

"She'll be at Sen's."

I stared at her.

"Red door."

Marra nodded.

"Of course."

"Tell her narrow, not wide."

"Yes."

I had delivered one parcel and somehow acquired the rest of the errand.

Sen's red door was two lanes back.

I had almost reached it when someone called my name.

I kept going.

"Greg."

I stopped.

Nessa was standing outside a shop with three coils of cord over one
shoulder and a small paper packet in her hand.

"Hall."

"I'm going."

"Now."

"I have an errand."

"So do I."

She handed me the paper packet.

I looked at it.

"No."

"What?"

"Nothing."

The packet was full of something hard and small.

"What's this?"

"Hooks."

"Where?"

"Hall."

"You're going to the hall."

"No."

"Where are you going?"

Nessa looked past me toward the next street.

"Somewhere else."

Then she left.

I now had a message for Jessa and a packet of hooks for the theatre.

Neither was heavy.

Together they were irritating.

I went to Sen's.

The red door was closed.

I knocked.

A man opened it.

"Jessa?"

"No."

"Is she here?"

"No."

"Do you know where she is?"

"Yes."

I waited.

He waited.

"Where?"

"Marra's."

I looked back toward the lane I had just crossed.

"No."

He shrugged.

"She was going there."

"When?"

"Before."

"How long before?"

He thought.

"Some."

I hated Carrow.

I went back to Marra's.

Jessa was there.

She was standing exactly where I had put Lyssa's parcel.

Marra was not.

I pointed at her.

"Narrow, not wide."

Jessa nodded.

"Good."

"Do you know what that means?"

"Yes."

"Excellent."

She looked at the packet under my arm.

"What is that?"

"Hooks."

"For what?"

"I don't know."

She smiled.

"You are useful today."

"Apparently."

I left before she could give me anything.

By the time I reached East Market Hall, my palm hurt and I was late
enough that Rinna was standing in the doorway.

She looked at me.

I looked at her.

"I have hooks."

"You're late."

"I also had cloth."

"Do you still have cloth?"

"No."

"Then I don't care."

I handed her the packet.

She opened it.

"These aren't ours."

I stared.

"Nessa gave them to me."

"These are costume hooks."

"Yes?"

"She needed curtain hooks."

"They are hooks."

Rinna looked at me.

I understood how Pell felt around Hessa.

"Where is Nessa?"

"Somewhere else."

"Helpful."

"I asked."

"Did you ask what kind of hooks?"

"No."

Rinna handed the packet back.

"Take those to costumes."

"Where are the curtain hooks?"

"With Nessa, apparently."

I carried the wrong hooks twelve paces into the building and gave them
to a woman sewing a brown coat.

She looked inside.

"Good."

"Are they?"

"Yes."

That improved nothing.

Teren was onstage.

"Greg."

"Yes."

"Third Man."

"My goat?"

"Your goat."

Finally.

"Then after, take this."

He handed me a folded note.

I did not take it.

"Where?"

"South market. Lamp oil seller beside the cooper."

"Why?"

"Tell him six small tins tomorrow, not four. Rinna will pay on
delivery."

"Why me?"

"You're going that way."

"I'm not."

"You are now."

I took the note.

"I have a show."

"Yes."

"When am I supposed to go?"

"After."

"After the show?"

"After your piece."

"There's more show after my piece."

"Correct."

"Do I come back?"

"If you want."

This was not a schedule.

It was weather.

I put the note inside my coat.

The Petition ran that afternoon.

I got my goat back.

I stood behind the side cloth, listened to Jori call the first entrance,
and waited for the line before mine.

The curtain moved smoothly.

I noticed again.

Then I stopped noticing.

Sellen said, "My neighbor has complained to half the street and all the
watch."

That was not my cue.

I waited.

Veya answered.

Sellen crossed.

Then came the line.

"My neighbor says the animal has damaged his property."

Jori pointed.

I went.

"My neighbor's goat has eaten all my patience and half my fence."

Sellen turned.

"My father sent me because the goat is innocent."

The audience laughed.

I did not add anything.

Sellen continued.

I continued.

The scene worked.

It was almost disappointing.

Afterward, Teren caught me at the side gap.

"Good."

"That's all?"

"What do you want?"

"I don't know."

"Then good."

He shoved the red doorframe toward me.

"Move."

I moved it.

The repaired foot held.

The curtain worked.

The fake petition went back into its box.

Nobody asked me about magic.

For almost an hour, I forgot I had any.

Then Pell passed carrying a painted bucket.

"Magician."

"Props."

He kept walking.

That was the entire conversation.

I liked it better.

I left for the south market after my piece.

The lamp seller beside the cooper was farther south than Teren had
implied.

Everything was farther when somebody else described it.

I had made it halfway when the cloth around my right crutch grip twisted
again.

I stopped beside a cobbler's stall and fixed it.

The cobbler watched.

"That's terrible."

"It was free."

"Still terrible."

He had a basket of leather scraps under the bench.

I looked at it.

He looked at me.

"How much for a strip?"

"What size?"

I showed him the grip.

He took my crutch before I could object, turned it once, then pulled a
narrow brown strip from the basket.

"This."

"How much?"

"One copper."

"That's too much for scrap."

"It's good scrap."

"It's in a basket on the ground."

"Then use your cloth."

I looked at the cloth.

The cloth looked back.

It was not going to improve.

"Half."

"No."

"Half and I don't tell people you charge full copper for rubbish."

"One and I wrap it."

"Half and you don't."

"One."

I paid one copper.

He wrapped it.

That changed the argument.

The leather went around the grip tight and smooth, with the seam away
from my palm. He tied it under itself and cut the end.

I put weight on the crutch.

Better.

Annoyingly better.

"Good scrap," he said.

"I dislike you."

"Walk on it."

"I don't walk on that end."

He laughed.

I left.

The lamp seller was beside the cooper.

Teren had been right about that part.

I gave him the note.

He read it.

"Six?"

"Yes."

"Tomorrow?"

"Yes."

"Not four?"

"Apparently this is important."

"It is if he wants six."

"Do you have six?"

"Tomorrow."

"Good."

He folded the note.

"Tell Teren I want the old tins back."

"Why?"

"Because they're mine."

"That seems fair."

"Tell him."

I had become a message with crutches.

On the way back, I passed the Guild street.

I did not need to go there.

I kept going.

Then a boy in a gray Guild vest came out of the side entrance and
called, "Greg?"

I stopped.

This was becoming a problem.

He held a folded paper.

"For you."

"From Hessa?"

He looked at the front.

"Yes."

I took it.

"Do I need to go in?"

"No."

"Good."

I opened it on the street.

DAY AFTER TOMORROW. AFTER MIDDAY. BRING THE QUESTION. NO TESTING BEFORE.

Under that, in smaller writing:

THIS INCLUDES PAPER.

I read it twice.

The boy was still there.

"Does she usually add that?"

"I don't know."

"Good answer."

He went back inside.

Two workers were carrying a long shallow crate through the side
entrance. One of them complained that it had been marked for east
storage. The other said something about south.

I kept walking.

Hessa had scheduled direction.

Not tomorrow.

The day after.

I folded the note and put it with Teren's lamp message, then remembered
I had already delivered Teren's lamp message.

My coat was becoming an office.

I reached the hall during the last piece.

Nobody had missed me.

That was useful information.

Rinna was counting coins.

"Six tins tomorrow," I said. "He wants the old tins back."

"Good."

"I bought leather."

"I can see."

"One copper."

"Why are you telling me?"

"I don't know."

She looked at my hand.

"Better?"

"Yes."

"Then stop complaining."

"I wasn't."

"You were preparing."

She was right.

I helped clean.

A costume sleeve had torn under the arm. The woman with the brown coat
was already fixing it using one of the hooks Nessa had given me.

So the hooks had not been wrong.

They had merely been wrong for Rinna.

That felt familiar.

Jori rolled his cue page.

Sellen stole the last baker roll.

Hara accused him.

He denied it while chewing.

The hall had another evening show.

I did not.

Rinna told me I was done.

"Paid?"

She gave me one copper.

It replaced the one I had spent on leather.

That felt less like earning money than moving it through myself.

"Tomorrow?"

"Morning setup. Evening maybe."

"Role?"

"Maybe."

"Don't."

She smiled.

I left.

Lyssa was not home.

I was beginning to suspect she had a job.

I put Hessa's note on the table beside my folded question.

FORWARD?

MY FORWARD OR BOARD FORWARD?

The note looked like an answer to a different question.

DAY AFTER TOMORROW.

I had tomorrow first.

There was still one copper from the previous days on the table, plus the
copper I had just earned. I had spent one on the grip, so the pile had
not changed much.

I touched the new leather.

Worth it.

Then I realized I had forgotten to tell Jessa anything except narrow,
not wide.

That was exactly what Lyssa had told me to say.

So perhaps I had succeeded.

Lyssa came home after dark carrying a basket.

"Did you take it?"

"Yes."

"To Marra?"

"Yes."

"Did you tell her left side?"

"Yes."

"Jessa?"

"Eventually."

Lyssa stopped.

"What happened?"

"She wasn't at Marra's."

"She was supposed to be."

"She went to Sen's."

"Why?"

"I don't know. Then she went back to Marra's."

Lyssa closed her eyes.

"I found her."

"Did you tell her?"

"Narrow, not wide."

"Good."

"That is all I know."

"That is all you needed to know."

I pointed at her.

"Exactly."

She put the basket down.

Inside were onions, a small wrapped piece of cheese, and two things that
might have been turnips.

"What's that?"

"Food."

"I know what food is."

"Do you?"

"I had beans yesterday."

"That is evidence."

I took the basket to the table.

"Hessa sent a note."

"When?"

"I passed the Guild."

"You weren't going to the Guild."

"I didn't."

"Then how did you get a note?"

"They found me."

Lyssa looked at the paper.

"Day after tomorrow."

"Yes."

"Direction?"

"Probably."

She read my question.

"MY FORWARD OR BOARD FORWARD?"

"Yes."

"That's ugly."

"Thank you."

"What does it mean?"

"I don't know yet."

"Good question."

I looked at her.

She started cutting an onion.

"You sound like Hessa."

"Hostile."

"Accurate."

I sat.

My palm still hurt, but the crutch grip no longer rubbed the sore place
when I leaned it against the table.

Lyssa noticed the leather.

"New?"

"One copper."

"Good."

"The man was a thief."

"Is it better?"

"Yes."

"Then maybe not."

"He charged me for scrap."

"He wrapped it."

I hated that everyone understood this except me.

We ate late.

The turnips were turnips.

Neither of us had expected the other to have food ready, so the meal was
bread, onion, cheese, boiled turnips, and the last of yesterday's beans.

It was fine.

Lyssa's day had involved three people changing what they wanted after
cloth had already been cut.

Mine had involved carrying her parcel to the correct green door, walking
to the red door anyway, carrying Nessa's hooks, performing a goat
complaint, buying leather, carrying Teren's message south, receiving
Hessa's message while not visiting Hessa, and returning to a theatre
that had continued perfectly well without me.

"Whose was worse?" I asked.

"Mine."

"You didn't hear mine."

"I lived mine."

"Unfair."

"Yes."

I ate cheese.

Afterward I put tomorrow's theatre time on a scrap beside Hessa's note.

MORNING HALL. EVENING MAYBE.

Under that:

DAY AFTER TOMORROW. HESSA. QUESTION.

I looked at the two pieces of paper.

Neither moved.

I did not ask them to.

Tomorrow I had theatre.

The day after, I had magic.

Tonight I had dishes.

I did those first.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 185

## THE REGULAR

The new leather grip was already dirty.

I decided that meant it belonged to me.

It was also better.

My palm still had a sore patch from the curtain cord, but the crutch no
longer twisted a wad of cloth into it every time I leaned. I noticed
this on the way to East Market Hall because I was carrying nothing.

This lasted until the door.

Rinna handed me three empty lamp tins.

"Morning."

"Good morning."

"Find the other three."

I looked at the tins.

"Where?"

"If I knew, I would have six."

The hall was already awake.

Someone had opened the rear doors, and a pale strip of morning crossed
the floor through dust and paint smell. Jori was onstage with the
curtain half open. Hara stood in one stocking beside a costume basket,
arguing that two brown stockings were not the same brown. Veya was
reading a page while somebody pinned the back of her dress. Pell's legs
were visible beneath a table.

I stepped around them.

"Why do we need six?"

"The oil man is coming."

"I know."

"Then why did you ask?"

"I was hoping the number had changed."

"It hasn't."

Three tins knocked together under my arm.

I knew where the lamp shelf was.

That was new enough that I noticed it, then annoying enough that I
stopped.

Two more old tins sat behind the blue lamp pieces, both empty except for
a greasy smell and one dead moth.

Five.

"Five," I called.

"Six," Rinna called back.

"I understand counting."

"Then find six."

Pell's legs disappeared under the table.

I looked at the stage.

The sixth tin could have been anywhere.

That was the problem with a place where everything became something
else. A crate was a seat until it was a wall. A bowl was supper until it
was a crown. A lamp-oil tin was probably lamp oil until Pell saw it.

"Pell."

Nothing.

"Pell."

"What?"

"Where is the sixth oil tin?"

"Which one?"

"The sixth."

His head appeared beneath the table.

"That's not a description."

"The small square ones. We have five."

"We have more than five."

"Excellent. Where?"

He disappeared again.

I waited.

A hand emerged holding a wooden wheel.

"Not a tin."

"I know."

"Then why did you give it to me?"

"I didn't."

I put the wheel on the table.

Nessa came through carrying a folded painted cloth.

She handed one end to me without stopping.

I took it.

"Where?"

"Back rail."

I knew the back rail.

I followed.

The cloth was painted with pale stone blocks on one side. Nessa walked
backward, guiding it around a bench.

"Not on the floor."

"I know."

"Then lift."

I lifted.

We got it onto the rail.

She tied one corner, looked at my side, and said, "Higher."

I raised it.

"Other higher."

I raised the wrong hand.

"No."

"I know what other means."

"Apparently not."

We got the cloth hung.

She left.

I looked at the five tins still under my arm.

I had forgotten I was holding them.

That seemed dangerous.

I put them beside Rinna's table.

A young man I had seen twice but never spoken to came through the side
door carrying the fake loaf.

"Where?"

"Prop shelf. Second level, left."

He went.

Nessa's voice came from behind me.

"No."

I closed my eyes.

The young man stopped.

"Where?"

"Kitchen box today," Nessa said.

He turned around.

I pointed at Nessa.

"She changes things."

"It was on the shelf yesterday," I said.

"Yesterday it wasn't in The Empty Table."

"What is The Empty Table?"

"Third piece."

I looked at the young man.

"Kitchen box."

He went.

Nessa looked at me.

"I was right yesterday."

"Very useful today."

Then she left.

I knew enough to be wrong faster.

That was progress of a kind.

The oil seller arrived before we found the sixth tin.

He came through the front with a wooden carrier holding six sealed tins
and the expression of a man who had been told he would receive six old
tins.

Rinna met him.

"Five now," she said.

"Six old."

"Yes."

"Six new."

"I can see them."

"Six old."

Rinna looked toward me.

I disliked being included in this.

"Looking."

The seller put the carrier down but kept one hand on it.

"No old, one copper extra."

"For the tin?"

"For not returning it."

"It's my tin."

"Then return it."

Rinna looked at me again.

I went to find Pell.

He was no longer under the table.

The table had acquired a woman sewing a hat.

"Where's Pell?"

She pointed down.

I looked.

No Pell.

"Not there."

"He was."

"Everyone was."

I went backstage.

Jori was testing the repaired curtain wheel. It rolled quietly enough
that the cloth moved without the old scrape.

"Seen Pell?"

"Rear."

I went rear.

Davin was crouched beside a wagon wheel with a hammer and a block of
wood.

"Seen Pell?"

"Yes."

"Where?"

Davin pointed toward the hall.

I had just come from the hall.

"Recently?"

"No."

"Thank you."

He glanced at the new leather grip.

"Better."

"Yes."

That was the whole conversation.

I went back inside.

My right shoulder had started to complain before my palm did. This
seemed unfair but predictable.

Hara was now wearing both stockings.

They were not the same brown.

Nobody had died.

"Have you seen Pell?"

"Yes."

"Where?"

"He asked me for red powder."

"When?"

"Morning."

"It is morning."

"Earlier morning."

"What did he want red powder for?"

Hara shrugged.

That was enough.

Red powder meant props or paint or fake blood, depending on which person
had said it.

I checked the prop shelf.

No Pell.

I checked the side table.

No Pell.

Then I saw a square metal corner beneath a stack of folded rags.

I pulled them aside.

The sixth lamp tin sat open on the table.

It was full of nails.

I stared at it.

Pell appeared behind me.

"Those are mine."

"The nails?"

"Yes."

"The tin?"

"No."

"Good."

He reached for the nails.

I stopped him.

"Where do they go?"

"In the tin."

"Not anymore."

"Then where?"

I found a cracked wooden cup.

Pell looked offended.

"That's the blood cup."

"Is there blood in it?"

"No."

"Then it's the nail cup."

"It leaks."

"Nails don't."

He considered this.

"Fine."

We poured the nails into the cup.

One fell through the crack.

Pell picked it up.

"See?"

"It lost one."

"You found it."

"Still."

I took the tin.

"Rinna is waiting."

"Tell her I needed it."

"You didn't."

"I needed something like it."

"That's worse."

The oil seller got his sixth tin.

Rinna got six new ones.

Nobody paid an extra copper.

The entire crisis had lasted long enough for Jori to finish checking the
curtain, Hara to change stockings again, Nessa to hang two cloths, and
Teren to decide the afternoon bill did not need The Empty Table after
all.

The fake loaf moved back to the prop shelf.

The young man who had asked me where it went looked at me.

I pointed.

"Don't."

He laughed.

By midday I had swept the left passage, carried two benches, taken a
coat to costumes, returned the coat because it was the wrong coat, and
moved the ghost tube before someone put a basket on it.

Nobody told me to move the tube.

I just did.

Pell came around the corner carrying the basket.

He stopped.

"Where'd it go?"

"Tube."

"Oh."

He put the basket somewhere else.

That felt better than magic.

Not more important.

Just more useful before lunch.

Teren found me sitting on the low curtain box with my crutches against
my knee.

"Evening."

"Maybe?"

"Yes."

"That means yes?"

"It does now."

"Role?"

He handed me four pages.

"Witness."

"In what?"

"Third."

Then he walked away.

I stared after him.

"Third what?"

He did not answer.

Jori did.

"The Empty Table."

I looked toward the prop shelf.

The young man with the fake loaf was already laughing.

"Of course."

The Empty Table turned out to be about an inheritance.

There was a dead aunt, three cousins, a missing will, a servant who knew
too much, and a table nobody was allowed to touch because the aunt had
supposedly died beside it.

The table was not empty.

It had a fake loaf, a wooden knife, a cup, two folded papers, and a bowl
with one real onion in it.

I pointed this out.

Veya said, "The title is old."

"Was the table empty before?"

"No."

"Then why?"

"Greg."

"Yes?"

"Read."

I read.

The Witness had six lines.

I had seen the piece once from the back wall, but I had not known it was
called The Empty Table because nobody had needed me to know.

My job was to enter after the second cousin accused the servant, say I
had seen the aunt alive after sunset, identify which cousin had been at
the rear gate, then leave after everybody decided I was lying.

I liked the Witness.

He knew one thing.

Everyone hated him for it.

Simple.

We ran the entrance once.

Jori called it.

I entered from the wrong side.

Veya stopped.

"Other."

"I know."

"You said that this morning."

"That was cloth."

"This is you."

I went around.

The second entrance worked.

During the break I went outside for air.

Carrow was busy in the ordinary way that had nothing to do with me.

A fish seller was throwing gray water into the gutter. Two boys rolled a
barrel downhill until a woman shouted at them. A wagon blocked half the
lane while somebody argued over sacks. Across the market, I saw Lyssa.

She was talking to a woman I didn't know.

The woman held a folded strip of yellow cloth. Lyssa had one hand on her
hip and the other pointing toward the cloth as if the cloth had
personally caused trouble.

Lyssa looked up.

She saw me.

I raised a hand.

She raised two fingers.

Then the woman said something, and Lyssa turned back to her.

I went inside.

I did not know who the woman was.

I survived.

The baker rolls arrived before the evening show.

Teren stood beside the tray.

I approached carefully.

"No."

"I haven't done anything."

"You were going to."

"That is not evidence."

"It is experience."

He turned away to answer Jori.

I took a roll.

Nobody stopped me.

I stood there with it in my hand, suspicious.

Hara walked past.

"You look guilty."

"I succeeded."

"At bread?"

"Yes."

"Congratulations."

She took one too.

The evening crowd came in slowly.

I worked the door until Rinna told me not to.

A man asked where the privy was.

I knew.

A woman asked whether the left passage was open.

"No."

"Why?"

"Quick change."

"Whose?"

"I don't know."

She went around.

That was apparently the correct amount of truth.

A boy carrying two tickets asked me where the ghost was.

"Not tonight."

"Tomorrow?"

"I don't know."

He looked disappointed.

"Paper?" Pell said from behind me.

"Go away."

The boy looked at Pell.

"What paper?"

"Nothing," I said.

Pell went away.

The rumor was dying.

I appreciated his effort to save it less than he probably intended.

The first piece was a short comedy I did not know.

I carried a stool on, carried it off, and missed nothing.

The second was The Red Door.

The repaired frame held.

A child did not tell anyone to go around.

This felt like a loss.

Then came The Empty Table.

I waited on the correct side.

The leather grip had darkened under my hand from sweat and dust, but it
stayed smooth.

Jori pointed.

I entered.

Veya, playing the oldest cousin, turned on me.

"You were at the lane."

"I was."

"You saw her?"

"After sunset."

"Alive?"

"Angry."

A laugh.

That line was written.

Good.

"Who else was there?"

I looked at the younger cousin.

He looked guilty.

This was also his job.

"Him."

The younger cousin stood.

"You're lying."

"No."

"You've hated me since spring."

"I met you this morning."

That got a bigger laugh.

That line was also written.

I liked this play.

The servant crossed behind the table.

Veya said, "Then why were you at the rear gate?"

I answered.

"Because your aunt paid me to watch it."

The younger cousin came toward me.

That was where I was supposed to back away.

I did.

My crutch hit the edge of the table.

The fake loaf fell.

It landed on the floor.

For one very long moment, everyone looked at it.

The loaf was painted cloth over something light. From the house it
looked acceptable.

On the floor, upside down, it looked like a dead pillow.

The younger cousin looked at it.

I looked at it.

He said, "Even the bread wants out."

The audience laughed.

I bent automatically.

Veya said, "Leave it."

I stopped.

The scene continued.

The loaf stayed on the floor for another two lines until the servant
kicked it under the table without looking.

Backstage, Nessa was waiting.

"I know."

"What?"

"Don't hit the table."

"No."

I blinked.

"No?"

"Don't pick up the loaf."

"I didn't."

"You started to."

"It fell."

"The audience saw it fall. If you stop the scene to fix it, they watch
you fix it."

I looked toward the stage.

The servant was accusing Veya of hiding the will.

The loaf was gone.

"Right."

Nessa handed me a folded cloth.

"Costumes."

I took it.

"Which chest?"

"Wall."

I hesitated.

She looked at me.

"Actually wall?"

"Yes."

"Today?"

"Greg."

I went.

The rest of my work was ordinary.

That was the part I noticed most.

I knew where the folded cloth went.

I knew not to use the left passage during Hara's change.

I knew which stool had the bad leg and put it against the wall before
anyone sat on it.

I knew the lamp boy needed the long taper, not the short one, for the
last piece.

I knew the blue lamp was not a lamp anyone should light for ordinary
light.

I knew where Pell would be when he wasn't where he was supposed to be.

Usually.

I was wrong twice.

Once about a coat.

Once about a rope.

The rope had moved to the rear peg because tomorrow's setup needed the
front peg clear.

I said, "It was here."

Rinna said, "Yes."

That was all.

The building changed while I learned it.

Apparently that was allowed.

After the show, Rinna paid me one copper.

"Witness and crew."

"I dropped bread."

"You hit a table."

"The bread fell."

"Yes."

"The audience laughed."

"Not because you were good."

"I didn't say that."

"You were going to."

I put the copper away.

"Tomorrow?"

"No morning."

I waited.

"After midday," she said. "Maybe evening."

"I have Hessa after midday."

Rinna looked at me.

"Paper?"

"Direction."

"Is that better?"

"I don't know."

"Then come after."

"If we're done."

"With paper?"

"Yes."

She nodded.

"Come after paper."

That was the entire scheduling negotiation.

Magic had become something I had to tell work about.

I was not sure how I felt about that.

Mostly I felt tired.

When I left, the hall still had people in it.

Nessa and the brown-coat woman were arguing about a hem. Jori was
writing on tomorrow's cue page. Pell had reclaimed the cracked wooden
cup for nails and was pretending this had always been the plan. Teren
was eating the last baker roll.

I pointed at him.

"You stopped me yesterday."

"Yesterday was yesterday."

I hated this place.

I went home smiling.

Lyssa was already there.
