That was unusual enough that I checked the room as if I might have
entered the wrong one.

She was sitting at the table with her shoes off and a wet cloth around
one ankle.

"What happened?"

"Nothing."

"That's wet."

"Yes."

"Why?"

"My ankle hurts."

"What did you do?"

"Worked."

I put my crutches down.

"Hostile."

"Accurate."

There was bread on the table.

I sat.

"Hessa tomorrow."

"I know."

"How?"

"You left the note under the cup."

"Right."

Lyssa looked at my coat.

"You smell like oil."

"Six tins."

"Of oil?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

"Because there were five."

She waited.

I realized that did not explain anything.

"There were supposed to be six old tins. Pell put nails in one."

"Of course."

"You don't know Pell."

"I know enough."

I ate bread.

"My grip works."

"Good."

"One copper."

"You told me."

"It still works."

"Excellent."

"I was a Witness."

"To what?"

"An inheritance."

"Did you inherit anything?"

"One copper."

"Good inheritance."

"I also dropped bread."

"Real?"

"Fake."

"Then you didn't drop bread."

I thought about Hessa.

"I dropped the representation of bread."

Lyssa stared at me.

"No."

"Fair."

I got up to wash.

My coat had a pale streak of stage dust along one sleeve. There was
something black under my thumbnail, probably charcoal or paint. The
leather grip was dirty.

Tomorrow's note was still under the cup.

AFTER MIDDAY. BRING THE QUESTION.

I took out the question.

FORWARD?

MY FORWARD OR BOARD FORWARD?

I did not improve it.

Today I had found a lamp tin, been wrong about a loaf, been wrong about
a door, been right about a passage, carried a stool, played a Witness,
knocked fake bread onto the floor, and earned one copper.

Tomorrow I was going to ask paper which way forward was.

I put the question back under the note.

Then I went to bed before anything else could need carrying.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 186

## THE TESTER

Lyssa's ankle was still wrapped when I woke.

Not badly. She was already standing on it.

I pointed.

"No."

"You don't know what I was going to say."

"Sit down."

"That wasn't it."

"It was close."

She put bread on the table and sat long enough to cut it.

I had Hessa's note beside my cup and my question under it.

FORWARD?

MY FORWARD OR BOARD FORWARD?

Lyssa read it again.

"Paper today?"

"Direction."

"Which direction?"

"That's the problem."

She nodded as if this were a reasonable answer.

"Are you working?"

"Yes."

"With the ankle?"

"Yes."

"Hessa would object."

"Hessa doesn't supervise cloth."

"She should expand."

Lyssa gave me bread.

I was still smiling sometimes for no reason.

Not at paper anymore. I had learned that staring at paper made Lyssa
hostile.

I smiled at the bread instead.

She saw.

"Very small amount."

"Yes."

"Under glass."

"Yes."

"While supervised."

"Yes."

"You are still pleased."

"Extremely."

"Good."

Then she left before me.

I did not go to the hall.

That felt wrong.

I had spent enough mornings there that walking past the turn toward East
Market made me feel as if I had forgotten something. I checked my coat,
my question, and the copper in my pocket.

Nothing forgotten.

The Guild had me after midday.

Until then, I had nowhere I was required to be.

This was almost suspicious.

I bought a meat bun and sat near the lower market long enough to eat it
while it was still hot.

Nobody handed me anything.

Nobody asked where Pell was.

Nobody needed a fake loaf.

It was peaceful.

I disliked it after about ten minutes.

By the time I reached the Guild, Hessa was not ready.

Of course.

The clerk at the front knew me now.

That was not necessarily good.

"Wait."

"For Hessa?"

"Yes. Sit."

I sat.

The chair was against the wall near the same long table where people
left things they did not want to hold while arguing. Today it had two
wrapped bundles, a cracked prepared plate in a shallow tray, and a box
with three seals hanging from strings.

The cracked plate had acquired another note.

DO NOT REPAIR UNTIL CLEARED.

Someone had written beneath it:

I WASN'T.

I liked the Guild slightly more.

The gray-coated woman passed through carrying a stack of papers.

She saw me.

"Paper man."

"No."

She kept walking.

That was worse than Pell.

Hessa appeared several minutes later with ink on two fingers and
irritation on her face.

"Come."

"Good afternoon."

"No."

I followed her.

"Bad afternoon?"

"Unfinished."

"That seems normal here."

She looked back at me.

"Do you want to wait?"

"No."

"Then don't improve it."

The room was not the same one we had used last time.

The old table was there, or one much like it, but the wall behind it had
shelves with shallow boxes, folded cloth, weights, and a stack of wooden
frames. Hessa had cleared the center.

The apparatus sat on the table.

I recognized it and didn't.

The paired wooden guides remained. The glass shield remained. The paper
fins were new again, cut from the same pale sheet and folded at the
bottom. Hessa had added a flat square board beneath the guides.

There were four black marks on the board, one near each edge.

One was a line.

One was two lines.

One was a dot.

One was a cross.

"Directions?"

"Marks."

"Those are directions."

"They will be if I tell you they are."

I put my crutches against the wall.

"That's annoying."

"Good."

She held out her hand.

I gave her my question.

She read it.

Then she read it again.

"Better."

I stared at her.

"This is the ugliest question I've brought you."

"Yes."

"And this is better."

"Yes."

"Why?"

She put the paper beside the board.

"What is your forward?"

I pointed away from my chest.

"That."

"What is board forward?"

I pointed toward the black line.

"That."

"If I turn the board?"

"The line turns."

"If you move?"

"My forward turns."

"Good."

She looked at the question.

"It asks two things I can separate."

"I liked my other questions."

"They were prettier."

I was offended because she was right.

Hessa checked the table legs.

Then the wedges.

Then the glass.

She removed the shield and set two fins into the guides.

"Do not touch the table."

"I know."

"Do not touch the chair after I set it."

"I know."

"Do not move your crutches once we begin."

"They're against the wall."

"Good."

She placed the chair.

I sat.

The table was slightly too far away.

I shifted the chair.

Hessa waited.

"Now?"

"Yes."

She moved it half a hand closer.

"Now?"

"Yes."

"Then stay."

I put my good foot flat.

The missing leg did what it always did, which was not participate.

Hessa looked down.

"Can you hold that position without bracing on the table?"

"Yes."

"Show me."

I held it.

My shoulder objected mildly.

"Fine."

She put the glass shield down.

The fins stood beneath it.

One on the left.

One on the right.

Tiny.

Ridiculous.

Real.

I wanted one to move so badly that I had to look at the table edge for a
moment instead.

Hessa mixed two small wooden squares beneath the table and set one
beside each guide with the marked sides hidden.

Same as before.

I would not know which fin she had designated until she showed me.

"Minimal first."

I nodded.

The draw came cleanly.

It was still small.

Still supervised.

Still familiar.

I had spent days not touching it, and the sensation returned with an
almost insulting lack of ceremony. A narrow inward gathering. The same
difficult-to-name awareness along the wrist edge. The same sense that
one side of the shape was easier to find than the other.

I held.

Hessa watched.

"Release."

I released.

She wrote.

That made twenty-five successful supervised minimal draws.

"Again. Shape only. No external target."

I drew again.

The deliberate shape took longer.

The wrist-edge asymmetry was there.

I kept myself from leaning toward it.

Hessa saw something in my face.

"Don't chase."

"I'm not."

"Good."

I formed the shape as evenly as I could.

Held.

Released.

Twenty-one successful supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Hessa wrote again.

Then she lifted the wooden square beside the left guide.

A black mark underneath.

"Left target."

I looked at the left fin.

"Direction?"

"Line."

The black line on the board pointed away from me.

Board forward and my forward were the same.

"That's not helpful."

"It is first."

"Right."

She checked the glass again.

"Begin when settled."

I drew.

Shaped.

The target sat beneath the glass.

Forward.

Barely.

Not move.

Not jump.

Not slide across the table.

Forward.

I tried to make the intention smaller than the thing itself.

The familiar asymmetry sharpened at the wrist edge.

The paper moved.

It was tiny.

A dry little scrape.

I opened my mouth.

Hessa raised one finger.

I shut it.

She leaned over the glass without touching it.

The left fin had shifted forward perhaps half the width of my smallest
fingernail.

The right fin had not visibly moved.

Hessa checked the wedges.

She checked the board.

She checked the guide.

She crouched to look at my foot.

"I didn't move."

"I know."

"Then why are you looking?"

"Because knowing is not checking."

I shut up.

She marked the board beside the fin.

Then she lifted the glass, removed both fins, and examined their bases.

"Nothing obvious."

"Good?"

"It means nothing obvious."

She wrote.

That was the tenth supervised external-effect attempt.

One movement.

In the intended direction.

Also away from me.

Also board forward.

It proved almost nothing about the question.

I hated useful preparation.

Hessa turned the entire square board.

The black line now pointed to my left.

The guides turned with it.

The chair did not.

"Same target side?"

"Different fin. Different assignment."

She changed both fins.

Mixed the wooden squares again.

This time the right fin was target.

"Direction?"

"Line."

I looked at the line.

My left.

Board forward.

Not my forward.

"Try."

I drew.

The effort felt the same until it didn't.

I shaped toward the line.

Left.

The wrist-edge sensation wanted to become important.

I refused to decide what it meant.

The fin did nothing.

I held until Hessa said, "Release."

Nothing.

Control nothing.

Eleventh supervised external-effect attempt.

Hessa inspected anyway.

"Again?" I asked.

"No."

She rotated the board back.

"Why?"

"Because I want a different question."

She stood behind me.

"Get up."

I reached for the crutches.

"Careful."

"I know how to stand."

"Not without shaking my table."

That was fair.

I stood without touching it.

Hessa moved the chair to the opposite side of the table.

This took longer than the magical part.

I had to get around the table without hitting the board, the glass, the
chair, the shelf, or Hessa. My crutch tip caught the chair leg once.

Hessa said, "Stop."

I stopped.

She moved the chair herself.

"This is humiliating."

"This is furniture."

"Same thing sometimes."

When I sat on the opposite side, the board's black line pointed toward
me.

Board forward had not changed.

My forward had.

"Now they're opposite."

"Yes."

She changed the fins again.

New assignment.

Left target.

"Line again?"

"Line."

I stared at the target.

To move board forward, it would have to move toward me.

That felt wrong before I even drew.

I did not know if that mattered.

I drew.

Shaped.

The familiar asymmetry returned.

I tried to aim through it toward the line.

Toward me.

Nothing happened.

I held.

Nothing.

"Release."

I released.

Twelfth supervised external-effect attempt.

No movement.

Hessa inspected.

Nothing.

I leaned back.

"So it doesn't like toward me."

"No."

"It moved away from me before."

"Once today."

"And before."

"Under different arrangements."

"It moved forward twice before."

"Board forward."

"Which was also away from me."

"Yes."

I smiled.

Hessa did not.

"Don't."

"I didn't say anything."

"You were going to."

Everyone had become unbearable.

Hessa rotated the board again.

Now the line pointed to my right.

From my new seat, my forward was toward the opposite wall.

The line was sideways.

"Different intended direction," I said.

"Yes."

"Still board forward."

"Yes."

She changed the target assignment.

Right fin.

I settled.

My shoulder had begun to ache from holding myself too carefully. The
mana itself was not painful. The attention was worse. Every tiny thing
in the room had become a possible explanation.

My foot.

My breath.

The glass.

The table.

The paper base.

The board.

My crutch against the wall.

Hessa's sleeve.

I understood why she was annoying.

This did not make her less annoying.

I drew.

Shaped toward the line.

Right.

The paper moved.

Not right.

Away from me.

A tiny forward slide relative to my body.

Which, from the board's perspective, was sideways.

I saw it.

Hessa saw it.

Neither of us spoke.

She inspected.

Control unchanged.

Wedges unchanged.

No contact.

The target fin had moved less than the first one, but enough to see
against the ink mark.

Hessa marked it.

Thirteenth supervised external-effect attempt.

I waited.

That was difficult.

Finally I said, "That wasn't the line."

"No."

"It went away from me."

"Yes."

"So my forward."

"Once."

"Twice today if you count the first."

"The first cannot distinguish them."

"Right."

"And the failed attempt with the line sideways?"

"Nothing."

"And the attempt with board forward toward you?"

"Nothing."

I looked at the paper.

"Again."

Hessa looked at me.

"Please."

"No."

"Why?"

"You're tired."

"I'm not."

"Your shoulder has moved three times since you sat down."

I looked at my shoulder as if it had betrayed me.

"It hurts."

"Yes."

"That's not mana."

"No."

"Then it shouldn't count."

"It counts as you moving."

I hated her.

She removed the glass.

I watched the target fin as she lifted it.

It had moved perhaps a few grains' width.

That was all.

Enough to make my heart beat harder.

Not enough to make Hessa let me do it again.

She laid the fins side by side.

"What can I say?"

"About today?"

"Yes."

"You produced two observed movements during four external attempts."

"Both away from me."

"One was away from you and board forward."

"The other was away from me and not board forward."

"Yes."

"And the two failures were directions that weren't away from me."

Hessa looked at me.

"One was sideways relative to you. One was toward you."

"So."

"So today suggests movement may be easier away from your position."

I waited.

"That's it?"

"That is more than you knew this morning."

"Can I say direction follows me?"

"No."

"Can I say I can push away?"

"No."

"Can I say it prefers away?"

"Not yet."

"What can I say?"

She took my ugly question paper and wrote beneath it.

POSSIBLE BIAS: AWAY FROM GREG.

NOT ESTABLISHED.

I read it.

"That's terrible."

"It's accurate."

"It sounds weak."

"It is weak."

"I moved it twice."

"Yes."

"With magic."

"Yes."

That still worked on me.

The irritation fell away for a moment.

"Yes," I repeated.

Hessa capped the ink.

"Do not become stupid because I agreed with one word."

"I moved paper twice."

"Under this setup."

"Yes."

"In two arrangements."

"Yes."

"With two failures."

"Yes."

"And today's evidence suggests, but does not establish, that movement
may be easier away from your position."

"Yes."

"Good."

I looked at the board.

"What about the wrist thing?"

"No."

"I didn't even ask."

"You were going to connect them."

"They both have a side."

"Everything has a side if you describe it badly enough."

I laughed.

Hessa did not.

She wrote the counts.

Twenty-five successful supervised minimal draws.

Twenty-one supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Thirteen supervised external-effect attempts.

I pointed.

"Only one shaping?"

"One isolated shaping before external work. The external attempts are
already deliberate external shaping."

"Right."

"Do not inflate your own record."

"I wasn't."

She looked at me.

"At work," I added.

That almost got something from her.

Almost.

Hessa took a fresh scrap of paper.

She wrote:

IS THERE AN EASIER DIRECTION?

Then below it:

TEST AWAY FIRST.

I frowned.

"I want to know if I can choose."

"I know."

"That seems more useful."

"Later."

"Why?"

"Because if one direction is easier, I want to know that before you
spend six sessions failing to prove you can do something harder."

"That sounds reasonable."

"Yes."

"I don't like it."

"I know."

She handed me the paper.

"When?"

"Not tomorrow."

"Of course."

"I have other work."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"The gray woman called me paper man."

"She has other work too."

"I noticed."

Hessa began clearing the fins.

"Restrictions remain."

"No independent draw."

"Yes."

"No shaping."

"Yes."

"No paper."

"Yes."

"No testing whether things move away from me."

"Correct."

"What if Pell moves away from me naturally?"

Hessa stopped.

"Go to the theatre."

"That's where Pell is."

"Then suffer."

I left before she could improve the instruction.

East Market Hall was already in the middle of something when I arrived.

The front doors were open.

A bench sat sideways in the aisle. Two costumes hung from the ticket
table. Somebody was singing behind the curtain, badly and with
confidence.

Rinna saw me.

"Paper?"

"Complicated."

"Can you work?"

"Yes."

"Good. Chairs."

She pointed.

I moved chairs.

That was it.

Nobody asked whether I had discovered a fundamental property of magic.

I put six chairs against the right wall, moved two benches forward, and
carried a basket to costumes.

Pell found me halfway through the basket.

"Paper?"

"Yes."

"Better?"

"Maybe."

"Can you move it sideways?"

"Maybe."

He grinned.

"Better magic."

"Nessa needs you."

"Does she?"

"I don't know."

He looked toward backstage.

Then went anyway.

Useful.

Jori handed me a folded page.

"House tonight."

"Role?"

"House."

"That's not a role."

"It is if people keep sitting where they shouldn't."

Fair.

I worked the door.

A woman wanted the aisle seat because her husband had a bad knee.

I moved two people.

A man wanted to know whether The Red Door was on.

It wasn't.

A girl dropped a ribbon.

I picked it up.

The evening passed without requiring me to move anything with magic.

I earned one copper.

When Rinna paid me, she asked, "Direction?"

I looked at her.

"Paper may prefer away from me."

Rinna put the copper in my hand.

"Reasonable."

"What does that mean?"

"Most things do."

Then she called Jori over because tomorrow's bill had changed.

I went home.

Lyssa was not there yet.

I put Hessa's new paper under the cup.

IS THERE AN EASIER DIRECTION?

TEST AWAY FIRST.

Under my old question, Hessa's answer looked annoyingly small.

POSSIBLE BIAS: AWAY FROM GREG.

NOT ESTABLISHED.

I sat.

My shoulder hurt.

My palm hurt less.

The room contained paper, cups, coins, bread, cloth, a chair, and
several other things I was not allowed to attempt to move away from
myself.

I behaved.

Lyssa came in later carrying a bundle under one arm.

"Paper?"

"Yes."

"Which direction?"

"Maybe away from me."

She put the bundle down.

"Can you make it come back?"

"No."

"Then don't lose it."

I stared at her.

"That's almost exactly the problem."

"Good."

She started untying the bundle.

I looked at Hessa's question again.

Tomorrow the hall wanted me.

Hessa did not.

After that, apparently, we were going to find out whether my magic had a
favorite direction.

For now, it had moved two pieces of paper less than a fingernail each.

That was enough to keep me awake longer than it should have.

Not long enough to test anything.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 187

## THE SUBSTITUTE

We had no bread.

This was Lyssa's fault because I had thought she was buying bread.

It was also my fault because she had thought I was buying bread.

We established both facts before either of us had properly dressed.

"I bought it last time," she said.

"I don't remember that."

"You ate it."

"That doesn't establish who bought it."

"It establishes you were present."

Her ankle wrap was still on, though looser than yesterday. She stood
with most of her weight on the other foot while tying her hair back.

I pointed at the ankle.

She pointed at my crutches.

We abandoned that argument.

"I'll get bread," I said.

"Good."

"If I'm near Marra's, I am not carrying anything."

"I didn't ask you to."

"You were going to."

"I was going to tell you Jessa said your coat button is loose."

I looked down.

The second button was loose.

"That's worse."

"How?"

"I was prepared for a parcel."

Lyssa smiled.

She had work west again. I had the hall.

Hessa's papers remained under the cup.

POSSIBLE BIAS: AWAY FROM GREG.

NOT ESTABLISHED.

And beneath that:

IS THERE AN EASIER DIRECTION?

TEST AWAY FIRST.

I put the cup back over them.

Nothing moved.

Good.

Outside, a shop door opened away from me.

I noticed.

Then I was annoyed that I had noticed.

By the time I reached East Market Hall, I had bought bread, eaten part
of it, and stopped assigning directions to doors.

Rinna was waiting inside with a coil of narrow cord over one arm.

"You're late."

"I'm early."

"Good. You're here."

She handed me the cord.

"What's this?"

"Back drop."

"Today?"

Rinna looked at me.

"Yes."

That felt like progress.

Nessa was already on the rear platform with a folded length of blue-gray
cloth. She saw the cord in my hand and pointed upward.

"Back rail."

"I know."

"Today."

"I know that too."

She gave me one end of the cloth.

We raised it.

I got the first corner right.

The second sagged.

"Other peg," Nessa said.

I moved it.

"Not that other peg."

"There are too many pegs."

"There are six."

"Exactly."

She fixed it while I held the cloth.

Nobody explained what the drop was for.

I did not ask.

Across the hall, Jori was checking the curtain line. Hara stood in one
stocking and one bare foot, arguing with a costume worker about whether
brown meant brown or the other brown. Veya sat on the stage edge reading
a page while somebody pinned the side of her dress.

Pell was nowhere visible.

This should have worried me.

It did not yet.

Rinna passed me on her way toward the front.

"Tell Pell narrow tube tonight. Not long."

"Narrow tube."

"Tonight."

"Yes."

She kept walking.

I repeated it once to myself.

Narrow tube tonight.

Then Jori called, "Greg."

I looked over.

A young woman I had seen twice before was holding the fake petition
stack.

"Where does this go?"

I almost answered.

Then I remembered the loaf.

"Today?"

Jori looked pleased in a way I distrusted.

The young woman said, "The Petition."

"Prop box by left wing."

Jori nodded.

I pointed.

"Bottom shelf. Wax side up."

She carried it away.

I had become dangerously competent.

Nessa handed me a broom.

That corrected the feeling.

I swept the left passage.

I moved two benches.

I took a red coat from a chair to costumes and was told it belonged on
the chair.

"Today?" I asked.

"Today," the costume worker said.

I returned it.

Someone asked where the blue lamp was.

"Upper prop shelf, wrapped."

It was.

Someone asked where the bad stool was.

"Which bad stool?"

"The one with the loose back."

"Rear wall."

It was not.

Pell had moved it.

I found him under the stage with his feet sticking out.

"Narrow tube tonight," I said.

He slid halfway out.

"What?"

"Narrow tube. Tonight."

"Why?"

"Rinna."

"For what?"

"I don't know."

"Ghost?"

"Probably."

"Which narrow tube?"

I stared at him.

There were two.

Of course there were two.

"The narrow one."

"They're both narrow."

"One must be narrower."

"Yes."

"Use that one."

Pell considered this.

"Good."

I left.

That was when Hara called me to hold a costume basket.

Then Jori needed the second stool.

Then Teren arrived carrying three folded pages and said, "You're in
second if Evin isn't here."

I forgot about the tube.

"Who is Evin?"

"Clerk."

"In what?"

"Debtor's Uncle."

"I don't know Debtor's Uncle."

"You've seen it."

"Once."

"Good."

Teren handed me a page.

There were eight lines marked in charcoal.

I read the first.

"I have no authority to release the chest."

I looked at Teren.

"This is a terrible role."

"It's a clerk."

"Exactly."

"Learn it."

"What if Evin comes?"

"Then he does it."

Teren walked away.

I looked at the page.

Nobody had asked whether I wanted the role.

That was also progress.

Debtor's Uncle turned out to be a short comedy about a man who inherited
a chest from an uncle who owed money to everyone in the district. The
chest contained almost nothing. This did not stop six people from trying
to claim it.

The clerk existed to tell everyone no.

I liked him immediately.

Veya found me reading beside the left wing.

"Entrance after second knock."

"Which side?"

"Right."

"Today?"

She looked at me.

"Yes, Greg."

"I am learning."

"Slowly."

She walked away.

I read the lines again.

The second knock.

Right side.

No authority.

Chest remains sealed.

Receipt first.

No, your cousin cannot sign.

Eight lines.

Easy.

Which meant something else would go wrong.

At midday, Evin still had not arrived.

Rinna had not seen him.

Jori had not seen him.

Teren had apparently expected this because he did not look surprised.

"Why is he late?"

"His sister's boy is sick," Hara said while eating an apple.

"How do you know?"

"He told me yesterday."

"Did he tell Teren?"

Hara looked toward Teren.

"Apparently not."

Teren heard.

"He told me he might be late."

"That seems relevant."

"It was."

"Did you tell me?"

"I just did."

Fair.

We ran the entrance once.

I came in on the right.

The first knock sounded.

I started.

Veya said, "Second."

"I know."

"You moved."

"I didn't enter."

"You leaned."

"That's not entering."

"Do it again."

The second time I waited.

Second knock.

Enter.

I made it to the desk.

The chest was a square wooden box with iron-colored paint at the
corners. It was light enough that Sellen had carried it one-handed five
minutes earlier.

I was not supposed to touch it.

That helped.

My first line was to Veya.

"I have no authority to release the chest."

She answered.

I gave the second.

Sellen came in too early.

Veya told him to go back out.

He did.

Nobody treated rehearsal as sacred.

Somebody hammered backstage through half of it.

Nessa crossed behind us carrying a rolled painted wall.

Pell shouted something about a hinge.

Jori called, "Again from the knock."

We did.

By the third time, the clerk existed.

Not much.

Enough.

During the break, I went to find Pell.

He was not under the stage.

I remembered Rinna's message.

Narrow tube tonight.

Good.

I had delivered it.

Mostly.

At the rear wall, one of the tubes was already out.

It was the longer one.

I stared at it.

Pell came through the side door with a handful of chalk.

"Why is that out?"

"Ghost."

"Rinna said narrow."

"That is narrow."

"The other narrow."

He looked at me.

"You said narrower."

"Yes."

"This one is narrower than the long one."

"There are three?"

"Yes."

I closed my eyes.

Pell pointed toward the prop shelves.

"Long, narrow, narrower."

"Why?"

"Different rooms."

"Rinna said narrow tube, not long."

"That's narrow."

I had delivered exactly the words.

I had also known he had asked which one.

I had decided I was done.

That part was mine.

"Wait."

I found Rinna near the ticket table.

"Which narrow tube?"

She looked at me.

"The short one."

"Pell has the middle one."

"I told you narrow, not long."

"There are three."

"I know."

"I didn't."

"You do now."

"Do you want the short one?"

"Yes."

"Good."

I turned.

Rinna said, "Greg."

I looked back.

"I told you this morning."

"Yes."

That was all.

I went back.

"Pell. Short one."

"That's narrower."

"I don't care what it's called."

"Then how will I know which one?"

"Short."

"Good."

He put the middle tube away.

Nothing had broken.

Nobody died.

We lost perhaps four minutes.

I had been relied upon to carry six words across one building and had
managed to make them insufficient.

This seemed unfair.

It was not.

I went outside for bread.

The market was loud enough that the hall noise disappeared after twenty
steps.

A fish seller was shouting prices. Two boys rolled an empty barrel
between them and nearly hit a woman carrying eggs. A wagon had stopped
crooked beside a stack of sacks, and three men were arguing about
whether the wagon or the sacks had been there first.

I saw Lyssa across the street.

She was with the woman from yesterday, the one with the yellow cloth.

Today the woman had green cloth.

This seemed like a development.

Lyssa saw me.

I lifted the bread.

She looked at it, then at me.

I pointed at myself.

She nodded approval.

Neither of us crossed the street.

I did not know who the woman was.

I survived again.

Back at the hall, Evin still had not arrived.

Teren saw the bread.

"Where did that come from?"

"I bought it."

He looked disappointed.

"Why?"

"Because yesterday you oppressed me."

"The baker arrangement ended."

I stopped.

"What?"

"No rolls today."

Behind me Pell said, "What?"

Rinna said, "Paid work ended."

Pell came around the curtain.

"We're still working."

"Different problem," Rinna said.

Pell looked genuinely betrayed.

I tore my bread in half.

He held out his hand.

"No."

"You're cruel."

"You called me magician for three days."

"That was praise."

"It was inaccurate."

"Half."

"No."

He wandered away.

I ate both halves.

This was one of my better performances.

The first piece went on without me.

I moved a stool before it and moved the same stool back afterward.

Nobody applauded.

Evin had still not arrived.

Teren found me.

"Clerk."

"Yes."

"Second knock."

"Yes."

"Right."

"Today?"

He stared at me.

"Go."

The house was not full, but it was good enough that the room changed
when the curtain opened.

That still happened.

Empty hall became audience.

Painted wall became a room.

Sellen became a man with a dead uncle and six creditors.

Veya became his cousin, who claimed the chest contained family property
despite never having seen it.

I waited behind the right side.

First knock.

I stayed.

Second knock.

I entered.

The clerk's coat was too wide through the shoulders and slightly too
short in the sleeve. Nobody had altered it for me because I was not
supposed to own the role.

I crossed to the desk.

Veya demanded the chest.

"I have no authority to release the chest."

"Then find someone who does."

"I have no authority to find someone who does."

That got a laugh.

Not a large one.

Enough.

The scene continued.

Sellen tried to sign for his dead uncle.

"No, your cousin cannot sign."

"He's not my cousin."

I looked at Veya.

"I met you this morning."

The audience laughed harder.

That line belonged to Evin.

I had heard him do it once.

He did it better.

Mine still worked.

A creditor entered.

Then another.

The chest acquired more owners without moving.

My job was mostly to prevent everyone else from progressing.

I understood bureaucracy spiritually.

Near the end, Sellen put both hands on the chest.

I said, "Receipt first."

He said, "For my own uncle?"

"Especially for your own uncle."

That got another laugh.

Then Veya took the scene away from me with a long complaint about how
the dead man had owed her three spoons, a blanket, and an apology.

She was better.

The audience followed her.

Good.

I stood behind the desk and looked annoyed.

Also good.

The piece ended.

Backstage, Teren said, "Fine."

"Fine?"

"The show happened."

"I got laughs."

"So did the chest."

The chest had not said anything.

I chose not to argue.

Evin arrived during the next piece.

He was thin, red-faced from hurrying, and angry before anyone spoke to
him.

"Boy's fever broke," he said to Hara.

"Good."

"Cart wheel split."

"Bad."

"Took the south road."

"Worse."

He saw me in his clerk coat.

"Oh."

"Hello."

"Did you do it?"

"Yes."

"How bad?"

"Teren said fine."

Evin nodded.

"Good."

That was all.

For the evening repeat, Evin took the coat back.

Obviously.

I still felt a small, stupid disappointment when he did.

Then I watched him run the clerk scene and understood why it was his.

He did less.

That was the irritating part.

He barely moved behind the desk. He let everyone come to him. His first
"no" was almost bored. By the time he said, "I met you this morning,"
the audience was already waiting for him to refuse something.

His laugh was bigger than mine.

I hated him for approximately six seconds.

Then Jori handed me a cue page.

"Hold."

I held it.

"Not there."

I moved.

"Other side."

I moved again.

The evening continued.

The ghost used the short tube.

It worked.

Pell did not mention magic.

I noticed this halfway through the piece and was offended.

Then he whispered, "Move."

I looked at him.

"What?"

"You're on my cord."

I moved my crutch.

He pulled the cord free.

That was better.

Later, Nessa handed me a gray cloth.

"Back rail."

"Today?"

"Tomorrow."

I stopped.

She nodded once.

"Good."

I put it on the marked shelf instead.

That felt suspiciously like learning.

Then I carried the wrong rope to Jori.

"It was there yesterday," I said.

Jori pointed to the front peg.

"Today."

"Yes."

The building kept changing while I learned it.

By the end of the evening, my shoulder hurt, my right palm was tender,
and the new leather grip had another dark mark across it.

It still did not twist.

Worth one copper.

Rinna paid me two.

I looked at the coins.

"Two?"

"Morning, clerk, evening crew."

"I was only clerk once."

"You were only paid for it once."

"Good."

"Did you want less?"

"No."

She closed her box.

I put the coins away.

"Do you need me tomorrow?"

"Afternoon."

"Role?"

"Maybe."

"Don't."

Rinna smiled.

"Tell Pell the short tube goes back on the upper shelf."

I looked toward Pell.

He was six paces away.

"PELL."

He looked over.

"SHORT TUBE. UPPER SHELF."

Rinna said, "Excellent."

"I've developed a system."

Pell shouted, "WHICH UPPER SHELF?"

I looked at Rinna.

She closed her eyes.

I went home.

Lyssa was already there.

Her ankle wrap was off.

The ankle itself looked slightly swollen.

I pointed.

She said, "Don't."

"Better?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"Your button is worse."

I looked down.

The loose button was now hanging by one thread.

"That also seems like work."

"Sit."

I sat.

She took the coat.

"How was paper?"

"Yesterday."

"I know. I wanted to see if you remembered."

"You're becoming cruel."

"Work."

She found a needle.

I watched her repair the button.

"Busy day?"

"Yes."

"Bad?"

"No."

I thought about it.

"Busy."

She nodded.

"Mine too."

"Yellow cloth woman had green cloth today."

Lyssa looked up.

"You saw us?"

"Across the market."

"That isn't her cloth."

"Whose is it?"

"Someone else's."

"Good."

She returned to the button.

I waited.

Nothing else came.

I had no idea who the woman was.

Still alive.

"I was a clerk."

"At the theatre?"

"Yes."

"What did you do?"

"Told everyone no."

"Good casting."

"I was excellent."

"How excellent?"

"Teren said fine."

Lyssa laughed.

"The real clerk came back."

"And?"

"He was better."

"That's rude."

"Very."

She bit the thread and handed me the coat.

"What did you earn?"

"Two copper."

"Better than fine."

I put the coat beside me.

On the table, Hessa's paper was still under the cup.

Nothing had moved.

I had thought about away twice during the day.

Once at the door.

Once when Pell finally walked away from me carrying the correct tube.

Neither counted.

I did not test anything.

Tomorrow the hall wanted me in the afternoon.

Hessa had not asked for me at all.

That was fine.

Probably.

I put one copper beside the bread money and kept the other.

Lyssa leaned back and stretched her sore ankle.

I stretched my shoulder.

Neither of us said anything useful about that.

People had needed me all day.

Cord.

Cloth.

A message.

A clerk.

A cue page.

A rope.

A tube.

A place to put something.

I had gotten some of it right.

I had gotten some of it wrong.

The show happened anyway.

That felt less impressive than moving paper.

It was considerably more tiring.

I went to bed before anyone could ask me where the short tube belonged.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 188

## THE HOUSEMAN

Lyssa was gone before I woke.

There was bread.

This was an improvement.

There was also a bowl on the table that did not belong to us.

I knew this because ours had a chip on the rim and this one did not. The
bowl contained nothing. Beside it was a folded scrap of paper.

MARRA.

That was the entire note.

I ate bread while looking at the bowl.

Then I looked at the door.

"No."

Nobody answered.

I finished eating.

The bowl was still there.

I carried it to Marra's.

This was how they got you.

The morning was cool enough that the leather on my right crutch did not
immediately grow damp under my palm. The soreness was better than
yesterday. Not gone. Better. The grip stayed where the cobbler had put
it, seam turned away from the tender place.

I passed the Guild street without looking toward it.

Mostly.

Hessa had not sent for me.

There was no reason to go in.

I had no paper.

I had no glass.

I had no permission.

A cart wheel rolled away from a boy in the street and struck a wall.

Away.

I kept walking.

Marra's green door was open.

Marra was kneeling beside a low stool with three skirts hanging from
pegs behind her. Jessa stood on the stool in her shift while another
woman pinned a dark hem around her.

I held up the bowl.

Marra looked at it.

"Good."

"Is that all?"

"Yes."

Jessa looked down at me.

"Your button?"

"Repaired."

"Show me."

"No."

"Then why did I tell Lyssa?"

"So she could repair it."

Jessa seemed dissatisfied by the successful completion of her own
instruction.

I put the bowl on a shelf.

Marra said, "If you see Lyssa, tell her west after second bell."

I stared at her.

Marra smiled.

"No."

"Why?"

"I came here to return a bowl."

"And now you know something."

"I refuse."

"Fine."

Jessa said, "Tell her west after second bell."

I left.

I had become a message with crutches again.

The hall did not want me until afternoon.

This left me with time.

I bought an onion.

I stood beside a fruit stall and considered buying an apple, decided
against it, then bought one because I had already spent too long
considering it.

I sat on a low wall near the lower market.

Nobody needed me.

A man repaired a basket across the street. Two women argued about fish.
A dog slept under a cart until somebody moved the cart, at which point
the dog moved three feet and went back to sleep.

I ate the apple.

Nobody handed me a rope.

Nobody shouted my name.

Nobody asked which tube was narrow.

It was peaceful.

I lasted perhaps fifteen minutes.

Then I went to the hall early.

Rinna was at the front table counting small coins into stacks.

She looked up.

"Afternoon."

"It is almost afternoon."

"It isn't."

"I can leave."

"Don't."

I leaned my crutches against the table.

"Role?"

"No."

I waited.

Rinna kept counting.

"That's all?"

"Unless something goes wrong."

I considered this.

"That sounds like a role."

"It isn't."

"It sounds like one."

"Carry the wall."

There was a wall.

Of course there was.

It was a narrow painted flat with a window on it, leaning against the
left side of the stage. Pell had one end.

I took the other.

"Where?"

"Rear."

"Today?"

Pell looked at me.

"Why do you keep saying that?"

"Experience."

We carried it rear.

The painted window showed a strip of dark blue sky and one pale square
that might have been another building. Up close, the paint was streaked
and the square looked crooked.

"Which piece?"

"The Miller's Son."

"I've seen that."

"Good."

"Is it still the same?"

Pell stopped.

"What?"

"Today."

He stared at me.

Then he laughed hard enough that the wall shook.

"Don't."

We set it down.

Nessa was at a worktable with a small lamp opened in front of her.

Not the blue lamp.

This one had a brass frame, cloudy glass, and a thin prepared strip set
into the base. The strip was dark at one end.

I knew enough to stop two steps away.

"It isn't lighting," Hara said.

She stood beside Nessa with one sleeve rolled up and a smear of paint on
her wrist.

"Did you change the oil?" Nessa asked.

"Yes."

"Wick?"

"Yes."

"Catch?"

"Twice."

Nessa touched the wooden side of the lamp, not the prepared strip.

"Who moved it?"

Hara pointed at Pell.

Pell pointed at me.

"I just arrived."

"Then not Greg," Nessa said.

This was the nicest thing anyone had said to me all day.

I leaned closer.

"Can I help?"

"No."

"Good."

Nessa looked at me.

"Get Sava."

"Who is Sava?"

"Rear yard. Gray apron."

I went.

Sava was a broad woman with a shaved strip above one ear and both hands
blackened with lamp soot. I had seen her before without knowing her
name.

"Nessa wants you."

"Why?"

"Lamp."

"Which?"

"Brass. Cloudy glass. Prepared strip dark on one end."

She wiped her hands on the gray apron.

"Good."

That was apparently enough.

I brought the right person.

This felt suspiciously mature.

Sava looked at the lamp, swore once, and asked for a narrow blade.

I left before anyone could mistake my presence for competence.

Jori found me near the curtain.

"Tonight you're rear."

"Rear what?"

"Rear."

"That's not a job."

"It is now."

He handed me a small wooden block.

I looked at it.

"What does this do?"

"Door."

"Which door?"

"The Miller's Son."

I looked toward the painted wall.

"The fake one?"

"The real fake one."

"Good."

Jori pointed behind the rear hanging.

There was a short board on the floor, another upright board, and a metal
latch fixed to nothing I could see.

Pell came through carrying a bucket.

"He's crowd."

Jori said, "You're crowd."

"I thought I was rear."

"Same place."

"How many jobs are in rear?"

"All the ones the audience shouldn't see."

That seemed excessive.

The Miller's Son had a scene I remembered badly. A young miller owed
grain to three men, money to two women, and an apology to his mother.
Near the end, people gathered outside his house while he tried to
convince Veya, playing his sister, that leaving through the window was
reasonable.

Apparently the people outside were mostly Pell.

"How many?" I asked.

"Seven," Pell said.

"How many people?"

"Two."

"Who's the other?"

"You."

I looked at Jori.

He was already reading his cue page.

Pell showed me the arrangement.

The rear side of the stage had two strips of flooring. One was bare
board. One had an old woven runner nailed down. There was a hanging door
panel that the audience saw from the other side. Beside it, a loose
shutter rested against the wall. A tin cup sat on a shelf.

Pell pointed.

"Three steps on board. Stop. Knock."

"Me?"

"You."

"Then what?"

"Move to runner. Different voice."

"What voice?"

"Not yours."

"I have several voices."

"No."

"You haven't heard them."

"I have."

That was probably true.

Pell crossed the boards with heavy steps.

He knocked twice.

Then he moved behind the hanging, changed the angle of his body, and
called, "Open up, Len!"

It sounded like one man.

He stepped onto the runner, softened his feet, leaned toward the
opposite side of the hanging, and said in a higher, irritated voice, "We
know you're in there."

Different man.

Not completely.

Enough.

Then he rattled the loose shutter while striking the wall once with his
heel.

From where I stood, it was obviously Pell making noise.

Jori said, "Greg, board."

I crossed three steps.

"Too even."

"I'm walking."

"You're counting."

"I was told three."

"Don't sound like three."

I tried again.

Apparently I had been walking like a man demonstrating the concept of
footsteps.

Pell shook his head.

"Angry."

I walked harder.

"Now you're a horse."

"Pick a species."

"Person."

I did it again.

Better.

"Knock," Jori said.

I knocked.

Veya, onstage beyond the hanging, turned toward the door.

I watched her.

"Greg."

I knocked again.

Jori looked at me.

"What?"

"Late."

"She looked at the door."

"That was your warning."

"I thought that was the cue."

"No. She looks. Then Sellen says the line. Then knock."

Pell said, "Not when they look. After."

"I understand."

"Good."

"Today?"

Pell started laughing again.

We reset.

Veya looked toward the door.

Sellen said, "Nobody knows I'm here."

Jori lifted two fingers.

I knocked.

Veya flinched.

The knock sounded better because she flinched.

That was annoying.

Pell immediately crossed behind me, gave two heavy steps from farther
left, and called, "Miller!"

Then I moved to the runner.

"Open up," I said in a low voice.

Pell stopped.

"No."

"What?"

"Never that voice."

"It was different."

"It was a dying cow."

"I don't know what cows sound like here."

"The same."

Jori said, "Use your voice. Turn away."

"That isn't a different person."

"Audience can't see you."

I turned my head toward the rear wall.

"Open up."

It sounded different.

Not much.

Enough.

Pell nodded.

"Better cow."

We continued.

He rattled the shutter.

I struck the wooden block against the upright board once.

Veya crossed away from the door.

Sellen looked at the window.

The two of them made the room feel crowded before we made another sound.

I noticed that.

Then Jori snapped his fingers at me because I had missed my next step.

"Work."

"Yes."

The lamp problem took longer.

Sava had the prepared strip out on a cloth. Nessa held the lamp body.
Hara stood nearby, increasingly impatient.

"Can it run tonight?" Hara asked.

"Not like this," Sava said.

"What does that mean?"

"It means no."

"What if we use the other catch?"

"No."

"What if we use the blue lamp?"

Nessa said, "Wrong light."

I had an idea.

This was dangerous.

I kept it.

Then Nessa looked at me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

Sava asked me to fetch a plain taper lamp from the upper shelf.

I knew where that was.

I brought it.

Nessa set it behind a thin cream screen, moved it twice, and looked from
the house.

"Closer."

Hara moved it.

"Too close."

They moved it again.

The broken prepared lamp stayed on the worktable.

The ordinary taper became the replacement.

I did not know exactly what the prepared one had been meant to do.

Nobody explained.

The show was going to happen anyway.

At the front, a woman asked me whether The Red Door was on tonight.

"No."

"What is?"

"The Miller's Son, The Widow's Room, and something short after."

"What's short?"

"I don't know."

She looked at me as though I worked there.

This was unreasonable.

"Is it funny?"

"Probably."

Rinna, behind the table, said, "Yes."

I looked at her.

She said, "Sell the seat."

I looked back at the woman.

"Yes."

She bought two.

I was learning commerce.

Poorly.

Before the house opened, I remembered Marra.

I had not seen Lyssa.

I found a scrap of paper.

WEST AFTER SECOND BELL.

I wrote LYSSA above it.

Then I realized I had no reliable way to get it to her.

I put it in my pocket.

This was not useful.

The house filled enough that I stopped thinking about it.

I worked the aisle first.

A man wanted to move three seats because his wife could not see around a
tall hat.

The woman wearing the tall hat objected to being described as a hat.

I moved the man and his wife instead.

A boy dropped a wooden animal under a bench.

I retrieved it.

An older woman asked whether the hall was always this warm.

"Only when people come."

She stared at me.

I decided I should not work the house.

Then Jori called from the side.

"Rear."

Better.

The first piece did not need me.

I stood behind the hanging and listened.

Audience laughter came through cloth differently. Softer. Less like
people and more like weather.

Pell was repairing a strap beside me.

"Paper," he whispered.

"No."

"Didn't ask."

"You were going to."

"I was going to ask if you had paper."

"Why?"

He held up the strap.

"Need a shim."

I gave him a folded scrap from the prop shelf.

He tore it.

"Thanks."

That was the entire magic conversation.

The Miller's Son began.

I took my place.

The painted window was six feet from me and looked terrible.

From the house it had looked like night.

The replacement taper behind the cream screen made a pale square across
one side of the set. It was not the same as the prepared lamp. I could
tell because Hara had complained twice.

The audience did not know what had been planned.

The scene moved.

Veya and Sellen argued.

The miller lied.

His sister did not believe him.

Offstage, Pell held up one finger.

Not yet.

Veya looked toward the door.

Sellen said, "Nobody knows I'm here."

Jori's hand lifted.

I crossed three hard steps.

Not counting.

I knocked.

Veya flinched.

A murmur went through the house.

Pell moved left.

"Miller!"

I moved onto the runner and turned away.

"Open up."

Different enough.

Pell struck the shutter.

I hit the upright with the block.

Someone in the audience laughed.

Onstage, Sellen backed toward the painted window.

Veya caught his sleeve.

"No."

"They're at the door."

"There are six of them."

There were two of us.

Pell made another voice from farther right.

I moved behind the hanging and stamped once on bare board.

Veya turned toward that sound before I made it.

For half a second, I felt late.

Then I remembered.

After.

I waited.

Her head turned.

Sellen said, "The back."

I struck the wall.

The audience's attention shifted.

I could hear it.

Not a sound exactly.

A change.

Pell rattled the shutter.

The room outside the fake house filled with people who did not exist.

I nearly missed my last knock because I was listening to them.

Jori touched my sleeve.

I knocked.

The scene continued.

When it ended, Pell grinned.

"You were four."

"Four what?"

"People."

"I was at least five."

"No."

"Why?"

"Bad cow only counts half."

"I didn't use the cow."

"It remains."

We reset the block.

No one in the audience had seen me.

I had enjoyed that more than I expected.

The Widow's Room used the painted doorway but not the blue lamp. The
ordinary taper replacement stayed in Miller's Son storage.

The short final piece turned out to be Hara and Evin arguing over a
missing shoe.

It was funny.

I had no role.

I watched from the side until Nessa handed me a basket and said,
"Costumes."

I carried it.

Afterward, the hall became itself again.

People stood.

Benches scraped.

Coins clicked at the front.

Pell carried the short tube toward the wrong upper shelf.

I pointed.

"Other."

He changed direction without arguing.

That was disturbing.

Sava had wrapped the bad prepared strip separately and written something
on the cloth.

I did not ask what.

Hara was already complaining about tomorrow.

Jori rolled his cue page.

Veya took off one shoe and walked around in the other.

Teren was nowhere I could see.

Rinna paid me one copper.

I looked at it.

"Rear?"

"House and rear."

"I was four people."

"Then split it with them."

I closed my hand.

"One is fine."

"Good."

She reached under the table.

"For you."

It was a folded note.

My name was on the outside in Hessa's writing.

I opened it.

DAY AFTER TOMORROW.

AFTER MIDDAY.

AWAY FIRST.

Below that, smaller:

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

I read it again.

Rinna said, "Paper?"

"Day after tomorrow."

"Good."

"Why is that good?"

"You can work tomorrow."

I looked at her.

She had already turned away.

Of course.

Outside, it was fully dark.

I had forgotten Marra's message.

I remembered it halfway home.

Second bell had been hours ago.

I stopped in the street.

"Excellent."

A man passing me said, "What?"

"Nothing."

Lyssa was home.

I knew before opening the door because light showed at the bottom and I
could hear her moving something inside.

She was sitting on the floor with a basket between her knees.

Her ankle was wrapped again.

I held up one finger.

She said, "No."

"I haven't said anything."

"You were going to."

"Marra said west after second bell."

Lyssa stopped.

"When?"

"This morning."

She stared at me.

"I remembered."

"When?"

"Now."

"Greg."

"I wrote it down."

"Where?"

I pulled the paper from my pocket.

She took it.

"Very useful."

"I had work."

"So did I."

"Did you need the message?"

"No. Jessa found me."

"Good."

"At third bell."

"Less good."

Lyssa put the paper in the basket.

"What did you do?"

"I was four people outside a door."

She looked at me.

"How many were you?"

"One."

"That sounds dishonest."

"It was theatre."

"Same thing."

"Not according to Teren."

"Who were the other three?"

"Pell."

"All three?"

"Some of him."

She nodded as though this clarified it.

I showed her Hessa's note.

"Paper?"

"Day after tomorrow."

"Away?"

"First."

"Then what?"

"I don't know."

"Good."

"You say that too much."

Lyssa returned the note.

"What did they pay you for being four people?"

"One copper."

"Did the other three get paid?"

"I hope not."

We ate bread, onion, and the last piece of cheese.

My coat smelled faintly of dust and lamp oil even though I had not
touched the bad lamp.

There was black on the side of my hand from the wooden block.

My right palm still hurt.

Lyssa's ankle still hurt.

Tomorrow the hall wanted me.

Day after tomorrow Hessa wanted me.

Somewhere in between, Marra would probably want a bowl carried again.

I put Hessa's note under the cup.

DAY AFTER TOMORROW.

AFTER MIDDAY.

AWAY FIRST.

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

Nothing on the table moved.

At the theatre, two men had become a crowd.

At home, one onion remained one onion.

I did not ask it to go anywhere.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 189

## THE SHADOW

Lyssa was wrapping her ankle when I woke.

Not because it was worse.

I knew this because she told me before I asked.

"It's better."

"I didn't say anything."

"You looked."

"I have eyes."

"So do I."

She pulled the cloth snug, tied it, stood, and put most of her weight on
the foot before I could object.

I looked again.

She pointed at my crutches.

This argument had developed rules.

I ate bread.

Lyssa drank water and sorted three folded pieces of cloth into a basket.
None were yellow or green. I did not ask whose they were.

This was growth.

She put the basket beside the door.

"If Marra tells you anything today, don't bring it home after dark."

"I wrote it down."

"That wasn't the problem."

"I was working."

"So was Jessa. She still found me."

"Jessa has two legs."

Lyssa stared at me.

"That's what you're using?"

"No."

"Good."

I tore another piece of bread and wrapped it in cloth with half an onion
and a little salt.

Lyssa watched.

"Food?"

"The rolls are gone."

"You knew they would be."

"I knew the agreement ended. I didn't know that meant the food would end
emotionally."

"That's usually what ending means."

I put the food in my bag.

The Hessa note was still under the cup.

DAY AFTER TOMORROW.

AFTER MIDDAY.

AWAY FIRST.

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

Except it was not day after tomorrow anymore.

It was tomorrow.

I knew this without touching the paper.

I left it there.

Nothing moved.

Outside, Carrow was already noisy.

A wagon blocked half the street near the cooper. Someone had dropped a
basket of pale roots, and three people were collecting them while a
fourth argued that they were doing it wrong. A boy ran past me with a
long strip of red cloth tied around his shoulders.

I did not think about direction.

I thought the cloth was going to catch on something.

It did.

He stopped hard enough to nearly sit down.

I kept walking.

East Market Hall had both front doors open.

That usually meant work or smoke.

Today it was work.

Nessa was onstage with a coil of cord around one arm. Jori stood in the
house staring upward. Pell was behind a painted wall with his feet
sticking out. Rinna had a chalk board propped on the ticket table.

My name was on it.

Not large.

Not important.

Still there.

GREG: PORTER / REAR.

I read it twice.

Rinna said, "You can read."

"I like confirmation."

"Then confirm the rear window is still on the rear rack."

"Today?"

She looked at me.

"Yes."

I went.

The rear window was not on the rear rack.

This pleased me more than it should have.

I came back.

"It isn't."

Rinna stopped writing.

"Where is it?"

"I don't know."

"Less useful."

"But correct."

"Find it."

I found it behind the left hanging, where Pell had moved it because the
rear rack was needed for two wall flats.

"I moved it," Pell said from under the wall.

"I know."

"How?"

"You move everything."

"Not everything."

"Where's the bad stool?"

"Which?"

I pointed at him.

"Exactly."

He slid farther under the wall.

I carried the window back toward the stage.

It was not really a window.

It was a wooden frame with stretched pale cloth, painted around the
edges to suggest stone. From the front it could be a high window in a
house, a low window in a tower, or possibly a very disappointing
painting depending on what they put around it.

Nessa saw me.

"Not there."

"I was told rear rack."

"Later."

"Where now?"

"Stage left."

"Today?"

"Greg."

"Yes."

I put it stage left.

Teren came through the side door carrying a bundle of pages and a heel
of bread in his mouth.

He pulled the bread out.

"You're Porter."

"I saw."

"Six lines."

"How many things do I carry?"

"One."

"That seems low."

"You complain about carrying things."

"I complain about unpaid carrying."

"Good. This is paid."

He handed me a page.

The piece was called The Borrowed House.

I had not seen it.

My role was a porter delivering a small chest to the wrong house.

This caused a woman to believe her husband had bought jewelry, the
husband to believe the woman had bought poison, and a neighbor to
believe both of them were leaving town.

My six lines were not difficult.

"I was told this house."

"By whom?"

"A man."

"What man?"

"The one who told me this house."

I liked the Porter.

He was an idiot with procedural integrity.

Teren pointed at the right entrance.

"Second piece."

"Which side?"

"Right."

"Today?"

Teren chewed.

"Keep doing that."

I narrowed my eyes.

He walked away.

I rehearsed the lines while carrying the window again because Nessa had
changed her mind.

Or, more accurately, because I had misunderstood "stage left" as its
final location instead of where she wanted it while she measured
something.

She did not explain this distinction.

She just said, "Back."

I carried it back.

Then she gave me a coat on a stick.

I looked at it.

It was an old black coat tied across a short wooden crosspiece. Above
the shoulders, another piece of wood rose into a battered round hat.
There was no head.

One sleeve had been stuffed lightly with cloth. The other hung empty.

I looked at Nessa.

"No."

"What?"

"Whatever this is."

"Man."

I looked at the coat.

"No."

"Tonight."

I looked again.

"That's a man tonight?"

"Window."

She pointed.

The pale-cloth window was now upright behind part of the set for The
Borrowed House.

I understood less.

Pell emerged from behind the wall and took the coat-stick from me.

"He's too close."

"I haven't done anything."

"Not you."

He carried the coat behind the window.

Nessa had placed a plain lamp several feet farther back.

Pell stood between lamp and cloth.

A dark shape appeared on the pale window.

From where I stood beside it, the shape was obviously a coat hanging on
wood.

Nessa walked into the house.

"Back."

Pell stepped backward.

The shape softened.

"More."

He stepped again.

The shoulders changed.

The hat stopped looking like a hat balanced on a stick and became the
top of a head I could not see.

Nessa said, "Lower."

Pell lowered it.

"Stop."

I moved into the house beside her.

There was a man behind the window.

Not a good man.

Not a detailed man.

A man.

I went back.

Coat.

House.

Man.

Back.

Coat.

I did this three times.

Pell said, "Are you broken?"

"Maybe."

Nessa said, "Greg holds it tonight."

"Why?"

"Pell changes coat."

Pell pointed at himself.

"In the scene."

"You are in this?"

"I'm the neighbor."

I looked at the coat.

"Who is he?"

"No one."

"Then why is he there?"

Nessa said, "Because Veya sees him."

That was apparently the entire explanation.

The scene had the husband and wife arguing near the end. Veya, playing
the wife, looked up toward the window and saw someone outside. The
husband turned. The shadow moved away.

That was it.

Four seconds.

Maybe five.

I took the stick.

It was not heavy.

It was awkward.

I could not stand behind the cloth safely while holding it with both
hands because I needed one hand for a crutch. Nessa looked at me, looked
at the space, and dragged a low stool into position.

"Sit."

"I can stand."

"Sit."

I sat.

My crutches went against the rear wall.

With both feet placed badly, I shifted.

Nessa pointed at my good foot.

"Flat."

I put it flat.

The coat-stick rested lightly against the floor beside my knee.

This was much easier.

"Up," Nessa said.

I raised it.

The shadow appeared.

"Back."

I moved the stick backward.

"Not you."

I stopped.

"Move the bottom."

I angled the base away while keeping the shoulders roughly in place.

The shadow changed.

"Too much."

I corrected.

Pell stood in the house.

"Stick."

"What?"

"I can see the stick."

I looked at the shadow.

The lower vertical line showed beneath the coat.

"How do I hide it?"

"Lower coat."

"If I lower the coat, the man gets shorter."

Pell shrugged.

"Then shorter man."

I lowered it.

Nessa said, "Good."

Apparently we had sacrificed height for having legs that were not one
wooden pole.

We ran the scene.

I waited behind the window.

Veya and Hara argued onstage.

I could hear them clearly through the cloth.

Veya said, "Then why was it delivered here?"

Hara said, "Because the porter is an idiot."

I took this personally.

That was not my cue.

I waited.

Veya crossed.

She looked toward the window.

I raised the coat.

Too early.

Her eyes had not landed yet.

The shadow was already standing there when she found it.

Nessa said, "Down."

I lowered it.

Veya stopped.

"Again."

We reset.

"What's the cue?" I asked.

Nessa said, "Her."

"She looked."

"Not enough."

This was becoming familiar.

Veya heard us.

She said, "I turn. I see it. Then you give it to me."

"That's three things."

"Yes."

"Which one?"

"The seeing."

"How do I know when you see something that isn't there?"

Veya smiled.

"You'll know."

I disliked actors.

We ran again.

Veya turned.

Nothing.

Her eyes rose.

Nothing.

Then her body stopped.

Not much.

Just enough.

I lifted the coat.

The shadow appeared in the place she was already looking.

"Oh," I said.

Nessa said, "Don't."

"What?"

"Talk."

"During rehearsal?"

"Practice."

We did it again.

I got the timing right.

Then I moved the shadow away.

Smoothly.

Too smoothly.

Pell, watching from the house, said, "Board."

"What?"

"It slides."

"It is on a stick."

"Man doesn't know that."

"How does a man leave?"

Pell stood and took three steps sideways.

His shoulders moved a little. His height changed a little. His head
shifted.

"Walk it."

I tried.

The coat bounced like a drunk scarecrow.

Pell covered his face.

"Less man."

"You said walk."

"Not a parade."

Again.

Small rise.

Small settle.

Pause.

Then out.

From the house, it was enough.

Not good.

Enough.

I had a role to rehearse too.

The chest was real wood and mostly empty. I carried it onstage with one
hand while using a crutch in the other.

Teren stopped me.

"No."

"What?"

"You're working too hard."

"I'm carrying a chest."

"Porter does this every day."

"He has two legs."

"So?"

I stared at him.

Teren pointed toward the entrance.

"Use both hands. Sellen brings it to the mark."

Sellen was playing a servant in the first scene. He could carry the
chest to just inside the wing before my entrance. I could brace, put
both hands on it, and take the last four steps without a crutch.

I tried.

It worked.

Not elegantly.

Better.

My first line came while I was still holding the chest.

"I was told this house."

Veya said, "By whom?"

"A man."

"What man?"

"The one who told me this house."

She stared at me.

I stared back.

Someone behind the set laughed.

Teren said, "Don't wait for it."

"I wasn't."

"You were."

"I was breathing."

"Faster breathing."

The next run was cleaner.

At midday, I ate my bread and onion sitting on the rear step.

Pell sat beside me with nothing.

I looked at him.

He looked at my bread.

"No."

"I didn't ask."

"You were going to."

"I was going to ask what kind."

"Bread."

"What kind?"

"Mine."

He sighed.

"Rolls were better."

"Paid work ended."

"We're still working."

"Different problem."

He looked offended.

I had been waiting to use that.

The afternoon disappeared into small work.

A buckle broke.

Not mine.

A paint pot spilled.

Also not mine.

Jori could not find a cue page because he was holding it.

Hara changed stockings twice.

Sava came in, collected the wrapped prepared strip from yesterday, and
left with it. Nobody asked me to help.

The plain taper remained in use.

The audience began arriving before sunset.

I worked the front for ten minutes, then Teren called me to dress.

Porter had a brown coat with a patched elbow and a cap that smelled like
someone else's hair.

I put it on.

The first piece went without me.

Then The Borrowed House began.

I waited right.

Sellen brought the chest to the mark.

He said, "Yours."

"Today?"

He looked at me.

"Forever."

"That seems unlikely."

My entrance came.

I set the crutch aside where it would not fall, braced, took the chest
with both hands, and walked the four steps.

The audience saw a porter carrying a chest.

They did not see Sellen move it most of the distance.

"I was told this house."

Veya looked at the chest.

"By whom?"

"A man."

"What man?"

"The one who told me this house."

A laugh.

Not huge.

Enough.

I liked it.

The husband entered.

The misunderstanding grew.

I had three more lines, one of which was simply, "I don't know."

That got the best laugh.

I left.

Sellen handed me my crutch.

"Good."

"Which part?"

"You left."

I hated actors.

I stripped the cap off and went rear.

Pell was already changing into the neighbor's coat.

The fake man waited against the wall.

Coat.

Stick.

Hat.

Nothing else.

I sat on the stool.

Crutches against wall.

Good foot flat.

The lamp was lit.

The pale window glowed.

Onstage, Veya and Hara argued about the chest.

The audience laughed.

I held the stick loosely.

Not yet.

Veya crossed.

Not yet.

She turned.

Not yet.

Her eyes rose.

Then she saw him.

I lifted.

Someone in the audience stopped laughing.

A small sound came from the front.

Not quite a gasp.

Close.

Hara turned toward the window.

The shadow stood there.

A man.

I knew there was no man.

I could see the stuffed sleeve hanging six inches from my hand.

Veya stepped backward.

"Who's that?"

I moved the bottom of the stick.

Too much.

The shadow leaned strangely.

I corrected before it became obvious.

Then I walked it away.

Small rise.

Settle.

Pause.

Out.

The audience made another sound.

Gone.

I lowered the coat.

My shoulder immediately complained.

Four seconds.

Maybe five.

Pell came behind the hanging in his neighbor's coat.

"Did it work?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"How do you know?"

"They shut up."

Then he went onstage.

I stayed behind the window and grinned at a coat.

Jori appeared.

"Why are you smiling?"

"Man."

He looked at the coat.

"That's not a man."

"Tonight."

Jori looked toward the stage.

"Fair."

Then he handed me a rope.

"Hold."

I held it.

The rest of the show did not care that I had just been a Porter and a
man who did not exist.

There was a missed entrance in the final piece.

Not mine.

A cup rolled off a table and did not break.

Veya forgot a glove and used the wrong hand for an entire scene.

Nobody died.

The audience laughed.

Afterward, I carried benches.

The fake man came apart.

Hat onto shelf.

Coat into basket.

Crosspiece against wall.

The man disappeared faster than the audience.

Nessa checked the pale cloth for soot.

Pell complained about food again.

Rinna counted money.

She paid me one copper.

"Porter and rear."

"I was also a man."

"One copper."

"I was two visible people."

"One of them was a coat."

"The audience disagreed."

"Ask them for money."

I took the copper.

Tomorrow was Hessa.

The thought arrived without warning.

Not day after tomorrow.

Tomorrow.

Away first.

I looked at the fake man's crosspiece leaning against the wall.

Then at my hand.

Then stopped.

That was not paper.

That was not the apparatus.

That was not anything.

I put the copper in my pocket.

On the walk home, my shoulder hurt more than my palm.

The right grip still did not twist.

Worth one copper.

Possibly the best financial decision I had made in Carrow.

Lyssa was home before me.

Her ankle was unwrapped.

She was standing at the table cutting thread.

I looked down.

She did not look up.

"It's better."

"I didn't say anything."

"You looked."

"You're developing magic."

"Mine works."

I sat.

She glanced at my coat.

"How was theatre?"

"I delivered a chest to the wrong house."

"On purpose?"

"Professionally."

"Good."

"Then I was a man in a window."

Lyssa stopped cutting.

"Were you?"

"No."

She waited.

"It was a coat."

"That sounds less impressive."

"Audience thought it was a man."

"Did you wear the coat?"

"No."

"Was anyone wearing the coat?"

"No."

She looked at me.

"So you were a coat."

"I was operating a man."

"That's worse."

"It worked."

"Did they pay the man?"

"One copper."

"Good for him."

I put the coin on the table.

She slid it back toward me.

"Yours."

"Hessa tomorrow."

"That's not money."

"No."

She looked toward the cup.

The note was still underneath.

I pulled it out.

DAY AFTER TOMORROW.

AFTER MIDDAY.

AWAY FIRST.

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

The first line was wrong now.

I found charcoal and crossed out DAY AFTER TOMORROW.

I wrote TOMORROW above it.

Lyssa watched.

"Scientific."

"Very."

"Does Hessa know you changed her note?"

"No."

"Good."

I put it back under the cup.

Tomorrow, Hessa would put paper under glass and ask whether away was
easier.

Tonight there was a wooden crosspiece against a theatre wall that had
been a man for five seconds because a lamp, a coat, Veya's eyes, and my
hand had agreed to lie at the same time.

I did not write that down.

My shoulder hurt.

Lyssa's ankle was better.

There was bread for morning.

That was enough work for one day.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 190

## THE SUBJECT

Lyssa was gone before I finished changing Hessa's note.

Not because she had written it wrong.

Because time kept attacking the accuracy.

TOMORROW.

AFTER MIDDAY.

AWAY FIRST.

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

I crossed out TOMORROW and wrote TODAY.

The paper now had three different versions of the first line on it.

This seemed scientifically important.

Lyssa had seen me doing it while tying her shoe.

"Your science keeps changing."

"The science is fine. The calendar is unstable."

"That's worse."

Her ankle was unwrapped. She still favored it for the first few steps,
then less once she had moved around. I noticed without saying anything.

She noticed me noticing without saying anything.

We were improving.

She left with a cloth bag over one shoulder and told me she would
probably be late.

"Where?"

"Out."

"Excellent detail."

"You'll survive."

She kissed my cheek and left.

I ate bread.

The note stayed under the cup.

Nothing moved.

Today it would.

Maybe.

That word had become expensive.

I got to East Market Hall early enough to be useful and late enough that
Rinna did not accuse me of arriving yesterday.

The chalk board had changed.

GREG: SET / FRONT UNTIL MIDDAY.

No role.

I looked at Rinna.

"Paper," she said.

"Today."

"After midday?"

"Yes."

"Then you're mine until food."

This was apparently how scheduling worked when two institutions wanted
pieces of me.

Nessa had the pale window down on the floor. The man from last night was
no longer a man. His coat was in a basket. His hat was on a shelf. The
wooden crosspiece had been tied into a bundle with three other pieces of
scrap wood.

I looked at it once.

Then Nessa handed me a bucket.

"Water."

I got water.

The morning was mostly walls.

One wall went up.

Another came down.

A narrow painted arch had to move from rear storage to the right side
without scraping the fresh edge. Pell and I carried it together.

"Your end up," he said.

"My end is up."

"More up."

"Then yours is down."

"Correct."

We reached the turn.

The arch did not.

We backed out.

"Other way," Pell said.

"You said this way."

"I was wrong."

"That was easy."

"I practice."

We turned the arch and tried again.

It fit.

Jori had me check three stool positions for the afternoon bill, then
changed one after I checked it. I asked whether he wanted me to check
again.

"No."

"How will we know?"

"I moved it."

"That has failed before."

He pointed at the front doors.

"Go sell something."

I worked the front until the smell of somebody's fried onions outside
became stronger than my loyalty.

A man asked whether The Borrowed House was playing again.

"Tomorrow."

"Is the porter the same?"

I paused.

"No."

He looked disappointed.

This was flattering for almost half a breath.

Then he said, "Good."

I sold him a seat anyway.

At midday Rinna pushed a copper-colored token across the ticket table.

"Food."

"What is this?"

"Marker. Bring it back."

"Why?"

"Because it is mine."

I looked at the token.

"Does it buy food?"

"No."

"Then this is a terrible food system."

"It means I know you left."

"Why do you need to know I left?"

"So I know you're gone."

I took the marker.

"Very advanced."

She ignored me.

I ate bread and a small piece of cheese in the lower market, returned
the marker, collected my crutches from behind the ticket table, and left
for the Guild.

Rinna called after me.

"Greg."

I turned.

"Paper."

"Yes."

"Don't be late tomorrow because paper happened."

"I don't know what paper is going to do."

"Neither do I. That's why I'm telling you now."

This was unreasonable and probably useful.

The Guild front clerk saw me and raised one finger before I spoke.

I stopped.

"I hate that."

"Wait."

"I haven't asked anything."

"Good."

I waited.

The front room smelled of dust, old wood, and something sharp from the
corridor to the left. A woman I had seen before was arguing with a clerk
over a bundle of prepared cord.

"I brought six."

"You brought five."

"I tied six."

"There are five."

"Then one is somewhere."

"Yes."

This did not appear to satisfy either of them.

The cracked prepared plate was not on the shelf where I had seen it
before. A chalk mark remained on the wood beneath the empty space.

The note was gone too.

I did not ask.

A gray-coated woman came through carrying two narrow boxes.

"Paper Man."

"No."

She kept walking.

I had lost this fight permanently.

Hessa appeared from the opposite corridor with a folded board under one
arm and a cup in the other.

She looked at me.

"Shoulder?"

I blinked.

"Hello."

"Shoulder."

"Sore."

"Why?"

"I was a man."

She stopped.

"At the theatre."

"That did not improve it."

"I held a coat on a stick behind a window."

Hessa looked at me for a moment.

"Of course you did."

"It worked."

"Your shoulder?"

"The man worked."

"Sit."

This was becoming a theme in my life.

The room was not the same one as last time.

It was smaller and had one high window, two shelves of boxed pieces,
folded cloth, iron weights, and three empty wooden frames stacked
against the wall. The table was broad enough for the apparatus but left
little room around it.

Hessa set down the board.

"Today is not whether you can move paper."

"I can."

"Yes."

I enjoyed that.

She took a drink.

"Today is whether one requested direction produces the response more
reliably than another."

"Which way works."

"If you need to make it worse."

"I do."

"Which way works."

I liked Hessa.

She did not always deserve it.

The apparatus had changed.

The square board was gone. In its place was a long pale board marked
with a center line and three shallow positions. One nearest me. One
farther away. One crossing the center at right angles.

Hessa had paired guides for each position, target and control, with
enough space between them that the fins would not touch.

The glass shield was the same.

Or looked the same.

"Same glass?"

"Yes."

"Today?"

Hessa stared at me.

I smiled.

She checked the table legs, pressed two corners, then wedged the rear
left leg with a thin piece of wood.

"Chair."

I put it where I thought it belonged.

"Back."

I moved it.

"More."

I moved again.

"Stop."

My good foot sat flat. My other leg rested where it did not press the
table. The crutches went against the wall.

Hessa touched my shoulder lightly, then had me lift the arm.

"Pain?"

"Muscle."

"That was not the question."

"Yes. A little."

"Does it change if you hold here?"

I moved.

"Yes."

"Then don't."

She made a mark beside the chair leg.

"You're not bracing on the table."

"I know."

"You're tired."

"I'm always tired."

"Useful."

"How?"

"It means I don't have to ask."

She laid out the fins.

They were small, folded from pale paper, matched as closely as I could
tell. She made me inspect the bases.

Flat.

Edges similar.

No obvious crease differences.

Then she took six little wooden counters from a pouch.

Three had black marks.

Three did not.

"Order?" I asked.

"Mine."

"Why?"

"Because if you choose, you'll choose away."

"That's the point."

"The point is to compare it."

She closed the counters in her hand.

I disliked science again.

First came the draw.

I settled.

Breath.

The familiar inward gathering came slowly, then narrowed.

The same slight wrongness at the wrist edge.

Nothing new.

Nothing dramatic.

Hessa watched my hand.

"Hold."

I held.

"Release."

I released.

She made a mark.

"Twenty-six."

I knew.

I did not say it.

That felt like another kind of progress.

Then shaping without a target.

No paper under glass.

Just the familiar deliberate attempt to make the narrow gathered thing
become something more directed without letting it run away from me.

The wrist edge crowded first.

I wanted to correct harder.

"Don't chase."

I didn't.

The shape held for two breaths, rough but deliberate.

"Release."

I did.

"Twenty-two."

Then the paper came out.

Hessa placed the first pair in the straight guides.

Target assignment by counter.

Left.

She set the black-marked counter beside it.

"Requested direction?"

"Away."

"Say it once."

"Away."

The target fin sat between us and the far end of the board.

I drew.

Shaped.

This time the shape did feel different.

Not stronger.

Cleaner was too generous.

Less crowded, maybe.

Like there was one fewer thing in the way.

I held the idea of away.

The fin moved.

Barely.

A tiny forward slip, less than the width of my smallest fingernail.

The control did not visibly move.

I breathed out.

Hessa's finger rose.

I closed my mouth.

She inspected the glass.

Then the board.

Then the guide.

Then the fin.

She checked the wedge under the table.

"Movement."

"I know."

"Good."

"That sounded almost kind."

"It wasn't."

Attempt fourteen.

Movement.

Away.

The second pair went into the same guides.

New target assignment.

Right.

Hessa drew another counter.

"Toward."

I looked at her.

"You hate me."

"Toward."

I drew.

Shaped.

The feeling was not painful.

Not blocked.

Just worse.

Crowded sooner.

I tried to hold the requested direction without pushing harder.

Nothing moved.

I released.

Hessa inspected anyway.

Attempt fifteen.

No movement.

"Again?" I asked.

"Different."

She moved the guides to the cross position.

Now the target and control sat sideways relative to me.

New fins.
