New counter.

Left target.

"Requested direction?"

"Away."

I frowned.

"The guide is sideways."

"Yes."

"So if it moves away, it leaves the guide."

"Possibly."

"That's rude."

"Shape."

I did.

The first breath felt messy.

The second settled.

Away.

The target twisted.

Not much.

Its front edge shifted out of the shallow guide and stopped.

The control stayed where it was.

I looked at Hessa.

Her finger was already up.

I had not even opened my mouth.

"I wasn't going to say anything."

"You were."

She inspected.

The fin had not traveled forward along the guide.

It had moved partly out of it, in the direction farther from me.

Hessa marked the page.

"Movement."

"Away."

"Observation."

"Away."

"Yes."

Attempt sixteen.

Movement.

Away.

Something warm moved through my chest that had nothing to do with mana.

Hessa replaced everything.

"Water."

"I'm fine."

"Water."

I drank.

She waited longer than I wanted.

The room had a high narrow window. Somewhere outside it, someone dropped
metal and swore. A cart rolled over stone. Two people passed the door
arguing about a frame number.

Hessa read something on another sheet while I sat there.

Her day continued even during mine.

"Ready?" she asked.

"Yes."

"Say that after you move your shoulder."

I moved it.

It hurt.

"Less yes."

"I can do another."

"I know."

She set the next pair.

Straight guides.

Target right.

"Requested direction?"

"Sideways. Left."

"That's not away."

"Correct."

I drew.

Shaped.

Tried to keep left.

The shape wanted to smear.

That was the only word I had for it.

I corrected once.

"Don't chase."

I stopped correcting.

The target gave a tiny tremble.

Or I thought it did.

Then one corner shifted.

Not left.

Slightly away.

Hessa leaned closer.

"Release."

I released.

She inspected.

"Movement?"

I asked.

"Maybe."

"Direction?"

"Not left."

"Away."

"Possibly."

She wrote longer this time.

Attempt seventeen.

Possible movement.

Requested sideways left.

Observed displacement too small for clean directional claim, with
possible away component.

I hated the sentence before she finished it.

"That's disgusting."

"It happened."

"Maybe."

"Yes."

"You enjoy this."

"No."

I did not believe her.

Attempt eighteen was toward.

Nothing moved.

Not the target.

Not the control.

The shape felt crowded almost immediately. My shoulder wanted to lift.
Hessa stopped me before I did.

"Release."

I did.

She checked the apparatus.

No movement.

Toward.

Failure.

I stared at the fin.

"I don't like toward."

"Noted."

"That was a joke."

"I know."

"You wrote it."

"I wrote that you reported increased difficulty maintaining the
requested shape."

"That is not what I said."

"It's what I heard."

I considered arguing.

She was right enough to be irritating.

"One more," she said.

"Only one?"

"Yes."

"Away?"

She showed me the counter after drawing it.

"Away."

The final pair went into the far position.

Still along the same line from me, but farther from the edge of the
table than the first away attempt.

I noticed.

"Distance."

"Not today."

"You changed it."

"I know."

"Then it matters."

"It may. Which is why I am recording it."

I wanted to ask three questions.

I asked none.

Draw.

Shape.

Away.

The first breath was bad.

The second was better.

The familiar wrist-edge asymmetry sat there, annoying and known.

I left it alone.

Away.

The target moved.

A tiny slip.

Then stopped.

Not farther than the first.

Maybe less.

Still visible.

Control unchanged.

My whole body wanted to lean toward it.

I did not.

Hessa raised no finger this time.

I stayed still anyway.

She looked at me.

"Good."

That was worse than the finger.

She inspected everything.

Attempt nineteen.

Movement.

Away.

Hessa sat.

I kept looking at the paper.

Three away requests.

Three movements.

Two toward requests.

No movements.

One sideways request.

Something too small and wrong-directed to call cleanly, maybe away.

I knew what I wanted to say.

Hessa knew too.

She started writing before I could.

"So away is easier."

"Probably."

I stopped.

That was not one of her usual words.

"What?"

"Probably."

"You said probably."

"I know."

"Write that."

"No."

"Why?"

"Because I'm writing what happened."

"What happened is probably."

"What happened is three requested-away trials produced visible target
movement today. Two requested-toward trials did not. The sideways trial
produced an ambiguous small displacement that did not follow the
requested sideways direction."

"And went away."

"Possibly."

"Mostly."

"Possibly."

I leaned back.

My shoulder objected.

Hessa pointed at it.

"Also that."

"That is theatre."

"That is your body."

"Different problem."

"Not while your body is part of the apparatus."

I hated when she did that.

She finished writing.

I tried to read upside down.

She turned the page.

AWAY RESPONSE REPEATED UNDER MULTIPLE TARGET POSITIONS.

TOWARD RESPONSE NOT OBSERVED TODAY.

DIRECTIONAL PREFERENCE RELATIVE TO GREG'S POSITION SUPPORTED.

NOT GENERALIZED.

I read it twice.

"Supported."

"Yes."

"Not possible."

"It remains possible in the larger sense."

"You changed the word."

"Because we have more evidence."

I looked at the fins again.

They were pathetic.

Tiny pale folded pieces under glass.

Nothing about them looked like an achievement.

"So away is easier."

Hessa rubbed ink from the side of one finger.

"That is currently the better explanation."

I smiled.

"Don't."

"I'm not doing anything."

"You are."

"Can I practice away?"

"No."

"That was fast."

"No independent draw. No independent shaping. No paper testing. No
coins. No thread. No trying larger objects. No trying to discover
whether every object in your room dislikes you."

"I wasn't going to."

"You were."

"I was going to think about it."

"You may think."

"Generous."

"Quietly."

I looked at the page again.

"Does this mean the wrist edge..."

"No."

"You let me get farther that time."

"No."

"Everything has a side."

"Everything has many sides if you describe it badly enough."

"That sounds familiar."

"It should."

She pulled a fresh sheet toward herself.

"Next question."

"How hard can I push?"

"No."

"How far?"

"Closer."

I waited.

She drew two short lines on the page.

One near.

One farther.

"Does distance from you change the response if the requested direction
remains away?"

I looked at the far line.

"You already moved the last pair farther."

"Yes."

"And it moved."

"Yes."

"So distance doesn't matter."

Hessa stared at me.

I sighed.

"Fine."

"Good."

"You're going to make me fail at two distances."

"I'm going to compare them."

"Same thing with better handwriting."

She wrote:

AWAY ONLY.

NEAR / FAR.

I pointed at it.

"When?"

"Not tomorrow."

"Of course."

"I have other work."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"People keep saying that."

She gathered the counters.

I looked at the record one more time.

"Twenty-six."

"Yes."

"Twenty-two."

"Yes."

"Nineteen."

"Yes."

"And away is probably easier."

Hessa paused.

"Supported as a directional preference in the current setup."

"Probably."

She gave me the cup.

"Leave."

I drank the last of the water first.

Then I left.

The gray-coated woman was in the front room.

She looked up.

"Paper Man."

"Away."

She frowned.

I kept walking.

This was a victory.

East Market Hall was still open when I returned.

I had not planned to go back.

My feet apparently had.

Rinna saw me.

"Paper?"

"Away."

Pell was carrying a basket behind her.

He immediately took one step backward.

"Working."

I hated him.

Rinna said, "Can you carry chairs?"

"Yes."

"Better."

I carried chairs.

Not with magic.

There were six.

Pell carried four at once and made a point of it.

I carried two.

At home, Lyssa had beaten me back.

She was sitting with one foot up on the other chair, ankle bare.

Better.

Actually better.

I put Hessa's new paper under the cup.

Lyssa watched me.

"Paper?"

"Yes."

"Did it move?"

"Three times."

Her eyebrows went up.

"Away."

"All three?"

"All three away attempts."

"Did the other ways move?"

"Toward didn't. Sideways did something stupid."

"Like you."

"Very much like me."

She held out her hand.

I gave her the sheet.

She read Hessa's wording.

"DIRECTIONAL PREFERENCE RELATIVE TO GREG'S POSITION SUPPORTED," she
said.

"See?"

"That sounds important."

"It sounds terrible."

"Does Hessa agree?"

I sat down.

"More than before."

Lyssa nodded once and gave the paper back.

That landed better than congratulations would have.

I put the sheet under the cup.

Beside it I put Hessa's next note.

AWAY ONLY.

NEAR / FAR.

Not tomorrow.

No date yet.

The room contained bread, a cup, two chairs, a table, thread, my copper,
Lyssa's basket, and several other things that could theoretically be
farther from me than they currently were.

They remained exactly where they were.

I was getting very good at magic.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 191

## THE WITNESS

Lyssa was gone before I woke properly.

There was bread on the table, half an apple beside it, and a strip of
paper under the cup.

Not Hessa's paper.

LYSSA: DON'T FORGET THEATRE BECAUSE PAPER HAPPENED.

I stared at it.

Rinna had said almost exactly the same thing.

This was either evidence of a conspiracy or evidence that I had become
predictable.

I ate the apple first because it seemed less judgmental.

Hessa's actual sheet remained under the cup.

AWAY ONLY.

NEAR / FAR.

No date.

Nothing I was allowed to do about it.

I left it there.

On the way to East Market Hall, a carter shouted at his boy to move a
sack away from the wheel.

The word caught.

I hated that.

The boy dragged the sack with both hands.

I kept walking.

East Market Hall had both front doors open and three different people
trying to occupy the same patch of floor.

A rolled hanging blocked half the entry. Two benches stood outside.
Someone had left a basket of costume shoes directly where nobody could
avoid it.

I stepped over the basket and looked at the chalk board.

KING: MAREK / HARA / SELLEN BORROWED HOUSE: VEYA / HARA / EVIN / PELL
THE LETTER BOX: VEYA / SELLEN / GREG / NESSA REAR: JORI / PELL FRONT:
RINNA

I read my name.

Then I read it again.

GREG: WITNESS.

No if.

No maybe.

No missing person.

No little mark beside it that meant somebody else had failed to arrive.

Rinna came past with a roll of tickets in one hand.

"You're on time."

"You threatened me."

"I scheduled you."

"Same thing."

She pointed at the board.

"I saw."

"Good."

"What is The Letter Box?"

"Piece you're in."

"Useful."

"Pages are on the table."

She kept moving.

That was it.

I stood there for another second.

Nessa put a folded green cloth into my hands.

"Right table."

I took it.

Theatre had apparently decided I had experienced enough emotion.

The Letter Box was a short domestic comedy about a locked wooden box
that belonged to nobody until everybody wanted it.

Veya played Mara, the woman whose dead aunt had left the box in her
room.

Sellen played Corren, her brother, who claimed the aunt had promised him
everything worth stealing.

Nessa played the landlady, who claimed anything left in the room after
rent was late belonged to the house.

I was a municipal witness.

My job was to stand there, record who said what, and repeatedly refuse
to decide anything.

I liked him immediately.

There were eighteen lines.

Not forty-three.

Not eight.

Eighteen.

Enough that I had to learn them. Not enough that I needed to reconsider
every decision that had brought me to Carrow.

The important object was not the box.

That was the first problem.

The important object was a little brown purse on the table beside it.

The purse contained the key.

The audience was not supposed to know that until late.

I knew because the page said so.

This became a problem almost immediately.

Teren arrived while I was reading.

He had bread in his mouth and two pins stuck through the cuff of his
shirt.

"You're Witness."

"I gathered."

"Know it?"

"I've seen part."

"Then see the rest."

He pulled one pin from his cuff and used it to point.

"Entrance after Nessa says, 'Then let the town hear it.' Left side. You
have ledger. You don't touch box."

"Today?"

He looked at me.

"Yes."

"Good."

"Keep doing that."

Then he walked away.

I looked after him.

That might have been praise.

I decided not to ruin it by checking.

Rehearsal started with a missing ledger.

The ledger was eventually found under the folded cloth I had moved to
the right table.

This was somehow not my fault.

The first run lasted less than a minute before Teren stopped us.

"Greg."

I had entered correctly.

I had my ledger.

I had said the first two lines correctly.

I looked at him.

"What?"

"Stop looking at the purse."

I looked at the purse.

Then I realized I had looked at the purse.

"I'm not."

Veya laughed.

Teren pointed at me.

"You just did it again."

"Because you said purse."

"Before that."

"I was looking at the table."

"At the purse."

"The purse is on the table."

Sellen said, "He's got you there."

Teren ignored him.

"Your Witness doesn't know the key is in it."

"I know."

"That's the problem."

"I am aware of what my character doesn't know."

"Your face isn't."

I looked at Veya.

She was standing close enough to the purse that her sleeve nearly
touched it.

She had not looked at it once.

I knew it was there.

Because I knew it mattered, I kept seeing how carefully she did not see
it.

This was deeply irritating.

Teren reset us.

"Again."

We went again.

Nessa entered as the landlady and demanded the box.

Sellen accused her of theft.

Veya accused Sellen of being cheap enough to steal from the dead.

Nessa said, "Then let the town hear it."

I entered with the ledger.

"I was told there was a dispute."

"There is," Veya said.

"There isn't," Sellen said.

Nessa pointed at both of them.

"There."

I opened the ledger.

"Good. A dispute."

Small laugh from Pell, who was not the audience and should have been
working.

I continued.

"Names."

They all spoke at once.

I looked down to write.

Then my eyes flicked to the purse.

Teren clapped once.

"Greg."

"Damn it."

"Again."

On the third run, I solved it by staring determinedly at the box.

Teren stopped us.

"Now the box is guilty."

"It is the subject of the dispute."

"You are making it look like it contains a body."

"What do you want me to look at?"

"The people."

This seemed unreasonable.

I tried the people.

Veya was much harder to accidentally reveal.

Sellen was impossible to make look unimportant, but that was his
problem.

We got through the scene.

At the reveal, Veya finally picked up the purse, opened it, and removed
the key.

The action felt larger because she had spent the previous part of the
scene treating the purse like cloth.

I noticed.

Then Teren said, "Greg, line."

I had missed it.

Of course.

"I object to the opening."

Sellen looked at me.

"You object now?"

"Municipally."

Pell laughed again.

Teren rubbed his face.

"That's not the line."

"I know."

"Then use the line."

"I had it."

"Find it."

The actual line was, "I have not witnessed consent to open the box."

Less funny.

Probably better.

We reset.

The hall kept working around us.

A shoe buckle snapped during the next run. Nessa left the scene, fixed
it while still wearing her landlady shawl, and came back before we
reached her next entrance.

Someone spilled a little red paint near the rear wall.

Pell stepped in it.

He spent the next ten minutes leaving half a red footprint everywhere
until Jori noticed and made him clean both the floor and his shoe.

Hara changed stockings because the first brown pair had a run at the
ankle.

Then she changed back because the second pair were apparently the wrong
brown.

I asked what wrong brown meant.

She told me to become older.

I did not ask again.

By midday I had most of the Witness in my mouth.

Not good.

Present.

There was a difference.

I ate bread outside with Pell and EvIn.

Pell had food today.

This disappointed me.

EvIn was reading the Porter pages from The Borrowed House while eating a
cold potato.

"You're Porter?" I asked.

"No."

"Who is?"

He pointed with the potato.

A young man I had seen carrying benches was practicing my old lines
beside the wall.

I watched him.

"I was told this house," he said.

"By whom?" EvIn called.

"A man."

"What man?"

"The one who told me this house."

Pell looked at me.

"Better."

"He hasn't performed it yet."

"Still."

The young man had less trouble with the chest because he had two good
legs and no need to arrange the final four steps around a crutch.

I resented this unfair advantage.

Rinna came outside.

"Greg."

"I am eating."

"After."

"Then why say my name now?"

"So you know after exists."

She went back in.

Pell nodded.

"Good system."

"I hate all systems."

"Paper."

"Don't."

He took another bite of bread.

That was the entire conversation.

After food, Rinna had me move two benches away from the front wall.

Away.

I noticed the word.

Then I moved the benches with my hands because I was not an idiot.

Mostly.

The afternoon rehearsal got better.

I stopped looking at the purse.

Then I started avoiding looking at the purse so aggressively that I
turned my body away from the table every time I crossed.

Veya stopped me.

"Now you hate the purse."

"I don't look at it."

"You move around it."

"I am not touching it."

"Exactly."

She took my place.

"Watch."

She entered.

She crossed the room.

She spoke to Sellen.

She put one hand on the table beside the purse.

She shifted the ledger.

She took the chair.

She never looked at the purse.

She also never avoided it.

It existed.

That was all.

She gave me my place back.

I tried again.

Better.

Not good.

Better.

Teren said, "Fine."

I took it.

Then I ruined something else.

During Sellen's long complaint about the dead aunt promising him the
box, I turned toward him too fully and blocked Veya from the house.

"Greg."

I moved.

"Other way."

I moved too far.

"You're not furniture."

"I have been furniture here."

"Not today."

"Today?"

Teren pointed at the floor mark.

I found it.

The next run worked.

No one applauded.

A cart outside lost a board with a crash loud enough that everyone in
the hall stopped.

Jori went to the front door.

"Not ours."

We continued.

By late afternoon, the floor had been swept twice and was dirty again.

The hall smelled of warm bodies, old paint, lamp oil, damp wool from
coats, and the onion someone backstage had cut without permission.

Rinna opened the house.

People came in.

The man who had asked about the Porter came back.

I saw him take a seat near the left aisle.

He saw me at the side curtain.

His expression changed.

I could not tell whether this was good.

I was in the Witness coat.

Gray-brown, plain, narrow collar.

The ledger was real enough to be heavy.

My shoes were mine.

Nessa had looked at my crutches, looked at the entrance, and moved my
waiting position half a pace closer to the wing without comment.

The Borrowed House played first.

I watched the new Porter from behind the side hanging.

He entered with the chest.

"I was told this house."

The audience laughed earlier than they had for me.

I disliked him.

"By whom?"

"A man."

"What man?"

"The one who told me this house."

Bigger laugh.

I hated him for approximately four seconds.

Then he missed the next line.

I liked him again.

The piece continued.

He recovered.

The audience man near the aisle laughed.

Good.

Fine.

Whatever.

Then came The Letter Box.

I stood left.

Ledger.

Crutches placed where I could reach them after exit.

The purse sat on the table.

I did not look at it.

I knew exactly where it was.

That was worse.

Nessa entered.

Sellen entered.

Veya entered.

The argument built.

The audience laughed when Sellen claimed the dead aunt had always loved
him best.

Veya said, "She called you a damp boot."

"Affectionately."

Bigger laugh.

Nessa stepped between them.

"Then let the town hear it."

My cue.

I entered.

No stumble.

No wrong side.

"I was told there was a dispute."

"There is," Veya said.

"There isn't," Sellen said.

Nessa pointed.

"There."

I opened the ledger.

"Good. A dispute."

The audience laughed.

Not huge.

Enough.

"Names."

They spoke over each other.

I looked at them.

Not the purse.

Sellen tried to hand me the box.

I stepped back.

"I have not witnessed transfer."

"I'm transferring it."

"I have witnessed an attempt."

"That's the same thing."

"It is written differently."

That got a laugh I had not expected.

I did not wait for it.

Teren had trained that out of me by violence.

Metaphorical violence.

Mostly.

The scene moved.

Veya crossed near the table.

Her hand passed within an inch of the purse.

Nothing.

It was just a purse.

Sellen accused Nessa of hiding the key.

Nessa accused him of swallowing it.

I wrote this down because the Witness wrote everything down.

Then Sellen said, "Ask her where she was when the aunt died."

Veya went still.

That was the turn.

The audience quieted.

I knew the purse was about to matter.

My eyes wanted it.

I kept them on Veya.

She looked at Sellen.

Then at Nessa.

Then down at the table as though the table had only just occurred to
her.

Her fingers touched the purse.

The audience followed.

There.

Now it mattered.

She opened it.

The key came out.

A small sound moved through the room.

Not a gasp.

Recognition.

Sellen said, "You had it."

Veya said, "I had her purse."

"With the key."

"I didn't know."

I had my line.

"I have not witnessed consent to open the box."

Sellen turned on me.

"You have witnessed the key."

"Yes."

"Then open it."

"No."

"Why?"

"I have no authority."

The audience laughed.

Sellen stared at me.

"What do you have?"

I looked at the ledger.

"Excellent notes."

That was scripted.

I wanted everyone to know.

The laugh was mine anyway.

The box opened later.

It contained three old letters, a broken comb, and a receipt proving the
aunt had already sold the valuable thing everyone thought was inside.

Veya got the final line.

"She left us the argument."

She was better than all of us.

The audience went quiet for the line, then laughed after.

I stood there and let her have it.

This was harder than it sounded.

We exited.

Sellen handed me my crutch.

"Fine."

"That word is destroying me."

"You want bad?"

"No."

"Then fine."

I had no answer.

Backstage immediately became work.

Jori needed the narrow cord.

Pell had put it on the correct shelf, which made me suspicious.

A costume basket had to move.

Someone had lost a glove.

The red paint footprint returned because apparently Pell had not cleaned
the edge of his sole.

Nessa saw it.

Pell said, "Impossible."

She pointed.

He cleaned it again.

The last short piece had no Greg.

I watched half of it until Rinna gave me a stack of used tickets to
count.

I counted wrong.

"Again," she said.

"I can move paper."

"Not that paper."

I counted again.

Thirty-seven.

She checked.

Thirty-seven.

"See?"

"You needed two tries."

"Repeatable."

She pushed the stack away from me.

I looked at her.

She looked at me.

"Don't."

"I didn't."

"You did."

I hated everyone.

Cleanup took longer than the last piece.

The audience left dirt, two apple cores, one ribbon, and a wooden button
under a bench.

Nobody claimed the button.

Nessa claimed the ribbon for repairs.

Pell claimed one apple core was not his.

Nobody had accused him.

Rinna paid me one copper.

"Witness," she said.

"And front."

"One copper."

"And tickets."

"One copper."

"And I moved benches."

"Do you want less?"

"No."

"Good."

She turned to the board.

"Tomorrow."

I looked.

My name was not written yet.

"What tomorrow?"

"Work."

"Role?"

"Maybe."

"That's how this started."

"What?"

"Nothing."

She erased part of the board.

I put the copper away.

Outside, Carrow had gone blue with evening.

Shops were closing in pieces. One shutter down, another still open. A
woman swept flour from a doorway. Two boys argued over a hoop in the
road. Somewhere farther off, somebody was singing badly and confidently.

I went home.

Lyssa was not there.

I was first.

This felt unusual enough that I stood in the room for a moment as if I
had entered the wrong house.

Then I put bread on the table, set my copper beside the others, and took
off the Witness coat.

No.

My coat.

The Witness coat belonged to the hall.

This was how it happened.

I sat.

Hessa's sheet waited under the cup.

AWAY ONLY.

NEAR / FAR.

No date.

I did not touch it.

Lyssa came in after dark carrying a narrow bundle wrapped in pale cloth.

I looked at the bundle.

She looked at me looking.

"No."

"I didn't ask."

"You looked."

"This is becoming oppressive."

She put the bundle against the wall.

"How was theatre?"

"I was a municipal witness."

"Did you witness anything?"

"A purse."

She waited.

"I wasn't supposed to."

"Then why did you?"

"I knew it mattered."

"Did it?"

"Later."

"So you were right."

"Incorrectly."

Lyssa took off her shoes.

Her ankle looked almost normal.

I did not look long enough to get caught.

Probably.

"What did the witness do?" she asked.

"Told everyone I had no authority."

"Good casting."

"I've already had this joke."

"Then stop earning it."

I told her about the purse.

About Teren telling me to stop looking at it.

About Veya standing beside it without making it exist until she needed
it.

Lyssa listened while unwrapping the pale bundle.

Inside was dark cloth.

Not yellow.

Not green.

Not mine.

I did not ask.

"Did you get it right?" she asked.

"In the show."

"Then good."

"Fine."

"What?"

"Teren said fine."

"You said good."

"I am improving the review."

She smiled.

I showed her the copper.

"One."

"For witnessing?"

"And front. And tickets. And benches."

"Underpaid."

"Rinna asked if I wanted less."

"Did you?"

"No."

"Then expertly negotiated."

We ate bread and the last of the cheese.

Later, Lyssa moved the dark cloth to the chair.

I noticed that it went farther from me.

I noticed that I noticed.

Then I noticed that I was an idiot.

Hessa's paper remained under the cup.

Twenty-six draws.

Twenty-two shapings.

Nineteen external attempts.

Away was probably easier.

The purse had been important all afternoon.

The best thing I had done with it was pretend I did not know.

I did not write that down either.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 192

## THE SELLER

The dark cloth was gone in the morning.

So was Lyssa.

There was bread.

This was enough continuity for one household.

I ate, put Hessa's paper back under the cup after checking that it still
did not contain a date, and left for East Market Hall early enough that
Rinna could not accuse me of being late.

She accused me of something else.

"Move those."

I had barely entered.

"Good morning."

"Those."

She pointed at the rear benches.

There were six of them.

"Why?"

"Because nobody bought them."

That answered a different question, but I knew better than to ask for
the first one again.

The hall looked wrong.

Not empty. It was too early for empty to mean anything. But the benches
had been laid for a larger house, and the spaces between them made the
room feel stretched. A whole section near the rear wall had no chalk
marks on the ticket board.

Rinna was erasing seat numbers.

"Bad sales?" I asked.

"Thin."

"How thin?"

"Move the benches."

I moved the benches.

Pell arrived while I was on the second one and took the other end
without being asked.

"Closer," Rinna said.

I was grateful for the word.

We carried the bench forward.

"Closer."

Again.

By the fourth bench, the rear of the house had become open floor. Rinna
had us turn the last two sideways against the wall.

"Now what?" Pell asked.

"Nothing."

"Good."

He left.

I stayed.

Rinna looked at me.

"What?"

"You can also leave."

"I was told work."

"Board."

The board had changed.

KING: MAREK / HARA / SELLEN SHOPKEEPER: VEYA / GREG / HARA LETTER BOX:
VEYA / SELLEN / GREG / NESSA SHORT: EVIN / HARA FRONT: RINNA / GREG
REAR: JORI / PELL

I looked at Shopkeeper.

I had not played it since the road.

Not here, anyway.

The part felt old enough that I knew where the first line lived before I
found the pages.

"Both?" I asked.

Rinna nodded.

"Shopkeeper and Witness?"

"Yes."

"Front too?"

"Until house."

"One copper?"

"If you're lucky."

"I've learned not to negotiate with you."

"No, you haven't."

That was true.

The Shopkeeper rehearsal was barely a rehearsal.

Veya wanted the counter moved six inches because the new bench layout
changed the sightline from the left side.

Hara could not find the narrow brown scarf and used a gray one instead.

Teren came in, watched three minutes, and said, "Again from debt."

We did.

The scene was familiar.

Not memorized like a prayer. Better than that. I knew where Veya would
move when she became angry. I knew Hara liked to take half a beat before
the line about interest. I knew the counter edge caught my sleeve if I
stood too close.

I also knew the joke that usually got a laugh.

That knowledge became a problem later.

For now, it was useful.

"You're less annoying," Veya said after the run.

"Thank you."

"That wasn't praise."

"It was comparative."

She considered that.

"Fine."

Everyone had become infected.

The Letter Box needed less work.

I entered.

I looked at people.

The purse existed without becoming the most important object in the
world.

Teren watched once.

"Fine."

I stared at him.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

He left to argue with Jori about whether The King needed the second
stool.

It did not.

Then it did.

Then Hara needed it for the short piece, so The King lost it again.

The stool survived all of this without opinion.

By midday, Rinna had sold nineteen seats.

I knew because she made me count.

"Twenty," I said.

She tapped the board.

"Nineteen."

"One man paid for two."

"Two seats."

"Then twenty."

"He hasn't chosen the second."

"That's still a seat."

"Not until someone sits in it."

I looked at her.

"This is a terrible accounting system."

"Sell another."

She handed me the ticket roll.

The market outside was busy enough to make the hall's thin sales
insulting.

People passed with baskets, bolts of cloth, fish wrapped in leaves, hot
food, children, dogs, arguments, and absolutely no urgent need to see
theatre.

I stood at the door.

A woman slowed.

"What's playing?"

"The King, The Shopkeeper, The Letter Box, and a short piece."

"Is The King sad?"

"Sometimes."

She kept walking.

Rinna leaned around the door.

"Sell the seat."

"I answered."

"Poorly."

A man stopped.

"Funny?"

"Some of it."

He kept walking.

Rinna closed her eyes.

"What?"

"Do you want them inside?"

"Yes."

"Then tell them why."

"They asked questions."

"Yes."

Another pair approached.

I tried again.

"The Shopkeeper is funny."

Rinna made a small sound behind me.

I ignored her.

"The Letter Box is also funny."

The older of the pair asked, "The King?"

"Less funny."

"Good."

They bought two seats.

I turned.

"See?"

"You found people who wanted less funny."

"I identified the market."

"Go eat."

I ate bread on the rear step.

Pell had an onion and nothing else.

"That's bleak," I said.

"It's an onion."

"Yes."

He bit it.

I moved farther away from him.

Not magically.

Hara came out carrying one shoe.

"Anyone seen the other?"

"No," I said.

Pell shook his head.

She looked at his feet.

"I don't have it," he said.

"I didn't say you did."

"You looked."

Hara went back inside.

Pell frowned.

"People are becoming unreasonable."

I ate my bread.

A few more seats sold.

Not many.

By house, Rinna had twenty-seven people for a room that could take far
more.

She closed the rear completely.

A family tried to sit there.

"Forward," she said.

"We like the back."

"Not today."

The father looked at the empty benches against the wall.

"Why?"

"Better seats."

He accepted this because Rinna said it like a fact.

They moved forward.

Another woman wanted the aisle.

A boy wanted to sit beside the wall.

A tired-looking man said he did not care where he sat and then rejected
two places.

I helped until Jori called me back.

From behind the hanging, the house sounded strange.

Twenty-seven people made more individual noises than a crowd.

One cough belonged to a specific man.

A child whispered and could be heard.

Someone unwrapped something dry and crackling.

A woman cleared her throat three times.

There was no broad murmur to cover any of it.

The King went first.

Marek entered.

The opening line usually got a small laugh.

Nothing.

He continued.

Hara came in.

Nothing.

Sellen's first complaint landed into a silence so clean I could hear a
cart outside.

I looked at Pell.

Pell was checking a strap.

He did not seem concerned.

Onstage, Marek got smaller.

Not quieter exactly.

Closer.

He stopped throwing the lines at the back wall because there was nobody
at the back wall.

Hara did the same.

They played to each other.

The audience watched.

They were not restless.

They simply did not laugh.

Then a man near the front laughed alone at a line I had never heard get
much before.

Nobody joined him.

Marek continued.

At the end, the applause was modest but real.

"Bad?" I whispered when Hara came off.

"No."

"They didn't laugh."

"It's The King."

"They usually laugh more."

Hara was already taking off the wrong brown scarf.

"Then they usually laugh more."

That was all I got.

The Shopkeeper was next.

I put on the coat.

My right palm was almost normal now, but the leather grip stayed. I had
paid for it. It could continue being useful.

Veya adjusted the counter.

"Don't shout."

"I don't shout."

She looked at me.

"I project."

"Don't project at twenty-seven people like they're seventy."

That seemed like a distinction invented to hurt me.

We went on.

The room was visible.

That was the first difference.

With a full house, faces became a mass beyond the first rows. Tonight I
could see the man who had rejected two seats. I could see the child
leaning against his mother's arm. I could see a woman in a yellow
headcloth who looked tired enough to sleep sitting upright.

I could also see the man who laughed alone during The King.

This became important.

The opening worked.

Veya accused me of cheating her brother.

I denied it.

Hara entered with the account.

I said, "That account is honest."

Veya said, "Your handwriting bends around the truth."

Usually a laugh.

Tonight, two people made small sounds.

Not silence.

Almost worse.

We continued.

I was fine.

Then came the debt line.

It was one of mine.

"If your brother wanted mercy, he should have borrowed from a priest."

The lone-laughing man laughed.

A real laugh.

Sharp.

Immediate.

Mine.

The rest of the house gave me a little more.

I felt it.

That was the problem.

My next line was not a joke.

It was supposed to be irritated.

"Debt does not disappear because you dislike the number."

I gave it more.

More voice.

More face.

More Shopkeeper.

The line died.

Not quietly.

It died in a room where I could see every person who had refused to save
it.

Veya looked at me.

Not Greg.

Shopkeeper.

Her expression said I had become embarrassing.

Which worked for the scene.

This was unfair.

She stepped closer.

"Then perhaps the number should learn to be likable."

Small laugh.

She had not pushed.

I hated her.

We continued.

For the next few lines, I could feel myself wanting the room.

I wanted another laugh to prove the dead line had been an accident.

Sellen was not in this piece, so he could not tell me to stop being
stupid.

Veya could.

She did not.

She just played the scene.

Eventually I had to play it with her.

The room came back.

Not much.

Enough.

At the end, the lone man laughed again.

This time I did not try to marry him.

Backstage, Teren was waiting.

"You chased it."

"I didn't."

"You did."

"I said the line."

"At them."

"It was for them."

"Leave them alone."

Veya passed us.

"That's what I said."

"You said don't shout."

"Same thing."

"It is not."

Teren pointed toward the stage.

"Letter Box."

I had no time to defend myself.

Good.

The Letter Box started with a dropped cup.

Not in the script.

Nessa set it on the table badly. It rolled off during Sellen's entrance
and hit the floor.

One woman in the audience laughed.

Sellen looked down.

"Even the crockery is leaving."

That got the best laugh of the day.

Nessa picked up the cup.

"Rent is due from it too."

Another laugh.

I waited in the wing.

The audience had apparently decided pottery was superior to actors.

Fine.

Then the scene settled.

The purse was on the table.

I did not look at it.

This was easier today.

Not easy.

Easier.

"Then let the town hear it," Nessa said.

I entered.

"I was told there was a dispute."

"There is," Veya said.

"There isn't," Sellen said.

Nessa pointed.

"There."

I opened the ledger.

"Good. A dispute."

Yesterday, laugh.

Today, nothing.

I felt the silence.

I did not fix it.

"Names."

They all spoke.

We continued.

The tired woman in the yellow headcloth was watching now.

The child had stopped leaning against his mother.

The man who laughed alone was quiet.

Sellen tried to hand me the box.

"I have not witnessed transfer."

Nothing.

"I'm transferring it."

"I have witnessed an attempt."

A small laugh from somewhere left.

"That's the same thing."

"It is written differently."

Yesterday, good laugh.

Today, one cough.

I wanted to sharpen it.

I did not.

The scene moved.

Veya reached the turn.

"Ask her where she was when the aunt died."

She went still.

The room went still with her.

That part worked.

Not because anyone laughed.

Nobody was supposed to.

The purse became important when Veya let it become important.

The key came out.

The tired woman leaned forward.

Sellen said, "You had it."

Veya said, "I had her purse."

"With the key."

"I didn't know."

"I have not witnessed consent to open the box," I said.

The lone man laughed.

Wrong place.

I nearly looked at him.

I didn't.

Sellen turned on me.

"You have witnessed the key."

"Yes."

"Then open it."

"No."

"Why?"

"I have no authority."

The child laughed.

One bright laugh.

Then his mother did.

Then three more people.

Not huge.

Enough.

Sellen said, "What do you have?"

I looked at the ledger.

"Excellent notes."

The laugh was smaller than yesterday.

It was still there.

I let it go.

At the end, Veya said, "She left us the argument."

Silence.

For half a breath I thought the room had lost her.

Then the tired woman laughed softly.

Someone else did.

The applause came.

Twenty-seven people could still make applause.

It just sounded like twenty-seven people.

Backstage, Sellen took the box from me.

"You were loud in Shopkeeper."

"I know."

"Good."

"Teren already told me."

"Then why are you telling me?"

"I didn't start this conversation."

"You looked conversational."

I walked away.

The short piece was delayed because Hara's missing shoe had finally been
found inside the costume basket under two aprons.

Nobody knew how.

Hara blamed Pell.

Pell said, "I don't even wear that size."

"That isn't a defense."

"It should be."

EvIn missed his first entrance because he was helping move a bench for a
late-arriving couple.

Jori filled the silence by knocking twice from behind the side wall as
though someone in the scene was at the door.

Hara turned toward it.

"Not now."

The audience laughed.

EvIn came in.

The scene survived.

By the end, the house had warmed a little.

Or we had.

I could not tell.

Afterward, Rinna counted coins at the front table.

There were fewer than yesterday.

I knew that without counting.

Pell came up beside me.

"Bad house."

Rinna did not look up.

"They paid."

Pell considered this.

"Good house."

"Go clean."

"Bad Rinna."

"Go."

He went.

I helped collect cups.

There were fewer cups.

Less dirt.

One child had left a length of string tied around the leg of a bench.

I untied it.

The lone-laughing man stopped at the door.

"You were the shopkeeper."

"Yes."

"Funny."

"Thank you."

He left.

I stood there.

Rinna looked up.

"Don't."

"What?"

"Whatever you're about to become."

"I wasn't becoming anything."

"Good."

She gave me one copper.

"Shopkeeper, Witness, front."

"That sounds like at least three copper."

"One."

"I was also benches."

"Do you want less?"

"No."

"You're learning."

Teren came past carrying the ledger.

"Tomorrow's not set."

"Work?"

"Probably."

"Role?"

"Probably."

I waited.

He looked at me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

He left.

Fine.

Outside, the market was nearly done.

I bought an onion because apparently Pell had damaged me.

Then I went home.

Lyssa was there.

The pale wrapping from yesterday was folded on the chair.

The dark cloth was gone.

I put the onion on the table.

She looked at it.

"Why?"

"Food."

"That's one onion."

"It remains one onion."

She looked at me again.

"What happened?"

"The audience hated me."

"All of them?"

"Twenty-seven."

"You counted?"

"Rinna did."

Lyssa cut the onion.

"They paid to hate you?"

"Apparently."

"Good business."

"They didn't laugh."

"At anything?"

"At a cup."

"Was the cup funny?"

"Very."

She put half the onion in the pan.

I told her about The Shopkeeper.

About getting one laugh and trying to get another one too hard.

About Veya making the next line work by not doing that.

Lyssa stirred the onion.

"So they didn't hate you."

"They were quiet."

"That's different."

"I know that now."

"Did they clap?"

"Yes."

"Then stop insulting your customers."

I sat.

Hessa's paper was still under the cup.

AWAY ONLY.

NEAR / FAR.

No date.

I slid it back where it had been without reading it a second time.

Lyssa saw.

"Nothing?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

"Why does everyone say that when nothing happens?"

"Because you keep making nothing difficult."

The onion cooked.

Twenty-seven people had watched us.

Some had laughed.

Some had not.

One man had laughed at nearly everything except the parts I expected.

Tomorrow I would probably work.

At some point Hessa would decide how far away to put the paper.

Tonight the onion stayed in the pan.

I left the audience alone.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 193

## THE DINER

Lyssa was still home when I woke.

This felt suspicious.

She was sitting at the table with one knee up, eating the last piece of
bread and reading something that did not belong to me. The pale wrapping
from yesterday had disappeared. So had the dark cloth. In their place
were two onions.

I looked at them.

Lyssa looked at me.

"No."

"I didn't say anything."

"You bought one onion."

"Yes."

"Now there are two."

"That seems like growth."

"I bought the other one."

"Then we're both contributing."

She tore the bread in half and gave me the smaller piece.

"Your contribution was worse."

I ate it anyway.

Her ankle did not have a wrap on it. I noticed because I had eyes. She
noticed me noticing because apparently she had developed a professional
interest in my eyes.

"It's fine."

"I didn't ask."

"You looked."

"Everyone keeps accusing me of looking."

"Stop being visible."

"That's not how looking works."

She folded the paper she had been reading.

"What are you doing today?"

"Theatre. Probably."

"When?"

I stopped.

That was a useful question.

"Teren said tomorrow."

"That's not a time."

"He also said probably."

"That's worse."

I considered going to the hall immediately.

"I should check the hall."

Lyssa said, "Not at breakfast because somebody might need you at supper."

"I need to know when."

"Then ask when you're actually going."

She stood, put the folded paper into the pocket of her skirt, and took
one onion.

"That's mine."

"You bought the other one."

"I bought this one."

"Prove it."

She left with my onion.

I sat at the table with the remaining onion and Hessa's note.

AWAY ONLY.

NEAR / FAR.

Still no date.

I did not read it twice.

This was becoming a skill.

I had no reason to be at the hall before midday.

I knew this because I went there to check.

Rinna was at the front table with her hair tied back and a cup beside
one hand. She looked up when I entered.

"No."

"I haven't asked anything."

"Good."

"What time?"

"After third bell."

I looked toward the board.

It was blank except for old chalk smears.

"Role?"

"After third bell."

"That isn't a role."

"It's also not now."

I stood there.

Rinna drank from her cup.

"Are you going to make me assign you something so you'll leave?"

"No."

"Good."

I left.

This was humiliating.

I had several hours.

Carrow was already busy. The lower market had opened fully, and the
morning traffic had settled into the part of the day where everybody
seemed to know where they were going except me.

I went to the cobbler first.

Not because anything was wrong.

That was new.

The leather grip on my right crutch had darkened where my palm sat. The
seam still stayed where it belonged. It had not twisted once. My hand
was nearly healed.

The cobbler glanced at it when I came in.

"Still there."

"Yes."

"Good."

"I came to tell you."

"No, you didn't."

"I could have."

He went back to a boot sole.

I looked around.

There was nothing I needed.

"How much for soap?"

He looked up.

"Wrong shop."

"I know."

"Do you?"

I left.

The soap seller was three stalls down.

I bought a small hard square for one copper after arguing with myself
longer than I argued with the seller.

It was soap.

I had paid for magic garbage for the same price.

This seemed unfair to soap.

Then I bought a meat bun.

That cost less thought.

I carried both to the lower market and sat on the same low wall where I
had once discovered I could tolerate peace for about fifteen minutes.

Today I had food.

This improved peace.

A dog slept under a cart with one eye open. A woman repaired the bottom
of a basket by threading fresh reeds through the old broken edge. Two
fish sellers argued about whether one of them had moved a bucket.
Neither mentioned magic.

A boy chased a hoop past me.

An old man sat at the far end of the wall and removed one shoe.

I ate the bun.

Nobody asked me where Pell was.

Nobody handed me a message.

Nobody told me to move a bench.

I finished eating and stayed.

The dog changed sides.

The basket got a new bottom.

The fish argument ended without agreement.

The old man put his shoe back on.

I stayed long enough that I stopped measuring it.

That was probably because I was tired.

Eventually a bell rang.

Not third bell.

I knew this because I was not completely useless.

I got up and went nowhere important.

There was a public pump two streets over. I washed my hands, then used
the new soap because I had bought it and wanted evidence that the
purchase had not been stupid.

The water ran gray from my fingers.

Paint.

Dust.

Lamp soot.

Something brown that I decided was food.

The soap smelled faintly of pine and strongly of soap.

I washed my face too.

A woman waiting behind me said, "You missed a spot."

"Where?"

She pointed at my ear.

I washed my ear.

"Other one."

I stared at her.

She smiled.

I washed the other one.

By the time third bell was close enough to be arguable, I went to the
hall.

Rinna looked up.

"Better."

"I was exactly on time."

"You're early."

"Close enough."

"Board."

There was writing now.

KING: MAREK / HARA / SELLEN PETITION: VEYA / SELLEN / GREG / EVIN WIDOW:
VEYA / HARA / NESSA SHORT: HARA / EVIN FRONT: RINNA REAR: JORI / PELL
GREG: THIRD MAN / SET

I read it.

Third Man.

Again.

No emergency.

No explanation.

Just work I already knew.

"Goat?" I asked.

Sellen was behind me.

"Don't."

"I asked one word."

"You asked the wrong one."

"Is it the same piece?"

"Today?"

I looked at him.

He looked at me.

"Yes," he said.

"Good."

"You're getting worse."

Nessa handed me a rolled brown hanging.

"Back wall."

"Today?"

"Yes."

Sellen laughed.

I carried the hanging.

The afternoon setup was boring in the best possible way.

The Petition used the same table but a different chair because the old
chair had developed a loose front rung. Pell had tied it once. Davin had
untied it and written BAD on the seat in chalk.

Someone had still moved it back toward the stage.

Nessa caught it before it got there.

"Who?"

Nobody answered.

Pell said, "Could be anyone."

"It has your cord on it."

"Many people use cord."

"Your knot."

Pell looked offended.

"My knots are not signatures."

Davin walked past.

"They are."

Pell carried the chair away.

I helped hang the brown wall, moved the petition stack to the correct
shelf, then moved it again because Veya wanted more room on the table.

The fake loaf was not in tonight's bill.

I checked anyway.

It was in the kitchen box.

Correctly.

This bothered me.

At rehearsal, The Petition took twelve minutes.

I knew the entrance.

I knew the line.

I knew Sellen was not a goat.

This was useful.

Teren watched from a bench.

"Again from Greg."

I entered.

"My neighbor's goat has eaten all my patience and half my fence."

Sellen came in.

"Your fence was mostly hope."

A laugh existed only in our memory because there was no audience.

I said the next line.

We continued.

Teren stopped us once because EvIn was too far upstage.

He stopped me once because I had set my crutch where Veya needed to
cross.

"Sorry."

"Move it."

I moved it.

That was the entire correction.

No one explained what it meant for my future.

I appreciated this.

Then Teren left.

He had somewhere else to be.

I did not know where.

Nobody told me.

Sellen sat on the edge of the stage and took a small wrapped packet from
his pocket.

"What is that?"

"Food."

"What kind?"

"Mine."

"I was asking what kind."

"Still mine."

I hated everyone.

He opened the packet.

Dried fish.

I changed my mind.

"You can keep it."

"I was going to."

We ate before house.

Not together exactly.

Near each other.

I had another piece of bread from a stall outside. Sellen had dried
fish. Pell had acquired an egg.

"Cooked?" I asked.

He cracked it against the edge of a box.

It was cooked.

I felt disappointed.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You expected raw."

"No."

"You did."

"You're becoming predictable."

He peeled the egg.

Hara came through in a clean blue overdress with one sleeve pinned but
not yet sewn.

"Nessa wants you."

Pell stood.

"Why?"

"Didn't ask."

He went.

Sellen watched him leave.

"He's good with the shutters."

"I know."

"That's why Nessa wants him."

"I know."

Sellen ate another piece of fish.

"You sound upset."

"I'm not."

"You looked upset."

I considered leaving the theatre forever.

Instead, I asked, "Do you live near here?"

Sellen glanced at me.

"Why?"

"No reason."

"Then no."

"That doesn't answer."

"It answered the question you should've asked."

"What question?"

"Can you come bother me after work?"

"I wasn't going to."

"Then we're done."

I ate bread.

After a moment, he said, "South side."

That was all.

I did not ask where.

It felt like winning something I had not entered.

House was better than yesterday.

Not full.

Better.

Rinna had opened one more row.

People came in carrying the day's heat with them. The hall warmed
quickly. Someone had tracked dry straw onto the floor. A little girl
wore a paper crown that kept slipping over one eye.

I had no front work.

This felt luxurious.

I stood backstage and did almost nothing for four whole minutes.

Then Jori handed me a stool.

"King."

Of course.

The King went cleanly.

The audience laughed more than yesterday.

Marek did not seem relieved.

Hara did not seem surprised.

Sellen missed one word, replaced it with another, and the world did not
end.

I watched from the wing until Nessa told me to stop standing where she
needed to pass.

The Petition was next.

I put on the Third Man coat.

It smelled like somebody else.

Probably because it belonged to everybody.

The petition stack had the real wax seal on top and nonsense beneath.
The chair was the good chair. My crutch was where Veya would not hit it.

I waited.

The scene began.

Veya wanted the road repaired.

EvIn wanted the town to admit the road belonged to somebody else.

Sellen wanted everybody to stop saying his goat had caused anything.

Then my cue came.

I entered.

"My neighbor's goat has eaten all my patience and half my fence."

A decent laugh.

Not huge.

I did not ask it to become one.

Sellen entered.

"Your fence was mostly hope."

Bigger laugh.

I looked at him.

As Third Man.

Annoyed.

Correct.

The scene moved.

There was a point later where I had to hand Veya the petition stack.

I handed it to her upside down.

She looked at it.

I looked at it.

The seal was now at the bottom.

For half a second, my brain tried to fix reality.

Veya did it for me.

She turned the stack over and said, "At least the signatures know which
way the road goes."

The audience laughed.

I said, "More than the council does."

That was not the line.

It was also not terrible.

Sellen made a small sound through his nose.

Veya continued.

The scene survived.

Backstage, Sellen said, "That was fine."

"Which part?"

"The wrong papers."

"I gave them upside down."

"I noticed."

"Veya fixed it."

"Yes."

"I added a line."

"Yes."

"It worked."

Sellen looked at me.

"For everyone."

I stopped.

He had done that on purpose.

"You've been waiting months to say that."

"Days."

"It feels longer."

"That's because you talk."

Nessa came through carrying a basket.

"Move."

We moved.

Widow's Room used the painted doorway.

I had seen it enough times that the false depth barely interested me
from backstage anymore. It was cloth, frame, paint, floor seam, crate.
From the house, people still looked into a room that was not there.

I did not need to learn anything about it today.

I carried a costume basket instead.

The short piece lost a spoon.

This became a problem for approximately six minutes.

The spoon was required for Hara to accuse EvIn of stealing it.

Without the spoon, the accusation became philosophically difficult.

Everyone searched.

I checked the prop shelf.

Pell checked under the stage.

Nessa checked the kitchen box.

EvIn checked his own pocket twice.

Hara said, "Use the knife."

"The knife changes it," EvIn said.

"Into what?"

"Murder."

"Fair."

Eventually Jori found the spoon inside the cup from The Letter Box.

Nobody knew why.

The Letter Box was not even playing tonight.

"Yesterday was yesterday," I said.

Nessa looked at me.

"Don't become Teren."

"I don't know where he is."

"Good."

The short piece happened with the spoon.

After the show, Rinna paid me one copper.

"Third Man. Set."

"I was also stool."

"One copper."

"Petition recovery."

"Veya recovered it."

"I assisted."

"By causing it?"

"Essential."

"One copper."

I took it.

There was no immediate rush to go home.

This was partly because Pell said, "Food?"

It was not a question for me specifically.

It became a group.

Rinna did not come.

Nessa said no before anyone finished asking.

Hara had somewhere to be and left with her blue overdress half unlaced
at the back.

EvIn went with her for the first block, then turned east.

Jori disappeared.

Teren was still absent.

That left me, Pell, Sellen, and eventually Marek, who joined because he
had heard the word food from across the hall.

We went to a stall near the south edge of the market where a woman sold
bowls of beans with chopped greens and small pieces of pork if you paid
extra.

I paid extra.

This was reckless wealth.

The bowl cost enough that I noticed and not enough that I changed my
mind.

We sat on low stools around a table that leaned toward Pell.

"It likes me," he said.

"It's broken," Sellen said.

"Same thing."

Marek ate quietly.

For a while nobody talked about theatre.

This was strange.

Pell complained about a landlord who had raised the price of a room
because the roof had been repaired.

Sellen said that was the point of repairing the roof.

Pell said the old hole had been free.

Marek asked if anyone knew a cheap cart going north in three days.

"Why?" I asked.

He looked at me.

"Going north."

I waited.

Nothing else came.

Apparently this was allowed.

Sellen owed Pell a copper.

I learned this when Sellen put a copper on the table.

Pell immediately handed it to Marek.

Marek put it in his pocket.

"What just happened?" I asked.

"Debt," Sellen said.

"For what?"

"No."

That was the entire explanation.

I ate my beans.

They were good.

Better because I had bought them after work instead of receiving them
because someone else had negotiated food into a job.

Not much better.

Enough.

A group at the next table started arguing about whether the cider was
watered.

The stall woman told them it was, and if they wanted stronger they could
pay for stronger.

The argument ended.

This seemed like excellent governance.

Pell told a story about falling through a chicken-house roof when he was
fourteen.

I did not believe half of it.

Sellen believed less.

Marek believed none.

The story improved under these conditions.

By the time we left, the market lamps were lit.

Sellen went south.

Now I knew that meant something.

Pell went east after remembering he had promised to return a latch.

Marek went north, perhaps because he was practicing.

I went home.

Lyssa was not there.

That was normal.

There was a folded paper under the cup.

Not Hessa's old paper.

A new one.

I opened it.

THREE DAYS.

AFTER MIDDAY.

AWAY ONLY.

NEAR / FAR.

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

I read it once.

Then I put it beneath the old note.

Three days.

Good.

I had time to do absolutely nothing about it.

Lyssa came home while I was taking off my coat.

She carried no mysterious cloth.

This was almost disappointing.

"How was theatre?"

"Fine."

She stopped.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You said it wrong."

"I was Third Man."

"Again?"

"Yes."

"Goat?"

"Everyone knows too much."

She put a small wrapped bundle of greens on the table.

I told her about the upside-down petitions.

She laughed before I got to Veya's line.

"That's not the funny part."

"It is from here."

"I recovered."

"Veya recovered."

"I assisted."

"By causing it?"

I stared at her.

"Do you speak to Rinna?"

"No."

"Suspicious."

I told her I had eaten after work.

"Without me?"

"You stole my onion."

"I bought that onion."

"You cannot prove that."

She unwrapped the greens.

I showed her the new Hessa note.

"Three days," she said.

"Yes."

"Good."

"Apparently."

"What happens then?"

"Near and far."

"Both away?"

"Yes."

"How far?"

"I don't know."

"How near?"

"I don't know."

She nodded.

"You know a lot."

"I moved paper."

"Three days ago."

"Recently."

She pushed the greens toward me.

"Cut those."

I cut them.

No theory emerged.

The soap sat in my pocket until I remembered it and put it by the basin.

Lyssa picked it up.

"You bought soap."

"Yes."

"For us?"

I had not thought that far ahead.

"Obviously."

She smelled it.

"Good."

That one I accepted.

We ate the greens with what was left of the onions.

Tomorrow the hall would probably want me again.

In three days Hessa wanted paper farther away and paper nearer.

Sellen lived somewhere south.

Marek needed a cheap cart north.

Pell had once fallen through a chicken-house roof, unless he had not.

I had bought soap.

It was not a dramatic day.

I liked it anyway.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 194

## THE HAND

The soap was already wet.

I stood at the basin holding it.

Lyssa was still asleep.

This meant either she had used it before bed or somebody had broken into
our room during the night to wash.

I chose Lyssa.

I washed my face, my hands, and both ears without supervision.

Then I put the soap back exactly where it had been.

This was adulthood.

There was bread on the table. Not much. I cut it in half and left one
piece under a cloth for Lyssa. Beside it I put a note.

THEATRE.

That seemed sufficient.

I considered adding PROBABLY.

I did not.

Hessa's two papers stayed under the cup.

THREE DAYS had become two without either of us doing anything.

I liked that kind of progress.

The East Market Hall was already making noise when I arrived. Hammering
came from inside in an irregular pattern that suggested either repair or
murder.

It was repair.

Davin was on one knee near the left edge of the stage with a short pry
bar under a floorboard. Jori held a lamp low. Nessa stood above them
with both arms full of folded costumes.

"Don't step there," Davin said.

I had not stepped there.

"Good."

A chalk cross marked one board.

"What happened?"

"Splinter."

"To who?"

"Board."

I looked at him.

Davin looked at me.

"Also EvIn."

EvIn was sitting on a bench pulling a long pale splinter from the heel
of his shoe.

"Not me," he said.

"Good."

"Through the sole."

"Less good."

Nessa pushed the folded costumes into my arms.

"Rail."

"Which?"

"Costume."

"There are two."

"Left."

"Today?"

She stared at me.

"Yes."

I carried them away.

The board had already been written.

KING: MAREK / HARA / SELLEN PETITION: VEYA / SELLEN / GREG / EVIN WIDOW:
VEYA / HARA / NESSA SHORT: HARA / EVIN GREG: THIRD MAN / SET

Yesterday.

Almost exactly.

I looked at Rinna.

"Did you forget to erase it?"

"No."

"So I'm Third Man."

"For now."

I did not like those words.

"How long is now?"

"Until it isn't."

This was not an answer, but it was accurate enough that Hessa might have
approved.

I went back to the costume rail.

One of the wooden pegs had pulled halfway out of the wall. Nessa had
apparently decided the solution was to put fewer clothes on it and more
clothes on me.

"Hold that."

Davin had appeared beside me with a hammer.

"What?"

"The rail."

I held it.

He pulled the peg out, looked into the hole, made a dissatisfied sound,
and put the peg in his mouth.

"Tool."

"Which?"

He pointed with the hammer toward an open box.

There were seven things in it.

"That narrows it."

"Thin chisel."

I found the thin chisel.

Wrong one.

He took a narrower one.

"That's thinner."

"Yes."

"You could have said narrowest."

"I said thin."

"This is why civilization fails."

He scraped old wood from the hole.

I held the rail.

Nessa took two costumes off my shoulder without thanking me.

Then she put one back.

"Why?"

"Wrong rail."

"Whose fault is that?"

"Yours now."

I held the rail harder.

The first change came before Davin finished.

Marek walked in carrying a folded scrap of paper and said, "I have a
cart."

Rinna looked up.

"When?"

"Last bell before dark."

"Today?"

"Today to arrange it. Leaves in two days."

"How long?"

"An hour. Maybe."

Rinna looked at the board.

I looked at the board.

Marek looked at me.

"No," I said.

Nobody had asked.

Rinna erased MAREK from The King.

"Who?"

Hara was pinning the sleeve of a brown coat.

"Not me."

"You are already in it."

"I know."

Sellen said, "I can take the King."

Rinna erased something else.

"You are already in it."

"I know."

"I hate all of you."

Teren came through the side door at that moment, carrying a rolled page
and a small bag of nails.

"Good."

Rinna pointed at the board.

"Marek has to leave."

Teren looked at Marek.

"Cart?"

Marek nodded.

Teren looked at Sellen.

"King."

Sellen nodded.

"Hara?"

"Still Hara."

"Fine."

"What about his part?" I asked.

Everyone looked at me.

"Marek's part."

Teren said, "Sellen."

"Sellen was already Sellen."

"Not in The King."

I had forgotten that names were not roles.

This was getting complicated.

Rinna erased half the board and rewrote it.

KING: SELLEN / HARA / EVIN PETITION: VEYA / GREG / EVIN WIDOW: VEYA /
HARA / NESSA SHORT: HARA / EVIN GREG: THIRD MAN / SET

Sellen stared.

"Who am I in Petition?"

"Not in it," Teren said.

"Why?"

"Because you're King."

"Those happen at different times."

"Your coat doesn't."

Nessa said, "And I need the brown coat open."

Sellen looked at the coat.

The coat did not apologize.

Teren went to the stage.

"King first."

I kept holding the costume rail.

Davin tapped the new peg into place.

"Done."

I let go.

The rail stayed on the wall.

"Good."

"Yes."

"Do I get paid for this?"

"No."

"Then less good."

Davin moved back toward the floorboard.

Rehearsal became a sequence of people asking which version of today
existed.

Sellen knew the King well enough to take Marek's part with the page in
his hand for the first run. EvIn took Sellen's smaller place and
immediately complained that the hat smelled like Marek.

"It smelled like that before," Hara said.

"Then Marek smells like hat."

"Line," Teren said.

They continued.

I moved the petition table.

Then Rinna told me not to because The Petition might move later.

"Where?"

"Later."

"That's a time."

"Good."

I left it.

Marek went out to arrange his cart.

I caught him at the door.

"What are you taking north?"

He looked at me.

"Myself."

"That seems difficult to ship."

"I'm going with it."

"Why?"

"See my sister."

There it was.

A whole answer.

I was not prepared.

"Oh."

"She's having a baby."

"You're helping?"

"No."

"Then why are you going?"

Marek gave me the same look Lyssa gave me when I asked questions that
had already been answered.

"See my sister."

"Right."

He left.

I stood there for a moment.

Rinna called, "Greg."

I turned.

"Table."

Of course.

The second change came because Hara had to leave before evening.

This information arrived from a girl I did not know, who came through
the front doors and said, "Hara?"

Hara went outside.

She came back three minutes later.

"I have to go after Widow."

Rinna said, "Why?"

"Work."

"This is work."

"Other work."

"Better?"

"Different."

Rinna looked offended on behalf of employment.

Teren looked at the board.

Hara said, "I can do King and Widow. Not Short."

"Fine."

EvIn said, "I can do Short alone."

"No," three people said.

He looked hurt.

The Short disappeared from the board.

This created space.

Then it created a different problem.

Rinna had sold the evening as four pieces to at least six people.

"Add Letter Box," she said.

I looked at her.

"No."

"You like Witness."

"I do."

"Good."

"I didn't say yes."

"You are Witness."

The chalk changed again.

KING: SELLEN / HARA / EVIN PETITION: VEYA / GREG / EVIN WIDOW: VEYA /
HARA / NESSA LETTER BOX: VEYA / SELLEN / GREG / NESSA GREG: THIRD MAN /
WITNESS / SET

I read my line twice.

"You made it worse."

Rinna said, "I made it four."

"I have two coats."

Nessa said, "One coat."

"Third Man and Witness wear the same coat?"

"No. Witness wears gray."

"That's two coats."

"Third Man doesn't need the brown one today."

"He did yesterday."

"Today."

I closed my eyes.

"Good."

Nessa patted my shoulder once.

I think it was mockery.

We rehearsed The Petition first because everyone in it was present.

That was where I made the wrong mistake.

"My neighbor's goat has eaten all my patience and half my fence."

EvIn entered.

Not Sellen.

I knew that.

We continued.

Veya took the petition.

I said, "And if the council wishes to call that a road, perhaps the
council should walk it barefoot."

Teren said, "No."

I stopped.

"What?"

"Not that cut."

I looked at Veya.

She looked at me.

"What cut?"

"Road cut," Teren said.

"We're not on the road."

"I know where we are."

"Then why are you using it?"

"Because I know it."

EvIn said, "That's a strong defense."

"Thank you."

"It wasn't."

Teren held out his hand.

"Home cut. From the seal."

I knew the home cut too.

That was the problem.

I started again.

Veya held the petition stack.

I said, "The seal is real. That is more than I can say for the road."

"Yes," Teren said.

"Today?"

"Yes."

I pointed at him.

"See? Useful question."

"Keep going."

We kept going.

That was all.

Nobody gathered around to discuss the danger of knowing multiple
versions.

At midday, the floorboard was fixed.

Davin had cut the damaged edge back, set a narrow replacement strip, and
hammered it flush. The new wood showed pale against the old stage.

EvIn tested it with his shoe.

Davin said, "Don't."

EvIn stopped.

"Why fix it if I can't step on it?"

"Glue."

"Ah."

He stepped around.

Pell arrived late carrying a latch.

"Returned it."

"To who?" I asked.

"The person."

"Which person?"

"The latch person."

"That explains everything."

"It did to them."

He saw the board.

"Greg's two people."

"I'm always one person."

Pell looked unconvinced.

Marek returned with dust on his trousers and said the cart was arranged.

"Cheap?" Sellen asked.

"Cheap enough."

"North?"

"Still."

Rinna told him he was no longer King.

Marek looked at the board.

"Good."

I was offended.

"You don't care?"

"I have to pack."

"That's not the point."

"What is the point?"

I did not know.

He took a piece of bread from the table and left again to find Rinna's
string.

Apparently packing had already begun.

The third change was smaller.

The Widow's Room lost its pale shawl.

It had not disappeared.

Nessa had it in her hands.

It had torn under the arm.

"Use the dark one," Hara said.

"No."

"Why?"

"Wrong."

"How wrong?"

Nessa held the dark shawl against Hara.

We all looked.

Hara looked like a widow who had murdered her husband personally.

"Fair," Hara said.

Nessa repaired the pale shawl.

Nobody changed the board.

This felt like mercy.

By house, I smelled less like pine soap and more like old wool.

So the soap had lasted approximately half a day.

Still worth one copper.

The audience was normal.

I had forgotten normal could be noticeable.

Not full. Not thin. Not loud. Not strangely quiet.

People came in, sat down, talked until the lamps lowered, then mostly
stopped.

A baby cried once and was taken outside.

A man sneezed during The King.

Nobody built a chapter around it.

The King went first.

Sellen was good.

Not Marek-good in Marek's exact way. Sellen made the King's irritation
sharper and less tired. Hara adjusted without making it visible. EvIn
remembered the hat and most of the lines.

One audience member laughed where nobody usually did.

Sellen did not marry him.

I approved professionally.

Then The Petition.

I wore the right coat.

This mattered because I had nearly put on the gray Witness coat until
Nessa caught my sleeve.

"Later."

"I knew that."

"You had it on."

"Partly."

"Later."

I changed.

Onstage, the home cut stayed in my head.

"My neighbor's goat has eaten all my patience and half my fence."

EvIn entered.

"Your fence was mostly hope."

He did not say it like Sellen.

He said it like EvIn.

The audience laughed anyway.

I had a brief irrational objection.

Then Veya spoke and I had work.

The petition stack stayed upright this time.

I used the correct line about the seal.

Nobody applauded my accuracy.

The scene ended.

Backstage, I changed coats.

The gray Witness coat caught on the top of my crutch.

I pulled.

It caught harder.

Nessa slapped my hand away.

"Stop."

"I can get it."

"You're making it worse."

She lifted the cloth off the handle in one motion.

I put it on.

"Thank you."

"Go."

The Letter Box was already starting.

I had enough time.

That was all I needed.

Nessa's line came.

"Then let the town hear it."

I entered from the left with the ledger.

"I was told there was a dispute."

"There is," Veya said.

"There isn't," Sellen said.

"There," Nessa said.

I opened the ledger.

"Good. A dispute."

A laugh.

Normal.

I continued.

The purse sat on the table.

It was a purse.

I did not have to fight myself about it.

When the key came out later, the audience made the same small sound
audiences often made.

I gave the consent line.

Sellen argued.

I had no authority.

I had excellent notes.

The laugh was good.

Not enormous.

Good.

Veya got the last line.

"She left us the argument."

The audience laughed, then applauded.

I went offstage.

That was the whole performance.

I had done two roles.

Neither had changed my life.

I liked that too.

Hara left almost immediately after Widow's Room, still carrying the
repaired pale shawl over one arm.

"That's theatre property," Nessa called.

"I'm changing outside."

"Why?"

"Because I'm late."

"For what?"

Hara was already through the door.

Nessa looked at me.

"I don't know."

"I didn't ask."

"You looked."

This disease was everywhere.

Cleanup took longer than the show felt.

The floor collected scraps of thread, one crushed nut shell, two ticket
halves, and a small smear of something sticky near the third bench.

Pell touched it.

"Why?"

I asked.

He looked at his finger.

"Jam."

"How do you know?"

He licked it.

I walked away.

Davin checked the repaired floorboard.

The pale strip held.

Rinna counted coins at the front table.

There were more than yesterday.

I knew because the pile looked less insulting.

She paid me one copper.

"Third Man. Witness. Set."

"Two roles."

"One copper."

"Two coats."

"One copper."

"Rail."

"Davin fixed the rail."

"I held civilization together."

"Do you want less?"

"No."

"Good."

She pushed the copper toward me.

I took it.

On the board, tomorrow had already begun.

Not fully.

Just names and question marks.

I saw GREG once.

Then Rinna erased it.

I did not ask.

This required effort.

She noticed.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You look ill."

"I'm growing."

"Do it somewhere else."

I went home.

Lyssa was not there.

The bread I had left was gone.

My note was still on the table.

THEATRE.

Under it, in Lyssa's writing:

OBVIOUSLY.

I kept the paper.

I did not know why.

Hessa's note stayed under the cup.

Two days.

No new instructions.

No reason to touch it.

I washed my hands with the soap.

The water ran gray again.

The theatre had defeated cleanliness in less than one shift.

Lyssa came home after dark carrying a narrow wooden hoop and a bundle of
pale thread.

I looked at both.

She looked at me.

I looked away.

"Better," she said.

"How was work?"

"Changed."

"Bad?"

"No."

"Then?"

"Changed."

This was apparently an acceptable answer when other people used it.

I told her Marek was going north to see his sister because she was
having a baby.

"That's nice."

"He said he's not helping."

"With the baby?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"I asked why he was going if he wasn't helping."

Lyssa slowly put the wooden hoop on the table.

"What?"

"He said to see his sister."

"Yes."

"I understood eventually."

"Did Marek survive this conversation?"

"Barely."

She took off her outer layer and hung it.

I told her the board changed three times.

"How many people were you?"

"Two."

"That's too many Gregs."

"I said that."

"No, you didn't."

"I said I'm always one person."

"Different."

She was right, which was irritating.

I told her I had nearly worn the wrong coat.

"Did you?"

"Partly."

"Then you did."

"Nessa stopped me."

"Useful Nessa."

"Everyone is very useful when I'm wrong."

Lyssa smiled.

"Convenient."

We ate bread, cheese, and the last of the greens.

I did not mention the one copper until she asked whether I had been
paid.

"One."

"For two Gregs?"

"Exactly."

"Underpaid."

"Rinna offered less."

"Expertly negotiated again."

I pointed at her.

"That joke belongs to me."

"No."

After we ate, she took the wooden hoop and pale thread to the far side
of the room and began doing something with both.

I did not ask.

This also required effort.

Tomorrow the chalk board might say my name.

It might not.

Marek had a cart north in two days. Hara had other work nobody had
explained to me. Davin's floorboard needed another night before EvIn was
allowed to test it by standing on it. The pale shawl had survived.

Hessa wanted me after midday in two days.

Near.

Far.

Away only.

Nothing about today had moved because I wanted it to.

A surprising amount had changed anyway.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 195

## THE CARRIER

Lyssa had turned the wooden hoop into cloth.

Not all of it.

The hoop was still wood. The cloth had simply been stretched across it
so tightly that I could no longer understand why the thread had been
necessary.

I stood over her shoulder.

"What is it?"

"You lasted longer yesterday."

"I wasn't awake enough."

"Apparently."

Pale cloth filled the hoop. A torn place near one edge had been drawn
together with stitches so small I had to lean closer to see them.

Lyssa put two fingers against my forehead and pushed me back.

"You're breathing on it."

"I breathe most places."

"Not there."

I sat down.

Morning had gotten farther than usual before either of us left. Light
came through the window. Somebody below us was arguing about a bucket.
The soap sat beside the basin with one corner already rounded from use.

On the table, Hessa's note remained under the cup.

Tomorrow.

After midday.

Away only.

Near / far.

I knew what it said without lifting the cup.

That was convenient.

Lyssa cut the repaired cloth free from the hoop.

"Hold this."

She handed me the hoop.

Then the cloth.

Then a folded dark blue piece from the chair.

"Separate?"

"Yes."

I put the hoop on my lap and the two cloths beside each other.

She handed me a smaller pale bundle.

"Separate."

"From which?"

"Both."

"This system needs labels."

"This system has me."

"That seems fragile."

She ignored me and went to wash.

By the time she came back, I had four things.

"Where are we going?"

"Marra."

"We?"

"You have theatre?"

"Later."

"How much later?"

I had gone to the hall the previous night without asking tomorrow's
time. This had felt like personal growth until now.

"I don't know."

Lyssa stopped tying her hair back.

"You didn't ask?"

"No."

She looked impressed.

I did not like it.

"I can go ask."

"No."

"I need to know."

"You can ask after."

"After what?"

"Marra."

This was how I learned I was going to Marra.

Lyssa took the hoop from me and gave me a larger bundle.

"I thought I was holding that."

"You were."

"What happened?"

"Now you're holding this."

The theatre had prepared me for nothing.

We left after eating the heel of yesterday's bread and one piece of
cheese between us.

Lyssa carried the hoop under one arm. I had the large bundle tucked
against my side, one crutch under the opposite arm, and a smaller folded
piece balanced over my forearm.

Halfway down the stairs, she put another bundle on top.

"How many hands do you think I have?"

"The useful number."

"That's not a number."

"Keep walking."

We walked.

Carrow in the morning had already heated at the edges. Bakers had their
shutters open. A cart loaded with narrow boards blocked half a crossing
while three men discussed where the cart ought to have been. A woman
carrying flowers passed us going the other way, leaving a sharp green
smell behind her.

Lyssa adjusted her pace at the first crowded corner.

I adjusted mine.

Neither of us said anything.

At the next crossing she reached for the top bundle before I needed to
change my grip. I gave it to her, stepped around a basket seller, then
took it back on the other side.

That was apparently how we did streets now.

Marra's place was busy enough that nobody noticed us for almost a
minute.

I liked this.

Jessa stood on a low stool pinning the side of a green dress on a woman
who had both arms lifted and the expression of somebody regretting every
decision that had led to clothing.

Marra was behind the long table cutting dark fabric.

Another woman sat near the wall with a half-finished sleeve in her lap.

Lyssa walked in.

Marra said, "There."

Not hello.

There.

Lyssa put the hoop down.

I waited.

Jessa glanced over.

"You delivered him."

"I carried myself."

"Late?"

"No."

"Then somebody else did it."

The woman in green looked at me.

I looked at Jessa.

"That was one message."

"It was a memorable message."
