"It was not."

"You wrote it down and still didn't deliver it."

"I eventually delivered it."

Lyssa put a bundle on the table.

"Hours later."

I looked at her.

"You were there."

"Yes."

"You're supposed to be on my side."

"I am. That's why I remember."

Marra pointed at me without looking up.

"Blue on the chair."

I looked down.

I was holding dark blue, pale, and something brown that had appeared
somewhere between our room and here.

"Which blue?"

"The blue."

"There are two."

Every woman in the room looked at me.

I checked again.

There were apparently not two.

One was blue.

The other was, according to Jessa, "blue-gray."

"That is blue."

"No."

"It contains blue."

"So does a bruise."

"That is not helping."

The woman in green laughed.

Jessa said, "Chair."

I put the actual blue on the chair.

"Other one?"

"Table."

I put the blue-gray on the table.

Lyssa immediately moved it six inches to the left.

"Why?"

"Pins."

There were pins where I had put it.

I had not seen them.

"This room is hostile."

Marra said, "Only to blood."

"That is not reassuring."

She finally looked at me.

"Hold."

A long piece of brown cloth came across the table.

I took it.

"How?"

"Up."

I held it up.

"More."

I raised my arm.

"Not you. Cloth."

I raised the cloth.

"Stop."

I stopped.

Marra and Lyssa looked at it.

Jessa looked at it from the stool.

The woman in green looked too, because she had nothing better to do
while wearing half a dress.

"Too warm," Lyssa said.

Marra nodded.

I looked at the brown cloth.

It was brown.

"What does warm brown mean?"

Jessa said, "Not this one."

"That doesn't answer the question."

"It answers the cloth."

The woman in green laughed again.

I had become entertainment without pay.

Marra took the cloth.

Then she handed me another.

This one was also brown.

I decided to survive.

For the next half hour, my work consisted of holding things, moving
things, and not placing things where pins lived.

I failed the third one twice.

Lyssa repaired the pale cloth from the hoop at the table beside Marra.
It was not embroidery. It was a torn inner panel from some larger piece,
and the hoop had only kept the fabric stretched while she worked the
tear back together.

This made sense once somebody else explained it to Jessa.

I pretended I had understood already.

A customer came in and greeted Lyssa by name.

Then another.

The second asked whether she had finished "the narrow one."

Lyssa said, "Tomorrow."

Nobody told me what the narrow one was.

Marra sent Jessa into the back for black thread.

Jessa came out with black thread, a red ribbon, and a complaint about a
missing box.

Lyssa knew which shelf the box had been on last time.

Marra knew who had moved it.

The woman with the sleeve knew who had borrowed it.

None of them needed me.

I held brown cloth.

This was peaceful in a different way from the lower market.

Eventually Marra said, "Done."

I lowered my arms.

"With me?"

"With that."

She took the cloth.

Lyssa folded the repaired pale panel and put it with the blue-gray.

I picked up the blue.

"No."

I stopped.

"What?"

"That stays."

"You brought it."

"I did."

"So it goes back."

"No."

I looked at Lyssa.

She pointed at the blue-gray.

"That."

I picked it up.

Then the pale panel.

"No."

I stopped again.

"That stays too."

"Why did I carry things here if I'm not carrying them back?"

"Because that was the job."

"This work is badly designed."

Marra put a different bundle in my arms.

"Take this."

"Where?"

"Home."

"Whose?"

"Lyssa's."

I looked at Lyssa.

She was smiling.

"This is how you get furniture," I said.

"What?"

"You keep putting things on it."

Jessa said, "Furniture doesn't complain."

"Bad furniture."

The woman in green said, "He's more like a shelf."

"Thank you."

"You shouldn't."

Apparently shelves were not aspirational.

Lyssa had one more stop.

A thread seller two streets over.

I waited outside because the stall was narrow and because I had become a
shelf.

She came out with two small paper-wrapped spools.

"How much?"

"Not yours."

"I can contribute."

"You can carry."

"That's not currency."

"It is today."

We bought food after.

That part I was allowed to understand.

Bread, a small wedge of white cheese, and three carrots.

I paid for the carrots.

Lyssa let me.

This felt suspiciously ceremonial, so I did not mention it.

We ate one carrot raw while walking.

It was not good enough to justify doing this regularly.

At the turn toward East Market, Lyssa stopped.

"I go west."

"I go east."

"I know."

She took the smaller spool bundle from under my arm.

I handed it over before she asked.

She took half the bread too.

"That was mine."

"Was."

"You're becoming Rinna."

"No."

"Marra?"

"Worse."

She bit the carrot.

"See you tonight."

"Probably."

She pointed the carrot at me.

"Don't start."

I went east.

The hall board had my name.

It had apparently survived since last night.

GREG: SET / NEIGHBOR.

I stood in front of it.

Rinna came past with a stack of tickets.

"After fourth bell."

"What?"

"You're early."

"I went to Marra."

"Why?"

"I don't know."

"Good."

"What's Neighbor?"

She pointed at a rolled page.

"Four lines."

"New?"

"Old."

"Mine?"

"Today."

I picked up the page.

The piece was The Broken Gate, which I had seen but never performed.
Neighbor entered once to complain about a cart blocking the lane, left,
returned near the end to discover the gate had fallen down, and blamed
the wrong person.

Four lines.

I read them.

"That's it?"

Rinna stopped.

"Do you want more?"

"No."

"Good."

This was the most successful negotiation we had ever had.

Marek was near the rear door with a canvas bag, two shirts, and a length
of cord.

"Packing?"

"No."

I looked at the bag.

He looked at the bag.

"Yes."

"Need help?"

"Know where Pell put the short tying cord?"

"Upper shelf. Left of narrow tubes. Not the long narrow one."

Marek stared.

"What?"

"Nothing."

I found the cord.

It was exactly where it should be.

Pell was not there to witness this miracle.

Marek tied the bag.

"Leaving morning?"

"First bell."

"Back when?"

"Depends."

"On baby?"

"On sister."

"Right."

I had learned.

He pulled the knot tight.

"Tell them not to replace me."

"Who?"

"Everyone."

"They already replaced you yesterday."

"Then tell them badly."

"That I can do."

He nodded.

That was goodbye.

No one embraced.

No one promised anything.

He carried the bag out to wherever he was sleeping before morning.

I went to set.

The Broken Gate needed a gate.

We had one.

It was painted wood on a frame with a hinge that had been repaired
enough times to become mostly repair.

Davin was checking the bottom brace.

"Can I step on the floor now?"

He looked at me.

"Yes."

I stepped on yesterday's pale replacement strip.

Nothing happened.

"Good."

Davin said, "Yes."

"Strong day."

He went back to the gate.

Rehearsal for Neighbor took six minutes.

Teren was there.

He gave me an entrance, a place to stop, and one correction.

"Don't stand in the gate."

"I thought I'm complaining about it."

"From beside it."

"Why?"

"Because EvIn needs to come through."

"That seems selfish."

"Again."

We did it again.

I stood beside the gate.

EvIn came through.

Civilization survived.

That was all.

The rest of the afternoon I moved two benches, carried a lamp, fetched a
cup for Nessa, and helped Jori hang a narrow painted strip that had
nothing magical about it and did not become important later.

I appreciated this.

The audience was decent.

The Broken Gate ran second.

My first line was, "Whose cart is blocking my lane?"

EvIn said, "Not mine."

"It has your name on it."

"Then someone has stolen my name."

A laugh.

I waited where I was supposed to wait.

"My wife says move it."

EvIn said, "Tell your wife I am considering it."

"I did. She said consider faster."

A better laugh.

I left.

Later I returned.

The gate was down.

It was supposed to be down.

I looked at it.

Then at Hara.

Then at EvIn.

"I leave for ten minutes."

Hara said, "It was twelve."

"Then this is my fault."

The audience laughed.

I blamed Hara.

Hara blamed EvIn.

EvIn blamed municipal drainage.

The piece ended.

I went backstage.

Nobody corrected me.

Nobody said fine.

I almost went looking for somebody to do it.

Instead I carried the gate frame out of the way for the next piece.

This was healthier.

The last show ran a little late because a strap broke on a costume and
Nessa refused to let the actor go out with it hanging.

She fixed it.

The actor went out.

The audience never knew they had waited for thread.

Afterward, Rinna paid me one copper.

"Neighbor. Set."

"Gate."

"Set."

"Floor testing."

"No."

"Carrots."

She looked at me.

"Unrelated."

"You don't know my accounting."

"I know enough."

"One?"

"One."

I took it.

I did not stay for food.

Pell arrived as I was leaving and asked if I was coming.

"No."

"Why?"

"I have bread."

"That's not a reason."

"It was yesterday."

He accepted this.

Outside, the air had cooled.

I walked home with the remaining bread under one arm and the bundle
Marra had given me under the other.

I had carried the bundle through the entire theatre shift without
opening it.

This felt like evidence of character.

Lyssa was home first.

She had the hoop out again, but no cloth stretched across it.

She looked at the bundle.

"You still have it."

"Nobody told me to lose it."

"Good."

I put it on the table.

"What is it?"

"Mine."

"I know that."

"Then you're improving."

She opened it.

Two finished dark sleeves.

I stared.

"I carried sleeves all day?"

"Yes."

"They weigh nothing."

"Did you want heavier sleeves?"

"I wanted my labor to have shape."

Lyssa held one against her arm.

"It had shape."

"Not enough."

She laughed.

We ate bread, cheese, and cooked carrots because raw carrots had failed.

I told her I had been a Neighbor.

"Whose?"

"Apparently everyone's."

"Did you know them?"

"No."

"Bad neighbor."

"I was excellent."

"Did someone say that?"

"No."

"Then how do you know?"

"I wasn't corrected."

Lyssa considered this.

"Fine."

I pointed my knife at her.

"Don't."

She smiled.

After food, I finally lifted the cup.

Hessa's note was still there.

THREE DAYS.

It no longer meant three days.

Tomorrow.

After midday.

Away only.

Near / far.

Same restrictions.

I read it once.

Lyssa saw.

"Tomorrow?"

"Yes."

"Excited?"

I put the paper back.

"A little."

That was true.

"Nervous?"

"A little."

Also true.

"What do you do?"

"Sit where she tells me."

"Good start."

"Then draw if she tells me. Shape if she tells me. Ask the paper to move
away."

"Near and far."

"Yes."

"What does far mean?"

"I don't know."

Lyssa nodded.

"What does near mean?"

"I don't know."

"You know a lot again."

"I was hoping you'd forgotten that."

"No."

I put the cup back over the papers.

Nothing moved.

I did not ask anything to.

Marek would leave north at first bell.

Lyssa had work.

The hall would open whether I was there or not.

After midday, Hessa would put paper somewhere near me and somewhere
farther from me, and we would find out whether distance mattered inside
one very small thing I was actually allowed to claim.

Tonight I had carried cloth, sleeves, a gate, bread, and several objects
nobody had trusted to themselves.

That seemed like enough.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 196

## THE SITTER

Marek left north at first bell.

I knew this because the cart made enough noise to announce itself to
three streets.

Lyssa was already dressed when the first wheel struck something outside
and produced a wooden crack followed by a man's opinion of the road.

"That him?" I asked.

"Probably."

I went to the window.

The cart was long, low, and loaded with sacks, two barrels, a wooden
chest, and enough rope to make Pell emotional. Marek sat on the rear
edge with his canvas bag between his boots.

He looked up because I was leaning out the window.

"See your sister," I called.

"That was the plan."

"Don't get replaced."

He lifted one hand.

The cart moved.

That was goodbye.

Lyssa handed me bread.

"You weren't going down?"

"He was already moving."

"You could have."

"I said goodbye yesterday."

"Then why did you hang out the window?"

"Confirmation."

She ate.

I looked at the cup on the table.

She looked at me looking at the cup.

"After midday."

"I know."

"Good."

"I've been very good."

"At not touching paper?"

"At several things."

"Name two."

I ate bread.

Lyssa left before I did.

She had the narrow thing to finish.

I still did not know what the narrow thing was.

This had begun to feel deliberate.

I washed, used the soap, checked my right palm, and left the cup where
it was.

My palm had stopped being interesting. The skin was still newer where
the bad grip had rubbed it, but it no longer complained every time I put
weight through the crutch.

This was excellent.

Body parts should become boring whenever possible.

At the hall, Rinna was counting tickets that did not yet belong to
anyone.

"I have Guild after midday."

She kept counting.

"Fine."

"I don't know how long."

"Less fine."

"Do you need me tonight?"

"Probably."

"Role?"

"Probably."

I waited.

She looked up.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

I had become extremely mature.

The board said:

KING: SELLEN / HARA / EVIN

BROKEN GATE: EVIN / HARA / GREG

WIDOW: VEYA / NESSA / HARA

GREG: NEIGHBOR / SET

Marek's name was gone.

Nobody had written a little memorial around the empty chalk.

Sellen was carrying a crown.

"You're king again."

"I can read."

"How does it feel?"

"Like a hat."

He put the crown on a shelf.

I worked until midday.

Nothing important happened.

This was not entirely true. Davin discovered that one of the rear bench
braces had cracked. Nessa discovered somebody had hung a damp shirt
among dry costumes. Jori discovered the lamp oil count was wrong by one
small tin.

All of these were important to the people holding them.

None of them changed my life.

I helped Davin turn the bench over.

The underside contained dust, a bent ticket, and one dried pea.

Davin tapped the cracked brace.

"Hold."

I held.

He removed two nails, replaced the brace with a shorter piece from
behind the work shelf, and nailed it in.

"Done?"

"Not yet."

He drove another nail.

"Done?"

"Yes."

We turned the bench back.

I sat on it.

Davin looked at me.

"Testing."

"You test everything by sitting?"

"Only things designed for it."

The bench held.

"Excellent."

At midday I ate the rest of the bread with a little cheese.

Rinna came past.

"Guild?"

"After."

"Go."

"I have time."

"Go slowly."

"I don't think that's how appointments work."

"It is if you leave now."

I left.

Hessa's room looked exactly like it had been waiting for me.

The table was clear except for the square board, the paper fins, the
glass covers, two small weights, Hessa's notes, and a piece of chalk.

There was also a chair.

I looked at the chair.

Hessa looked at me.

"Sit."

"I was afraid of that."

"Why?"

"I've developed a reputation."

"For sitting?"

"Apparently."

She pointed.

I sat.

The chair had been placed at one end of the table rather than the side.
A chalk line crossed the tabletop a little more than a forearm from the
edge nearest me. Another line crossed it near the far end.

The board rested beside Hessa, not on either mark.

"Eat?"

"Yes."

"Sleep?"

"Yes."

"Hand?"

"Mostly boring."

"Show me."

I held out my right hand.

She turned it once, pressed the healed skin near the base of my fingers,
then let go.

"Shoulder?"

"Fine."

"Wrist?"

"Fine."

"Any draw since last time?"

"No."

"Shaping?"

"No."

"External attempt?"

"No."

"Paper?"

"No."

"Coin?"

"No."

"Thread?"

"No."

She looked at me.

"I know."

"You asked."

"Good."

She sat opposite the side of the table, where she could reach the board
without standing.

I looked at the chalk lines.

"Near and far."

"Yes."

"Those?"

"Yes."

"Near is that near?"

"Today."

Of course.

Hessa put the board on the first chalk line.

The center of the target fin sat roughly a forearm and a hand beyond the
table edge in front of me. Far enough that I could not accidentally
breathe on it. Close enough that I could see the cut edge of the paper
clearly.

She set the glass cover over it, checked the two small control fins,
then marked something on her page.

Then she moved the entire board to the far line.

It was more than twice as distant. I could still see the target, but the
smallest cut marks disappeared into paper.

"Far," she said.

"That is definitely farther."

"Yes."

"Good experiment."

"Sit back."

I had leaned forward.

I sat back.

She moved the board near again.

"Your position does not change."

"At all?"

"Not intentionally."

"Comforting."

"Feet."

I adjusted my foot.

"Crutch."

I had leaned it against the table.

She pointed to the wall.

I moved it.

"Hands."

"Where?"

"Same place each attempt."

She had me rest my left hand on my thigh and my right hand open on the
other.

This felt like being arrested by furniture.

"Draw first," she said.

I stopped joking.

The familiar part was still not easy.

That bothered me less than it used to.

I settled my breathing and waited until Hessa told me to begin.

The draw came narrow.

Not smooth. Not pleasant. But narrow.

I found the small controlled pull without reaching for more. Heat
gathered under my ribs and behind my sternum, not enough to frighten me,
enough to make my shoulders want to join the work.

I kept them out of it.

"Stop."

I stopped.

Hessa watched my face.

"Again?"

"No."

I blinked.

"That was a question?"

"How do you feel?"

"Oh."

I checked.

"Fine. Warm."

"Where?"

I told her.

She wrote.

That made twenty-seven successful supervised minimal draws.

She waited until my breathing was ordinary again.

"Shape."

The shaping was worse.

It always wanted to become movement somewhere in my body instead of
remaining the small deliberate pressure Hessa had been making me
practice.

My wrist tightened.

"Release."

I did.

"Again?"

"You just said one."

"I asked how you feel."

"You're using the same word for too many things."

"How do you feel?"

"Wrist tight. Shoulder fine. Breathing fine."

She wrote again.

Twenty-three supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Then she put the board on the near line.

My stomach tightened.

That was ridiculous.

It was paper.

Hessa checked the target fin, then the controls. She lifted the glass,
replaced it, and looked at me.

"Away only."

"Away from me."

"Yes."

"Not board-forward."

"Not board-forward."

"Not left, right, north, south, spiritually distant."

She waited.

"Away from me."

"Yes."

I looked at the target.

I did not imagine a hand.

I did not imagine a shove.

I did not imagine the paper flying across the table and proving
something much larger than it had ever proved.

I tried to ask for the same small external change we had already gotten,
in the direction away from where I sat.

The pressure narrowed.

My wrist wanted to tighten.

I let it stay small.

The paper fin moved.

Not much.

The upper edge gave a tiny turn away from me, enough that the cut shadow
beneath it changed.

I saw it.

Hessa did not react.

The control fins did not move.

"Stop."

I stopped.

She leaned in, checked the target, checked both controls, then wrote.

"How much?"

"Less than I'd like."

"Observed."

"Half a fingernail?"

"Less."

"That's insulting."

She ignored that.

External attempt twenty.

Near.

Response observed.

I knew better than to celebrate paper.

I still wanted to.

Hessa gave me water.

We waited.

Then she moved the board to the far line.

The same board.

Same fins.

Same glass.

Same direction relative to me.

The target looked smaller from here.

"Do I lean?"

"No."

"I thought not."

"Same position."

I put my hands where they had been.

Hessa checked them.

"Ready?"

"Yes."

"Away."

I asked.

Nothing happened.

The sensation was there. The small shaped pressure was there. My wrist
tightened a little earlier than it had near.

The paper did nothing.

I held too long.

"Stop."

I stopped.

Hessa checked the target.

Controls unchanged.

Target unchanged.

She wrote.

I stared at the far fin.

It looked smug.

"Don't."

"I didn't say anything."

"You leaned."

I sat back.

External attempt twenty-one.

Far.

No observed response.

"Rest."

"I'm fine."

"Rest."

I drank more water.

The room had a small high window. Somebody outside passed close enough
that their shadow crossed the lower wall and disappeared.

Hessa turned her page.

I looked at the far chalk line.

"So."

"No."

"I haven't said anything."

"You were going to."

"This is becoming a city problem."

She waited.

I did not say closer was easier.

This required effort.

After a few minutes she moved the board back to near.

She checked the fins again.

"Same."

"Same."

"Away."

The second near attempt felt worse.

Not dramatically.

Just enough that I noticed the first attempt had been cleaner.

My shoulder tried to lift.

I lowered it.

The pressure narrowed.

The target fin gave a small twitch.

Then, after a moment, a clearer turn.

Away.

Tiny.

Real.

The controls remained still.

Hessa stopped me almost immediately.

She checked everything.

"Observed?"

I asked.

"Yes."

"Good."

She wrote.

External attempt twenty-two.

Near.

Response observed again.

I smiled.

Hessa looked up.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You're smiling."

"I'm allowed."

"Yes."

This was generous of her.

She moved the board far.

The smile became less useful.

"Same?"

"Same."

I rested my hands.

The target was far enough away that I had to resist narrowing my eyes at
it.

Hessa waited until I stopped.

"Away."

I tried.

The first few breaths felt wrong.

I released before she told me.

Hessa said nothing.

"Again within the same attempt?"

"No."

I looked at her.

"No?"

"You released."

"I thought that was good."

"It was."

She checked my wrist.

"Pain?"

"No."

"Tight?"

"Yes."

"Shoulder?"

"Starting."

She wrote something.

"Are we done?"

"Not yet."

I looked at the far board.

She waited longer this time.

My shoulder settled.

My wrist did not entirely.

"One more external attempt," she said.

"Far?"

"Far."

"Then we're ending on the one that doesn't work."

"We are ending on the condition we have less evidence for."

"That is a much worse sentence."

"It is more accurate."

I put my hands back.

She checked my position.

The far target waited under glass.

"Away."

I tried again.

Nothing.

Then maybe something.

I stopped breathing for half a second.

The upper edge of the target seemed to tremble.

I could not tell whether it had actually changed position or whether I
had stared at it long enough to invent mercy.

Hessa said, "Stop."

I stopped.

She went to the board.

She did not immediately write.

That was worse.

"Did it move?"

"Maybe."

"That's not one of your usual words."

"It is when I am not sure."

She checked the control fins.

Then the target.

Then the glass.

"One control may have shifted also."

I leaned forward.

"How much?"

"Enough."

"So it doesn't count."

"It counts as an attempt."

"Of course it does."

She wrote.

External attempt twenty-three.

Far.

Uncertain target movement. Possible control movement. Not usable as
evidence of response.

I hated this.

It was exactly correct.

I hated it more.

"Again."

"No."

"One clean far."

"No."

"Because of the control?"

"Because your wrist is tightening earlier, your shoulder has joined
twice, and this would be the fifth external request after draw and
shaping."

"I can do one."

"Probably."

"Then."

"No."

I looked at the paper.

Hessa closed her notes.

That was unfairly final.

"Near worked twice."

"Yes."

"Far failed once."

"Yes."

"And far maybe did something useless."

"Yes."

"So near is easier."

"Here."

I looked at her.

"Today."

"Yes."

"In this arrangement."

"Yes."

I sighed.

She opened the notes again.

"I liked it better closed."

She wrote slowly enough that I could read upside down if I leaned.

I did not lean.

She turned the page toward me when she finished.

AWAY RESPONSE OBSERVED TWICE AT NEAR POSITION.

FAR RESPONSE NOT ESTABLISHED.

NEAR RESPONSE MORE RELIABLE THAN FAR RESPONSE TODAY.

POSSIBLE DISTANCE DEPENDENCE IN CURRENT APPARATUS.

NOT A RANGE.

NOT GENERALIZED.

I pointed.

"You wrote the part I was going to say."

"Which?"

"Near is easier."

"I did not write that."

"You wrote it with more words."

"No."

"It is emotionally the same."

"No."

"Fine."

She added beneath it:

REPEAT.

NEAR / FAR.

AWAY ONLY.

No date.

I noticed.

"When?"

"Not today."

"I know that."

"Good."

"When later?"

"I'll tell you."

"Tomorrow?"

"No."

"Day after?"

"I'll tell you."

"You're becoming Rinna."

Hessa stared at me.

"Never mind."

She checked my wrist again.

Then my shoulder.

"Same restrictions."

"All of them?"

"All."

"No paper."

"No."

"No coin."

"No."

"No thread."

"No."

"No external testing."

"No."

"No independent draw."

"No."

"No independent shaping."

"No."

"I remembered."

"I know."

That was almost praise.

I decided not to damage it.

"Twenty-seven draws," I said.

"Successful supervised minimal draws."

"Twenty-three shaping."

"Supervised deliberate shaping attempts."

"Twenty-three external."

"Supervised external-effect attempts."

"You make every number longer."

"That is because you shorten every claim."

I thought about that.

"Fair."

She looked suspicious.

"I can say fair."

"Go home."

"I have theatre."

"Then go there and do not test anything."

"I don't usually test things at theatre."

"Good."

I stood.

My right wrist complained when I picked up the crutch.

Not pain.

Just enough fatigue to make me shift the grip.

Hessa saw.

"Light work."

"Tell Rinna."

"I am telling you."

"Less effective."

"Your problem."

This was also fair.

The hall had moved on without me.

It had done this very successfully.

When I came in, Pell was carrying a stool upside down.

"Guild?"

"Yes."

"Good?"

"Paper."

He nodded as though that answered anything.

Maybe it did.

Rinna saw me.

"You came back."

"You said probably."

"I say many things."

"I've noticed."

She looked at my wrist.

"Hessa say anything?"

"Light work."

"House."

"That's light?"

"Compared to scenery."

I could not argue.

I spent the evening taking tickets, moving one stool, answering where
the privy was, and telling two people The Broken Gate was second.

It became third.

I told the next person third.

The performance happened without me.

This felt slightly strange.

Not bad.

Just strange.

I heard laughs through the door.

At one point I heard my Neighbor line delivered by somebody else.

I could not tell who from the front.

The audience laughed.

I objected privately.

Then a man asked whether he could bring a cup inside.

"No."

"Why?"

"I don't know."

He looked at me.

I looked toward Rinna.

She said, "No cups."

I looked back.

"No cups."

"Why?"

"Authority."

Rinna made a sound.

The man left the cup.

At cleanup she paid me one copper.

"House."

"Guild fatigue."

"Not my expense."

"I returned."

"That is why you got paid."

"Excellent system."

"Go home."

Everyone had become Hessa.

Lyssa was not home when I arrived.

I washed my hands and wrist in cool water.

The soap had become smaller again.

This was the problem with useful things.

I did not lift the cup.

There was no reason.

The old note underneath it was now wrong in every temporal sense and
still correct in every instructional one.

When Lyssa came in, she had no hoop.

She had a small wrapped parcel and a smear of something dark near one
thumb.

"Near or far?" she asked before taking off her shoes.

"Near."

She looked at me.

"Near here."

"Better."

"Far was worse."

"How worse?"

"Nothing once. Maybe something once, but a control may have moved too,
so Hessa refused to count it as evidence."

Lyssa put the parcel on the table.

"Near?"

"Twice."

"Both moved?"

"Yes."

"Far?"

"Not established."

She smiled.

"That sounds like Hessa."

"I said near was easier."

"What did she say?"

"Here."

Lyssa laughed.

"Then today."

"Yes."

"In this arrangement."

"Yes."

She laughed harder.

"I don't see what's funny."

"You."

"That's not useful."

"It is to me."

I sat.

My shoulder felt tired now that nobody needed it.

Lyssa unwrapped the parcel.

Inside was a piece of flatbread with onions folded into it.

I looked at her.

"Two?"

"One."

"For us?"

"Obviously."

That word had also become infected.

She tore it in half.

I ate.

After a while she asked, "Again when?"

"I don't know."

"Hessa doesn't know?"

"She probably knows. She hasn't told me."

"What next?"

"Same thing."

Lyssa paused.

"Again?"

"Near and far. Away only."

"No new thing?"

"No."

"Good."

I looked at her.

"Why good?"

"Because the last thing barely moves paper."

This was difficult to dispute.

I ate more flatbread.

Twenty-seven successful supervised minimal draws.

Twenty-three supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Twenty-three supervised external-effect attempts.

Near had worked twice.

Far had not worked cleanly at all.

That was more than I had known this morning.

It was less than I wanted to know.

Hessa would have liked that sentence.

I did not tell her.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 197

## THE PETITIONER

My wrist was stiff in the morning.

Not hurt.

Stiff.

There was an important difference, and I spent several seconds proving
it by rotating my hand until Lyssa looked up from tying her hair.

"Stop."

"I'm checking it."

"You've checked it four times."

"That is how you establish repeatability."

"No."

I stopped.

She had already put bread on the table. There was also a narrow strip of
dark blue cloth beside her elbow.

I pointed at it.

"The narrow thing?"

Lyssa looked down.

"Part of it."

"What is it?"

"Binding."

I waited.

She ate.

"That's all?"

"What did you think it was?"

"I didn't know. That's why I asked."

"It's binding for a sleeve edge."

I stared at the cloth.

Three days of mystery had produced sleeve binding.

Lyssa watched my face.

"Disappointed?"

"No."

"You are."

"I had assigned it no importance."

"Good."

"I was still expecting more object."

She folded the strip and put it in her bag.

"Work?"

"Theatre."

"All day?"

"Probably."

"You know when?"

"Morning."

She nodded.

This was progress.

I ate.

The cup remained on the table.

There was no new paper under it.

I knew because the edge of the old note was still visible in exactly the
same place.

I did not lift the cup.

Lyssa noticed.

"Nothing?"

"Nothing."

"Then leave the cup alone."

"I did."

She kissed the side of my head on the way past.

"Go to work."

This made it difficult to continue the argument.

Outside, Carrow had already warmed enough that the stone near the market
smelled dry.

A man was selling fried dough from a pan set over coals. I bought one
piece because bread had not entirely convinced me breakfast had
occurred.

The dough was hot, greasy, and folded around chopped greens.

It cost enough that I regretted it for one bite.

Then I stopped regretting it.

This was becoming financially dangerous.

At the next corner two men were arguing about whether a cart had damaged
a drain cover.

The cart was gone.

The drain cover was still there.

Neither man appeared to own either.

Carrow remained well governed.

I reached the hall with grease on two fingers and soap still faintly on
my wrists.

By the time I went inside, the hall removed both improvements.

Pell was sanding the edge of a painted frame.

Dust settled on my sleeve.

I looked at it.

"Morning," he said.

"You've ruined me."

"Already?"

"I was clean."

He looked at my coat.

"No."

"Cleaner."

"Maybe."

The board was short.

KING: SELLEN / HARA / EVIN

PETITION: VEYA / SELLEN / GREG / EVIN

WIDOW: VEYA / NESSA / HARA

SHORT: HARA / EVIN

GREG: PETITIONER / SET

Marek was still absent.

The company had survived the night.

I found this encouraging.

"Petitioner?" I asked.

Rinna was tying a stack of ticket slips with cord.

"Yes."

"Which petitioner?"

She looked at me.

I pointed at the board.

"There are several people petitioning in that piece."

"You're the fence."

"Third Man."

"No."

I looked again.

"Not Third Man?"

"No."

"Then which fence?"

"First petition. Two lines. Then you come back with the drainage
complaint."

I stared at her.

"That man has a drainage complaint?"

"Today."

"Did he yesterday?"

"No."

"Good."

Rinna stopped tying tickets.

"What?"

"I asked."

"Congratulations."

I found Teren near the rear wall with Davin and a door hinge.

The hinge belonged to a narrow painted door that had been removed from
its frame and laid across two benches.

Davin held the old pin between two fingers.

"It bends."

Teren said, "Then don't use it."

"I wasn't planning to."

"Good."

I waited.

Teren looked at me.

"Petitioner."

"Apparently."

"First petition. Fence line. Leave. Back after Sellen's tax line.
Drainage."

"How many lines?"

"Four."

"Entrance?"

"Same side as Third Man."

"Coat?"

"Nessa."

"Version?"

Teren looked at me.

"Today."

"Excellent."

He pointed toward the stage.

"Go ask Veya."

This was the entire meeting.

Nessa had three coats on a rail and one coat over her shoulder.

I knew two of them.

The third was Marek's king coat.

Sellen stood with his arms out while Nessa pulled the coat across his
back.

It did not want to be Sellen's coat.

Marek was broader through the shoulders.

Sellen was not.

The fabric gathered beneath the collar and the sleeves hung too low.

Nessa folded one cuff inward.

Sellen said, "That's uneven."

"So are your arms."

"My arms are not uneven."

Nessa looked at me.

"Are his arms uneven?"

"I don't know."

Sellen turned.

"Don't involve him."

"I was invited."

Nessa pinned the inside fold at the shoulder.

Sellen flinched.

"That is me."

"Then stop moving."

"I was stabbed."

"You were touched."

"With metal."

"Hold this."

She handed me a length of narrow cord.

I held it.

Sellen looked down.

"Why does he get the safe job?"

"Because I don't need him."

"That feels insulting."

"It is."

Nessa tied the back of the coat tighter, folded the second cuff, then
stepped away.

Marek's coat now looked less like Marek had left it behind and more like
Sellen had borrowed something expensive from a larger cousin.

"Fine," Nessa said.

Sellen lowered his arms.

"Can I breathe?"

"No."

"Good."

He walked away.

I still held the cord.

"Nessa."

She was already working on another sleeve.

"Nessa."

"What?"

"The cord."

"Rail."

I put it on the rail.

This was how objects migrated.

My petitioner coat was brown, short, and smelled faintly of old smoke.

I had worn it before in something else.

I could not remember what.

That seemed fair. The coat probably could not remember me either.

Veya was at the petition table with the stack of papers.

"Fence," she said.

"I have one."

"Not yours."

"I know."

"Do you?"

I took my place.

The new petitioner was not complicated.

I entered angry about a neighbor moving a boundary post.

Veya, as the official, did not care.

I left.

Later I returned angry about water running from the public lane into my
yard.

Veya cared even less.

This was apparently the entire man.

We ran it once.

"My neighbor moved the boundary post two feet into my garden."

Veya looked at the petition.

"Then move it back."

"I did."

"Why are you here?"

"He moved it again."

"Move it again."

I paused.

Teren said, "Don't wait."

"I thought she might help."

"She isn't going to."

"That seems bad government."

Veya looked at me.

"You're learning."

We continued.

Later I came back.

"The lane drain is pouring into my yard."

Veya said, "Then your garden is watered."

"It has my chair in it."

"Move the chair."

"Where?"

She looked at me.

"Inside."

Teren said, "Good."

That was rehearsal.

I liked the petitioner.

He had problems.

Nobody respected them.

This was familiar.

The rest of the morning was less theatrical.

Davin needed the door held upright while he fitted the new hinge pin.

I started to take the heavier side.

My wrist reminded me about yesterday.

Not pain.

Just a small tightness.

I shifted.

"Pell."

He looked over.

"Other side."

"Why?"

"Guild."

He came over without asking anything else.

We held the door together while Davin drove the pin.

Pell took more weight.

I did not explain.

Davin did not thank either of us.

The door opened.

He closed it.

Opened it.

Closed it.

"Good."

Pell looked at me.

"He tests everything by doors."

"Only things designed for it."

Davin pointed at us.

"Move."

We moved.

At midday Hara arrived with her hair only half pinned and a paper parcel
under one arm.

Rinna said, "You're late."

"I was working."

"Here is work."

"I know."

Hara put the parcel on a bench.

Pell reached toward it.

She slapped his hand without looking.

"Not yours."

"What is it?"

"Food."

"What kind?"

"Mine."

I looked at Sellen.

He looked at me.

This was spreading.

Hara unwrapped two little meat pies.

Pell looked wounded.

"You have two."

"I know."

"That means one is extra."

"No."

"Mathematically."

"No."

He went away.

I ate bread and the last piece of white cheese.

Sellen had nothing.

I watched him not eat.

"Where's your food?"

"Didn't bring any."

"Why?"

"I forgot."

"You forgot food?"

"Apparently."

This was the first truly disturbing thing I had learned about him.

I tore off a piece of bread and held it out.

He looked at it.

"Why?"

"Food."

"I know what bread is."

"Good."

He took it.

"You want money?"

"No."

"Then why are you looking at me?"

"I wasn't."

"You were."

I looked away.

Sellen ate the bread.

After a while he said, "Marek would have brought dried apples."

"That sounds like criticism."

"It is."

"When is he back?"

Sellen shrugged.

"After the baby."

"That's not a time."

"It is to Marek."

Nobody seemed worried.

I was not worried.

Marek had gone north to see his sister have a baby.

The world contained enough dangerous things without making childbirth
travel into one from a distance.

Rinna came through with a chalk stub.

"House is decent."

"How decent?" Pell asked.

"Decent."

"Numbers."

"No."

"That's not a number."

"Work."

He did.

The hall filled slowly through the afternoon.

Not full.

Not thin.

People came in warm from the street and brought dust with them.

A woman argued with Rinna about whether her boy counted as a full seat
if he spent half the show on her lap.

Rinna said yes.

The woman said he was small.

Rinna said the bench did not charge by weight.

The woman paid.

I loved watching professionals.

Before the first piece, I moved a small wooden writing box from beside
the petition table because it was in the path where I entered.

Nessa saw me.

"Put that back."

I stopped.

"It's in my way."

"It's for Widow."

"That's after Petition."

"Yes."

"Why is it here?"

"Because it needs to be."

"For a later piece?"

"Yes."

"Now?"

"Yes."

I put it back.

Then I entered from six inches farther left.

This solved everything.

The King went first.

Sellen wore Marek's coat.

From backstage I could see the folded cuffs.

From the house, nobody cared.

He did not play Marek's king.

I had expected him to, without realizing I expected it.

Marek's king carried irritation like a personal injury. Sellen's seemed
to assume everyone else had become stupid while he was asleep.

The audience liked him.

Hara got the first big laugh.

Sellen got the second.

EvIn dropped a rolled petition, kicked it by accident, then incorporated
the kick into crossing the room.

Nobody died.

I watched until Pell needed the space.

"Move."

I moved.

The Petition came next.

My coat sat wrong at the neck.

I fixed it.

Nessa fixed what I had fixed.

Then I went on.

"My neighbor moved the boundary post two feet into my garden."

Veya barely looked up.

"Then move it back."

"I did."

"Why are you here?"

"He moved it again."

"Move it again."

I did not wait for help.

The audience laughed.

Not a large laugh.

Enough.

I left.

Backstage, Sellen was waiting for his next entrance.

"Your government is terrible."

"Not mine."

"You're in it."

"That is not how government works."

"That explains a lot."

He went on.

Later I returned.

"The lane drain is pouring into my yard."

Veya said, "Then your garden is watered."

"It has my chair in it."

"Move the chair."

"Where?"

"Inside."

A woman near the middle laughed before anyone else did.

Then several people joined.

I left again.

Four lines.

One coat.

No disaster.

I could have done more.

This was not a complaint.

It was simply true.

Then I had to get the coat off because Nessa wanted it for a different
man in Widow.

"Already?"

"Yes."

"I just became attached."

"Don't."

She took it.

The Widow's Room had the writing box I had tried to move.

It turned out to contain three folded letters and a piece of chalk.

None of them mattered to my entrance.

I found this insulting.

During the scene, Veya got the room quiet without asking it.

Nessa sat beside the painted doorway and said almost nothing for long
enough that I forgot I was waiting to carry a bench afterward.

Then Hara crossed the false room, opened the writing box, and found the
wrong letter.

The audience made one small sound together.

That was the strongest moment of the afternoon.

It had nothing to do with me.

Good.

When the scene ended, Pell said, "Bench."

I remembered my job.

I grabbed one end.

My wrist tightened.

I stopped.

Pell looked at me.

"Other hand?"

"Other person."

He called EvIn.

EvIn took my side.

Nobody gave a speech about responsibility.

I carried the lighter stool instead.

This was irritatingly sensible.

The short piece nearly failed because the spoon was missing again.

This time it was not in the Letter Box cup.

It was in Hara's paper parcel.

She stared at it.

Pell said, "You stole your own spoon."

"I did not."

"It is in your food."

"It wasn't."

"It is now."

Hara looked at Nessa.

Nessa said, "Use it."

Hara wiped meat grease off the spoon with a cloth.

"Disgusting."

Pell said, "The audience won't know."

"I will."

"Then act."

Hara threw the cloth at him.

The short happened.

The audience laughed.

At cleanup I found the wooden writing box on the wrong shelf.

I picked it up.

Then I stopped.

"Nessa."

"What?"

"Where?"

She pointed without looking.

I put it there.

This was safer than memory.

Rinna paid me one copper.

"Petitioner. Set."

"Also restraint."

"No."

"I didn't move a box."

"Congratulations."

"I also didn't lift a bench."

"You're negotiating for things you didn't do now?"

"I am expanding the field."

"One copper."

"Fine."

She handed it over.

I pocketed it.

Outside, the afternoon heat had broken.

Not into coolness.

Carrow did not believe in gifts.

But the sun had moved behind the roofs and the stone no longer reflected
it directly into my face.

I stopped at a food stall and bought two little fried cakes with chopped
onion and something green inside.

The woman wrapped them in paper.

"Two?" she asked.

"Yes."

"For you?"

"One is."

She looked at me.

"Then two."

This was unnecessary.

I carried them home.

Lyssa was not there.

I put the food on the table.

The cup remained where it had been.

Still no new note.

I washed.

The wrist felt better after cool water.

The shoulder was already ordinary again.

I sat for a while.

Not because I was practicing peace.

Because sitting existed.

Lyssa came in after dark enough that the window had become a black
square.

She put her bag down.

"Food?"

"One is yours."

She looked at the two wrapped cakes.

"Which?"

"I don't know."

"Then how do you know?"

"I bought two."

She took the larger one.

"That was mine."

"You didn't know."

"I know now."

We ate.

After a few bites she asked, "Theatre?"

"Petitioner."

"Again?"

"Different one."

"What did you want?"

"A fence."

"What did you get?"

"Water."

She waited.

"I had two complaints."

"Did anyone fix them?"

"No."

"Sounds official."

"It was."

I told her Marek's coat did not fit Sellen.

I told her Nessa made it fit anyway.

I told her Hara had stolen a spoon from herself.

I did not tell her about the writing box until she asked why I was
smiling at nothing.

"I moved a box."

"Why?"

"It was in my way."

"Was it yours?"

"No."

"Did you know where it belonged?"

"No."

"Good system."

"I put it back."

"Excellent."

"Then later I asked where it belonged instead of deciding."

Lyssa nodded solemnly.

"Growth."

"Don't."

She ate.

After a while she looked at my wrist.

"Better?"

"Yes."

"Actually?"

"Yes."

I rotated it once.

She looked at me.

I stopped.

"Still no paper?"

"No."

"From Hessa?"

"No."

"I meant you."

I looked at her.

"No."

"Good."

There it was again.

I ate the last bite of my cake.

Twenty-seven draws.

Twenty-three shapings.

Twenty-three external attempts.

Near had worked twice yesterday.

Far had not worked cleanly.

Today I had moved no paper.

I had moved a stool, a coat, part of a door, one petition, one writing
box incorrectly, and one writing box correctly.

None of those counted.

This was probably healthy.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 198

## THE GUARD

Lyssa was gone before I woke.

This was not unusual.

The unusual part was that she had left me the larger piece of bread.

I stared at it for a while.

There were several possible explanations. Generosity was one. Accident
was another. She might also have decided the bread was bad.

I smelled it.

It smelled like bread.

The soap sat beside the basin where I had left it. The edge had already
gone soft from use. I washed my face and hands, then checked my wrist by
moving it once.

Once was enough.

This was disappointing. I had built a whole system around checking
things repeatedly.

The wrist felt nearly ordinary. There was a little stiffness when I bent
it all the way back, but no complaint when I closed my hand. My shoulder
had stopped being interesting too.

Nothing new waited under the cup.

I did not lift it.

I ate the suspiciously large bread and went to work.

Carrow was already warm.

A cart loaded with baskets had stopped halfway across a narrow turn
because a wheel had found a hole everyone else had apparently agreed not
to see. Three people were helping. Four more were explaining how they
were helping incorrectly.

I went around.

At the hall, Rinna was standing just inside the front doors with a small
slate in one hand and a piece of chalk in the other.

She looked at me.

"Guild when?"

"Good morning."

"Guild when?"

"I don't know."

"Tomorrow?"

"I don't know."

"Day after?"

"I don't know."

She lowered the slate.

"This is useless."

"I agree."

"That does not improve it."

"I could lie."

"No."

"Then we have reached the limit of my contribution."

She looked down at the slate again.

There were names on it. Some had little marks beside them. Hara's name
had been written twice, once near the top and once near the bottom.
Marek's had a line through it.

Mine was near the edge.

Rinna tapped it with the chalk.

"Until the Guild sends for you, third bell."

I looked at her.

"Every day?"

"If I need you earlier, I'll send."

"Where?"

"Where you live."

"That seems invasive."

"Then don't live there."

I considered this.

"And if the Guild sends?"

"Tell me before midday if you can."

"What if they send after midday?"

"Then tell me after midday."

"This system has weaknesses."

"So do you. Third bell."

I looked at the slate again.

"Does this change my pay?"

"No."

"Then I object to the administrative burden."

Rinna wrote something beside my name.

"What did you write?"

"Complains."

"That is not scheduling."

"It helps me."

She pointed past me.

"Set."

That was apparently the end of negotiations.

I had been scheduled.

It felt suspiciously similar to what had already been happening, except
now Rinna had chalk.

The hall smelled of old cloth, sanding dust, and something sharp from
the paint shelf. Pell was on the floor beside a low bench with both
hands underneath it.

I stopped.

"Are you trapped?"

"No."

"Do you need help?"

"No."

"Why are you under the bench?"

"Looking."

"For what?"

He slid farther underneath.

"The thing."

I waited.

Pell's hand came out holding a small square nut.

"Found it."

"Excellent."

He slid back out.

"What was it for?"

He looked at the bench.

We both looked at the bench.

"Probably that."

Davin came through carrying a short iron wrench.

"Move."

Pell moved.

Davin took the nut from him, crouched, and fitted it onto the end of a
bolt that ran through the bench brace.

Pell pointed.

"That."

Davin tightened it.

The bench stopped wobbling.

I put one hand on the back and pushed.

Davin looked up at me.

"What?"

"Checking."

"I checked."

"Good."

I removed my hand.

My reputation was spreading.

The board had been written for the afternoon.

KING: SELLEN / HARA / EVIN

PETITION: VEYA / SELLEN / EVIN

WIDOW: VEYA / NESSA / HARA

SHORT: HARA / EVIN

GREG: SET

I read it.

Then I read the blank space after my name.

"Set."

Rinna had followed me in.

"I see that."

"Good."

"Third bell for set?"

"Today you're early."

"I arrived when I usually arrive."

"That is why I fixed it."

I was still deciding whether to be offended when Teren came through the
side passage carrying two folded pages.

He stopped at the board.

"Where's Davin?"

"Bench," Rinna said.

"Where's Jori?"

"Rear."

"Where's Greg?"

I raised a hand.

Teren looked directly at me.

"Good. Guard."

Rinna erased SET.

"Wait."

She wrote:

GREG: GUARD / SET

I pointed.

"You just made a system."

"It survived longer than I expected."

"What guard?"

"The King," Teren said.

"Today?"

He stared at me.

I stared back.

"Today," he said.

"Which version?"

"Local."

"Entrance?"

"Right."

"How many lines?"

"Six."

"Prop?"

"No."

"Costume?"

"Nessa."

Teren handed me one of the pages.

"See? Useful."

Rinna said, "Third bell."

"This is before third bell."

"I sent for you."

"You did not."

"You were here."

"That is not sending."

"Then leave and come back."

I took the page.

"I accept the exception."

The Guard had six lines.

This was too many for a man whose main responsibility appeared to be
standing beside a door.

The first two were announcements. The third refused entry to a
petitioner. The fourth admitted the same petitioner after Sellen ordered
me to. The fifth objected to a man carrying a chicken into the royal
chamber.

There was no chicken.

I checked.

"Why is there no chicken?"

Teren was pinning a page to the side board.

"There used to be."

"What happened to it?"

"Road cut."

"So why is the line still there?"

"It isn't."

I held up the page.

"It is."

He took the page from me, looked at it, and crossed out the fifth line.

"Five."

"This is why I ask which version."

"Yes."

I felt extremely vindicated.

The last line was, "He says it is a gift."

I looked at Teren.

"What is?"

"The basket."

"What basket?"

"The petitioner brings a basket."

"With no chicken."

"Correct."

"What is in it?"

"Nothing."

"Then why do I say it's a gift?"

"Because he says it's a gift."

"This government is deteriorating."

"Learn the lines."

Nessa found me beside the costume rail ten minutes later and held up a
dark coat.

"No."

I had not said anything.

"That one makes you look like a clerk."

"I have been a clerk."

"Not today."

She pushed it aside and pulled out a shorter brown coat with a stiff
collar.

I put it on.

The right sleeve was fine. The left caught slightly where I moved the
crutch.

Nessa watched me cross once.

"Again."

I crossed.

She folded the cuff under by less than the width of a finger and pinned
it inside.

"Again."

I crossed.

"Good."

"Can I breathe?"

"You're not Sellen."

"I've seen what you do to kings."

"Kings complain."

"So do guards."

"Less."

She walked away.

I looked down at the coat.

It was apparently finished.

Sellen came through in Marek's coat with both cuffs folded again. One of
Nessa's inside ties showed for half a second before he tucked it back.

"You have my government now," he said.

"I guard the door."

"Badly?"

"We haven't rehearsed."

"Then probably."

He kept walking.

I followed him into the stage space.

The repaired painted door from yesterday stood in its frame near the
right side. Davin had done something else to it after the hinge pin. It
now opened without the little sideways lift Pell had been using for
days.

I opened it.

It swung cleanly.

I closed it.

It shut harder than I expected.

The crack echoed through the empty hall.

Everyone looked at me.

Davin, from halfway up a ladder, said, "Don't."

"Understood."

I opened it again.

This time I let it close under my hand.

Quiet.

Davin went back to the hanging.

No theory was offered.

The door was simply a door with new behavior, and I had already lost one
argument with it.

Rehearsal took eleven minutes.

My first entrance was before Sellen's second petition.

I stood too close to the door.

Teren said, "Half a step."

I moved half a step.

"Other way."

I moved the other way.

"Good."

The position left room for Hara to pass behind me without catching my
coat. My crutch sat close enough to my body that it did not become a
second guard.

My first line was an announcement.

"Petitioner from East Lane."

Sellen, sitting in the king's chair, looked at me.

"And?"

I checked the page.

"That's the line."

"I know."

"Then why did you say and?"

"Because the king wants more."

"The petitioner from East Lane has a drainage complaint."

Sellen sighed.

"Everyone has a drainage complaint."

That was not on the page either.

Teren said, "Keep going."

I looked toward the imagined petitioner.

"You may enter."

Teren stopped us.

"No. You refuse him."

"I just announced him."

"Yes."

"Why announce someone I won't let in?"

Sellen said, "Government."

I looked at him.

"Fine."

We started again.

I announced the petitioner.

Sellen asked what he wanted.

I told him.

The petitioner tried to enter.

I held up one hand.

"Not until the king calls you."

Sellen said, "I just called him."

"You asked what he wanted."

"I am the king."

"That doesn't make your sentences clearer."

Teren lowered his page.

"Greg."

"Script."

"Please."

I let the petitioner in.

The scene continued.

On the basket entrance, EvIn came toward the door carrying an empty
covered basket.

I said, "You can't bring that in."

EvIn stopped.

"Why?"

"Because it is a basket."

"Is that forbidden?"

I looked at Teren.

Teren said, "Line."

I looked back at EvIn.

"He says it is a gift."

Sellen said, "For whom?"

EvIn said, "You."

Sellen leaned back in the chair.

"Then the guard was right. Take it away."

That got a laugh from Hara, who was sitting on the edge of the stage
eating an apple.

Teren made us do the basket section once more.

Then he left.

No one told me where he was going.

This continued to be allowed.

By midday, Rinna's slate had changed twice.

Hara's second mark disappeared because she had somewhere else to be
after the Widow. EvIn's name moved on the short piece. Pell got added to
rear for the last change. My name stayed where it was.

I found this comforting for almost three minutes.

Then Rinna walked past and said, "Tomorrow, third bell."

"Already?"

"That's what schedules do."

"What am I tomorrow?"

"I don't know."

"So we've scheduled ignorance."

"We've scheduled you."

She kept walking.

I stood there with a folded piece of brown cloth in my hands.

Nessa called from behind the costume rail.

"That stays."

I looked at the cloth.

"I wasn't moving it."

"You were thinking."

"This workplace has become oppressive."

"Put it on the shelf."

"Which shelf?"

She pointed.

I put it there.

At food, Sellen had remembered to bring something.

This was good because I had not brought enough bread to support another
adult.

He had a small paper packet of beans and onions. Pell had half a pear.
Hara had nothing because she said she had eaten before coming.

Nobody believed her.

"I did."

Pell looked at her.

"When?"

"Before."

"Before what?"

"Here."

"That's not a time."

Hara pointed at me.

"He's spreading."

"I have standards," I said.

"You have questions."

"Standards require questions."

Sellen ate a bean.

"Marek doesn't ask this many questions."

"Marek is north."

"Peacefully."

"His sister is having a baby."

Hara looked at me.

"You know why he went?"

"Yes."

"How?"

"I asked."

Everyone stared at me for a moment.

I looked around.

"What?"

Pell said, "Nothing."

"I hate all of you."

"No, you don't," Hara said.

This was an inappropriate amount of confidence.

The afternoon house was normal.

There were enough people that Rinna opened the same rows she usually
opened when nobody needed to have a discussion about rows. A man near
the front had a cough he tried to hold until the quietest possible
moments. Two women in the middle shared a paper twist of roasted seeds.
A boy near the aisle spent the first piece staring at the lamps instead
of the actors.

Nothing about them required strategy.

They had come to see a show.

We gave them one.

The King went first.

I waited at the right entrance in the stiff brown coat.

From there I could see Sellen in profile. Marek's coat still did not
belong to him. Nessa had made it stop advertising that fact.

Hara got the first real laugh.

Sellen got the second.

EvIn got a smaller one by looking at the king as though the king had
personally invented taxes that morning.

My entrance came.

I crossed to the repaired door, stopped where Teren had put me, and
announced the East Lane petitioner.

The audience barely noticed me.

This was appropriate.

The petitioner tried to come through.

I held up my hand.

"Not until the king calls you."

Sellen looked at me.

"I just called him."

I turned slightly.

"You asked what he wanted."

A laugh came from somewhere near the middle.

Not large.

Unexpected.

I heard it and kept going.

Sellen stared at me with the particular exhausted contempt of his king.

"I am the king."

"Then perhaps call more clearly."

That was not my line.

For one small, terrible fraction of a second, I knew it.

Sellen knew it too.

His mouth moved at one corner.

Then he said, "Enter before my guard begins governing."

The audience laughed.

The petitioner came through.

I stepped aside.

The scene continued.

Backstage, when I came off, Hara was waiting to enter from the other
side.

She looked at me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"That was not the line."

"No."

"Did it work?"

"Yes."

"Then don't become unbearable."

She went on.

This seemed unfair.

The basket section came later.

EvIn approached with the empty covered basket.

"You can't bring that in," I said.

"Why?"

"Because it is a basket."

"Is that forbidden?"

"He says it is a gift."

Sellen looked at EvIn.

"For whom?"

"You."

"Then the guard was right. Take it away."

This time the laugh was larger.

Not because of me.

I stood beside the door and let the king have it.

The strongest moment in the piece belonged to Hara anyway. She entered
late with a folded tax order, listened to Sellen complain for three
lines, then quietly turned the page upside down before handing it to
him.

The house saw it before he did.

The laugh began before Sellen even looked down.

He waited.

Looked.

Then said, "This kingdom has been printed incorrectly."

That was the biggest laugh of the afternoon.

I was standing six feet away.

I contributed by not doing anything.

After The King, Nessa took my guard coat before I had both arms out.

"Tomorrow?"

I asked.

"Maybe."

"Rinna has scheduled me."

"Condolences."

She hung the coat.

The Widow ran cleanly.

The writing box stayed exactly where it belonged.

I did not touch it.

I was not even tempted.

This was mostly because I was carrying a lamp at the time.

The ordinary hall problem arrived during the change for the short piece.

Someone had eaten one of the prop apples.

It was not Pell.

This caused immediate confusion because everyone looked at Pell.

He was offended.

"I didn't."

Nessa held up the remaining apple.

"There were two."

"I know."

"Where is the other one?"

"I don't know."

Hara came through fastening a cuff.

"I ate it."

Everyone turned.

She stopped.

"What?"

"Prop," Nessa said.

Hara looked at the apple in Nessa's hand.

Then at the core in her own hand.

"That was a real apple."

"They both are."

"Why?"

"So people don't bite painted wood."

Hara considered this.

"Reasonable."

"The short needs two."

"Use a pear."

Pell looked down at his half pear.

"No."

Nessa took it from him.

"Hey."

"You weren't using all of it."

"I was."

"Not anymore."

The short went on with one apple and half a pear.

From the house, this apparently remained theatre.

Afterward Pell demanded compensation.

Rinna gave him nothing.

I supported him in principle.

This also earned nothing.

Cleanup took longer than the last piece.

The repaired door had to come out of its frame because tomorrow's first
bill did not use it. Davin took the hinge side. Pell took the other.

I reached for the lower brace.

Davin said, "No."

I stopped.

"Why?"

"Three people."

There were already three people.

"Right."

I carried two folded cloths instead.

My wrist did not complain.

This was becoming boring again.

Good.

Rinna paid me one copper.

"Guard and set," I said.

"Yes."

"Also schedule compliance."

"You arrived before the schedule started."

"Advance compliance."

"No."

"Improvised governance."

"No."

"Prop preservation."

"You did not eat an apple."

"Exactly."

Rinna held out her hand.

I closed mine around the copper.

"Tomorrow, third bell," she said.

"What if I get a Guild note?"

"Tell me."

"What if I don't?"

"Third bell."

"What if you need me earlier?"

"I'll send."

"What if Lyssa is not home?"

"Then I'll send someone to the hall and complain that you're not there."

I thought about this.

"Strong system."

"Go home."

I went home by way of a food stall.

One copper of theatre pay had an alarming tendency to become food if I
looked at stalls too long.

I bought bread.

I considered cheese.

I did not buy cheese.

Then I went back and bought the smallest piece.

This was how poverty happened.

Lyssa was not home when I arrived.

The room smelled faintly of soap, old wood, and the onions we had not
yet finished. I put the bread on the table, the cheese beside it, and
checked under the cup.

There was a new folded paper.

I stared at it.

Then I unfolded it.

TWO DAYS.

AFTER MIDDAY.

REPEAT.

NEAR / FAR.

AWAY ONLY.

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

I read it once.

Then again.

The second reading was administrative.

I put it under the cup.

Twenty-seven draws.

Twenty-three shapings.

Twenty-three external attempts.

Still.

Two days.

Apparently I had schedules now.

Lyssa came home while I was cutting the cheese badly.

She put a small cloth parcel on the table, looked at the knife in my
hand, and said, "What happened to it?"

"It's cheese."

"It was."

"I bought it."

"That doesn't answer me."

I moved one of the crooked pieces toward her.

She took it.

"Good."

"There is that word again."

She ate the cheese.

I pointed at the cup.

"Hessa."

Lyssa looked at it.

"When?"

"Two days. After midday."

"And theatre?"

"Third bell."

She looked at me.

"Every day?"

"Apparently."

"You already did that."

"No. Before, I went there because they might need me."

"And now?"

"Now Rinna has declared that they might need me."

Lyssa ate another piece of cheese.

"So you'll stop going at breakfast."

"No promises."

She nodded as if this confirmed something.

"What did you do today?"

"I guarded a king."

"From what?"

"Petitioners. Baskets. Clear sentences."

"Did you succeed?"

"The kingdom survived."

"Good."

I told her about the missing prop apple.

She guessed Pell.

I told her Hara.

She laughed.

I told her about the half pear.

"Did Pell get paid for it?"

"No."

"Tragic."

"He thinks so."

We ate bread and cheese.

Lyssa opened her cloth parcel after we were nearly finished. It
contained three small wooden buttons and a length of pale binding.

I looked at it.

She looked at me.

"Don't."

"I didn't say anything."

"You were going to."

"I was going to ask whether it was the narrow thing."

"It is another narrow thing."

I stared.

"This city has too many."

She put the binding away.

The Hessa note remained under the cup.

Two days.

Near and far again.

Away only.

Same restrictions.

Tomorrow, third bell.

The day after that, third bell unless the Guild took me after midday, in
which case I had to tell Rinna before midday if possible, unless Rinna
sent for me earlier, in which case apparently the system changed.

I had acquired employment complicated enough to require remembering when
I was employed.

Lyssa reached across the table and took the last crooked piece of
cheese.

"That was mine."

"You didn't know."

"I absolutely knew."

"Too late."

I looked at the empty place where the cheese had been.

Then at the cup.

Then at the bread.

Nothing moved.

Nothing needed to.

Tomorrow I was a man expected after third bell.

In two days I was a man expected to sit in a marked chair and ask paper
to do almost nothing.

This was, somehow, a schedule.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 199

## THE ATTENDANT

Lyssa was still home when I woke, which meant I knew exactly what time I
was supposed to leave and had no excuse to leave then.

This was a problem.

She was sitting at the table with one of the dark sleeves across her
lap, picking out three stitches she had apparently decided were wrong
after already finishing them.

I ate bread.

She picked out a stitch.

I drank water.

She picked out another.

I looked at the door.

"Third bell," she said.

"I know."

"Then eat."

"I have a schedule."

"I know."

This was becoming irritating.

The Hessa note remained under the cup. Tomorrow. After midday. Repeat.
Near and far. Away only. Same restrictions.

I did not touch it.

Lyssa pulled out the third stitch, inspected the edge, then began
putting the stitches back in what looked to me like almost exactly the
same place.

"What was wrong with them?"

"They pulled."

"They were holding cloth."

"Wrong kind."

I looked at the sleeve.

It looked like a sleeve.

"It looks the same."

"It isn't."

"It looks good."

"That isn't the problem."

She bit the thread and set the sleeve aside.

"There is a bowl at Marra's."

"Of course there is."

"Bring it back."

"Why is our bowl at Marra's?"

"Because I brought her soup."

"When?"

"Yesterday."

"You brought someone soup and didn't tell me?"

Lyssa stood.

"I am sorry. Next time I will submit household food movements to the
theatre slate."

"That would help."

She kissed my cheek on the way past.

"Marra. Bowl. Then whatever you do until third bell."

"What do I do until third bell?"

"Anything."

This was not useful.

She left before I did.

I remained at the table.

The room had become offensively quiet.

There were things I could do. I swept. This took less time than expected
because our room was small and most of the dirt had concentrated itself
near the door. I rinsed the basin. I folded the blanket badly, unfolded
it, then folded it in a way Lyssa would probably still redo later.

I checked the leather wrap on my crutch.

Not magically.

It was just leather.

The seam still sat where it should. The lower ferrule had picked up a
thin line of pale grit from the street. I wiped it off with a damp rag.

That still did not make it third bell.

I went to Marra's.

The morning streets had settled into the period after people had decided
where they were going but before they regretted it. Bakers had stopped
shouting about the first loaves. Shop doors stood open. A woman with six
clay cups balanced on a board moved through a knot of men without
slowing down. Two boys were rolling a hoop along the gutter and arguing
about whether touching it with a hand counted if nobody saw.

I knew the route to Marra's without thinking about it now.

This was probably how cities happened to people.

Jessa was at the front table when I came in. She had a strip of red
cloth wrapped around one wrist and four pins between her lips.

I stopped inside the door.

"Bowl."

She pointed with her chin.

There were three bowls on a shelf.

I looked at them.

Jessa removed the pins from her mouth.

"Blue rim."

"That eliminates none of them."

"One has a blue rim."

"All three have blue."

"No."

I looked again.

One was mostly white with a thin blue line. One had little blue leaves.
One had a dark band that I considered blue and Jessa would probably call
storm-gray or dying-fish or some other hostile color.

I pointed to the white bowl.

"That one?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"You've improved."

"At bowls?"

"At asking."

"I hate this city."

Marra called from the back, "No, you don't."

I took the bowl.

"I am being monitored across rooms now."

"You're loud," Jessa said.

There was no reason to remain.

I remained for almost a minute because a customer came in carrying a
green overdress with one sleeve partly detached, and Jessa immediately
stopped talking to me. The woman knew her. Jessa knew the dress. Marra
called a price from the back without coming out.

I had become irrelevant.

This was convenient.

I left with the bowl.

At home I put it on the table.

Then I discovered that completing an errand did not cause time to
accelerate.

I considered going to the hall.

Third bell.

I did not go to the hall.

Instead I bought salt.

We did not desperately need salt, but we had little enough that buying
more could be defended in court.

The seller gave me a small folded paper packet and warned me not to get
it wet.

"I had planned to soak it."

"Then don't."

"Good advice."

I bought a cheap handful of greens from another stall because the bread
at home had begun looking lonely.

That still left time.

I walked past the theatre once.

This did not count.

I stayed on the opposite side of the street.

The front doors were open. Pell came out carrying a rolled hanging over
one shoulder.

He saw me.

I kept walking.

"Greg."

"No."

"What?"

"Third bell."

Pell looked up at the sky as if checking whether I had become religious.

"All right."

"Rinna scheduled me."

"Congratulations."

"It isn't a promotion."

"I didn't say it was."

"Good."

He shifted the hanging.

"Can you hold this?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Third bell."

Pell stared at me.

I stared back.

Then he laughed so hard the hanging slipped down his shoulder.

I kept walking.

This was discipline.

It felt terrible.

By the time third bell was close enough that I could hear the first
strike beginning from two streets over, I had returned the bowl, bought
salt, bought greens, swept the room, cleaned my crutch, walked past my
workplace, refused work, and eaten the heel of yesterday's bread.

I arrived at the hall during the last bell.

Rinna was at the ticket table.

She looked up.

"On time."

"I have a schedule."

"Don't become proud."

Then she handed me a stack of tickets.

That was it.

Apparently the schedule had already become boring.

"Front?"

"Until people come."

"People are why front exists."

"Until more people come."

I sat on the stool behind the little table and counted the tickets.

Forty-three.

I counted again.

Forty-two.

I counted a third time.

Forty-three.

This was concerning.

Rinna came back with a small coin tray.

"How many?"

"Forty-three."

"You sure?"

"No."

She took the stack, split it once, counted faster than I had, and said,
"Forty-three."

"Good."

"You've been here four minutes."

"I have already produced uncertainty."

"Set after."

She put the tickets down.

I looked toward the board.

GREG: FRONT / SET / PORTER

I knew the Porter.

The Borrowed House.

Six lines.

Chest.

Wrong house.

Procedural integrity.

I pointed.

"Porter?"

"Yes."

"Today?"

Rinna looked at me.

"I have to."

"Fine. Today."

"Same local version?"

"Ask Teren."

"Chest?"

"Ask Teren."

"Coat?"

"Nessa."

"Entrance?"

"Greg."

"That isn't an answer."

"It's the one you already know."

I did know.

This was annoying in a different direction.

Teren confirmed the local version without making me perform the entire
interrogation.

"Same entrance. Same chest. Same six."

"Any cuts?"

"No."

"Any chickens?"

He looked at me.

"No chickens."

"Good."

"Why?"

"Administrative history."

He walked away.

The hall was already in motion around me. Davin and Jori were shifting a
narrow painted wall piece that had nothing to do with Borrowed House.
Nessa had three costumes over one arm and was arguing with Hara about
whether a pale scarf could survive another wash. Sellen, still
temporarily king, stood beside the board eating something from a cup.

Marek's name remained absent.

Nobody had written a note beside it.

North was simply where Marek was.

Nessa threw the Porter's coat at me.

I caught it against my chest.

"Later," she said.

"I know."

She stopped.

"Good."

This was the problem with becoming competent. People stopped explaining
things and then acted pleased when you were not surprised.

I hung the coat where it belonged.

Then I moved two benches.

Then Rinna called me back to front because a family had arrived early.

Then Pell called me to hold the bottom of a rolled backdrop while he
retied the top cord.

Then Rinna called me back because Pell was apparently not authorized to
steal front staff.

"Staff?" I said.

"You."

"I have become plural."

"Sell seats."

The family wanted four.

The father wanted to know whether the little boy needed one.

Rinna, from behind me, said, "Bench still doesn't charge by weight."

The father looked confused.

"I was going to say that."

"Then say it before I do."

"I was thinking it."

"Thinking sells nothing."

They bought four.

I marked the little board incorrectly.

Rinna wiped out my mark.

"What did I do?"

"You put them in the second row."

"They asked for the second row."

"That mark is third."

I looked at the marks.

They were tiny scratches beside tiny numbers.

"Your system is bad."

"My system works when I use it."

She moved the family into the second row with one chalk stroke.

I had been employed for less than an hour and had already seated four
people in the wrong place without physically moving them.

This seemed efficient.

By the time the house began filling properly, Rinna took over front and
sent me inside.

The Borrowed House needed very little rehearsal.

I put on the Porter coat. Sellen passed behind me carrying a cup.

"Chest left," he said.

"I know."

"Good."

He kept walking.

I looked after him.

That had apparently been the rehearsal.

The chest was already in the left wing. It was not the heavy one. The
heavy one had metal corners and made my body negotiate with it. This one
was mostly painted boards and confidence.

I set my crutches where I had set them before, close enough to retrieve
after the visible carry. Sellen came back.

"You still know it?"

"Yes."

"Excellent."

"Are you Teren now?"

"No."

"Good."

"Why?"

"I don't know where he is."

Sellen nodded.

"Then he's Teren."

Teren was, in fact, outside talking to a man with a bundle of rolled
papers.

I never learned why.

Food happened in pieces.

I had brought the greens but not the salt, because even I understood
that loose salt was not lunch. Hara had bread and a little soft cheese.
Pell had something wrapped in cloth that turned out to be two cold
potatoes.

Sellen looked into the cloth.

"Why?"

"Food."

"Why cold?"

"They were hot yesterday."

"That is not how today works."

Pell bit one.

"It is now."

EvIn arrived, took one look at the potatoes, and produced a small
sausage from his pocket.

Everyone objected to this for different reasons.

"Why is that in your pocket?" Hara asked.

"Wrapped."

"That doesn't answer her," I said.

"It answers enough."

Sellen pointed at me.

"Government."

I ate my greens.

The first performance began with a decent house and no particular
weather inside it except heat.

The audience shifted on benches. Someone had brought a baby that made
one soft complaint during the opening and then apparently decided the
play was acceptable. A man near the back sneezed three times in exactly
the same place in three different scenes.

I spent the first piece moving things.

The Porter came in Borrowed House.

I put on the coat.

Nessa pulled the back straight as I passed.

"Was it wrong?"

"Now it isn't."

I took my position.

The chest came into my hands.

For the visible part of the entrance, I carried it without the crutches,
the same solution we had already worked out. Four steps. Set it down.
Recover the crutch. Continue.

Nobody applauded the logistics.

This was ideal.

"I was told to bring this here," I said.

Veya looked at the chest.

"Who told you?"

"A man."

"What man?"

"The one who paid me."

"Did he have a name?"

"Probably."

A laugh.

Not a large one.

I kept going.

The role lived in my body enough now that I did not have to think about
where the chest went. I only had to remember that the Porter had
absolute faith in a delivery system nobody else understood.

When Veya told me it was the wrong house, I said, "Then the house is
wrong."

That line got less than it had the first time I played it.

Fine.

Later, "I don't know" got more.

Also fine.

The scene belonged to Veya by the end. She had a stretch where three
people contradicted her at once, and she simply waited until all of them
finished before asking, "Would anyone like to be correct?"

The house laughed harder than it had at anything I said.

I was backstage by then, taking off the coat.

Nessa held out her hand.

I gave it to her.

"Good."

"Me or the coat?"

"The coat."

"Of course."

She was already gone.

Between pieces, I made my ordinary mistake.

Rinna told me to bring the short blue runner from rear storage.

There were two blue runners.

One was short.

One was shorter.

I knew this kind of problem.

I should have asked.

Instead I looked at them and decided the shorter one was obviously what
she meant because the other one was not, in my opinion, short enough to
deserve the name.

I carried it out.

Rinna looked at it.

"No."

I stood there.

"This is short."

"Other one."

"This is shorter."

"Yes."

"Then your words are wrong."

"My words are fine. That is the short blue runner. The other is the
little blue runner."

I looked toward the storage room.

"You have created a hierarchy after the fact."

"Bring the other one."

I brought the other one.

It fit the front of the low platform exactly.

The shorter runner would have left both corners bare.

I hated this.

Pell passed carrying a lamp.

"Wrong blue?"

"Wrong short."

He nodded as if this was a known disease.

The second piece went on.

Then Widow.

Then a short break before the evening bill.

Not a supper break. A sitting-on-crates-and-eating-whatever-remained
break.

Rinna sold enough seats for the evening that she opened another row.
Nobody celebrated. Jori moved the rope. Pell carried a bench. I carried
the other end because my wrist was no longer interesting and the bench
was not absurdly heavy.

My wrist remained boring.

Good.

Hara had to leave after the first evening piece.

She changed clothes quickly, pinned her hair differently, and left
through the rear door with half a piece of bread in her mouth.

"Work?" I asked.

She nodded.

"Where?"

She pointed at the bread with one hand as if this prevented speech.

Then she was gone.

I did not know where.

The bill adjusted.

Nessa took one of Hara's smaller parts in the short piece. A later piece
was dropped. EvIn complained because he had already changed stockings.

Rinna said, "Change back."

"I just changed."

"Then you're practiced."
