The evening house was a little louder than the afternoon one. Not rowdy.
Just fed.

Sellen's King got the best moment.

Not a line.

A chair.

The king's chair had been moved a little too far back during the change.
When Sellen sat, one rear leg landed on the edge of the low platform
instead of fully on it.

The chair tipped.

Sellen caught himself with one hand on the arm, froze, and looked slowly
at EvIn.

EvIn had not touched the chair.

The audience already knew this.

Sellen said, "Treason."

The room broke.

EvIn stared at him.

"I was standing here."

"Exactly where a clever man would stand."

Another laugh.

Pell, beside me backstage, whispered, "Chair did it."

"I know."

"Should we arrest it?"

"After."

The scene continued.

The chair remained innocent.

I had no role in The King that evening. Guard was not on the bill for
me. Someone else handled the entrance.

This did not bother me as much as I thought it might.

I was carrying a basket.

The last piece ended later than the afternoon one but earlier than
anyone had feared. The dropped piece bought time. People left in a slow
scrape of benches and shoes. A child had lost a ribbon. An old man had
left a folded paper on a seat. Rinna checked it, decided it was nothing
important, and put it on the front table in case he came back.

Cleanup began.

I returned the short blue runner.

The correct short blue runner.

Before putting it away, I asked Pell, "Where?"

He pointed to the middle shelf.

I put it there.

"The little one?"

"Bottom."

"Good."

"I thought you knew."

"I knew incorrectly."

"That's worse."

"Yes."

We folded the last cloth.

Rinna paid me two copper.

I looked at the coins.

Then at her.

"Two?"

"Two houses."

"I was also front."

"Yes."

"And set."

"Yes."

"And Porter."

"Yes."

"And runner specialist."

"No."

"I handled two different lengths."

"One of them wrong."

"That increased labor."

She held out her hand.

I closed mine.

"Two is correct."

"Thought so."

I had been paid more because I had worked more.

This was suspiciously straightforward.

Then I remembered.

"Guild tomorrow. After midday."

Rinna stopped writing on the slate.

"How long?"

"I don't know."

She looked at me.

"We've discussed the quality of this information."

"I am improving. I know when it starts."

"Morning, then."

I blinked.

"What?"

"Come morning. Set and front. Leave before midday."

"But my schedule says third bell."

"Tomorrow it doesn't."

"You made it yesterday."

"And now it changed."

"This institution has no respect for law."

"Morning."

"What if Hessa keeps me?"

"Then don't come back."

"What if she doesn't?"

"Come back if you can still work."

"That is very flexible."

"It means I don't know either."

I considered this.

"Finally, honesty."

Rinna pointed at the door.

I left before she could alter my pay.

Outside, Carrow had cooled enough that the stone no longer threw the
day's heat directly into my face.

I bought bread.

I bought a small scoop of cooked beans because two copper had made me
temporarily reckless.

I did not spend the second copper.

This was restraint.

I carried the food home.

Lyssa was already there.

She had one shoe off and the other still on, which suggested she had
arrived recently or had become only partly committed to footwear.

"You're late," she said.

"Two houses."

"Two?"

"Apparently I am a professional."

"How much?"

"Two copper."

"Then yes."

I put the bread and beans on the table.

"One is still alive."

"Copper?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"I bought beans."

"Still good."

"You're using that word too freely."

She took the bowl.

We ate.

I told her about the wrong short blue runner.

She said Rinna's naming system sounded perfectly clear.

I accused her of professional bias.

She said there was no profession in which short and little meant the
same thing.

I told her theatre had proved otherwise.

She asked whether I acted.

"Porter."

"Again?"

"Yes."

"Good?"

"I delivered the wrong chest to the wrong house exactly as required."

"So yes."

"Competently incorrect."

"Your specialty."

I ignored this.

After we ate, Lyssa began sorting the three wooden buttons from
yesterday against a strip of dark cloth. She rejected one, accepted two,
then rejected one of the accepted ones after holding it beside the cloth
again.

I watched.

"Tomorrow," she said.

"Hessa?"

"Yes."

"After midday."

"And theatre?"

"Morning."

She looked up.

"Morning?"

"Schedule changed."

"Already?"

"Rinna says this is what schedules do."

Lyssa smiled.

"I like her."

"You barely know her."

"I know enough."

I looked at the cup.

The note was still under it.

I did not move the cup.

Tomorrow, near and far again.

That thought sat in the room for a moment.

I was curious.

I was also tired.

Both could exist without fighting.

Lyssa pushed one wooden button toward the rejected side.

"Paper tomorrow?"

"Yes."

"Near and far?"

"Yes."

"Away?"

"Only."

She nodded.

Then she went back to the buttons.

That was all.

Twenty-seven draws.

Twenty-three shapings.

Twenty-three external attempts.

Nothing had changed today.

I had obeyed third bell.

I had worked two houses.

I had carried the wrong short blue runner and the correct one.

I had earned two copper and managed not to eat both of them.

Tomorrow I would go to the hall in the morning because my new schedule
had already changed, then leave for the Guild after midday because
another schedule said so.

I had wanted a life with structure.

Apparently structure moved.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 200

## THE RESPONDER

Lyssa was gone before I woke.

This solved one problem and created another.

I did not have to explain why I was looking at the cup.

There was nobody there to stop me from looking at it.

I ate bread instead.

The note remained under the edge exactly where it had been.

AFTER MIDDAY.

REPEAT.

NEAR / FAR.

AWAY ONLY.

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

I knew the words. Reading them again would not improve them.

I read them again anyway.

Administrative.

Then I put the cup back down.

The theatre wanted me in the morning.

The Guild wanted me after midday.

For the first time in my life, two institutions were competing for
portions of a day I had previously been willing to sell to almost anyone
with food.

This felt like progress.

It also meant I had to leave.

The hall was already open when I arrived. Rinna had the front table
turned sideways and was using it to support a stack of folded handbills
while Jori argued with one of the front shutters.

"You said morning," I told her.

Rinna looked up.

"It is."

"Good."

"Why?"

"I wanted confirmation."

"You came here to confirm morning?"

"I came here to work."

"Then do that."

She pointed at the rear.

The short blue runner was rolled beside the little blue runner.

I stopped.

Rinna saw me stop.

"Short."

"Short or little?"

She pointed at the larger roll.

I picked it up.

"Your system remains bad."

"It worked."

"Because I interrogated it."

"Platform."

I carried it to the platform.

That was my morning.

Not entirely.

There were benches to straighten, a front rope to move, two lamp tins to
count, and a chest from Borrowed House that had somehow acquired a
cracked handle between yesterday and today. Davin looked at it, pressed
the split wood once with his thumb, and said, "Other chest."

Pell said, "We used the other chest yesterday."

"Use it again."

"Problem solved," I said.

Davin looked at me.

"You didn't solve it."

"I witnessed the solution."

"Move."

I moved.

Marek remained north.

His absence had become part of the board. Sellen's name sat where
Marek's would have sat in The King, and nobody paused over it anymore.

Sellen came in carrying Marek's coat over one arm and his own breakfast
in the other hand.

"You have food," I said.

"I learned."

"From yesterday?"

"From hunger."

"Stronger teacher."

He held out the coat.

"Hook."

I took it.

There was a hook beside the costume rail.

I hung the coat.

Sellen ate.

No one thanked me.

This was increasingly how employment worked.

Teren arrived, changed two marks on the board, crossed out one short
piece, then disappeared into the rear with Nessa.

I had no acting role that morning.

This was useful because I was leaving before midday and because Rinna
had decided I belonged at front for the first part of the house setup.

There was no performance before I left. Only preparation for one that
would happen without me if Hessa kept me.

That felt stranger than it should have.

At one point Pell asked whether I was coming back.

"If I'm useful."

"Are you?"

"Rinna's standard."

Pell considered this.

"Probably not."

"Then I can go home."

Rinna, from the front, said, "I can hear you."

"That keeps happening," I said.

Before midday I ate a heel of bread, drank water, and told Rinna I was
leaving.

She did not look up from the ticket board.

"Guild?"

"Yes."

"Go."

"Do you need me after?"

"If you're useful."

"We've established uncertainty."

"Then come back and I'll decide."

This was an excellent system because nobody had to know anything.

I left.

The walk to the Guild was familiar enough that I no longer spent the
whole route thinking about the Guild.

A fish seller had moved his table farther into the lane and was being
punished for it by every cart. A woman carried a cage with nothing in
it. Two men were repairing a drain cover while a third man stood over
them explaining drains.

I passed a baker.

I considered buying something.

I had bread in me already.

This was evidence of maturity.

I kept my copper.

The Guild front room smelled like old paper, hot stone, and whatever
sharp oil they used on prepared metal.

A clerk I had seen before was arguing with a man holding five lengths of
cord.

"I brought six."

"You have five."

"I brought six."

"You are holding five."

"I know what I brought."

The clerk looked at the cords.

"Then one left."

The man looked behind himself as if the sixth cord might have become
ashamed.

I went around them.

Hessa was not waiting for me.

Good.

She was in the smaller room with the broad table, writing on a sheet
while a shallow wooden case sat open beside her. The long pale board was
already on the table.

She looked up.

"Eat?"

"Yes."

"When?"

"Before I left the hall."

"Water?"

"Yes."

"Wrist?"

"Boring."

"Show me."

I rotated it.

Once.

Hessa watched.

"Again."

I did.

No pain. A little ordinary pull at the far edge of the motion, maybe.
Less than yesterday. Less than I would have noticed if she had not
asked.

"Shoulder?"

"Also boring."

"What did you lift?"

"Runner. Lamp tins. One end of a bench. A coat."

"Chest?"

"No. Davin rejected it."

"Why?"

"Cracked handle."

She nodded as if Davin had personally preserved the experiment.

"Sit."

The chair was not where I remembered it.

I noticed because there was a chalk line on the floor and the front legs
were not on it.

Hessa moved it until they were.

"That line new?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Because I was about to accuse my memory."

"Don't use memory when you can use a mark."

I sat.

"That sounds like theatre."

"No."

"All right."

My crutches went against the wall.

Good foot flat.

No bracing against the table.

The board ran away from me across the table. Hessa had marked two
positions on it in chalk. One sat close enough that I could have reached
the paper fin easily if the glass were not there. The other sat much
farther across the table, beyond comfortable reach even if I leaned.

Near and far.

Both guides ran in the same direction.

Away from me.

Beside each target position was a matched control guide.

Hessa set four folded paper fins on a clean cloth.

"Same paper?"

"Same stock."

"Same folds?"

"As close as I can make them."

"You made them?"

"Yes."

"Then better than mine."

"Everything is better than yours if you leave it alone."

I looked at her.

She was already checking the glass.

"That was unnecessary."

"It was accurate."

She set the shield over the board.

I could see the tiny fins through it.

They looked stupid.

I had moved paper before.

Not much.

Enough that Hessa had stopped saying we did not know whether anything
happened at all.

Today we were trying to discover whether the paper cared where it was.

This was an unreasonable amount of attention for something that weighed
less than a bad excuse.

Hessa sat opposite me.

"Baseline first."

I nodded.

She watched my hands.

I let my breathing settle.

The draw was familiar now, which did not make it easy. Familiar was
different. I knew the place where I tended to reach too hard. I knew the
inward gathering. I knew the irritating tendency for the wrist edge to
feel crowded before the rest of the shape had decided what it was doing.

I did not chase any of it.

The draw settled.

Hessa watched for another breath.

"Enough."

I released.

She wrote.

"Twenty-eight," I said.

"Yes."

Successful supervised minimal draws.

Twenty-eight.

The number felt much larger than the thing.

"Shape."

I started again.

The same gathering.

The same wrist-edge crowding.

I held the deliberate shape badly at first, relaxed before it broke, and
tried again within the same supervised attempt.

It steadied.

Not beautiful.

Not useful.

Deliberate.

Hessa counted under her breath.

"Enough."

I let it go.

"Twenty-four."

She wrote that too.

Then she moved the first target into the far position.

Of course she started far.

I had wanted near.

That was probably why she started far.

"You chose that because I wanted the other one."

"I chose it before you arrived."

"Then you anticipated my complaint."

"That was easy."

She checked the target fin.

Then the control.

Both upright.

Both still.

Glass down.

"Requested direction?"

"Away from me."

"Distance?"

"Far."

"Do not change the question."

"I know."

"Say it."

"Away. Far."

Hessa sat back.

I looked at the little fin.

The far position was not dramatically far. It was the other side of a
table.

That made the whole thing worse.

If someone had told old me I would someday sit in a magical guild trying
to move a piece of paper across a table, I would have expected more
fire.

I gathered.

Shaped.

The wrist edge crowded.

I left it alone.

Then I made the request.

Away.

Nothing happened.

The target stayed upright.

The control stayed upright.

I held until Hessa said, "Stop."

I stopped.

The silence afterward felt louder than movement would have.

Hessa leaned forward and checked both fins without touching the glass.

"Nothing visible."

"I noticed."

"Twenty-four."

External attempts.

The number had caught the others.

"Far," she said.

"No observed response."

She wrote.

I wanted to argue with the paper.

There was nothing useful to say.

Hessa lifted the glass, removed both fins, and replaced them with the
second pair.

This time she set the target in the near guide.

My attention sharpened before I wanted it to.

Hessa noticed.

"Same effort."

"I know."

"Your face doesn't."

"My face is not part of the apparatus."

"Your body is."

I relaxed my jaw.

"Better?"

"No."

"Good."

Glass down.

Target near.

Control beside it.

"Distance?"

"Near."

"Direction?"

"Away."

I drew.

The familiar shape came with less fighting than the first external
attempt. Not stronger. Cleaner, maybe. Or I wanted it cleaner because
the fin was close.

That was exactly the problem.

I held.

Requested away.

The paper fin moved.

Barely.

Its lower edge slipped along the guide less than the width of my
smallest fingernail. The top leaned, corrected, and stayed in the new
position.

The control did not move.

I stopped breathing for a second.

Hessa did not.

"Stop."

I released.

She checked.

Then she lifted the glass and bent close to the guide.

"Movement away," she said.

"Yes."

"Control unchanged."

"Yes."

"Small."

"Yes."

"Clean."

I looked at her.

She wrote.

"Twenty-five."

External attempt twenty-five.

I smiled.

Hessa saw it.

"You have moved paper before."

"Not today."

"Now you have."

"Near."

"Here."

I stopped smiling slightly.

"Near here."

"Better."

I hated improvement in vocabulary.

Hessa removed the fins.

"Water."

"I am fine."

"That was not a question."

I drank.

She did not immediately set the next pair.

Instead she checked my wrist, had me open and close my hand, then
pressed two fingers lightly along the forearm where I had tightened
after the last session.

"Anything?"

"No."

"Shoulder?"

"No."

"Good."

There it was again.

Nothing hurt and everyone was delighted.

She went back to the board.

The third target went far.

I watched her set it.

"Again?"

"That is what repeat means."

"I thought repeat meant doing the part I liked."

"No."

Glass down.

Far.

Away.

I gathered.

The shape came slower.

I could feel myself wanting to make more of it because the first far
attempt had done nothing.

That was chasing.

I knew it before Hessa said anything.

I backed off.

"Better," she said.

I made the request.

For a moment I thought the paper twitched.

Not moved.

Twitched.

The upper corner changed against the background line, or I thought it
did. The lower edge remained where it had been.

I held.

Nothing else happened.

"Stop."

I released.

Hessa stayed still.

"Did you see that?"

"Maybe."

"That is a terrible answer."

"It is the correct answer."

She moved around the table.

The target fin leaned a fraction differently than I remembered.

The control looked exactly the same.

Hessa checked the guide, then the paper, then the chalk mark.

"Possible displacement."

"Away?"

"Not cleanly."

"It moved."

"Maybe."

"I saw it."

"You saw something."

"That sounds intentionally worse."

"It is intentionally narrower."

She wrote.

I tried to read upside down.

She covered the line with her hand.

"Rude."

"Rest."

External attempt twenty-six.

Far.

Ambiguous.

Not cleanly away.

That was not nothing.

It was also not useful enough to become something.

I drank again.

Outside the room, somebody dropped metal.

There was a sharp clatter, a curse, then another voice saying, "Don't
pick that up."

Hessa did not react.

The Guild continued existing around us.

A clerk passed the open doorway carrying three narrow wooden cases.
Somewhere farther down the corridor, the man with the cords was still
arguing. I heard "six" again.

This comforted me.

Eventually Hessa put the fourth pair into place.

Near.

I looked at it.

She looked at me.

"Don't."

"I didn't do anything."

"You were about to become hopeful."

"I am allowed."

"After."

This was unfair.

Glass down.

Near target.

Near control.

Both still.

"Direction?"

"Away."

"Distance?"

"Near."

I settled.

The first draw had been clean enough.

The shaping was tired now, but not collapsing. The wrist edge crowded. I
let it. The center held.

I asked for almost nothing.

Away.

The fin stayed still.

One breath.

Two.

Then the bottom edge gave a tiny slip.

Less than the first near movement.

Still away.

The control remained exactly where it was.

My whole body wanted to lean forward.

I did not.

Hessa said, "Stop."

I stopped.

She checked the glass.

The guide.

The control.

The target.

Then she sat down and wrote.

I waited.

She kept writing.

"That is too many words."

"No."

"How many?"

"Enough."

"Did it count?"

"Yes."

I exhaled.

External attempt twenty-seven.

Near.

Movement away.

Control unchanged.

Hessa finished the line and turned the sheet toward herself rather than
me.

"Read it."

"I can't."

"Then move your chair."

"My chair is marked."

"The test is over."

I moved the chair.

This felt illegal.

I went around the table and read over her shoulder.

AWAY RESPONSE REPEATED AT NEAR POSITION IN SECOND SESSION.

FAR RESPONSE NOT REPEATED CLEANLY.

NEAR RESPONSE MORE RELIABLE THAN FAR ACROSS CURRENT SESSIONS.

DISTANCE DEPENDENCE SUPPORTED IN CURRENT APPARATUS.

NO RANGE ESTABLISHED.

NOT GENERALIZED.

I read it twice.

"Distance dependence supported."

"Yes."

"So near is easier."

"In this apparatus, under these conditions, near has produced a response
more reliably than far."

"That is a very long way to say near is easier."

"It is a shorter way to avoid saying something we have not shown."

"Far doesn't work."

Hessa looked at me.

I corrected myself.

"Far has not worked reliably."

"Good."

"I resent you."

"No."

"You don't know."

"I have evidence."

I looked back at the paper.

Twenty-eight successful supervised minimal draws.

Twenty-four supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Twenty-seven supervised external-effect attempts.

Two sessions now where near had behaved better than far.

The magic had not become larger.

Nothing had become useful.

The far paper might have moved a little. The near paper had definitely
moved a little.

This was, somehow, exciting.

"What next?"

Hessa pulled another sheet toward herself.

"Not today."

"I know."

"You asked."

"I can ask without doing it."

"Good."

She drew three small marks in a line.

One close.

One far.

One between them.

I looked at the middle mark.

"Middle."

"Intermediate."

"Middle."

"Fine."

"Next time?"

"Possibly."

"That is not a date."

"No."

I waited.

She wrote:

NEXT QUESTION: INTERMEDIATE POSITION. AWAY ONLY. SAME APPARATUS IF
POSSIBLE. NO OTHER CHANGE.

Then below it:

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

I pointed.

"Still?"

"Yes."

"We repeated it."

"Yes."

"And got the same pattern."

"Broadly."

"So I have earned what?"

"Better evidence."

"I cannot eat that."

"You have theatre."

"That pays in copper."

"Then eat copper."

"That seems medically unsound."

"It is."

She gathered the fins.

"Do not test at home."

"I know."

"Do not test at the theatre."

"I know."

"Not with paper."

"I know."

"Not with anything else because you think it is technically not paper."

"I know."

"No coins."

"I know."

"No thread."

"I know."

"No larger objects."

"I know."

"No practice."

"I know."

She looked at me.

"I know," I said again.

"Good."

I hated that word.

She checked my wrist once more before letting me stand.

Still boring.

My forearm felt worked, not hurt. The distinction mattered enough that I
did not complain.

When I left the Guild, the man with the cords was gone.

There were five cords on the clerk's table.

I never learned whether there had been six.

The day was still alive outside.

I could go home.

I could also go back to the hall.

Rinna had told me to come back if I was useful.

This created a test with much poorer controls.

I went to the hall.

The afternoon performance had already started.

Rinna saw me come through the front.

"Useful?"

"Probably."

She pointed toward rear.

"Cloth basket."

That was all.

I went rear.

Pell was crouched beside a stack of stools.

"Paper?"

"Near."

He looked at me.

"Near what?"

"Exactly."

"Did it work?"

"Some of it."

"Good."

"Stop saying that."

He handed me a folded cloth.

"Costumes."

I carried it to Nessa.

She took it without asking about magic.

Better.

I spent the rest of the performance doing almost nothing important.

A stool moved.

A coat needed hanging.

Someone had left a cup behind the painted wall and I retrieved it after
the scene.

Sellen came offstage sweating in Marek's coat and said, "Water."

I handed him water.

He drank half.

"Guild?"

"Yes."

"Successful?"

"I moved paper almost nowhere."

"Excellent."

"Everyone here is broken."

"Probably."

He went back out.

I was not paid for a second house because I had arrived after most of it
and Rinna had apparently retained a functioning sense of arithmetic.

She did give me half a heel of bread left from the rear table.

I accepted this as institutional recognition.

At home, Lyssa was later than me.

I washed.

I ate the bread.

I looked at Hessa's new sheet, which I had carried folded inside my
coat.

I did not put anything near it.

Eventually Lyssa came in carrying a bundle of pale cloth and smelling
faintly of hot street dust.

She put the bundle down.

"Near or far?"

"Near."

She waited.

I sighed.

"Near here."

Lyssa smiled.

"That sounds like Hessa."

"It is contagious."

She sat.

"Far?"

"Nothing clean. Maybe a tiny movement once. Hessa won't call it."

"Near?"

"Twice."

"Again?"

"Again."

She took off one shoe.

"So you learned something."

"I learned that paper has preferences."

"No."

"I learned that paper near me has preferences."

"No."

"I learned that under one specific arrangement, tiny folded paper near
me is more cooperative than tiny folded paper farther from me."

Lyssa took off the other shoe.

"That definitely sounds like Hessa."

"I hate this."

"You look pleased."

"I can do both."

She nodded.

I showed her the sheet.

She read the conclusion without trying to improve it.

Then she saw the three marks.

"Middle?"

"Intermediate."

She looked at me.

"Hessa?"

"Yes."

"Thought so."

"No date."

"Good."

"There it is again."

Lyssa gave the paper back.

I put it under the cup with the others.

Twenty-eight draws.

Twenty-four shapings.

Twenty-seven external attempts.

Near had worked again.

Far had not worked cleanly again.

I still could not practice.

I still could not move anything useful.

Tomorrow the theatre wanted me at third bell unless Rinna changed her
mind, sent for me, forgot, or decided schedules were decorative.

Hessa wanted me at some future time she had not named so we could move
the paper to the middle of the table and ask it to do almost nothing
there.

This was progress.

Annoyingly, it was also evidence.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 201

## THE CLERK

The cup had nothing under it.

I knew because I looked.

This was not testing.

It was checking the mail.

Lyssa was sitting at the table with a dark sleeve turned inside out over
one hand. The pale binding I had spent several days refusing to
understand was now sewn along the edge. It looked like a sleeve.

This was disappointing only because it was exactly what she had said.

"No note?" she asked.

"No."

"Good."

I looked at her.

She did not look up.

"You're doing it deliberately now."

"Yes."

I ate bread.

The third bell was still far enough away that going to the theatre would
have been a violation of a system I had personally complained about
until someone created it.

I had no Guild work.

No errands.

No bowl.

No salt emergency.

This was dangerous.

Lyssa turned the sleeve right side out, checked the seam, then folded it
with another piece of dark cloth.

"Customer?" I asked.

"Yes."

"That's it?"

"That's what?"

"The narrow thing."

"One of them."

I pointed at her.

"No."

She smiled.

There were apparently infinite narrow things.

I washed the cup, which had done nothing wrong, and went outside before
I learned about another one.

Carrow was already moving.

The morning had warmed the stone but not yet the walls. A cart with
green onions passed me. A boy carried a stack of flat baskets taller
than his head and navigated entirely by looking around one side. Two
women stood in a doorway arguing about whether somebody named Orin had
paid for lamp oil. I did not know Orin.

This was healthy.

I bought nothing.

This was less healthy but financially responsible.

The third bell began while I was still half a street from the hall.

I arrived before the last strike finished.

Rinna was not at the front table.

This immediately made the entire schedule feel suspicious.

Pell was.

He had both hands inside a sack.

"Where's Rinna?"

"Rear."

"What are you doing?"

"Looking."

"For?"

He stopped.

"Don't know yet."

"Good system."

"Found it."

He pulled out a wooden mallet.

"Did you know that was in there?"

"No."

"Then you weren't looking for it."

"I am now."

I left him.

The board was crowded.

Someone had erased Sellen's name from The King and written MAREK over
it.

I read it twice.

Not because I was uncertain.

Because Marek was standing under the costume rail eating dried apples.

"You came back," I said.

Marek chewed.

"Apparently."

He held out the little paper twist.

I took one.

This was the correct form of reunion.

"Baby?"

"Yes."

"Good?"

"Yes."

"Sister?"

"Fine."

"Good."

Marek looked at me.

"You ask fewer questions now."

"I have evidence."

"What?"

"Nothing."

I ate the apple.

Sellen was standing three feet away in his shirt while Nessa attacked
Marek's King coat with pins.

More accurately, she was removing the attack she had previously
committed.

The folded cuffs had been opened. One shoulder pin was already between
Nessa's lips. The tie at the back hung loose.

Marek looked at the coat.

"What did you do to it?"

"Made it fit," Nessa said.

Sellen said, "Barely."

"It fit."

"I could not breathe."

"You didn't need to breathe in that scene."

"I had lines."

"Bad planning."

Marek took another dried apple.

"Did he ruin the King?"

Sellen answered before I could.

"Improved him."

Marek nodded.

"Then I'll ruin him back."

There was no ceremony.

No one asked Marek about the road.

No one asked whether he had missed us.

Nessa pulled the last temporary pin and handed him the coat.

"Try."

He put it on.

The shoulders sat where they were supposed to.

The sleeves ended at his wrists.

Marek rolled one shoulder.

"Better."

Sellen looked offended.

Nessa looked at him.

"Your body was wrong."

"Temporarily."

"Still."

Rinna came out of the rear carrying the small slate.

She saw me.

"Third bell."

"I know."

"Good."

"I don't like when you say that."

"Clerk."

"What?"

She pointed at the board.

My name was written beside THE DEBTOR'S UNCLE.

GREG: CLERK / SET.

The Clerk.

I had done it once when Evin was late. Eight lines then, if I remembered
correctly.

I did not trust remembering correctly.

"Today?"

Rinna sighed.

"Teren."

Teren was somewhere behind the painted wall.

I found him crouched over a stack of pages with one knee on the floor.

"Clerk."

"Yes."

"Same version?"

"Local."

"Eight lines?"

"Seven."

"Why seven?"

"Road cut."

"Which line?"

He held out his hand.

I gave him the page Rinna had shoved at me without my noticing she had
done it.

Teren crossed out a sentence near the bottom.

"There."

"What happened to it?"

"Nothing. We don't use it."

"That is a better answer than the chicken."

Teren looked up.

"What chicken?"

"Exactly."

He went back to the pages.

I had seven lines.

I knew six of them already.

The seventh was not new. It had merely survived a cut I had forgotten.

This was becoming the main hazard of repertoire.

Not learning.

Remembering which version of what I had learned was currently alive.

I went back to the board.

The King had changed around Marek's return. Sellen was back in Petition.
Evin had shifted one mark lower. Hara remained where she had been. The
short piece was back because Hara apparently did not have outside work
today.

Nobody explained this.

The board did.

Mostly.

Pell came up beside me.

"Marek brought apples."

"I know."

"You took one."

"I know."

"He only gave me half."

"Did you ask for two?"

"No."

"Then current evidence suggests he gave you half."

Pell stared at me.

"What happened to you?"

"Hessa."

"Can she fix it?"

"No."

I regretted that immediately.

One contamination of ordinary speech was enough.

I went to work.

The Clerk coat was gray.

Not Witness gray.

Different gray.

This was information I had not wanted.

Nessa handed it to me.

I started toward the hook where I had hung the Witness coat last time.

"Other."

I stopped.

"Of course."

"Second peg."

I hung it on the second peg.

"Why are there this many gray coats?"

"Because people own gray."

"Badly."

Nessa had already turned away.

The hall needed more chairs on the right because Rinna had sold a
cluster of seats together. Jori and Pell moved the heavy ones. I carried
two lighter chairs one at a time because my wrist was boring and I
wanted to keep it that way.

Davin had the repaired Borrowed House chest open on a bench.

"Fixed?"

"Yes."

I reached for the handle.

Davin slapped my hand away.

"I fixed it."

"That usually means testing."

"I tested it."

"How?"

"With the handle."

I withdrew.

"This place has become hostile to inquiry."

"It was always hostile to you."

Fair.

Marek had been gone long enough for one of the rear paths to change.

The narrow painted wall that used to stand flat against the brick was
now stored upright beside a costume basket because the cracked chest had
occupied its old place during repairs.

Marek came around the corner carrying the King ledger and stopped.

"Why is that there?"

Pell, under a bench again, said, "Tuesday."

"What happened Tuesday?"

"A lot."

Marek looked at me.

"Did you do this?"

"No."

"Good."

"Current evidence..."

He walked away.

I stopped.

Hessa was becoming a disease.

The rehearsal for Debtor's Uncle took less than ten minutes.

Evin was there, so I was not replacing him. The Clerk was simply mine
today because the bill had been arranged that way.

This felt different.

Not important.

Different.

The Clerk entered with a folded account, told Veya's uncle that the debt
had been entered twice, then had to explain that entering it twice did
not mean the debtor owed twice.

This should have been simple.

It was not.

"Then why write it twice?" Veya asked in character.

"Because the first copy went to the office."

"And the second?"

"The second proves the first existed."

"To whom?"

"The office."

"Which already has the first."

"Unless it doesn't."

Veya stared at me.

The line was hers.

The logic belonged to government.

Teren said, "Again. Faster."

We did it again.

It was funnier faster because nobody had time to save the system.

That was all the note we got.

No lecture.

No deeper truth.

The Clerk was an idiot in a coat who believed paper could repair paper.

I felt unfairly represented.

At food, Marek's dried apples became public property despite no formal
decision.

He had also brought a small hard cheese wrapped in cloth.

Sellen took a piece.

Marek slapped his hand.

"You ate while I was gone."

"I suffered."

"You had beans."

"Exactly."

Hara had a boiled egg and bread.

Pell had nothing.

This lasted until Rinna walked by, saw him looking at everyone else's
food, and threw him half a heel without stopping.

Pell caught it against his chest.

"She loves me."

Rinna, already ten feet away, said, "No."

I had bread from home and another dried apple.

Marek sat on an overturned crate.

"Girl," he said.

"What?" Hara asked.

"The baby."

"Good?"

"Yes."

"Your sister?"

"Fine."

Sellen said, "Name?"

Marek told him.

I forgot it almost immediately because someone at the front dropped a
stack of wooden seat markers.

No one repeated it for my benefit.

Marek had gone north.

His sister had a daughter.

He had seen her.

Now he was back eating cheese and complaining that Sellen had stretched
his coat, which was not physically possible.

This was enough information.

The afternoon house was normal.

Not full.

Not thin.

The front row had a woman with two girls who whispered until the first
scene started and then became silent. A man near the aisle had a cough
that sounded expensive. Someone in the rear smelled strongly of onions.

The King went first.

Marek walked on wearing his coat as if it had never belonged to anyone
else.

Sellen's King had spent several days looking at the kingdom like
everyone in it had become stupid overnight.

Marek's King took the first petition as a personal insult.

"Your road is flooded?" he demanded.

Hara said, "Yes, Majesty."

"And you brought the water here?"

The laugh came before Hara answered.

"No, Majesty."

"Then why have you brought me the problem?"

Different.

Not better.

The play had changed back without becoming the same.

Marek reached for the ledger on his right.

It was not there.

The ledger had been moved to the left during Sellen's run because
Sellen's entrance came from a slightly different angle.

Marek's hand found air.

For half a breath he looked at it.

Then he looked at Evin.

"You have moved my government."

Evin, who had not moved anything, said, "It was heavy."

The audience laughed.

Pell made a strangled sound backstage.

I looked at him.

"Did he know?"

"No."

Marek took the ledger from the left and continued.

Later, Hara got the strongest moment.

She had a petition about a wall built across a shared lane. Marek kept
interrupting before she could finish.

The first two times she tried to speak over him.

The third time she stopped.

She folded the petition.

Put it away.

Waited.

Marek finished a long complaint about citizens refusing to state simple
problems.

Hara said, "Are you ready?"

The room broke.

Marek stared at her.

"No."

She nodded.

"I'll come back."

Bigger laugh.

She turned.

Marek lunged half out of the chair.

"Wait."

That was the largest laugh of the piece.

I was backstage holding a stool.

Excellent work.

Debtor's Uncle came later.

My Clerk coat fit badly at the neck.

I ignored it.

The folded account was on the correct table.

I entered when I was supposed to.

The first two lines got nothing.

The duplicate-account exchange got a laugh.

I did not expect it.

I kept going.

Veya asked why the second copy proved the first existed.

I said, "Because the office has it."

She said, "The first?"

"The second."

"And the first?"

"That is why we made the second."

A better laugh.

This was scripted.

I took full credit internally.

Then I made my mistake.

I left the folded account on the table when I exited.

It belonged with me.

I knew that.

I had carried it out in rehearsal.

I had also spent enough time around Letter Box that a folded paper left
on a table looked completely reasonable.

Backstage, Teren held out his hand.

I looked at it.

"What?"

"Account."

I looked through the gap in the flat.

The account sat onstage.

Veya was still there.

"Ah."

"Yes."

"Do we need it?"

"Next entrance."

"I can get it."

"Not now."

So I waited.

Veya crossed the room during the scene, saw the folded account where it
should not be, picked it up as part of clearing the table, and carried
it toward the side.

She never looked at me.

At the edge, she let her hand fall behind the flat.

I took the account.

She continued speaking.

The audience never knew.

Teren looked at me.

"Thank you," I whispered toward Veya.

From onstage, without turning, she said her next line louder.

I took that as forgiveness.

My next entrance happened with the account.

No disaster.

No lesson.

Afterward Veya came off.

"You left it."

"I recovered it."

"I recovered it."

"I participated."

"You held your hand out."

"Critical infrastructure."

She drank water.

I carried the stool away.

The short piece used the apple.

A different apple.

Not Marek's dried apples.

I checked.

No one ate it early.

This represented institutional improvement.

Cleanup was louder than the show.

One bench leg had started squeaking. Pell insisted it had not squeaked
before. Davin said wood did not care about his testimony. Hara left with
someone I did not know, still arguing about whether she had time to eat
before wherever she was going next. Sellen changed out of a brown coat
and immediately put on his own brown coat, which made costume work feel
insulting.

Marek found the painted wall still in the wrong place.

"Does this live here now?"

Jori said, "For now."

"Why?"

"Chest."

"The chest is fixed."

Davin, across the room, said, "Tomorrow."

Marek accepted this.

He had been gone several days and had already learned the correct
answer.

Rinna paid me one copper.

"Clerk. Set."

"Account recovery."

"No."

"That required cooperation."

"Veya should charge you."

"Then don't tell her."

Veya, behind me, said, "I heard."

Rinna handed her nothing.

"Tomorrow, third bell," Rinna said.

"No Guild."

"I didn't ask."

"No Hessa note."

"I still didn't ask."

"I'm providing useful scheduling information."

Rinna looked at me.

"Third bell."

"Excellent."

"Go home."

I bought greens on the way.

Not because I had to.

Because bread had begun to feel like a personal failure.

The woman selling them added one small bruised onion after I paid.

"For soup."

"I didn't buy enough for soup."

"Then buy more tomorrow."

This was aggressive salesmanship.

I took the onion.

Lyssa was home before me.

The finished dark sleeve was gone.

So was the pale binding.

A different piece of cloth sat on the table.

I refused to ask whether it was narrow.

"Marek came back," I said.

Lyssa looked up.

"Baby?"

"Girl."

"Good?"

"Yes."

"Sister?"

"Fine."

She smiled.

"You got the whole report."

"I asked fewer questions."

"Growth."

"Don't."

I put the greens down.

"And Sellen?"

"Demoted by childbirth."

"Was he upset?"

"No. Nessa gave Marek his lungs back."

Lyssa waited.

I explained the coat.

She laughed.

We made something that was not soup with the greens, onion, bread, and
the last piece of cheese.

It became soup after enough water.

The seller had won.

I told Lyssa about Marek reaching for the ledger in the wrong place.

"And?"

"He accused Evin of moving the government."

"Did Evin?"

"No."

"Good."

"Current evidence suggests..."

Lyssa pointed the spoon at me.

"No."

I stopped.

"Thank you."

"Hessa is ruining you."

"I said that."

"No note?"

"No."

"Good."

I stared at her.

She ate.

The intermediate position could wait.

The paper could wait.

Hessa could decide when she wanted to put the middle of the table under
investigation.

Today Marek had gone north and come back.

Sellen had stopped being king because the other king returned.

I had been a Clerk because Rinna wrote it on a board.

I had forgotten an account onstage and Veya had quietly saved me.

One copper had become greens and a bruised onion.

Tomorrow I was expected at third bell.

No one had written anything under the cup.

For once, the schedule had stayed where we put it.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 202

## THE NEIGHBOR

There was still nothing under the cup.

I checked once.

Lyssa saw me do it and said nothing, which was worse than saying good.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You had a face."

"I have several."

"That one was waiting for paper."

"I was checking."

She tied a small bundle with cord and pulled the knot tight with her
teeth.

"Still nothing."

"Correct."

"Good."

I pointed at her.

"There it is."

She picked up the bundle.

It was not narrow.

I had checked.

"Third bell?" she asked.

"Third bell."

"Then why are you dressed?"

I looked down at myself.

I was wearing the same shirt I would have worn if I had not been going
anywhere.

"Because I own clothes."

"You've been ready for half an hour."

"I live here."

Lyssa smiled and left with the bundle.

I had time.

Again.

The problem with having a schedule was that it created areas around the
schedule where nobody had told me what to do.

I washed the bowl from last night's accidental soup. I swept the room
badly, then swept the part where the dirt had gone after I swept it
badly. I checked the leather on my crutch grip with my thumb and did not
go to the cobbler because nothing was wrong with it.

Then I sat down.

This lasted longer than I expected.

When I finally left, the third bell was still not close enough to
justify the theatre, so I walked the longer way through Carrow.

A fish seller was throwing water across the stones in front of his
stall. Two dogs objected. A woman had set six clay cups in a row on a
cloth and was arguing with a customer who wanted the seventh cup, which
was apparently not for sale despite being visibly a cup. A man on a
ladder painted the upper half of a sign while another man below
criticized every letter.

I bought a small heel of bread.

I did not buy cheese.

This was restraint.

The third bell began while I was finishing the bread.

I reached the hall before the last strike.

Rinna looked at me from the front table.

"Again."

"What?"

"On time."

"I've become dependable."

"No."

She pushed a folded paper toward me.

I looked at it.

"What's this?"

"Tomorrow."

"That is a day."

"West courtyard. Havel house."

I waited.

Rinna did not continue.

"Am I working?"

"I just told you where."

"That was a location."

"Tomorrow. Second bell. Here first."

"That's also a time."

"Good."

"How much?"

Rinna looked at me.

I smiled.

She looked down at the seat markers.

"Private supper. Four copper for you if you work the whole thing."

I stopped smiling.

Four copper was not silver.

It was also four normal local days arriving at once.

"Four?"

"Yes."

"What am I doing?"

"Whatever survives tomorrow."

"That sounds less specific than the pay."

"It usually is."

She pointed toward the board.

"Today first."

Today said:

BROKEN GATE: VEYA / EVIN / HARA / GREG

WIDOW: VEYA / NESSA / HARA

SHORT: SELLEN / MAREK

GREG: NEIGHBOR / SET

The Neighbor again.

I had been him before.

Four lines, depending on whether we counted the wife.

I preferred to count the wife.

She had the best line and did not have to appear.

"Same Broken Gate?"

"Yes."

"Local?"

Rinna stared.

"Ask Teren."

"I was going to."

"You were asking me."

"I was passing through."

She pointed rearward.

I passed through.

The hall was already half prepared.

Marek was carrying two lamps. Sellen had his own coat today and looked
healthier for it. Pell and Jori were arguing over a length of rope that
neither of them appeared to need yet.

Nessa had three costume pieces laid across a bench and was inspecting a
clasp.

Davin was beside her holding a chair.

It was a very good chair.

That was the problem.

It had a carved back, polished arms, and legs without visible repairs.

I stopped.

"Whose is that?"

"Havel," Rinna said from behind me. "Nobody uses it."

I turned.

"I only asked whose it was."

Davin said, "Sample."

"The tomorrow Havel?"

"Yes."

"Why is their chair here?"

"Sample."

This raised more questions than it answered.

Rinna came around me and put one hand on the polished back.

"They want their own chairs in the playing space."

"People usually do."

"They want one used in the second piece."

Teren appeared from the rear carrying pages.

"No."

Rinna said, "I told them no."

"Good."

I looked between them.

"Why?"

Davin tapped the chair leg.

"Because it costs more than you."

"Individually?"

"Probably."

"That's insulting."

"It isn't ours."

Teren set the pages down.

"The courtyard has tables on three sides. We play the open side. No
painted door. No smoke. Two comic pieces before sweets."

There it was.

Tomorrow had become a job.

Rinna added, "And nothing sad."

Veya, passing with a folded shawl, said, "Then don't let Pell speak."

Pell called from the floor, "I heard that."

"That was the sad part."

Rinna ignored them.

"Havel's man brought the chair so we could see the height. We are not
taking it. We are not touching their furniture during the performance.
We are not moving their tables. We are not using smoke under the
awning."

I raised a hand.

"No."

"I wasn't going to ask about smoke."

"What?"

"Food."

Rinna closed her eyes.

"Workers eat after."

"Included?"

"If you don't irritate the kitchen."

"That's a dangerous condition."

"Then bring bread."

Four copper and possible food.

Tomorrow had become financially significant.

Teren picked up the pages.

"Broken Gate."

"Same version?"

"Yes."

"Four lines?"

"Yes."

"Entrance?"

"Same."

"Good."

Teren looked suspicious.

"That's all?"

"I know the role."

"Then why are we talking?"

Excellent question.

We rehearsed once because the gate had been repaired again.

Not repaired because it was broken.

Repaired because the lower painted brace had started separating from the
frame.

This meant it stood a little straighter than the last time I had
complained beside it.

I took my usual place.

Teren said, "Too close."

"I stood here last time."

"The gate didn't."

I moved six inches.

That was the rehearsal.

My wife still wanted the cart moved.

The municipal drainage was still guilty.

Nothing had improved.

Afterward I carried the gate off with Pell.

He took the heavier side without comment.

My wrist did nothing.

This pleased me enough that I did not mention it.

The private supper began spreading through the hall without becoming a
meeting.

Nessa rejected two costume baskets because tomorrow's pieces only needed
one.

Davin measured the width of the narrow painted table, then rejected it
because the Havel courtyard already had tables and Rinna had just said
we were not moving them.

Jori asked about lamps.

Rinna said the household had wall lamps, but they were bringing four of
their own.

Pell asked whether that meant four lamps total or four extra.

Rinna said, "Four ours."

Pell wrote something on a scrap of board.

I watched him.

"Did you just make a list?"

"No."

"You wrote four lamps."

"That's not a list."

"What is it?"

"Four lamps."

This was the strongest argument I had heard all day.

The customer wanted two funny pieces.

The King was requested.

Of course it was.

Someone at Havel house had apparently seen it before and wanted it
again.

The second piece was still being argued over.

Teren wanted Broken Gate because it traveled light.

Rinna wanted something shorter because the host wanted both pieces
before sweets.

Veya wanted to know how much the host cared about the schedule.

Rinna said, "Enough to pay us."

That ended that part.

Tomorrow's work was not prestige.

It was somebody with a courtyard and money wanting to laugh before
dessert.

I understood this kind of art.

Before today's house opened, we discovered that one of the lamp tins was
nearly empty.

Not tomorrow's lamp tin.

Today's.

Pell shook it beside his ear.

"Enough."

Nessa took it from him, shook it once, and said, "No."

"Same sound."

"Different answer."

"How much is in it?"

"Not enough."

Jori went for oil.

Pell watched him leave.

"I could have done that."

Nessa handed Pell a basket.

"You can do this."

He looked inside.

"What's in it?"

"Things."

He carried it away.

I had begun to think theatre was mostly assigning objects to the person
least able to continue a conversation about them.

Food was scattered.

Marek's dried apples had survived one day and were now almost gone.

Sellen had bread and something that might have been cold beans wrapped
in a leaf.

Hara arrived with no food and stole one dried apple before Marek could
stop her.

"You brought them for us," she said.

"I brought them."

"Yes."

"From north."

"Generous."

Marek looked at me.

I ate my own bread.

"I have no opinion."

"Good."

I almost objected.

I was improving.

Rinna came through with tomorrow's folded paper again.

"Second bell here."

"I know."

"Bring your crutches."

I looked down.

"That was the plan."

"Someone will forget something."

"Why would it be my legs?"

"It won't be now."

She moved on.

Sellen watched her go.

"Four copper?"

I looked at him.

"How do you know?"

"She told me."

"How much are you getting?"

"More than you."

"How much?"

"Enough."

"Everyone here has become Rinna."

"Occupational."

The house opened.

Normal people came in.

A woman with a red scarf sat too close to the aisle and had to move her
basket twice. Two men in the back argued softly until the first piece
started. A child dropped a wooden horse and then spent several minutes
trying to retrieve it without his mother noticing.

I worked the right side for a few minutes, then went back for the
Neighbor coat.

Nessa handed me the brown one.

I put it on.

"Wrong."

I froze.

"You gave it to me."

"I changed my mind."

"This is abuse."

She pulled it off me and handed me a dull green coat.

"Why?"

"Brown catches the gate."

I looked at the sleeve.

There was a loose hook.

"Then fix the hook."

"Later."

"That seems inefficient."

"Green."

I put on green.

This was not my mistake.

My mistake happened two minutes later when I picked up the wrong cup.

There were two cups on the prop table.

One belonged to Broken Gate.

One belonged to Widow.

They were both brown.

One had a chip.

I remembered that the chipped one was ours.

This was true.

Both were ours.

I carried the chipped cup toward the wing.

Nessa said, "Other."

I stopped.

"Of course."

"Chipped is Widow."

"Since when?"

"Today."

"Why?"

"Because I said."

I exchanged them.

No one died.

The correct cup went onstage.

The chipped cup remained for Widow.

I did not ask why Widow needed a damaged cup.

I was becoming a professional.

Broken Gate began.

The gate stood straighter.

Evin's cart remained imaginary.

Veya was irritated before I entered, which was useful because my entire
function was to add one more person with a problem.

"Whose cart is blocking my lane?"

Evin said, "Not mine."

"It has your name on it."

"Then someone has stolen my name."

The laugh was smaller than last time.

Fine.

I continued.

"My wife says move it."

"Tell your wife I am considering it."

"I did. She said consider faster."

Better laugh.

I left.

Backstage, Pell whispered, "Wife is good."

"She has range."

He looked at me.

"Don't."

"That wasn't magic."

"I know. Still."

Apparently Hessa had damaged all of us.

Later the gate came down.

Not actually.

We laid the upper frame at an angle so it looked collapsed from the
house.

I returned.

"I leave for ten minutes."

Hara said, "It was twelve."

"Then this is my fault."

The audience laughed.

The blame moved from Hara to Evin to drainage.

The city survived.

I came offstage and immediately started helping Jori move the frame
because tomorrow it might be going with us.

Teren said, "Maybe."

I stopped.

"Maybe what?"

"Gate tomorrow."

"I thought Broken Gate was the second piece."

"So did I."

"What changed?"

"Nothing yet."

"Then why did you say maybe?"

"Because tomorrow exists."

I hated planning.

Widow went next.

The chipped cup mattered because Hara was supposed to notice it and
accuse Nessa's character of using the dead woman's things.

This made the chip visible from exactly no part of the house.

Veya still made the accusation work.

Nobody needed the chip.

I felt vindicated privately.

The strongest moment belonged to Nessa.

Not because of the cup.

Near the end, Veya demanded to know why the room had been left untouched
for years.

Nessa, playing the landlady, had a line about rent.

She said it simply.

Then she started folding a shawl while Veya continued arguing.

The folding was not in the line.

It was not dramatic.

By the time Veya reached the accusation, Nessa had folded the shawl into
a small square and put it on the dead woman's chair.

The room went quiet.

Hara looked at the shawl.

Then at Nessa.

Her next line was softer.

The audience stayed with them.

I was behind the flat holding the unchipped cup from the previous piece
because I had forgotten to put it away.

This was less useful.

I put it down carefully.

The short piece went last.

Marek and Sellen spent most of it arguing over a missing hat that both
men were wearing at different times.

The audience saw the second hat before Marek did.

The laughter built until he finally touched his head.

He took the hat off.

Looked at it.

Looked at Sellen.

Sellen said, "Found it."

That was enough.

After the house cleared, tomorrow became physical.

Nessa set one costume basket aside.

Then a second smaller one.

Then took three things out of the first and put them in the second.

Davin leaned the gate frame against the wall.

Teren still had not decided whether it was traveling.

Jori marked four lamp tins.

Pell found a length of rope that he actually needed.

Rinna stood at the front with a list.

A real list.

I showed Pell.

He looked offended.

"That's different."

"How?"

"More things."

Fair.

Rinna pointed at me.

"Blue bundle."

"Short or little?"

"Little."

I found the little blue bundle.

It contained folded cloths.

I put it beside the costume basket.

"Tomorrow?"

"Yes."

"What are they?"

"Tomorrow."

"That's not an object."

"It is now."

I accepted this.

I carried two lamp tins to the travel pile.

Then a box of cups.

Then the wrong stool.

Davin stopped me halfway across the room.

"Not that one."

I looked at the stool.

"Why?"

"Loose foot."

"It was in the pile."

"It was near the pile."

I put it back.

This was my second object error of the day.

I decided not to count the cup because Nessa had changed the system.

The stool was mine.

Davin handed me another.

"That one."

I carried it.

No lesson.

Rinna paid me one copper for today.

"Neighbor. Set."

"Private-job preparation."

"Included."

"In today?"

"In your life."

"That is not accounting."

"Tomorrow four."

I looked at the coin in my palm.

Then at tomorrow's pile.

Four lamps.

One costume basket.

Maybe gate.

Little blue bundle.

Cups.

A stool with all its feet.

Four copper.

I could buy several meals with four copper.

I could also fail to earn four copper by spending it in my head before
tomorrow.

I put today's coin away.

"Second bell?"

"Here."

"Not third."

"Correct."

"Schedule changed."

"That's what schedules are for."

This seemed wrong.

I went home.

I bought nothing on the way.

This was easier because I had already eaten bread and because four
imaginary copper had made every real copper feel temporarily suspicious.

Lyssa was not home.

There was still nothing under the cup.

I checked once.

Still mail.

I washed my hands.

There was green paint on the side of my shirt.

I had no idea where it came from.

I rubbed at it with water.

This made the green larger.

I stopped.

Lyssa came in while I was standing beside the basin staring at the
damage.

She looked at my shirt.

"No."

"I didn't ask."

"You were going to."

"I was going to say this happened at work."

"Yes."

"Can it come out?"

"Maybe."

"That's not confidence."

"It's paint."

She put down a parcel.

I did not ask whether it was narrow.

"Private job tomorrow," I said.

"Where?"

"West courtyard. Havel house."

"Who are they?"

"I don't know."

"What are you doing?"

"Whatever survives tomorrow."

"Useful."

"Four copper."

That got her attention.

"Four?"

"Whole job."

"What kind of job?"

"Two funny pieces before sweets. No smoke. Don't touch their chairs."

Lyssa nodded as if these were normal conditions.

"They feed you?"

"After."

"So you'll eat there."

"I'll be working."

"That wasn't my question."

I looked at her.

"Rinna said after."

"Then eat after."

"This household has become very controlling about meals."

"You forget them."

"Once."

Lyssa waited.

"Several times."

She took my shirt sleeve and looked at the paint.

"Nessa doesn't dress you?"

"For the show."

"Then stop painting your own clothes."

"I wasn't painting."

"Strong defense."

We ate the last of the accidental soup with more water and the heel of
bread Lyssa had brought.

It had become less soup and more memory.

No Hessa note came.

No one moved any paper.

Tomorrow I was due at the hall at second bell, which meant the
third-bell schedule had lasted exactly long enough to become adjustable.

The Havel house had a courtyard, expensive chairs, an awning that
disliked smoke, and enough money to make four copper sound ordinary.

I did not know what I would play.

I did know what I would carry.

Probably too much.

That was a job.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 203

## THE WORKHAND

Four copper was enough money to make me early.

Not theatre early.

I had learned.

I was only awake too early.

Lyssa was sitting at the table with a small piece of pale cloth across
one knee and three buttons beside her. I had no idea whether they
belonged together. I had stopped asking questions that created more
cloth.

"You have second bell," she said.

"I know."

"You've said that twice."

"I'm establishing it."

"No."

I ate bread.

There was nothing under the cup.

I did not check until Lyssa left.

Then I checked.

Nothing.

This was still mail.

By the time I reached the hall, I had successfully spent none of the
four copper I did not have.

Rinna was already outside.

"You're early."

"Second bell."

"It hasn't rung."

I looked toward the street as if the bell might be hiding behind a
building.

"Then I'm very dependable."

"No."

Behind her, the handcart was loaded badly.

This was not criticism.

It was simply loaded in a way that made the little blue bundle sit on
top of a lamp tin while one costume basket leaned against the side and
the good stool had been tied upright like a passenger.

Pell stood beside it holding the rope.

"I did that."

"I know."

"How?"

"The stool looks afraid."

Jori came out carrying another lamp.

"Move."

We moved.

The second bell rang while we were tying the last cloth over the cart.

Rinna counted heads.

Marek, Sellen, Hara, Veya, Evin, Nessa, Pell, Jori, Davin, Teren, me.

She counted again.

"Where's the gate?"

Teren said, "Not coming."

I looked at him.

"You decided."

"Yes."

"When?"

"Last night."

"Without me."

"Especially."

"How do I complain about a gate without the gate?"

Teren looked at me.

"The same way you complain about a wife without the wife."

Pell laughed hard enough to lose the end of the rope.

I pointed at Teren.

"That was almost instruction."

"Pull."

We pulled.

The cart went west.

Carrow changed slowly as we walked.

The market streets narrowed first, then opened again where the houses
grew wider and the shop fronts became less crowded. There were still
carts, children, laundry, dogs, people shouting from windows. The
difference was that some of the doors had polished knockers and more of
the upper windows had glass.

The Havel house did not look like a palace.

It looked like somebody had paid to keep repairing it before anything
broke.

Two broad windows faced the street. A narrow covered passage ran beside
the main door. We were sent through that.

Of course we were.

A woman in a dark apron met Rinna before the cart was fully inside.

"You're late."

Rinna looked toward the street.

"We were told second bell."

"Guests came early."

"That doesn't move the bell."

The woman stared at her.

Rinna smiled just enough to keep the money alive.

"I'm Sera. House steward."

"Rinna."

"I know."

Good.

They were going to get along terribly.

The passage opened into a rectangular courtyard.

The awning covered most of it, pale cloth stretched from the house wall
to three wooden poles along the far side. Tables ran along the left,
right, and back edges, leaving an open rectangle in the middle.

A smaller rectangle than promised.

Much smaller.

Guests had not arrived yet, but the tables had.

So had chairs.

Very expensive chairs.

I recognized one.

I pointed.

"Enemy."

Davin caught my wrist before I touched it.

"I wasn't."

"You were pointing too close."

The household wall lamps were already lit even though there was still
daylight above the awning. Four servants moved between courtyard and
kitchen passage carrying cups, folded cloths, bowls, and things that
smelled better than anything I had eaten that week.

Roast onions.

Meat.

Bread.

Something sweet with spice.

Lyssa had told me to eat after.

This suddenly felt unreasonable.

Sera walked Rinna around the playing space.

"Here."

Rinna looked at the floor.

"That is not what you marked."

"We added the small table."

"There are three small tables."

"Family."

"Family can watch from farther away."

Sera looked offended.

Rinna looked employed.

They compromised by moving exactly one chair.

Not a table.

The chair belonged to the house, so Sera moved it herself.

I respected the legal distinction.

The company spread into the only place left.

Backstage became a service passage beside the kitchen, screened from the
courtyard by a hanging woven panel. It was wide enough for two people if
neither person had elbows.

I had elbows.

So did everyone else.

Nessa looked once and reduced us further.

"One basket."

"We brought two," Pell said.

"Now we have one here."

The second basket went back to the cart.

Davin brought the stool in.

Teren looked at the open playing space, then at the stool.

"No throne."

Marek said, "I'm the king."

"You can stand."

"Kings sit."

"Not tonight."

Marek looked at Rinna.

Rinna was still negotiating with Sera.

He looked at the stool.

Then at the expensive Havel chairs.

"No."

"Good," Davin said.

The stool went behind the screen for something else.

Our four lamps were set along the open side, two low, two higher on
simple stands.

Jori lit none yet.

The wall lamps were enough until dusk.

"No smoke," Rinna called.

Jori held up empty hands.

"I know."

Pell said, "We could make very little smoke."

"No."

"I wasn't suggesting."

"You were becoming a suggestion."

I carried the little blue bundle into the service passage and set it
against the wall.

A kitchen worker came through carrying a pot large enough to wash a
child in.

"Move."

I moved.

She passed.

I put the bundle back.

Another kitchen worker came through with a tray.

"Move."

I moved again.

"Where am I supposed to put this?"

Nessa pointed without looking.

There was a narrow shelf above a stack of folded serving cloths.

"That's theirs."

"The shelf isn't."

I put the bundle on it.

Sera appeared at the passage entrance.

"Keep this clear."

"I have received that information."

She looked at the crutches.

Then at me.

"Can you?"

"Mostly."

She left.

Pell said, "Mostly."

"Accurate."

Teren had settled the bill.

The King first.

Broken Gate second.

No gate.

"How broken is it without a gate?" I asked.

"Very," Hara said.

"Good."

We walked the courtyard once.

Not rehearsed.

Walked.

The right entrance at the hall did not exist.

The kitchen passage was now one side. The street passage was the other.
The audience was close enough that if I extended my crutch badly I could
collect a guest.

Teren moved three entrances.

Mine became kitchen side.

"Neighbor comes from there."

"Through the kitchen?"

"Tonight."

"Where does my house live?"

"Outside."

"Which outside?"

Teren pointed.

"That one."

There were four walls.

I accepted one.

Nessa put Marek in the King coat and immediately pulled him back.

"No."

"What?"

"Collar."

"It is my collar."

"It is your sweat."

She changed the inner cloth at the neck.

Guests would be closer than the hall audience. Apparently this made
Marek's neck a public concern.

Sellen watched.

"I never had that problem."

"You had other problems," Nessa said.

He accepted this.

The guests began arriving before we were ready.

Not into seats.

Into dinner.

They entered talking, sat talking, continued talking while food arrived.
Cups knocked against tables. A man near the back laughed at something
that had nothing to do with us.

A child sat on the edge of a step until a woman pulled him into a chair.

Nobody looked at the lamps.

Nobody looked at the screen.

Nobody cared that a king was changing clothes ten feet from their soup.

I liked them.

They were honest.

Rinna came through the passage.

"Earlier."

Teren said, "How much?"

"Now."

"We have ten minutes."

"We have four."

"Why?"

"Fish."

I looked toward the courtyard.

"Fish did this?"

"The fish finished early."

Sera appeared behind Rinna.

"Master Havel would like to begin before the meat."

Teren looked at Rinna.

Rinna looked at the courtyard.

"Five."

Sera said, "Four."

Rinna said, "Five."

Sera left.

"Was that negotiation?" I asked.

"Coat."

Nessa shoved my green Neighbor coat into my chest.

"I don't need this until second."

"Put it where you can find it."

"That seems sensible."

"Don't become frightened."

The King started in five minutes.

Barely.

Marek stood instead of sitting.

The ledger had come.

The stool had not.

Hara and Evin were ready.

Sellen had a smaller court role that did not require Marek's coat, which
improved everyone.

I stayed behind the screen with Jori and the props.

The first lines competed with plates.

Not badly.

Just physically.

A spoon fell.

Someone asked for salt.

A servant crossed the far edge with a covered dish.

Marek waited half a breath before entering because the dish was hot and
the servant had priority.

Then he stepped into the open space.

"Bring me the first complaint."

Three people at the left table were still talking.

Hara entered.

By her second line, two had stopped.

The third was eating.

This seemed fair.

The King worked.

Not like the hall.

The laughs came from different places.

One table reacted quickly. Another seemed to need permission from an
older woman at the end before anyone laughed. A boy near the step
laughed at Evin dropping a rolled paper even though Evin had not meant
to drop it.

Evin looked at the paper.

Then at the boy.

"Evidence."

The boy laughed harder.

Marek said, "Pick up my government."

That got the adults.

Backstage, Pell whispered, "He said evidence."

"Don't."

"Hessa."

"Don't."

The strongest moment came later from Hara.

A guest at the right table said, very clearly, "He's right," while Marek
was refusing a petition.

Hara stopped.

She turned her head toward the voice.

Not all the way.

Just enough.

"Thank you."

The courtyard laughed.

Marek looked at the guest.

"You are not helping."

Bigger laugh.

The guest raised his cup.

Hara returned to Marek.

"One citizen has understood me."

"Then take him with you."

The guest's wife said, "Please."

That was the biggest laugh of the first piece.

Hara waited.

Marek waited.

Then Hara said, "No."

The whole courtyard went.

Behind the screen, Sellen pressed his fist to his mouth.

Teren looked pleased for approximately half a second, then pointed at
the ledger because it was in the wrong place.

I moved it.

The King ended.

We had almost no transition.

Sweets were apparently advancing.

This became obvious when two kitchen workers arrived in the passage
carrying trays.

"Move."

I moved.

Pell moved.

Jori moved a lamp stand.

Nessa did not move because Nessa was pinning Hara's sleeve.

The kitchen workers passed through us like weather.

I reached for my green coat.

It was not there.

I stared at the hook.

Nessa said, "Shelf."

I looked up.

The little blue bundle was there.

The coat was beside it.

I had put it there myself after she told me to put it where I could find
it.

This was insulting.

I put it on.

Broken Gate had no gate.

It had one narrow painted post because Teren had apparently compromised
with himself after saying no gate.

Davin held it upright long enough to decide where it went, then tied it
to a simple base.

From my side it was a post.

From the audience side it was also a post.

Nobody had promised more.

"That's the gate?" I asked.

Teren said, "Tonight."

I was beginning to hate that word.

The second piece started while plates were being cleared.

Veya entered.

Evin followed.

The post survived.

My entrance came from the kitchen side.

I almost went the other way.

Not onstage.

Before.

I turned toward the street passage from habit, took one step, saw Pell's
face, and stopped.

He pointed behind me.

"Tonight."

I pointed at him.

"You're enjoying this."

"Yes."

I reversed.

No one in the courtyard saw.

I entered from the kitchen side.

"Whose cart is blocking my lane?"

The first response was scattered.

A few laughs.

Someone was still moving a plate.

Evin answered.

I continued.

By the time I reached the wife line, the right table had settled.

"My wife says move it."

"Tell your wife I am considering it."

"I did. She said consider faster."

The older woman at the end of the left table laughed first.

Then the rest.

Good wife.

She traveled.

I left through the correct side.

Behind the screen, a kitchen worker handed me a bowl.

I took it.

She stared.

I stared.

"This isn't mine."

"No."

"Why did you give it to me?"

"You were there."

I handed it back.

She carried it away.

Pell whispered, "Professional."

"Shut up."

The later gate collapse was easier because there was almost no gate to
collapse.

Davin tipped the post.

That was the entire disaster.

I returned.

"I leave for ten minutes."

Hara said, "It was twelve."

"Then this is my fault."

The laugh came quickly.

Not huge.

Enough.

The piece continued.

The sweets arrived before we finished.

Not on the tables.

At the passage.

Rows of small pastries dusted with sugar and something brown.

I could smell them.

I had never hated theatre more.

Veya finished the last exchange.

Evin took the blame.

I blamed drainage.

The audience laughed.

We bowed.

Not deeply.

There was nowhere to go.

Then we got out of the way because the pastries were more important.

This was correct.

Packing was faster than setup because everyone wanted food.

The Havel furniture remained untouched.

I would like that recorded.

Not one chair.

Not one table.

The expensive sample chair had survived our entire relationship.

Jori cooled the lamps.

Davin untied the post.

Pell carried the costume basket to the cart.

I put the little blue bundle in the kitchen passage.

"Move."

I picked it up immediately.

The same kitchen worker from earlier looked at me.

"Learning."

"Slowly."

She pointed to the shelf.

I put it there until the passage cleared.

No one congratulated me.

Rinna disappeared with Sera.

They were gone long enough for Pell to become convinced we had been
cheated.

"We haven't been paid."

"The job ended thirty seconds ago."

"That's when payment happens."

"Sometimes people count."

"What?"

"Money."

He looked troubled.

Marek came past carrying the King coat over one arm.

"You worry too early."

"I worry efficiently."

Sellen said, "He saves time later."

Rinna returned with a small purse.

Pell stopped worrying so efficiently.

She paid people one by one.

When she reached me, four copper dropped into my palm.

I counted once.

Four.

Real.

Earned.

Still copper.

Still four.

"Whole job," Rinna said.

"I did the whole job."

"You did."

"I also protected several chairs."

"You didn't touch them."

"Exactly."

"Four."

"Kitchen passage compliance improved over time."

"Four."

"I was given a bowl."

"Why?"

"I don't know."

Rinna stared at me.

"Four."

I put the coins away.

Four was enough.

Then we ate.

Not at the guest tables.

Nobody had promised that.

The kitchen set out food for us in the back passage and near the cart
once the household had moved on to sweets and louder conversation.

There was bread.

A thick stew with meat scraps and onions.

Cooked greens.

Two plates of broken pastries that were apparently too ugly for the
courtyard.

They looked excellent.

Pell reached for two.

Nessa slapped his wrist.

"One."

"There are enough."

"One first."

"This is oppression."

"Eat."

We ate.

Marek had two bowls of stew.

Sellen accused him of storing food for the next northern journey.

Marek said, "My sister fed me."

"Then why are you eating?"

"I'm here now."

Hara took a pastry.

Then another after everyone had one.

Pell saw.

"Law."

Hara bit it.

"No."

Veya sat on the cart edge with her shoes loosened and argued with Teren
about which table had laughed at the gate.

"There was no gate," I said.

"There was a post," Teren said.

"That's not a gate."

"It was enough."

I looked at him.

He was already eating.

I decided not to ask anything that might become education.

Jori said the wall lamps had made ours unnecessary until halfway through
King.

Pell said that meant we carried four for nothing.

Jori said, "We used them."

"Later."

"That's still use."

Davin drank water and said nothing because Davin was a better person
than us.

Rinna came out with one last wrapped pastry.

She looked at the group.

Everyone looked at her.

"No."

She handed it to me.

I took it.

Pell looked betrayed.

"Why?"

Rinna said, "He asked yesterday if they fed us."

"That doesn't explain anything."

"Take it home."

I looked at the wrapped pastry.

"For Lyssa?"

Rinna was already walking away.

I did not know whether this meant yes.

I decided yes was safer than eating it.

The walk home was slower.

The cart was lighter.

We were heavier.

The west streets had gone dark except for lamps over doors and light
from upper windows. Carrow smelled less like dinner as we moved east and
more like wet stone, old smoke, drains, horses, and people closing
shops.

Nobody followed us.

Nobody watched from a doorway.

Nobody delivered a message.

We were tired because we had worked.

At the hall we unloaded what needed unloading.

The costume basket went inside.

The lamps went back.

The post leaned against the wall.

The little blue bundle returned to the same place it had started.

I looked at it.

Pell said, "What?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

Everyone had learned the word.

Rinna said, "Third bell tomorrow."

I stared at her.

"Tomorrow?"

"Yes."

"I worked today."

"That is usually how tomorrow works."

"Guild might send."

"Then tell me."

The schedule survived private employment.

I went home with four copper and one pastry.

Lyssa was awake.

Barely.

She sat on the bed with one foot tucked under her and a piece of thread
between her fingers.

I held out the wrapped pastry.

"I brought you something."

She looked at it.

"Did you steal it?"

"No."

"Good."

"There it is again."

She took the pastry.

"What is it?"

"I don't know."

She unwrapped it.

It was a small folded sweet with nuts inside.

Lyssa bit it.

Then looked at me.

"You ate?"

"Yes."

"What?"

"Stew. Bread. Greens. Pastry."

She narrowed her eyes.

"How much pastry?"

"One."

"Good."

"Stop."

She ate another bite.

I put the four copper on the table.

Not all my money.

Just today's pay.

Lyssa looked at it.

"Four."

"Current evidence supports that."

She pointed the pastry at me.

I stopped.

"Four," I said.

"Better."

"The chairs survived."

"I'm proud of them."

"I didn't touch any."

"Growth."

"Don't."

She asked about the job.

I told her the courtyard had been smaller than promised, the fish had
finished early, the kitchen passage hated me, Marek had been a standing
king, the gate had become one post, Hara had recruited a citizen, and I
had briefly been given a bowl for being present.

Lyssa listened until I got to the bowl.

"Why?"

"I don't know."

"Useful."

"That's what I said."

"No, you didn't."

"I thought it."

There was still nothing under the cup.

I did not check again.

Tomorrow was third bell unless Rinna sent earlier.

Hessa still had an intermediate distance somewhere in her head.

The Havel house had its chairs.

I had four copper.

Lyssa had half a pastry.

I had eaten.

For a private engagement, everything important had survived.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 204

## THE KEEPER

Four copper looked smaller after I slept.

This was probably healthy.

It had spent part of the night on the table because I had been tired
enough to consider the table a financial institution. In the morning I
put it away before Lyssa could point out that tables did not have locks.

She was already dressed.

The last half of the Havel pastry was gone.

I looked at the empty wrapping.

Lyssa looked at me.

"You brought it home."

"I did."

"That was generous."

"I had one."

"Then we're both alive."

I sat down.

My shoulders felt like I had carried a theatre across Carrow, which was
unfair because I had carried mostly cloth, small props, and one lamp
tin. My good leg was tired. My hands were dirty in places I had
apparently failed to wash the night before. My wrist moved normally
until I bent it as far as it would go.

There, at the very end, it was a little stiff.

I stopped.

Lyssa noticed.

"Done?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"I checked once."

"I counted once."

"That's invasive."

She tied a narrow bundle.

I looked at it.

She looked at me.

I did not ask.

This was growth, and I resented it.

There was nothing under the cup.

I checked.

Mail.

Still mail.

Lyssa left before me with the bundle under one arm. She said she might
be late. I asked where.

"Work."

"Specific."

"You'll survive."
