She kissed my cheek on the way past.

Then I had a room, four new copper somewhere not on the table, a
third-bell schedule, and enough time to make poor financial decisions.

I made practical ones instead.

It was terrible.

The lamp oil had been low for several nights. Not empty. Low enough that
we had both started trimming the wick as if precision could manufacture
fuel.

I took the little household oil flask with me.

Carrow was already moving.

Yesterday I had crossed half the city with actors and a handcart. Today
nobody cared.

A woman was washing the front stones of a bakery. Two boys carried a
plank between them and argued about which one was walking crooked. A man
with a basket of onions had stopped in the middle of the lane to
negotiate with someone in an upstairs window.

I went to the oil seller.

He took our flask, held it to the light, and said, "Small."

"It fits the lamp."

"Still small."

"I don't need to light the city."

He filled it and set it down beside a twist of coarse salt and two
little packets of lamp wick.

"One copper."

"For oil?"

"For oil and wick."

"I didn't ask for wick."

"You need wick."

I looked at our old wick.

I had brought it because I had apparently become the kind of person who
carried evidence to an oil seller.

He looked at it.

"You need wick."

"Fine."

One Havel copper disappeared.

Not all of it.

Just one.

This was how civilization worked.

I bought food next.

Not much.

A small piece of meat with more bone than ambition, a bunch of greens,
and two onions. The seller wanted one copper for the lot.

I stared.

"You want it?"

"Yes."

"Then one."

"I worked very hard yesterday."

"Good."

"That should make your food cheaper."

"It makes you able to buy it."

I paid.

Two Havel copper were now oil, wick, meat, greens, and onions.

This felt less like spending and more like being attacked by a
household.

I carried everything home first.

The oil flask went beside the lamp.

The wick went beside it.

The food went where Lyssa would find it.

I left the meat wrapped.

Then I still had time before third bell.

I sat.

That was getting easier.

Not emotionally.

Physically.

Third bell eventually arrived because bells were more dependable than
actors.

I reached the hall while the last sound was still hanging over the
roofs.

Rinna was inside.

She looked at me.

"Third bell."

"I know."

"I was confirming."

"No."

Yesterday's private engagement was spread across the hall like the
remains of a minor military campaign.

The handcart stood near the rear door. One wheel had dried mud along the
rim. Two lamp tins sat on the floor instead of their shelf. The painted
post from Broken Gate leaned against a wall with its traveling base
still tied on. Costume cloth hung over three chair backs. A coil of rope
had become something between a circle and an accusation.

Pell was inside the costume basket.

Not entirely.

His upper half.

"Why?"

"My knife."

"Did the basket take it?"

"I put it somewhere safe."

"Then this seems deserved."

Nessa came through carrying the little blue bundle.

"Blue."

"Little?"

"Open."

She handed it to me.

I untied it.

Inside were folded cloths.

Blue cloth outside.

Gray and brown cloth inside.

Nothing else.

I looked at Nessa.

"I carried cloth across half Carrow to protect other cloth."

"Yes."

"And lamps."

"Some."

"This is just cloth."

"Most cloth is."

I unfolded one.

It had soot on one edge.

Another had a pale grease mark.

Protective wraps.

For lamp parts, buckles, anything that should not rub against anything
else in the cart.

I had moved them out of a kitchen passage repeatedly.

Nessa pointed.

"Gray shelf. Brown basket. Blue back."

I started sorting.

Pell emerged from the costume basket holding his knife.

"Found it."

"Where?"

"Safe."

"Good."

He left before I could ask what that meant.

I put the gray protective cloths on the gray shelf.

Nessa came back.

"Other gray."

I looked at the shelf.

There were two.

Of course there were.

"Why?"

"One is clean."

I looked at the cloth in my hand.

It had soot on it.

I moved it.

No one learned anything.

The brown wraps went into the brown basket.

The blue outer cloth went back around the clean folded pieces that
stayed together.

The entire mystery was laundry with organizational problems.

I liked it less than I expected.

Davin was working on one of the lamp tins.

The cap had bent during travel.

I picked up the other three and started putting them away.

Davin held up the bent tin.

"Leave this one."

"The one you're holding?"

"Yes."

"Good system."

I put the three good tins away.

The fourth stayed with Davin.

Rinna passed with a stack of tickets.

"They paid," she said.

"Yes."

"Good."

That was the entire Havel review.

I followed her.

"Yesterday established a rate."

She kept walking.

"For Havel."

"I was the same person yesterday."

"Unfortunately."

"Four copper."

"Yesterday."

"What am I today?"

She stopped at the board.

"House."

I looked.

The board said:

KING: MAREK / HARA / EVIN

PETITION: VEYA / SELLEN / EVIN

WIDOW: VEYA / NESSA / HARA

SHORT: SELLEN / MAREK

GREG: HOUSE / SET

"No role."

"No."

"One copper?"

"If you work."

"Yesterday I worked."

"Then yesterday's money was very lucky."

She handed me the tickets.

"Front until the first piece starts. Then rear. Set between."

"Four copper."

"One."

"Current evidence..."

Rinna raised one finger.

I stopped.

"Hessa has damaged negotiations."

"One."

I took the tickets.

The hall smelled like yesterday again.

Not Havel yesterday.

Our yesterday.

Old boards, dust, lamp oil, cloth, dried paint, people.

The private courtyard had left mud on the cart wheel and soot on a
protective wrap. That was most of what remained.

Nessa had Hara's Widow shawl over one arm and was brushing something
from the hem.

"What is that?"

"Dirt."

"From Havel?"

"Probably."

"So we brought some of their courtyard home."

Nessa looked at me.

"Brush."

I took the brush.

Apparently I was now helping dirt leave.

At the front, Rinna showed me the seating rows for the afternoon.

Normal arrangement.

No private tables.

No expensive chairs.

No fish dictating the start time.

The first audience arrived while I was still counting tickets.

A woman with two children.

Then an older man who wanted the aisle.

Then three workers who smelled like cut wood and argued over which piece
they had seen before.

I counted once.

Then again because the first count was wrong.

Rinna held out her hand.

"How many?"

"Eleven."

"Tickets?"

"Ten."

I looked at the people.

One of the children was sitting on the other child's lap.

"Eleven people."

"Ten seats."

"That feels like fraud."

"Child's small."

The mother heard me.

"He is."

The child looked offended.

I gave Rinna ten.

She gave me the look that meant I had turned arithmetic into a social
problem.

I returned to the door.

By the time the first piece began, the house was not full.

It was not empty either.

It was a local afternoon.

People sat where they wanted until Rinna told them otherwise.

Someone coughed before the first line.

A bench creaked.

Pell whispered from behind the rear flat, "Davin."

Davin whispered, "Later."

Marek went on as King.

I went to the rear.

No role.

This felt strange for almost a minute.

Then Jori handed me a stool.

"Left."

I moved it left.

A folded petition needed to be on the table.

I put it there.

Evin needed the small brown cup.

I found the correct one on the second try.

The King ran.

I heard Hara get the first laugh.

Marek got another.

A man in the third row sneezed during a pause and said, "Sorry."

Marek looked toward him.

"So am I."

The audience laughed.

Marek continued.

Not the strongest moment.

That came in Petition.

Veya was arguing with Sellen over a request to repair a public well that
apparently belonged to three different offices and no one.

Sellen, playing the clerk who did not want the problem, kept trying to
hand the petition back.

Veya refused to take it.

He put it on the table.

She pushed it toward him.

He pushed it back.

The paper slid off the edge.

Neither of them looked down.

Sellen said, "You have dropped your complaint."

Veya said, "No. The town has."

The house laughed.

Sellen looked at the floor.

Then at her.

"Then the town may pick it up."

Veya folded her hands.

"No authority."

That got the biggest laugh of the afternoon.

Backstage, Pell made a noise into his sleeve.

I did not think about paper moving.

It had been pushed by a hand.

This was a normal thing paper did.

I was proud of myself for not needing a meeting about it.

Between Petition and Widow, I moved the wrong stool.

Not the Havel stool.

The normal hall stool.

There were two normal hall stools.

One had a slightly shorter leg and belonged behind the Widow table
because the uneven floor there made it sit level.

I carried the level stool instead.

Nessa saw it.

"Other."

I stopped.

"Why?"

"Floor."

I looked at the floor.

It was the same floor it had been for weeks.

I had apparently never needed to know this.

I exchanged stools.

"How many secret systems does this building have?"

Nessa was tying her landlady apron.

"All of them."

Then she went onstage.

Widow happened.

The chipped brown cup from yesterday's hall show was back where it
belonged.

No one ate anything meant to be a prop.

No one moved a piece of expensive furniture because we did not own any.

I worked rear.

At one point a lamp wick guttered.

Jori fixed it.

I held nothing.

This was also work.

The short piece went on last.

Sellen and Marek had their hats.

Both hats.

Pell checked twice.

I asked why.

"Yesterday."

"The hats didn't go to Havel."

"Exactly."

I did not understand.

He seemed satisfied.

The show ended.

People left.

One of the wood-smelling men told Rinna the well piece was good.

Rinna said, "Yes."

He waited.

She waited.

He left.

I swept under the third row.

There were seed shells, one broken bit of string, and a little wooden
bead.

The bead went into the lost-things cup.

The string went away.

Pell claimed the seed shells were not his.

"No one accused you."

"I know."

"Then why?"

"Evidence."

I pointed the broom at him.

"No."

He grinned.

The Havel cart rope finally got coiled properly.

Jori did it.

The painted post lost its traveling base and went back with the rest of
Broken Gate.

Davin set the bent lamp cap aside for tomorrow.

Nessa finished restoring the costume basket.

By the time Rinna paid me, the hall mostly looked like itself again.

One copper.

I held it.

"Yesterday..."

"No."

"I hadn't started."

"You had."

"One copper for house and set."

"Yes."

"Ticket arithmetic."

"Included."

"Stool correction."

"That was your mistake."

"Then correction should be extra."

"Do you want less?"

"No."

"Good."

She turned away.

"Third bell tomorrow."

"I know."

"Good."

This was getting oppressive.

I went home with one new copper after having spent two of yesterday's.

This was not a ledger.

I refused to calculate further.

Lyssa was home before me.

The lamp was burning.

Brighter.

I stopped in the doorway.

She looked at me.

"Oil."

"Yes."

"You bought oil."

"I did."

"And wick."

"I was forced."

"By?"

"An expert."

She looked toward the food.

The meat was no longer wrapped.

"Meat."

"Also forced."

"Same expert?"

"Different criminal."

Lyssa smiled.

"Four copper lasted well."

"Some of it still exists."

"I didn't ask."

"Good."

A folded note sat under the cup.

I saw it.

Lyssa saw me see it.

"That came after I got home."

"You didn't read it?"

"It has your name."

Hessa did not usually need my name.

Apparently the cup had become enough of an address.

I lifted it.

The note was short.

TWO DAYS.

AFTER MIDDAY.

INTERMEDIATE.

AWAY ONLY.

SAME APPARATUS.

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

I read it once.

Then put it down.

Lyssa waited.

"Two days."

"I can read."

"That was rude."

"You were going to read it to me."

"I was sharing."

"Paper?"

"Intermediate."

"Which means?"

"Between near and far."

"That seems suspiciously like what the word means."

"Hessa is very technical."

Lyssa put the meat into a small pot with one onion and some of the
greens.

I added water.

She added less water.

Apparently accidental soup had rules now.

"Tomorrow?" she asked.

"Theatre. Third bell."

"And the day after?"

"Theatre until I tell Rinna otherwise. Hessa after midday."

"So tell Rinna tomorrow."

"That system is becoming annoyingly functional."

"Good."

I looked at the note again.

Intermediate.

Away only.

Same apparatus.

Same restrictions.

No new question hiding inside it.

Just the next position between two positions we already knew.

I did not ask anything to move.

I did not need to.

The lamp burned steadily with oil I had paid for.

The meat smelled better than our usual dinner.

There was still bread.

Yesterday had been four copper.

Today had been one.

Some of yesterday had become light and food before I reached the hall.

Tomorrow I was expected at third bell.

In two days Hessa expected me after midday.

The schedule had moved again.

The room had not.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 205

## THE PORTER

The soup was better the second day.

This was suspicious.

I ate bread dipped into what remained of the broth while Lyssa stood by
the basin tying her hair back. There was less meat than yesterday
because we had eaten it. There were more onions because onions
understood survival.

The new wick burned cleanly in the lamp even though daylight made the
lamp unnecessary.

I had lit it anyway.

Lyssa looked at it.

"We have oil."

"I know."

"It's morning."

"I know."

She waited.

"I wanted to see if the wick worked."

"It did last night."

"That was one observation."

She pointed her spoon at me.

I blew out the lamp.

"Hessa has damaged this household."

"Hessa isn't here."

"That's how extensive the damage is."

My wrist was boring.

I moved it while reaching for the bread and did not think about it until
after. That seemed like the proper definition of boring. My shoulders
were less tired. My good leg still knew about Havel House, but only in
the way legs remembered work when stairs appeared.

The note remained under the cup.

TWO DAYS had become tomorrow without anyone rewriting it.

I did not lift it.

I knew what it said.

Lyssa picked up her bundle.

"Paper tomorrow?"

"After midday."

"Theatre?"

"Third bell today."

"Tomorrow?"

"I have to tell Rinna."

"So you don't know."

"I know one half."

"That's not a schedule."

"You're becoming Rinna."

Lyssa opened the door.

"Go to work."

"It isn't third bell."

"Then eventually go to work."

She left.

I had enough time to clean the bowl, not enough desire to sweep, and no
household purchase requiring intervention.

I considered visiting the hall early.

Then I remembered Rinna had created the third-bell system specifically
to prevent me from appearing whenever anxiety became employment.

I stayed home.

This was becoming a skill.

By the time I left, Carrow had warmed into the ordinary part of the day.

A man was repairing a basket on a doorstep. A woman carried three live
chickens by their feet and told one of them to stop complaining. It did
not. Two boys rolled a hoop down the lane until it struck a barrel, at
which point they blamed the barrel.

I understood their legal position.

The hall door was open when third bell rang.

I entered on the last strike.

Rinna was changing the board.

"Guild tomorrow," I said.

She kept writing.

"After midday."

She erased WIDOW.

I waited.

"Did you hear me?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"Tomorrow, first bell."

I looked at her.

"That's earlier."

"Yes."

"Because Guild?"

"Yes."

"This system has become hostile."

Rinna wrote GREG beneath the morning line.

"First bell. Set. Then leave before midday."

"What happens after Guild?"

"If you're useful, come back."

"How will I know?"

"You'll be here."

That did not answer anything.

It also probably did.

I looked at today's board.

KING: MAREK / HARA / EVIN

BORROWED HOUSE: VEYA / EVIN / PELL / GREG

SHORT: SELLEN / MAREK

WIDOW had been erased.

Under it, in smaller writing, Rinna had added:

IF HARA.

I pointed.

"What does that mean?"

"It means if Hara."

"That's not a sentence."

"It is today."

Hara had outside work.

No one knew when she would finish.

If she arrived in time, Widow would go last.

If not, it would not.

This was apparently enough information to operate a theatre.

My line said:

GREG: PORTER / SET

I knew Porter.

Not perfectly.

Enough.

The Porter in The Borrowed House brought a chest to the wrong house and
then spent several lines refusing to accept that the address had become
his problem.

I had played him before.

I had liked him.

He had procedural integrity.

Rinna handed me a stack of tickets.

"Front."

"Porter."

"Later."

"Set."

"Also later."

"One copper?"

She looked at me.

"I was confirming."

"No."

I went to the front.

Davin had the bent lamp cap on his work table.

It was round again.

Mostly.

I stopped.

"Fixed?"

"Yes."

I reached.

"No."

My hand stopped in the air.

"I was going to look."

"You are looking."

"I was going to look closer."

"No."

He picked up the lamp tin himself, turned the cap once, and set it on
the normal shelf.

"Fixed."

"Thank you for the demonstration."

Davin returned to his tools.

Pell came past carrying the Borrowed House chest.

The chest had been repaired recently too.

I did not touch it.

Davin looked at me anyway.

"I didn't."

"Good."

"This reputation is oppressive."

Pell shifted the chest.

"Help."

"I thought I wasn't allowed to touch repaired things."

"You're allowed to carry them."

"That seems inconsistent."

"Other side."

I took the lighter end because Pell had already angled it that way.

We moved the chest to the wing.

The Borrowed House set was familiar enough that most of the work
happened without anyone explaining it.

Painted wall.

Chair.

Small table.

Chest.

A cloth loaf that was not currently involved in anything and therefore
made me nervous.

I left it alone.

Teren arrived, looked at the board, looked at me, and said, "Porter."

"Yes."

"Entrance?"

"Left."

"Today?"

He looked annoyed.

I smiled.

"Left."

"Chest?"

"With Pell until wing. Then me for the last steps."

"Lines?"

"Six."

"Five."

I frowned.

"Which one died?"

"The address line."

"Why?"

"Too long."

"That line explains why I'm there."

"No one cares why you're there."

"I care."

"Five."

He walked away.

This was rehearsal.

I found my page.

Five lines.

The crossed-out address line had once been useful to me. I mourned
privately.

The first early audience member arrived while the board still said King
first.

He was a narrow man with a red scarf who asked what opened.

"The King."

"Good."

He bought a seat.

Then Evin came in through the rear door and told Rinna he had to leave
before the second bell after the show because his cousin had a cart
waiting.

Rinna looked at the board.

Teren looked at the board.

Sellen looked at Evin.

"Why?"

"Cousin."

"That isn't why."

"It's enough."

Apparently everyone had learned Rinna's grammar.

The bill changed.

Borrowed House moved first because Evin needed to leave.

King moved second.

Short stayed third.

Widow remained IF HARA.

I looked toward the door.

The man with the red scarf had already gone inside.

I found him in the second row.

"Correction."

He looked up.

"Borrowed House opens."

He shrugged.

"Fine."

"I previously said King."

"I remember."

"The board changed."

"Will there still be a king?"

"Yes."

"Fine."

He returned to eating roasted seeds.

I stood there another second.

He looked up again.

"Anything else?"

"No."

This was the correct amount of concern.

At the rear, Nessa was holding my Porter coat.

It was brown.

Everything was brown eventually.

I put it on.

She pulled the collar flat.

"Other side."

"What?"

"Fasten other side."

I had started the wrong hook.

I changed it.

"How did I wear this before?"

"Badly."

"That seems unsupported."

Nessa walked away.

The house filled to ordinary.

Not full.

Enough.

Warm bodies made the room warmer than the street. Someone had brought
onions wrapped in paper. Someone else had brought a baby who was
currently asleep and therefore the most professional person in the
building.

I worked front until Rinna took the tickets from me.

"Go."

Borrowed House first.

I got to the wing.

Pell had the chest.

"Ready?"

"Yes."

"Don't drop it."

"I wasn't planning to."

"Good plan."

Veya and Evin were already onstage arguing about the house.

The premise remained stupid.

Evin had borrowed a house from someone who had apparently not intended
the phrase literally enough. Veya believed the furniture inside proved
ownership. Pell, playing the neighbor, believed all disputes should
happen outside his door.

My cue came.

Pell and I moved the chest to the edge.

Then I took the visible part of the weight for the last few steps.

Both hands on the chest.

Crutches staged where I could reach them after.

Good foot.

Step.

Set.

No heroics.

No wrist complaint.

I delivered the first line.

"Delivery for this house."

Nothing.

Evin said, "Which house?"

"This one."

Small laugh.

He pointed at the painted wall.

"This isn't my house."

"I was told this house."

"By whom?"

The old address line would have answered this.

It was dead.

I used the current line.

"By the man who paid me."

That got a better laugh.

Veya said, "Then take it back."

"I was paid to bring it."

"Then you've brought it."

"Yes."

"Good."

I stood.

The Porter had no further solution.

That was the joke.

The audience understood before the characters did.

My next line got almost nothing.

Then Pell opened the chest from behind and found it empty.

His face did more work than the line.

The house laughed.

I kept being a Porter.

Eventually I left.

Backstage, I reached for my crutches.

Nessa was holding one.

"You left it too far."

"I staged it."

"You staged it for someone with two legs."

I took it.

"That seems discriminatory."

"Move."

King had to set.

No lesson.

No discussion.

I changed out of the Porter coat while Pell and Jori shifted the chest.

Marek was already in his King coat.

Hara was not there.

Rinna looked once toward the rear door and then stopped looking.

King went on without caring.

I carried the small table off and put the King stool on.

Correct stool.

I checked.

Not experimentally.

With my eyes.

The King ran cleanly.

Marek's first complaint got a laugh.

Evin, now playing a petitioner before leaving for his cousin's cart,
rushed one line badly enough that Marek stared at him.

"Are you fleeing?"

"Eventually."

That was not the line.

The audience laughed.

Marek said, "Then state your problem before it escapes with you."

Evin slowed down.

The scene continued.

Not the strongest moment.

That came from Sellen in the short piece.

Hara still had not arrived.

Rinna erased IF HARA.

Widow disappeared.

No announcement.

No apology.

The audience had not been promised it by name.

They got Short instead.

Sellen and Marek were doing the hat piece again, but one of the hats had
lost its ribbon.

Nessa had removed the loose ribbon before the show rather than let it
fall off during.

Sellen came on, saw Marek's hat, looked at his own bare hat, and said,
"Yours has rank."

Marek said, "Yours has survived."

Sellen took off his hat, inspected it, and put it back on backward.

The audience laughed.

He continued the scene with it backward.

Marek did not mention it.

That made it worse.

By the time Sellen finally discovered the problem through a polished
serving plate used as a mirror, half the audience had been waiting for
him to notice.

He stared at himself.

Then at Marek.

"You knew."

Marek said, "I am the king."

Biggest laugh.

Sellen bowed with the backward hat.

"Then perhaps call more clearly."

I made a noise backstage.

Marek looked toward the wing for half a second.

Sellen nearly broke.

They finished.

Afterward Sellen came off and pointed at me.

"Yours."

"No."

"You said it."

"Yesterday."

"Still yours."

"You stole my line."

"I improved its position."

"Current evidence..."

He raised a hand.

"Don't."

Good.

Hara arrived while the audience was leaving.

She came through the rear door carrying a narrow paper-wrapped parcel
and wearing a dark outer coat I had not seen before.

Rinna looked at her.

"Done?"

"No."

"Then why are you here?"

"Because I thought I could make Widow."

"You couldn't."

"I see that."

Hara looked at the erased board.

"Good."

That was the entire crisis.

She put the parcel under her own things.

Pell immediately looked at it.

"No."

"I didn't ask."

"You were going to."

"I was going to look."

Hara stared.

Pell left.

I did not ask what was in it.

This was spreading.

Evin had already gone.

No one said goodbye because his cousin apparently had a cart and that
was enough.

Cleanup began.

The repaired lamp tin worked.

Jori filled it only partway because it did not need more.

Davin watched the cap once and then stopped caring.

The Borrowed House chest went back to storage.

I carried one end.

The cloth loaf remained untouched.

This felt like an achievement I could not bill.

At the front, the red-scarf man passed me on his way out.

"King was good."

"Borrowed House opened."

"I noticed."

"I corrected you."

"You did."

"Good."

He left.

I was beginning to understand why Rinna did not explain herself to
customers.

Hara unwrapped her parcel enough to reveal two small wooden handles.

I still did not know what they were for.

She handed one to Nessa.

Nessa said, "Wrong size."

Hara said something rude under her breath and wrapped them again.

Their lives continued without my understanding.

I swept.

Pell and Jori argued over a rope coil.

Pell said Jori had coiled it backward.

Jori asked which end of a circle was forward.

Pell said that was not the point.

I stayed out of it because tomorrow I had a professional appointment
involving directions and did not need recreational ones.

Rinna paid me one copper.

I took it.

No negotiation.

She looked at me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You look ill."

"I've matured."

"No."

I put the copper away.

"Tomorrow. First bell."

"Set."

"Yes."

"Then I leave before midday."

"Yes."

"Guild after midday."

"I know."

"And after?"

Rinna was counting something on her fingers.

"Come back if you can still stand."

"That's not a time."

"It's not a promise."

Fair.

I went home.

The room smelled like yesterday's soup because tonight's dinner was
yesterday's soup with more water and the second onion.

Lyssa was not home yet.

The lamp lit easily.

I did not need to trim the wick three times.

I put Hessa's note on top of the cup instead of under it.

Tomorrow.

Not because the note had changed.

Because the day had.

Lyssa came in after dark with nothing in her hands.

This was unusual enough that I looked.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You usually have cloth."

"I put it down elsewhere."

"Can you do that?"

"Apparently."

She took off her outer layer and sat.

I gave her soup.

She tasted it.

"More water."

"Yes."

"Less meat."

"Time."

"Still good."

We ate.

I told her Hara had arrived too late for her own play and seemed
pleased.

Lyssa said, "Maybe she didn't want to do it."

"Then why come?"

"Work."

"That explains nothing."

"It explains most things."

I told her Evin left for a cousin's cart.

Sellen stole my line.

Marek helped.

The Porter remained committed to delivery.

Rinna had scheduled me first bell.

Lyssa stopped eating.

"Tomorrow?"

"Yes."

"I thought Guild was after midday."

"It is."

"So theatre first."

"Yes."

"Then Guild."

"Yes."

"And maybe theatre again."

"Apparently."

She looked at me.

"So everyone knows your schedule."

"I told you I knew it."

"What are you doing at Guild?"

"Intermediate."

"Away."

"Yes."

"Same apparatus."

"Yes."

"Same restrictions."

I narrowed my eyes.

"You've been reading my mail."

"You read it out loud."

"That is a security flaw."

Lyssa ate another spoonful.

"Are you excited?"

"A little."

"Nervous?"

"A little."

"Good."

"Why is that good?"

"Because if you said no to either, you'd be lying."

I considered arguing.

I did not.

The note sat on the cup.

I did not touch it again.

Twenty-eight draws.

Twenty-four shaping attempts.

Twenty-seven external attempts.

Those numbers had not changed today.

Today I had carried a chest, worn a brown coat, given one customer
outdated information, watched a play disappear because Hara had another
life, earned one copper, and come home for watered soup.

Tomorrow I had theatre at first bell.

After midday, paper.

For once, both jobs had agreed on the order.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 206

## THE SETTER

First bell was offensive.

It was not early in any absolute sense. Bakers had been awake. Carters
had been awake. Somewhere, people with livestock had probably been awake
long enough to develop opinions about livestock.

It was early for theatre.

I arrived while Pell was opening the rear shutters.

He looked at me.

"You live here now?"

"Rinna told me first bell."

"That doesn't answer the question."

Rinna was already inside.

She had three benches pulled crooked across the floor, two folded cloths
on the front table, and the expression of someone who had arranged work
specifically so no one could claim not to see it.

"You're here," she said.

"I was scheduled."

"Good."

"That's almost praise."

"Bench."

I took the near end of the first one.

Pell took the other.

We moved it two rows forward because the afternoon bill needed a wider
rear path. Then we moved another one back because apparently the first
movement had revealed a different problem.

"Why didn't we leave the first one?" I asked.

Rinna pointed.

There was a floor mark under it.

"What is that?"

"Where the Widow table goes."

"Widow didn't play yesterday."

"Today isn't yesterday."

This institution had a phrase for everything.

By the time Nessa arrived, we had shifted six benches, moved the
Borrowed House chest to the rear wall, and brought down a narrow painted
panel I did not remember seeing in at least a week.

Nessa looked at it.

"No."

Pell stopped carrying.

"What?"

"Wrong panel."

"It was beside the right panel."

"That doesn't make it right."

We put it back.

The right panel was behind it.

I had been at work less than an hour and had already moved furniture
twice for reasons that stopped existing after I moved it.

This seemed healthy preparation for Guild work.

Davin came in carrying a small wooden brace.

He fitted it under the edge of the King platform, pressed once, then stood.

I watched.

"Leave the brace," he said.

"I wasn't touching it."

"Good."

He walked away.

My wrist was boring.

My shoulder was boring.

My hands were dirty.

Hessa would ask about all three.

Around midmorning, Rinna gave me bread because I had brought none.

I looked at it.

"Is this pay?"

"No."

"Then why?"

"Because you're leaving."

"People who leave don't need food?"

"Eat."

I ate.

Teren arrived, changed two names on the afternoon board, and asked why
the narrow painted panel was out.

"It isn't," Pell said.

Teren looked at the panel we had returned to storage.

"Good."

I finished the bread.

No one rehearsed me.

I had no role.

I moved a stool, two lamp tins, a basket of folded coats, and one bundle
Nessa refused to identify because I had asked while she was carrying
pins in her mouth.

Before midday, Rinna looked toward the light at the front door.

"Go."

"I'm going."

"Then go faster."

I picked up my crutches.

Pell said, "Paper."

"Eventually."

"Middle paper."

"Intermediate."

He stared at me.

"Hessa."

"Right."

I left before he could improve the experiment.

The Guild was busier than the theatre and quieter about it.

A woman at the front table had three small brass plates laid in a row
and was arguing that the middle one had not been scratched when she
brought it. The clerk had apparently not accused her of scratching it.

"I am only saying it wasn't."

"I know."

"It was polished."

"I know."

"Look."

"I am looking."

I passed them.

A boy carried a wooden case down the side corridor with both arms and
nearly walked into a man carrying a longer wooden case. They negotiated
this by stopping and looking offended.

The gray-coated woman was at a side table.

She saw me.

"Paper Man."

"Intermediate Paper Man."

She frowned.

"That sounds worse."

"It is more precise."

"That is worse."

I kept walking.

Hessa had the smaller room ready.

The same long pale board sat on the broad table.

The same chair was aligned to the chalk mark on the floor.

The glass shield waited beside matched folded fins.

The near and far positions were still marked on the board.

There was a third mark between them.

Not exactly halfway by my eye.

I pointed.

"Intermediate."

"Yes."

"Why isn't it halfway?"

"Halfway between what?"

"The other two."

"The marks."

"Those are positions, not a scale."

I looked at the board.

"Then how did you choose that?"

Hessa tapped one of the shallow board divisions.

"It is a fixed position between the previous two. We can reproduce it."

"That is a disappointing answer."

"It is the useful one."

She looked at my sleeves.

"Work?"

"Theatre."

"Since first bell?"

"Yes."

"What did you lift?"

"Benches. One end. Lamp tins. Stool. Coats. Wrong panel. Right panel."

"Pain?"

"No."

"Wrist?"

"Boring."

"Show me."

I rotated it.

She watched the full movement, then had me do it again more slowly.

"Shoulder?"

"Boring."

"That is not a measurement."

"It is becoming one."

She pressed lightly along the shoulder and upper arm, then had me lift
and lower the arm.

Nothing meaningful hurt.

My good leg was tired from standing.

My hands had the ordinary grit of set work in the creases.

Hessa wrote something.

"Eat?"

"Rinna forced bread on me."

"Water?"

I hesitated.

Hessa looked up.

"I drank this morning."

"How much?"

"Some."

She gave me water.

I drank it.

"This experiment has a lot of water."

"Your body has a lot of water."

"Currently."

She waited.

I drank more.

Then the room became familiar.

Crutches against the wall.

Good foot flat.

Chair on its mark.

Hands resting where she wanted them.

No bracing against the table.

Board fixed.

Target and control guides matched.

Glass shield.

Same paper stock.

Same folds.

Same requested direction.

Away from me.

Only the position had changed.

Intermediate.

Hessa pointed to the near mark, then the new one, then the far mark.

"We are not testing near."

"Yes."

"We are not testing far."

"Yes."

"We are not testing whether you can produce a larger movement."

"Yes."

"We are not testing a different object."

"I know."

"What are we testing?"

"Whether the paper at the intermediate position responds to the same
request."

"Good."

"That sounded like praise."

"It wasn't."

Everyone had coordinated.

We began with the draw.

The gathering came more easily than it had months ago, which did not
mean easily.

I settled.

Breathed.

Found the familiar inward pull without reaching after it.

There was the small heat behind my sternum, the sense of something
collecting where I had learned not to grab at it, and the familiar
unevenness near the wrist edge.

Hessa watched my hands and shoulders more than my face.

"Hold."

I held.

"Release."

I did.

She wrote.

Twenty-nine successful supervised minimal draws.

Nothing new.

That was apparently success.

The shaping attempt came next.

I gathered again only when told.

This time I tried to give the gathered thing the rough deliberate form
we had been working with.

It wanted to crowd toward the same wrist-side edge.

My shoulder tried to become involved.

I noticed.

Lowered it.

The shape softened.

I nearly chased it.

"Don't."

"I wasn't."

"You were about to."

"That is not evidence."

"It is when I can see you."

I let the shape remain ugly.

It held for two breaths.

Then I released.

Twenty-five supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Also nothing new.

My reward was paper.

Hessa placed the first matched pair at the intermediate mark.

Target in its guide.

Control beside it.

Both under the glass.

I could see the cut edges clearly enough, but not as easily as at near.

Far had made the little fins feel almost absurdly remote.

This was not remote.

It was simply less close.

I disliked that this was now a meaningful category in my life.

"Same request," Hessa said.

"Away."

"Same effort."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"No."

"Good."

I looked at the target.

Not the control.

Not the glass.

Not Hessa.

I gathered only as much as I had been taught to gather.

The wrist-side pressure appeared.

I did not correct it.

I shaped.

Rough.

Small.

Deliberate enough.

Then I made the request.

Away.

Nothing happened immediately.

I held.

The lower cut edge shifted.

Not much.

It slid away from me by less than the width of the smallest part of my
fingernail, then stopped with the top edge turned slightly.

I released.

Hessa did not speak.

She leaned close to the board.

Then she moved around the table and looked from the other side.

She checked the control.

She checked the glass.

"Target displaced away."

I smiled.

"Small."

I stopped smiling less.

"Control unchanged."

"Intermediate."

"Attempt twenty-eight."

"Intermediate."

"Yes."

I wanted her to say more.

She did not.

She wrote.

The first response had been clean.

That was all it was allowed to be.

We rested.

I drank water.

Hessa had another sheet beside her notes with writing that had nothing
to do with me. She read part of it while I sat.

Someone knocked once.

A clerk opened the door far enough to ask about a prepared frame.

"Not mine," Hessa said.

"Whose?"

"Ask Soren."

"He's out."

"Then it remains not mine."

The door closed.

I liked the Guild more when it had other problems.

After the rest, Hessa replaced both fins.

Fresh matched pair.

Same intermediate position.

Same guides.

Same glass.

She checked my shoulder again.

"Fine."

"Wrist?"

"Fine."

"That is different from boring."

"Boring."

"Better."

Attempt twenty-nine.

I gathered.

The draw itself was not counted separately. We were inside the external
sequence now, working from the supervised state Hessa had already
approved.

The shape came rougher.

I backed off before it collapsed.

Hessa said nothing.

I requested away.

The paper remained exactly where it was.

I waited.

Nothing.

A tiny change in the shadow made my heart jump.

Then I realized I had shifted my head.

The paper had not moved.

I released.

"No observed response," Hessa said.

"I saw that."

"Good."

"Still not praise?"

"No."

She checked the control.

Unchanged.

Target unchanged.

Attempt twenty-nine was a failure.

I was annoyed.

Not surprised.

Annoyed was easier.

"Again?"

"Rest."

"I feel fine."

"That is why we rest before you don't."

I sat back.

My forearm had started to feel used.

Not pain.

The same kind of low complaint that came after holding something awkward
too long.

My shoulder was still quiet.

Hessa made me open and close my hand.

Then rotate the wrist.

"Anything?"

"Used."

"Where?"

I showed her.

She pressed once.

"Pain?"

"No."

"Good."

That one might have been praise.

I did not risk it.

We waited longer.

Outside, something metal hit the floor.

Someone swore.

Another voice said, "Don't pick that up."

The Guild continued being the Guild.

For attempt thirty, Hessa used another matched pair.

She checked the intermediate mark before placing them.

"Same."

"Same."

"No more because the last one failed."

"I know."

"No less because the first one worked."

"I know."

"Same request."

"Away."

I settled.

This time the shape formed cleaner at first and then tried to tighten.

I almost followed it.

Stopped.

Let it be smaller.

Hessa watched my shoulder.

I requested away.

The fin stayed still.

One breath.

Then the top edge turned.

It was not a slide like the first.

The bottom barely shifted at all.

The whole folded piece rotated a tiny amount so the far corner ended
farther from me than before.

I released immediately.

Hessa moved.

I did not.

She checked the target from both sides.

Then the control.

"Movement."

"Away?"

"Rotation with net displacement away."

"That is a very Hessa answer."

"It is an accurate answer."

"Control?"

"Unchanged."

I smiled again.

She let me.

For several seconds.

Then she wrote.

Attempt thirty.

Intermediate.

Response.

I looked at the far mark.

Hessa saw me.

"No."

"I didn't say anything."

"You looked."

"I am allowed to look."

"Not at that."

"Davin said the same thing this morning."

"About paper?"

"Wood."

"Good."

"I don't like that you two agree."

She put her pencil down.

"How does the forearm feel?"

"Used."

"Wrist?"

I rotated it.

The last part of the motion felt tighter than it had when we started.

Not hurt.

Tired.

"Shoulder?"

When I lifted it, there was a faint pull near the front.

Hessa nodded.

"Done."

"One more would make the pattern nicer."

"That is not a reason."

"It would if it worked."

"And if it did not?"

"Then the pattern would be worse."

"Exactly."

I considered this.

"You're protecting the pattern from me."

"I'm protecting the evidence from fatigue."

"And me?"

"Also you."

That was less funny.

I sat back.

Hessa wrote for a while.

I watched the three marks on the board.

Near.

Intermediate.

Far.

The middle one did not look important.

None of them did.

They were chalk and shallow grooves on a pale board.

At near, paper had responded repeatedly.

At far, it had not responded cleanly enough to establish anything.

Now, at the place between them, it had moved twice out of three
requests.

Microscopically.

Under glass.

In a room where I was not allowed to touch it.

This was somehow the most magic I had.

Hessa turned the sheet so I could read.

29TH SUCCESSFUL SUPERVISED MINIMAL DRAW.

25TH SUPERVISED DELIBERATE SHAPING ATTEMPT.

INTERMEDIATE POSITION.

ATTEMPT 28: AWAY RESPONSE OBSERVED. CONTROL UNCHANGED.

ATTEMPT 29: NO OBSERVED RESPONSE. CONTROL UNCHANGED.

ATTEMPT 30: ROTATIONAL RESPONSE WITH NET DISPLACEMENT AWAY. CONTROL
UNCHANGED.

Below that:

AWAY RESPONSE REPEATED AT INTERMEDIATE POSITION IN CURRENT APPARATUS.

INTERMEDIATE RESPONSE ESTABLISHED UNDER CURRENT CONDITIONS.

DISTANCE DEPENDENCE REMAINS SUPPORTED.

FAR RESPONSE REMAINS UNESTABLISHED.

NO RANGE ESTABLISHED.

NOT GENERALIZED.

I read it twice.

"So it reaches farther than near."

"It responded at the intermediate position."

"That is what I said."

"No."

"It is physically farther."

"Yes."

"And it responded."

"Yes."

"So."

"So we have evidence of response at that position."

I pointed at the sentence.

"You wrote established."

"Under current conditions."

"Which are the conditions."

"Current ones."

I looked at her.

She looked at me.

"Hessa."

"Greg."

I sat back.

"I can move paper somewhat less close to me."

"Under controlled conditions."

"Twice."

"Today."

"Out of three."

"Yes."

"This is exhausting."

"Good."

"That one was definitely praise."

"It wasn't."

She took a fresh sheet.

I watched her write two words.

INTERMEDIATE / FAR.

Then:

AWAY ONLY.

SAME APPARATUS.

I leaned forward.

"Next?"

"Possible next comparison."

"Possible?"

"I want today's record clean before deciding whether we repeat
intermediate alone or compare it with far."

"You just wrote compare."

"I wrote the question."

"That seems like deciding."

"It isn't."

I had begun to suspect precision was mostly a weapon.

"No date?"

"No date."

"Restrictions?"

She looked at me.

"Same."

"All of them?"

"All of them."

No independent draw.

No independent shaping.

No external testing.

No paper.

No coins.

No thread.

No larger objects.

No practice.

No loopholes I had not thought of yet.

I nodded.

Hessa looked at my forearm.

"No lifting for the rest of today."

"I have theatre."

"You had theatre."

"I might have theatre."

"No lifting."

"Can I stand?"

"Yes."

"Rinna said come back if I can still stand."

"Then your theatre manager has created a poor medical standard."

"She's not my manager."

"What is she?"

I thought about it.

"Rinna."

Hessa accepted this.

I left the Guild with thirty external attempts behind me and absolutely
no permission to move anything interesting.

The gray-coated woman was gone.

The brass-plate argument had ended.

One of the three plates remained on the clerk's table with a scrap of
paper beside it.

I did not know whether it had been scratched before.

This bothered me less than it would have earlier.

I went back to the hall.

Not because I intended to work.

Because Rinna had said come back if I could still stand, and technically
I could.

The afternoon show had started.

I stood at the rear door.

Rinna looked at me.

"No."

"I didn't say anything."

"Can you carry?"

"Hessa says no lifting."

"Then no."

"I can do tickets."

"Done."

"I can count."

"Already counted."

"I can stand."

"I can see that."

"Decoratively."

Rinna considered me.

"No pay."

"Then I'm going home."

"Good."

Pell passed behind her carrying a stool.

He saw me.

"Middle paper?"

"Twice."

He stopped.

"How many?"

"Three."

"Good."

"Don't."

He kept moving.

I went home.

Lyssa was not there.

The soup was finally gone.

This was probably for the best.

I washed my hands, drank water because Hessa had infected that part of
my life too, and sat with my forearm resting across my lap.

It was tired.

Not hurt.

My wrist had gone from boring to mildly opinionated and would probably
return to boring if I stopped asking it questions.

I did.

Lyssa came home near dark with a small loaf under one arm.

"You're early."

"Technically."

She set the bread down.

"Guild?"

"Yes."

"Intermediate?"

"Yes."

"Did it move?"

"Twice."

She looked at me.

"How many tries?"

"Three."

I narrowed my eyes.

"What?"

"You asked the correct question."

"I've met you."

"One did nothing."

"And two moved."

"At the intermediate position, under the current apparatus, away from
me, with controls unchanged."

Lyssa was quiet.

"I wasn't going to correct you."

"You were thinking it."

"I was thinking you sound like Hessa."

"That's worse."

She cut the bread.

I told her the first one had slipped.

The second had done nothing.

The third had turned.

I showed the amount with my fingers and then made the gap smaller
because I had exaggerated.

Lyssa looked at the gap.

"That much?"

"Less."

She made it smaller.

"Probably."

She looked at me.

"Magic."

"Apparently."

"What next?"

"Maybe intermediate and far."

"Maybe?"

"Hessa wrote it and then claimed writing it wasn't deciding."

"That sounds like Hessa."

"No date."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"You have work."

"So does she."

"Exactly."

We ate bread with the last onion fried in the bottom of the pot and a
little salt.

No meat.

No pastry.

No private courtyard.

The lamp burned steadily.

Twenty-nine successful supervised minimal draws.

Twenty-five supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Thirty supervised external-effect attempts.

The paper had moved at the intermediate position twice.

Far still had not established anything.

I had no range.

I had no new permission.

I had no spell.

I had spent the morning moving benches and the afternoon moving paper
almost nowhere.

It had been a productive day.

Annoyingly, both jobs could prove it.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 207

## THE SERVANT

My wrist was boring again.

This was disappointing only because I had spent enough time discussing
it that I felt it should have developed a personality.

I rotated it once over breakfast.

Lyssa saw.

"Done?"

"Done."

"Good."

There was nothing under the cup.

I did not check twice.

There was also no soup, no meat, and only the end of yesterday's loaf.

Ordinary life had recovered from Havel House.

Lyssa left before me with a folded strip of dark cloth over one shoulder
and no explanation. I did not ask. I had learned that asking about cloth
produced more cloth.

I waited for third bell.

This remained harder than magic.

At the hall, Nessa had six coats laid across the rear bench and was
pinning small scraps of paper to the collars.

I stopped.

"Names?"

"Pieces."

"Why?"

"Because I'm leaving."

That was apparently enough information for everyone except me.

"Where?"

"Work."

"This is work."

"Other work."

"Better work?"

Nessa looked at me.

"Paid work."

"How much?"

"No."

I respected the answer because it was the same one I would have given
Pell.

Rinna had already changed the board.

KING: MAREK / HARA / EVIN

EMPTY TABLE: VEYA / SELLEN / GREG

SHORT: EVIN / PELL

GREG: SERVANT / SET / COATS

I pointed.

"Servant."

Teren looked over from the front.

"Four lines."

"Important?"

"No."

"Excellent."

The Servant in The Empty Table carried in a tray, announced that the
missing guest had sent word, was blamed for the guest's absence, denied
responsibility, and left.

I had seen the piece twice.

Apparently that qualified me.

Nessa handed me a gray coat.

"Not yours."

"I know."

"Good. Sellen, second piece."

She handed me a brown coat.

"Also not yours."

"Veya after Empty Table."

"Why does Veya need a coat after the piece she's in?"

"Because she leaves."

"Right."

Nessa gave me a third coat.

I looked at it.

"Hara?"

"No."

"Marek?"

"No."

"Evin?"

"No."

"Then I have exhausted people."

"Pell."

I stared at the coat.

"Why does Pell have a coat?"

"He plays a magistrate."

Pell, from behind the flat, said, "Authority."

Nessa said, "Temporary."

She made me hang the coats on three separate pegs.

Then she moved two of them.

"Why?"

"Performance order."

"I used the order you handed them to me."

"I handed them to you. I did not schedule them."

This was Nessa's final act before abandoning us.

Her other job involved three fitted coats for some household
entertainment on the north side. That was all I learned. She left with a
roll of pins, a wrapped bundle, and a bag that looked heavier than
anything involving thread should look.

No one wished her luck.

Sellen said, "If they pay you before us, don't come back."

Nessa said, "I wasn't planning to."

Then she was gone.

The hall continued.

This was mildly alarming.

I had assumed certain people were structural.

Apparently the structure was accustomed to being missing pieces.

My Servant rehearsal lasted long enough for Teren to tell me not to limp
with the tray.

"I don't limp."

"You compensate."

"I am missing a leg."

"I know."

"Then this seems connected."

"Use the table edge before the entrance. Set the tray. Take the crutch.
Enter."

I tried it.

It was easier.

I disliked that.

"Again," Teren said.

The second time I picked up the wrong tray.

There were two.

One had cups.

One had a folded letter.

Teren pointed.

"Letter."

"I knew that."

"You were carrying cups."

"I was testing you."

"No."

We finished.

Before the house opened, I ate half an onion with bread and regretted
both separately.

Pell had beans.

Marek had acquired a pear from somewhere and refused to explain it.

Hara arrived with dust on one sleeve and immediately asked why Nessa's
pegs had paper tied to them.

"Because we're children," Sellen said.

"Accurate."

The house was ordinary.

Twenty-something people.

Two bench complaints.

One man wanted to know whether The King was the one with the tax
collector.

"No," I said.

"Which is that?"

"I don't know."

He accepted this.

The King played first.

I worked rear.

Marek was good.

Hara was good.

Evin missed the edge of the platform with his heel and recovered without
anyone caring.

No one needed me.

Then Empty Table.

I put on the Servant coat.

It was tan.

I had expected gray.

Apparently the gray coat belonged to Sellen later.

This would have mattered if I had been responsible for remembering it.

I was not.

I carried the letter tray to the table edge, set it, shifted my crutch,
picked it up again, and entered.

"The guest has sent word."

Veya turned.

"From where?"

"From elsewhere."

That got a small laugh.

It was supposed to.

Sellen said, "Elsewhere is not an address."

"I was not given the address."

Nothing.

Fine.

Later Veya asked whether I had delayed the guest.

"I carry messages."

"Poorly."

"I carry them completely."

That got a better laugh.

I left.

Four lines.

No revelation.

Backstage, I handed Veya the brown coat.

She looked at it.

"Why?"

"Nessa said after Empty Table."

"Not me."

I looked at the paper tied to the collar.

PELL.

I had read the wrong peg.

Veya handed it back.

"Authority."

"Temporary."

Pell appeared.

"Mine?"

"Unfortunately."

The short belonged to Evin and Pell.

Pell's magistrate coat was too broad in the shoulder and made him look
like someone had promoted a sack.

This improved the piece.

Evin's character kept asking permission to do things he had already
done.

Pell kept granting it afterward.

The strongest moment came when Evin moved Pell's chair before asking.

"May I move your chair?"

Pell looked down at the empty space where the chair had been.

"No."

Evin nodded.

"Good."

Pell remained standing for the rest of the scene because neither of them
could get the chair back without breaking the rhythm.

The audience laughed every time he looked toward it.

I was holding Sellen's gray coat.

It turned out Sellen did not need it at all because the piece had
changed.

I hung it back.

This was my entire contribution.

Afterward, Rinna paid me one copper.

I took it.

She waited.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"I negotiated yesterday."

"You didn't."

"Recently."

"Go sweep."

I swept.

Nessa had not returned.

No one knew whether she would.

Hara left before cleanup finished.

Marek and Sellen argued about the pear.

Pell found the chair.

The hall emptied.

It should have ended there.

Most days did.

I was carrying a sack of costume scraps toward the rear storage when
someone knocked on the front door.

Not the polite knock people used before a show.

Three hits.

Hard enough that Rinna stopped counting coins.

Pell looked toward the door.

"Closed."

The knock came again.

Rinna put the coins away.

"Stay."

This was addressed to everyone and therefore obeyed by almost no one.

I stayed because I had one crutch under my arm and a sack of cloth in
both hands.

Rinna opened the door halfway.

Two men stood outside.

I could see one clearly.

Heavy coat despite the warmth. Thick neck. Hair cut close. The other was
narrower and stood a step back where the street light did not reach him
as well.

The first man said something too low for me to hear.

Rinna answered normally.

"No."

He said something else.

Rinna's shoulders changed.

Not much.

Enough.

I put the sack down.

Marek had stopped arguing about the pear.

Sellen was no longer smiling.

Pell whispered, "Who?"

"No idea."

The man at the door pushed it wider.

Rinna pushed back.

Not enough to stop him.

He stepped inside.

The second man remained outside.

"Private conversation," the first man said.

Rinna said, "Then have it privately somewhere else."

His eyes moved past her.

Hall.

Benches.

Rear door.

People.

Me.

He was not impressed.

That was sensible.

"We were told this place has been doing better."

Rinna said nothing.

"Havel work."

That landed differently.

Not because Havel mattered.

Because he knew.

The man smiled.

"Private courtyards. Better houses. More coin."

Rinna's voice stayed flat.

"What do you want?"

"Small share."

"No."

"Then next time a cart wheel breaks, or a lamp goes missing, or someone
decides your door should stop opening, you can pay a larger share."

Pell made a tiny sound.

The man looked at him.

Pell stopped.

This was not theatre.

It was also not new.

Not to me.

Different city.

Different clothes.

Different words.

Same arithmetic.

A person did not need to be stronger than everyone in a room if he could
make the room imagine the people who were not there.

The man had brought one visible friend.

He wanted us to supply the rest.

Rinna said, "Leave."

He took another step.

Marek moved.

I said, "Don't."

Everyone looked at me.

Including the man.

This was inconvenient.

I had not meant to say it aloud.

The man looked down at my crutches.

Then up.

"You talking to me?"

"No."

This was true.

I had been talking to Marek.

The man smiled.

"Good."

He turned back toward Rinna.

Behind him, through the open door, the second man shifted his weight.

Street-side.

Watching both directions.

Not relaxed.

Not backup waiting to enter.

A lookout.

Two men.

Maybe more elsewhere.

Maybe not.

The first man wanted us to believe more.

That was useful.

I looked toward the rear.

The narrow painted panel from yesterday was still half out of storage.

Beside it hung the old dark backing cloth.

Pell followed my eyes.

I looked at the lamp.

Then at him.

He frowned.

I touched two fingers to my own chest, then pointed behind the panel.

Pell's frown deepened.

I pointed again.

He moved.

Slowly.

The man did not notice.

Rinna did.

She did not look at Pell.

Good.

I had no plan.

This was familiar too.

Plans were usually what people called the thing they discovered
afterward.

I picked up Sellen's gray coat.

"Put that down," Rinna said.

"Yes."

I did not.

The man looked at me again.

"Problem?"

"Probably."

"Greg," Rinna said.

I looked at her.

She knew my name.

The man now knew my name.

That was unfortunate.

I said, "He should leave."

The man laughed once.

"Why?"

I looked at the door.

At the lookout.

At the man's boots.

Clean except for pale grit at the inside heel.

Not Carrow mud.

Stone dust.

His coat had been brushed recently but not well. There was a darker line
near the left pocket where something heavy had rubbed.

Knife or tool.

His right hand was bare.

Left glove.

Not fashion.

Maybe injured.

Maybe hiding marks.

I did not know.

The important part was that I did not need to know all of it.

I needed him to think I knew more than I should.

"You came through Mason's Cut," I said.

His smile changed.

Tiny.

Good.

I had guessed from the pale stone grit.

Mason's Cut had pale dust. I had walked through it twice.

He said, "So?"

I looked at his left hand.

"Then you should have stayed there."

This meant nothing.

His eyes narrowed.

Better.

Rinna did not ask.

Excellent.

Behind the painted panel, Pell moved.

The backing cloth lifted.

Not much.

A person-shaped darkening appeared against the pale wall beyond it.

Too tall to be Pell because the panel cut him at the wrong place.

Unless he held something.

He did.

A hat.

Marek's King hat, probably.

The shadow acquired a head.

The man saw it.

His eyes flicked once.

There.

Then back to me.

I did not look.

Theatre had taught me that part.

Not magic.

Not even good theatre.

Just do not look at the thing you want someone else to notice.

From behind the panel came one quiet knock.

Then another.

Pell had found wood.

The man glanced again.

I said, "You brought two."

He looked at me.

The lookout outside stopped moving.

I had counted them.

That was all.

I continued before either could decide this was obvious.

"One at the door. One inside. No cart. No third man on the east corner."

The inside man's face changed.

There might have been a third man on the west corner.

I had no idea.

I had said east.

Specificity was dangerous.

Specificity was also useful.

"Who are you?" he asked.

There it was.

Not who do you think you are.

Who are you.

Different question.

I put Sellen's gray coat on.

It did not fit.

Too narrow through the shoulders.

Good.

People noticed wrong clothes when they knew what the clothes should
mean.

He did not know.

I did.

That was enough.

"Tonight?"

I looked around the hall.

"Servant."

Marek made a noise that might have killed us.

I kept going.

"Yesterday I was at the Guild."

The man's eyes sharpened.

True.

"Before that, Havel."

True.

"Before that, here."

True.

I shifted my crutch.

"You're asking the wrong question."

Also true.

He stared at me.

Behind the panel, the shadow moved.

Pell had apparently understood enough to make it turn away, as though
whoever stood there had become bored with us.

That was much better than looming.

The man saw that too.

"Guild what?"

I smiled.

Not because I was brave.

Because Greg the Servant had smiled twice tonight and my face knew how.

"Paper."

He stared.

I let him.

This was stupid.

It was also impossible to evaluate.

He had expected a frightened troupe.

Instead he had a one-legged actor in the wrong gray coat telling him
accurate things about his route, counting his men, mentioning the Guild,
and refusing to explain why a tall shadow behind a panel did not care
enough to join the conversation.

There was no lie large enough to catch.

Only small truths arranged badly.

The man said, "You're funny."

"Sometimes."

"You think this changes anything?"

"No."

True.

It did not change who he was.

It changed this minute.

That was all I needed.

I looked at his left glove again.

"You should go before your hand gets worse."

I knew nothing about his hand.

He looked at it.

That was the first real mistake he made.

Marek saw.

Rinna saw.

I definitely saw.

The glove mattered.

Maybe not the way I implied.

Did not matter.

The man stepped back.

One step.

Not retreat.

Recalculation.

"I'll come back."

Rinna said, "Don't."

He looked at her.

Then at me.

"Greg."

I hated that.

"Yes."

He smiled again.

Less.

Then he left.

The lookout went with him.

Rinna shut the door.

Locked it.

No one spoke.

Behind the panel, Pell said, "Can I come out?"

Rinna closed her eyes.

"Yes."

Pell emerged holding the King hat on the end of a broom.

Marek stared.

"My hat."

"It was authority."

"Temporary," Hara would have said if she were here.

Sellen sat down.

"What the fuck was that?"

I took off the gray coat.

"Extortion."

"I know that part."

Rinna looked at me.

"How did you know Mason's Cut?"

"Boots."

"What about his hand?"

"I didn't."

Silence.

Pell lowered the broom.

"You didn't?"

"No."

"He looked."

"Yes."

"Why?"

"I don't know."

Marek said, "You told him it was worse."

"I told him he should leave before it got worse."

"That means the same thing."

"No."

Rinna looked at me for a long time.

"It meant the same thing to him."

That was harder to answer.

I sat.

My good leg had started shaking.

This annoyed me because it was late.

Sellen noticed.

He did not say anything.

Good.

Pell put the King hat on the bench.

"You said Guild."

"I was at the Guild yesterday."

"You made it sound like Guild."

"I said I was at the Guild."

"You said paper."

"I did paper."

Pell stared.

"That's horrible."

"Yes."

Marek said, "The shadow?"

I looked at Pell.

"Pell did that."

Pell straightened.

"I did."

"With my hat."

"Authority."

Marek took the hat away.

Rinna went to the front window and looked through the narrow edge of the
shutter.

"They're gone."

No one relaxed.

That seemed appropriate.

She turned.

"We close together."

No argument.

"We do not walk alone tonight."

No argument.

"Pell, stop looking proud."

Pell stopped looking proud.

Mostly.

Rinna looked at me again.

"That wasn't nothing."

"I know."

"He may come back."

"I know."

"You don't do that again without me knowing what you're doing."

"I didn't know what I was doing."

"That is worse."

"Probably."

She rubbed her forehead.

"Tomorrow, third bell."

Sellen laughed.

It came out wrong.

Rinna looked at him.

"What?"

"Nothing. Just thought maybe we'd cancel theatre because of the
criminals."

Rinna stared.

"No."

"Good."

"Why?"

"I don't know."

We left together.

Marek and Sellen went one direction.

Pell walked with me.

Rinna walked behind us until the next crossing, then turned toward her
own street.

No one followed.

At least no one I saw.

I did not turn the walk into a hunt for shadows.

That was how you supplied your own extra men.

Pell stayed quiet for almost a whole block.

Then:

"Servant?"

"What?"

"You told him you were a servant."

"I was."

"That was your answer."

"Tonight."

"He asked who you were."

"Yes."

"And you said servant."

"Yes."

Pell thought about it.

"That was the scariest part."

"It shouldn't have been."

"It was."

At my street he stopped.

"You good?"

"No."

"Same."

He left.

Lyssa was home.

She had two pieces of cloth on the table and one expression on her face
when I came in.

"What happened?"

I put my crutches against the wall.

"Why does everyone ask that before hello?"

"Greg."

I told her.

Not well.

The knock.

The man.

Havel.

Money.

Threat.

Pell behind the panel.

King hat on broom.

Mason's Cut.

Guild.

Paper.

The glove.

Lyssa did not interrupt until I got to the glove.

"You didn't know?"

"No."

"You guessed?"

"I implied."

"You guessed."

"I didn't actually say his hand was injured."

"Greg."

"I said he should leave before it got worse."

She stared at me.

I sat.

The shaking had stopped.

That was better.

Or worse.

Lyssa came around the table and stood in front of me.

"Did he touch you?"

"No."

"Rinna?"

"No."

"Anyone?"

"No."

"Did he have a weapon?"

"Probably."

"Did you see one?"

"No."

I looked at her.

"That's Hessa's fault."

"What?"

"That question."

Lyssa did not smile.

Fair.

I told her Rinna had us leave together.

I told her the men were gone when we left.

I told her tomorrow was still third bell.

That finally got a reaction.

"Of course it is."

"Apparently criminals do not alter repertory."

"Good."

"Good?"

"No. I don't know."

She sat across from me.

For a while neither of us spoke.

Then she said, "You scared him."

"I confused him."

"Same thing?"

"No."

She looked at me.

I thought about Hessa.

"No. Definitely not."

The room was quiet.

The lamp burned.

Nothing moved under the cup.

No note.

No paper.

No magic.

I had not moved anything with mana.

Pell had moved a broom.

I had moved a coat.

The man had moved himself.

That last one felt different.

I did not like how much.

Lyssa reached across the table and took my hand.

"Eat."

"We don't have much."

"I know."

We had bread.

A little salt.

Half an onion.

Enough.

Tomorrow I would go back to the hall.

The man might come back.

He might not.

He might have more people.

He might have none.

I knew almost nothing about him.

That was the part Hessa would have approved of.

The rest, probably not.

I had told the truth.

Mostly.

He had done the lying for me.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 208

## THE DOORMAN

I woke because someone hit wood outside.

I was upright before I knew where I was.

Lyssa made a sound beside me that was less awake than annoyed.

The noise came again.

Wood. Wheels. A man swearing.

I waited.

A cart rolled past the window.

The wheels kept going.

Nothing knocked on our door.

I lay back down.

Lyssa said, "Cart?"

"Cart."

"Good."

She was asleep again before I was.

This seemed unfair.

By morning I had converted the night into one bad wake-up and several
hours of perfectly ordinary sleep. My good leg felt fine. My forearm had
stopped complaining. My wrist remained so boring that I rotated it once
and immediately felt embarrassed.

There was nothing under the cup.

No note.

Lyssa watched me put the cup back.

"Are you going?"

"Third bell."

"That wasn't the question."

"Yes."

She tore the last piece from the loaf and gave me the smaller half.

I looked at it.

"You're concerned for me."

"I'm hungry."

"That is less flattering."

"You can buy bread."

"With money."

"Yes."

I ate.

Lyssa had work near Marra's again. We left together because our routes
overlapped for three streets, not because either of us said we should.

At the second crossing she stopped.

"Men come back, you leave."

"That's not how doors work."

"Greg."

"I know."

She looked at me.

I corrected myself.

"If they come back and leaving is possible, I leave."

"Better."

"What if Rinna says stay?"

"Then Rinna can explain herself to me."

"That seems worse for her than the men."

"It might be."

She went left.

I went right.

Carrow was offensively normal.

A woman was arguing over radishes. Two boys were rolling a hoop downhill
where carts also wanted to exist. A dog had found something dead enough
to be valuable and was defending it from another dog.

Nobody knew I had frightened myself with a cart.

Good.

At the hall, the front door was locked.

I knocked.

Nothing.

I knocked again.

A voice inside said, "Who?"

"Pell."

The door opened.

Pell looked at me.

"You're not Pell."

"I wanted to see if you checked."

He started closing the door.

I put my crutch in the gap.

"Greg."

"Too late."

"Open."

He opened.

Inside, the hall looked mostly like itself.

Mostly.

Davin had the front door hinge exposed.

Jori was under the handcart.

Marek was standing at the rear bench with his King hat in both hands.

Pell pointed at him.

"He slept with it."

"I did not."

"You brought it home."

"I protected it."

"From crime."

"From Pell."

Rinna was at the front table.

She looked up.

"Third bell."

"Exactly."

"Door stays latched until house."

I nodded.

"Rear too."

"Fine."

"No one talks about Havel money outside the company."

"I didn't before."

"No one talks about Guild."

I considered this.

"I barely did before."

Rinna looked at me.

"Greg."

"Fine."

"No one leaves alone after close for the next few days if we can help
it."

Pell raised a hand.

"What if I want to?"

"No."

"What if I'm very fast?"

"No."

"What if I'm carrying a broom?"

Marek said, "No hat."

Rinna closed her eyes.

"Especially no hat."

Pell looked at me.

"We've lost our best man."

"It was a broom."

"It was tall."

"You put a hat on it."

"It worked."

"That is not the same as being good."

"It can be both."

Marek took the King hat away from him despite Pell not currently holding
it.

Davin kicked the front door lightly.

It did not move.

"Fine," he said.

"What was wrong with it?" I asked.

"Nothing."

"So you fixed nothing."

"I checked it."

"That's Guild work."

Davin looked at me.

"No."

"Good."

Jori came in from the rear.

"Cart's fine."

Pell looked disappointed.

Rinna noticed.

"Why are you disappointed?"

"I'm not."

"You are."

"I thought if they threatened the wheel, maybe they'd done something to
the wheel."

"That would be bad."

"Yes."

"You look disappointed."

Pell examined the floor.

Rinna looked at me.

"You."

I waited.

"You bought us time."

"Probably."

"You also gave him your name."

"I didn't."

"You let me do it."

"I was busy."

"Doing what?"

I looked at the painted panel.

It was back in storage.

"Making poor decisions quickly."

"Yes."

She tapped the table once.

"If he comes back, you don't start another performance without telling
me."

"I don't know how I would tell you first."

"Try."

"That sounds like planning."

"Try that too."

This was unreasonable, but I accepted it.

Before house, Rinna made us check the things that could actually cost
money.

Not everything.

That was important.

Pell wanted to check the roof.

"For what?" Rinna asked.

"Access."

"To what?"

"The roof."

"No."

He wanted to check the cellar next.

We did not have a cellar.

This did not stop him immediately.

Davin checked the lamp shelf because lamps had been named in the threat.
All four tins were where they belonged, including the one whose cap he
had repaired after Havel. He opened it, closed it, and put it back.

"Still fixed?" I asked.

"Yes."

"Could have changed overnight."

"It didn't."

"Good."

Jori checked the rear latch because doors had been named. The wood
around it was old and scarred in ways that predated all of us. He found
one screw working loose.

Pell became excited.

"Sabotage."

Jori held up the screw.

"Age."

"That's what sabotage wants you to think."

Jori tightened it.

Rinna sent Pell to move benches.

The benches had not been threatened.

This made them safer.

Marek watched the street through the front shutter once, then
deliberately stopped. Sellen arrived, heard half the story from Pell,
heard the other half from Rinna, and somehow ended with less information
than when he entered.

"So Greg is Guild now?"

"No," everyone said.

Sellen looked at me.

"Congratulations."

"Thank you."

Rinna pointed at him.

"No."

"Withdrawn."

Veya came in carrying a small paper packet of roasted seeds. She
listened while hanging her outer wrap.

"Did they say who sent them?"

"No," Rinna said.

"Did they name anyone?"

"No."

"Did they say how much?"

"'Small share.'"

Veya snorted.

"Small isn't a number."

I looked at her.

"That's what Hessa would say."

"Who?"

"Never mind."

She ate a seed.

That was the entire council.

Nobody drew a map.

Nobody assigned weapons.

Nobody discovered a criminal empire hidden under the floor.

Teren arrived, complained that the front bench had moved three inches,
and made us move it back.

For several minutes, this was the largest problem in the building.

I was grateful.

The board gave me no role.

GREG: HOUSE / DOOR / SET.

I pointed.

"Door."

Rinna said, "Yes."

"Because of yesterday?"

"Because you have two hands and people pay at the door."

"I have two hands every day."

"Then you've been qualified longer than I thought."

I took my place at the front.

For the first hour, nobody tried to extort us.

This was excellent business.

A bread seller came by with a basket and asked if we wanted yesterday's
heels cheap. Rinna bought several.

A boy delivered a message for Teren.

A woman wanted to know whether tonight had Widow.

It did.

A man selling lamp wicks tried to convince Jori that every wick in the
hall was dangerously old.

Jori showed him three new ones.

The man said those were also dangerously old.

Normal threats returned.

I preferred them.

Then a woman carrying a basket of green onions stopped at the door.

She had been at the hall before. I remembered her because she always
smelled like onions even when the basket contained something else.

"Who's your Guild man?" she asked.

I thought I had misheard.

"Our what?"

"Guild man."

Rinna looked up from inside.

"What Guild man?"

The woman leaned around me.

"The one you had at the door last night."

There was a silence.

Pell, somewhere behind the flat, made a sound.

Rinna said, "We did not have a Guild man."

The woman looked at me.

Then at my crutches.

Then back at Rinna.

"That's not what I heard."

"What did you hear?"

"That some collectors came asking and you had Guild standing inside."

"No."

"Gray coat."

I looked down.

My coat today was brown.

This felt safer.

The woman said, "One leg."

Pell coughed.

Rinna looked at me.

I said, "I was wearing Sellen's coat."

The woman frowned.

"Why?"

"It was available."

"You're Guild?"

"No."

"You work there?"

"No."

"I was there."

Rinna said, "Stop helping."

I stopped.

The woman adjusted the onion basket.

"So you don't have Guild protection."

"No," Rinna said.

The woman looked disappointed.

This was worse than Pell and the cart wheel.

"Shame," she said. "Sounded useful."

Then she left.

I watched her cross the street.

She spoke to nobody.

At least not before turning the corner.

Pell appeared beside me.

"Guild man."

"No."

"Gray coat."

"No."

"One leg."

"Unfortunately yes."

"That's a good description."

Rinna pointed toward the rear.

Pell disappeared.

I stayed at the door.

I had wanted one man to misunderstand me for perhaps thirty seconds.

I had not considered that he might explain his misunderstanding to
someone else.

Or that someone else would improve it.

There had been no Guild man at the door.

I had not been at the door.

I had been inside.

The coat had been gray.

That part survived.

The Guild had moved closer.

That part had grown.

Protection had appeared from nowhere.

This was bad.

It was also, for the moment, useful.

That was worse.

Rinna came to stand beside me.

"Don't correct everyone."

"I just did."

"Badly."

"I told the truth."

She looked at me.

I stopped.

"Right."

A broad man came up the street.

I noticed him before he noticed the hall.

Heavy shoulders.

Dark coat.

No visible basket.

He looked toward the door.

I shifted my crutch.

Rinna followed my eyes.

The man came closer.

His hands were empty.

No glove.

No pale grit that I could see.

He stopped directly in front of me.

"How much?"

I stared at him.

"For what?"

"The show."

"One copper."

He looked annoyed.

"For both of us?"

A woman was behind him.

I had not noticed her because I had been watching his shoulders.

"Yes."

He paid.

The woman said, "Is Widow tonight?"

"Yes."

"Good."

They went in.

Rinna said nothing.

I said, "He was broad."

"Terrifying."

"Dark coat."

"Criminal."

"Empty hands."

"Clearly a mastermind."

"I corrected."

"You charged him."

"Correctly."

"Good."

She went back inside.

I hated that she was right.

The house filled normally.

Not full.

Not thin.

Enough.

The onion woman did not come.

The broad man did.

So did his wife.

He was not dangerous unless Widow was bad.

The bill was King, Widow, then a short with Sellen and Veya.

I stayed at the door through the first lines of King, then latched it
and went rear.

The change was immediate.

Street became wall.

Door became door.

Marek became King.

People laughed.

My body stopped listening for knocks because Pell needed the low stool
and Jori had put it behind the wrong flat.

Work was useful that way.

It occupied the parts of you that otherwise invented people on corners.

King played cleanly.

Marek had his hat.

He checked it before going on.

Pell noticed.

"Trust issues."

Marek said, "Correct."

Widow came next.

Veya had the chipped cup.

Nessa had returned from her outside job sometime before house and was
already angry at a sleeve.

I had not seen her arrive.

"How much?" I whispered.

"No."

Good.

Hara and Veya played Widow.

The broad man laughed early and loudly.

His wife did not.

This seemed to embarrass him, so he laughed less.

Halfway through, Veya reached for the chipped cup and found it empty.

It was supposed to be empty.

She looked inside anyway.

Hara said, "Still poor?"

Veya turned the cup upside down.

"Improving."

A few people laughed.

Hara reached under the table, produced the small wooden spoon, and held
it up.

"Rich."

Veya took it with both hands.

The broad man's wife laughed first.

Then everyone else.

It was not a large moment.

It was the best one.

Backstage, Pell whispered, "Guild spoon."

I hit him lightly with the folded cloth I was holding.

"No."

He smiled.

Between pieces I found the gray coat from last night hanging where it
belonged.

I stopped in front of it.

Sellen came up behind me.

"Mine."

"I know."

"You wore it badly."

"I was under pressure."

"It pulled across the shoulders."

"Criminals didn't mention it."

"They noticed."

"You don't know that."

"I would have."

He took the coat down, shook it once, and put it on for a later change
that Teren had already decided not to use.

Then he took it off again.

"Why did you put it on?" he asked.

"I needed him to not know what it meant."

Sellen frowned.

"What did it mean?"

"Nothing."

"Then why that coat?"

"It was there."

He stared at me for a moment.

"That's irritating."

"Yes."

"Did you at least look mysterious?"

"No."

"Shame."

He rehung the coat.

That conversation should have made the thing smaller.

Instead it made me remember how little of last night had required
anything special.

A coat that meant nothing.

A broom.

A hat.

Boot dust.

A true place name.

A man with a glove.

Almost everything had already existed before I touched it.

I went back to moving the stool.

The short played after.

Sellen missed a line.

Veya waited.
