He found it.

Nobody died.

Nobody knocked.

The broad man left with his wife and thanked me at the door.

I watched his boots.

Mud.

Normal Carrow mud.

He noticed.

"Something wrong?"

"No."

I looked up.

"Sorry."

He glanced down at his boots.

"Stepped in something?"

"Probably."

He left.

I closed the door.

That was how easy it was.

Look at someone's boots long enough and they supplied a reason.

I did not like knowing that.

Cleanup took longer because everyone checked things that did not need
checking.

Jori checked the rear latch twice.

Davin checked the cart wheel again.

Pell checked behind the painted panel.

"For what?" I asked.

"Crime."

"Is it there?"

"No."

"Good."

Marek took the King hat home again.

Rinna saw.

"Really?"

"Yes."

"Fine."

She paid me one copper.

I took it without negotiating.

She noticed that too.

"Scared?"

"Growing."

"Good."

"I don't think that's what you meant."

"No."

Nessa was sorting the coats.

I asked whether she had heard anything.

"About what?"

"Last night."

"Yes."

"From who?"

"Someone who heard you were Guild."

I closed my eyes.

"How?"

"I didn't ask."

"Did you correct them?"

"No."

"Why?"

"I had three coats to fit."

This was deeply reasonable.

"What did they think I did?"

Nessa tied a bundle.

"Didn't ask."

"You're very bad at rumors."

"I'm excellent at coats."

She left.

Rinna made us leave in groups again.

Not a formation.

Not escorts.

Just no one alone if it was easy to avoid.

I walked with Jori and Pell for three streets.

Pell wanted to discuss the shadow.

I did not.

He wanted to discuss whether the shadow needed a better hat.

Marek was not present, so he was brave.

I still did not.

At the crossing, Jori went south.

Pell went east.

I had two streets alone.

Nothing happened.

A cat knocked over something behind me.

I turned.

Cat.

I continued.

At home, Lyssa was trimming thread ends from a pale cuff.

She looked up.

"They come back?"

"No."

"Good."

"People think I'm Guild."

Her scissors stopped.

"What?"

"Not everyone."

"How many?"

"At least two."

"That's too many."

"One was selling onions."

"That's worse."

"I don't know why."

"Onion sellers talk."

This seemed possible.

I told her about the woman at the hall.

Gray coat.

One leg.

Guild man at the door.

Protection.

Lyssa listened.

Then she asked, "Are you Guild?"

"No."

"Good."

"I am allowed inside."

"That is not the same."

"I know."

"Do they?"

"No."

There it was.

We ate bread with salt and the last raw onion.

The lamp burned steadily.

Still no note under the cup.

I told her Rinna had latched the doors before house.

Davin checked hinges.

Jori checked cart.

Nobody left alone after close.

No men returned.

The show happened.

Veya got rich with a spoon.

Lyssa nodded at the important parts.

"Tomorrow?"

"Third bell."

"Of course."

I sat back.

The rumor bothered me more than I wanted it to.

Not because it was false.

Most rumors were.

Because parts of it were true in exactly the wrong places.

Gray coat.

One leg.

Guild.

Men left.

Those pieces fit together better after they had left me.

I had controlled my words.

I had not controlled the story.

Outside, someone knocked on someone else's door.

I waited.

Once.

Twice.

Then a woman shouted that she was coming.

I ate the rest of my bread.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 209

## THE MESSENGER

There was still no note under the cup.

I checked because that was what the cup was for now.

Lyssa looked over from the table.

"Nothing?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

"Could be bad."

"It could also be nothing."

I put the cup back.

She had three narrow strips of dark cloth laid beside a pale sleeve. I
knew enough to understand that asking what they were for would produce
an answer involving a customer I had never met and a problem I could not
solve.

So I ate bread instead.

"Third bell?" she asked.

"Third bell."

"Men?"

"Unknown."

"That's not an answer."

"It's the most accurate answer available."

She looked at me.

"You're getting worse."

"I've had professional help."

She tied the sleeve and strips into a bundle.

Her work was taking her south of Marra's today. Mine was taking me to
the hall. We did not overlap, so we left at different times.

This felt less dramatic than walking together.

I preferred it.

Carrow had apparently survived another night without asking me.

A fish cart blocked half a crossing. A woman carrying two baskets
blocked the other half. Both parties believed this was the other's
fault.

I went around.

No one followed me.

No one asked if I was Guild.

A man with a gray coat passed on the opposite side of the street.

I noticed the coat.

Then I noticed he had two legs.

Progress.

At the hall, the front door was latched again.

I knocked.

"Who?"

"Greg."

The door opened.

Pell looked disappointed.

"Too easy."

"I considered lying."

"You should have."

"I did yesterday."

"That was better."

He let me in.

The hall smelled like dust, old wood, and something fried.

Nessa was already there.

That was unusual enough that I looked at the bell light through the
front shutter to make sure I had not arrived late.

"Third bell," Rinna said from the table.

"Good."

Nessa had a bundle open across two chairs. Not company coats. These were
finer, dark green with pale lining, and none belonged to our repertory.

I pointed.

"How much?"

"No."

"I was checking whether yesterday changed you."

"It didn't."

She folded one coat.

Then she said, "The man might be called Bren."

I stopped.

Pell stopped too.

Rinna did not.

She was writing.

"Who?" I asked.

"The one who came here."

"Heavy coat?"

"Maybe."

"Close hair?"

"Maybe."

"Left glove?"

Nessa looked at me.

"I said he might be called Bren."

"That's fewer details than I wanted."

"You wanted a biography?"

"Yes."

"No."

She tied the coat bundle.

I waited.

Nessa sighed.

"One of the women at the house yesterday asked why I work here."

"Reasonable question."

"She heard about the men."

"Less reasonable."

"Her brother works at a cart yard near Mason's Cut. Someone came there
last month asking for a small share to keep wheels from breaking."

Rinna stopped writing.

That mattered more than the name.

"Same words?" she asked.

Nessa shrugged.

"Small share. Wheels. More later if they refused. That's what she
remembered."

"Did they pay?"

"Didn't ask."

I stared at her.

"You had one job."

"I had three coats."

"Your priorities are terrible."

"My priorities paid me."

Rinna said, "Name."

"The woman said her brother called the man Bren. She didn't see him. I
didn't see her brother. I don't know if it's our man."

Good.

Annoying.

Useful.

Rinna wrote BRENN? on the edge of a scrap, then crossed out one N.

"Why one?" I asked.

"Because she said Bren."

"Could still have two."

Rinna looked at me.

"Do you want me to add three?"

"No."

"Then stop."

I leaned on the table.

"So where does Bren stay?"

"We don't know," Rinna said.

"Should we find out?"

"Not by you going looking."

"I didn't say I was going."

"Good. Keep it that way."

Nessa picked up her bundle.

"Also, someone at the fitting asked if our Guild man was handsome."

Pell made a noise.

I said, "What did you say?"

"No."

"You didn't answer?"

"I had three coats."

"Again."

"Yes."

She left for the north side with her bundle.

Her life continued to be badly organized around my information needs.

Rinna folded the scrap with Bren's possible name and put it beneath the
edge of her ledger.

"That is all we know."

"Another cart yard."

"Maybe."

"Same phrase."

"Maybe."

"Mason's Cut."

"The cart yard is near Mason's Cut."

"Which is where he came through."

"Which you guessed from stone on his boots."

"I did not guess."

"You observed grit and named a road."

"Correctly."

"That is still not the same as knowing where he lives."

I looked toward the door.

"I know."

"Good."

Pell said, "I know where I live."

Nobody asked.

The board was already up.

KING: MAREK / HARA / EVIN

LETTER BOX: VEYA / SELLEN / GREG

SHORT: PELL / EVIN

GREG: MESSENGER / SET

I read it twice.

"Messenger?"

Teren was behind me.

"Five lines."

"That's a lot of messages."

"One message."

"Five lines for one message seems inefficient."

"It's a play."

"Right."

The Letter Box had existed around me for months in different shapes.
Witness. Clerk. People carrying letters badly. People denying letters
existed. People reading things they should not.

Apparently it had a Messenger too.

Teren handed me a folded paper.

"Blue fold."

I looked at it.

It was not blue.

"The string."

A faded blue string held it closed.

"That is dishonest naming."

"Messenger enters after Veya asks whether anyone came."

"From which side?"

"Rear."

"Which rear?"

Teren pointed.

I nodded.

"Five lines?"

"Four if Sellen cuts you off correctly."

"Does he?"

"Sometimes."

This was reassuring.

I went to put the message on the prop shelf.

There were two folded papers there.

Both had blue string.

I stared at them.

One was smaller.

One had a dark wax mark.

I picked the smaller.

Veya said, "No."

She was passing with a chair.

"How do you know?"

"That's Widow."

"Why does Widow have a blue message?"

"It doesn't."

She took it from me and turned it over.

The string was green.

Old green.

I looked at my message.

Blue.

"Color is failing us."

"Your eyes are failing you."

"That seems more serious."

She kept walking.

No lesson occurred.

Before house, a man came to the door carrying a small brass lamp in both
hands.

Not a company lamp.

Household size.

The metal around its top had blackened.

He looked at me, then at the hall.

"You the Guild fellow?"

"No."

He hesitated.

"I heard there was one here."

"There isn't."

"You work there."

"I go there."

Rinna, from inside, said, "Greg."

I stopped.

The man lifted the lamp.

"It won't hold."

I looked at it.

"Hold what?"

He looked surprised.

"Light."

"Right."

The blackened top looked like every broken magical thing I did not
understand.

Maybe it was not magical.

That was worse.

"I can't fix that."

"You don't know?"

"No."

"But you're Guild."

"No."

"You said you go there."

"I do."

Rinna said, "Greg."

"I'm stopping."

The man frowned at the lamp.

"Know anyone who can?"

I thought of Varo.

Then stopped.

I did not know whether Varo repaired this kind of lamp. I knew he made
and repaired particular prepared things. That was not permission to
redirect every broken object in Carrow toward him.

"Ask the lamp seller on Cooper's Row," Rinna said.

The man nodded.

"Thanks."

He left.

I watched him go.

Pell appeared at my shoulder.

"You could have charged him."

"No."

"One copper."

"No."

"Half copper."

"We don't have half copper."

"Then one."

"No."

Rinna said, "Good."

I looked at her.

"Was that praise?"

"No."

"Very close."

"It was relief."

"I'll take it."

The rumor had acquired another shape.

Not protection.

Competence.

That was more dangerous in a quieter way.

A frightened man could leave.

A person with a broken lamp might hand me the lamp.

I did not want the lamp.

House came normally.

The possible Bren did not appear.

Neither did his lookout.

The onion woman did not appear.

A pair of older women did, arguing about whether one of them had already
seen The King.

A carpenter came in with sawdust still on one sleeve.

Three young men paid together and immediately separated to different
benches.

The broad man from yesterday did not return.

I sold tickets until Rinna took over, then went rear.

Marek wore the King hat on his head.

This was where it belonged.

Pell looked at it once.

Marek saw him.

"No."

"I didn't say anything."

"No."

The King opened.

I had nothing to do in it except move a low stool before the first line
and take it away afterward.

The audience laughed in the usual places and one unusual place when Evin
coughed during a silence.

Marek waited.

Evin coughed again.

Marek said, "Finished?"

Evin nodded.

"Good. Then the kingdom may continue."

The laugh was larger than the cough deserved.

Evin bowed from where he stood.

Marek refused to acknowledge him.

That was probably the strongest moment in King.

Letter Box came next.

I put on a plain brown outer coat.

No gray.

No mystery.

The blue-string message went into my hand.

Teren pointed at it before I entered.

"Blue."

"Actually blue."

"Go."

I went.

Veya was already arguing with Sellen over whether a letter had been
received.

"It was not delivered," Sellen said.

"You opened it."

"That is not delivery."

"It has my seal broken."

"Evidence of opening."

"By whom?"

"Unknown."

That got a small laugh.

Veya turned toward my entrance.

"Did anyone come?"

I entered.

"The guest sent this."

Sellen looked at me.

"To whom?"

I looked at Veya.

That was not my line.

My line was to say, "To this house."

I knew that.

Unfortunately, I had already looked at Veya.

Sellen saw it.

"Excellent," he said. "The messenger has chosen."

The audience laughed.

I said, "To this house."

Veya held out her hand.

Sellen held out his.

I stopped between them.

My next line was, "I was told to place it in the box."

The box was behind Sellen.

I said the line.

Both turned.

The box was not there.

For half a second I thought someone had moved it.

Then I remembered.

Wrong side.

The courtyard version of nothing had infected me even though we were not
in a courtyard.

I had put the letter box on the opposite side during set.

Veya looked toward where it should have been.

Then at me.

I could not fix it from the stage without crossing directly through
their argument.

Sellen said, "You were told poorly."

Veya said, "He was told by you."

"I deny it."

"You deny everything."

"I am consistent."

The audience laughed.

Pell, backstage, slid the box into the edge of the scene from the other
side with his foot.

Veya saw it first.

"There."

Sellen turned.

"I have never seen that box."

"Of course not."

That laugh was better.

I put the message inside.

My remaining lines happened.

One got nothing.

One got a small laugh.

Sellen cut off the fifth.

Teren had been right.

Backstage, I whispered to Pell, "Thank you."

He said, "One copper."

"No."

"Half."

"We still don't have half."

"Then one."

"No."

The short belonged to Pell and Evin.

No hats.

No shadows.

Pell played a minor official trying to collect a fee from a man who kept
producing the wrong document.

Evin had three folded papers.

The first was too old.

The second belonged to his brother.

The third was blank.

Pell stared at it.

"This says nothing."

Evin looked over his shoulder.

"Then it cannot be wrong."

Pell considered.

"That is not how records work."

"Then write something."

"What?"

"That I came."

"You are here."

"Good. We agree."

The audience laughed.

Pell looked toward the audience as if asking for help.

An older woman near the front said, "Charge him."

Pell pointed at her.

"Finally."

Evin turned.

"She's not an official."

Pell said, "Neither am I after today."

That was the strongest moment.

The older woman clapped once.

Greg the Guild man did not matter.

Bren did not matter.

For several minutes, a blank paper mattered because Evin was holding it
and Pell wanted money.

That felt healthy.

After the show, I moved the letter box back to its normal shelf.

The correct side.

Teren watched me.

I said, "Nothing happened."

"I know."

"Good."

Rinna paid me one copper.

Messenger and set.

No special rumor money.

No danger money.

No lamp-repair money.

I accepted it.

The hall emptied slowly.

The carpenter stayed long enough to tell Veya that Letter Box had been
better than last time.

Veya asked when he had seen it last.

He could not remember.

This weakened the review.

Rinna latched the front door after him.

No hard knock followed.

Nobody demanded a share.

Nobody broke a lamp.

Davin had checked the cart earlier and did not check it again.

This was progress of some kind.

Rinna took the folded scrap from beneath her ledger.

BREN.

She looked at it.

Then at me.

"Maybe."

"Maybe."

"Cart yard near Mason's Cut."

"Maybe."

"Same words."

"According to someone who heard them from someone."

"Yes."

I thought about the boot grit.

The glove.

The man looking at his own hand.

The second man outside.

The missing third man who had never existed except as a sentence.

A name was better than a shadow.

It was also less useful than I wanted.

"Do you know the cart yard?" I asked.

Rinna said, "No."

"Should we?"

"No."

"Should someone?"

"Maybe."

"Who?"

"Not you."

"That answer is becoming repetitive."

"Good."

She folded the scrap again.

"I'll ask people I know."

"Who?"

"People I know."

"Names?"

"No."

"This is very difficult for me."

"I know."

She put the paper away.

That was the difference between Rinna and me.

I saw a loose thread and wanted to pull it.

Rinna saw a loose thread and first checked whether it was holding up the
curtain.

We left in a group of four.

Me, Pell, Veya, and Sellen.

At the second crossing, Sellen went west.

At the third, Veya left us.

Pell and I walked another block.

He said, "Bren."

"Maybe."

"Sounds criminal."

"It's four letters."

"Exactly."

"What does that mean?"

"I don't know."

"Good."

At his turn, Pell stopped.

"You want me to walk you home?"

"No."

"You sure?"

"Yes."

He nodded.

Then, because he was Pell, he said, "I could bring the broom."

"Go home."

He went.

I walked the rest alone.

Nothing followed.

No cat this time.

At home, Lyssa was not there.

Her bundle was.

A different one sat on the table, tied badly enough that I knew she had
not tied it.

Customer work.

I put my copper away.

The cup was empty.

Still no Hessa note.

Lyssa came in later carrying a paper packet that smelled like beans.

"Trade," she said.

"For what?"

"Work."

"Good work?"

"Finished work."

"Better."

She put the beans between us.

"They come back?"

"No."

"What happened?"

"We might have a name."

She sat.

"Might?"

"Bren."

"Who says?"

"Nessa heard it from a woman whose brother works at a cart yard near
Mason's Cut."

Lyssa waited.

"The cart yard got the same small-share approach last month."

"Same man?"

"Maybe."

"Same words?"

"Apparently."

"Did Nessa see him?"

"No."

"Did the woman?"

"No."

"So you don't know."

"No."

That was cleaner than trying to improve it.

We ate beans with bread.

I told her about the broken lamp.

She looked at me.

"You didn't touch it?"

"No."

"You didn't tell him you could fix it?"

"No."

"You didn't take money?"

"No."

"Good."

"Everyone is very relieved when I don't commit fraud."

"You should notice that."

"I have."

I told her about the letter box being on the wrong side.

She cared less.

Correctly.

The lamp burned.

The cup remained empty.

Bren might be Bren.

A cart yard might have heard the same words.

The man with the glove had still promised to return.

We knew more than yesterday.

Not much.

But it was real.

For now, that was enough.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 210

## THE TENANT

There was still nothing under the cup.

I lifted it.

Nothing.

I put it down.

Lyssa was scraping the last beans from yesterday into a smaller bowl.

"You look disappointed."

"I'm maintaining continuity."

"With what?"

"Nothing."

"Good."

She divided the beans badly, which meant my portion was larger.

I corrected this by eating before she noticed.

She noticed.

"Thief."

"Evidence?"

"The missing beans."

"Could be natural loss."

"Eat."

I did.

My wrist was boring. My forearm was boring. My shoulder had stopped
having opinions about paper.

The room smelled faintly of onion and old lamp oil. The new wick burned
evenly even though there was enough daylight that we did not need it.

Lyssa pinched it out.

"We paid for that."

"Exactly. We should admire it."

"We can admire it tonight."

She had another bundle today. Dark cloth, pale thread, one wooden toggle
sitting loose on top until she wrapped it.

I did not ask.

This had become a skill.

"Third bell?" she asked.

"Third bell."

"Anything else?"

"No."

"Good."

That was becoming a dangerous word.

She left first.

I waited.

Nothing happened.

Then I went to work.

The handcart was missing a wheel.

Not missing in the sense that somebody had stolen it.

Missing in the sense that the wheel was lying beside the cart while Jori
held the axle up with both hands and Davin crouched in the dirt behind
the hall.

Pell stood nearby.

He had no useful job.

This was obvious from posture alone.

I stopped in the rear doorway.

"What happened?"

"Pin's gone," Davin said.

I looked at the wheel.

"That sounds repairable."

"It is."

"Good."

Davin looked up.

"Did you think that was the important part?"

"Usually."

Jori shifted his grip.

"Can we put this down?"

"No," Davin said.

"I've been holding it."

"I know."

"That was information."

"Keep holding it."

I moved closer.

The cart wheel had a thick wooden hub. The end of the axle passed
through it, with a hole near the end where a small retaining pin
normally kept the wheel from walking itself off.

The hole was empty.

No broken wood.

No snapped pin.

Just empty.

"Broke?" I asked.

Davin pointed.

"If it broke, part would still be in there or the hole would be
damaged."

"Unless it broke very conveniently."

"Wood doesn't care about convenience."

"People do."

"Yes."

He ran a thumb around the axle end.

"Pulled."

Jori said, "Thank you."

Davin looked at him.

"For confirming the thing I've been saying since I picked it up."

"Can I put it down?"

"Now."

They lowered the axle onto a block.

I looked toward the rear lane.

The gate was shut.

Rinna stood just inside it.

"They said wheel," she said.

"Yes."

Nobody needed more.

Pell said, "I checked the roof."

Rinna looked at him.

"And?"

"No cart pin."

"Useful."

"I also checked the front."

"For cart pins?"

"For men."

"Any?"

"Three."

We all looked at him.

"Normal men."

"How did you determine that?" I asked.

"They ignored me."

"Strong evidence."

Rinna pointed at the hall.

"Inside."

Pell went.

I stayed.

"When did we last know it was there?"

Jori wiped his hands on his trousers.

"I checked the cart after those men came. Wheel, other wheel, shafts,
bed."

"Yesterday?" I asked.

"Again yesterday."

Rinna nodded.

"So sometime after close."

"Or this morning," Jori said. "Rear lane was empty when I opened. Cart
was where we left it. I moved it half a turn before I saw the wheel
shift."

"Half a turn?"

"The wheel."

"Not the cart."

"I know what a wheel is."

"Good."

Davin stood.

"Nobody rode it. Nobody loaded it. Pin's gone. That's what we know."

"Bren?" I asked.

Rinna said, "No."

"I was asking if we're still calling him that."

"No."

"That's not what I asked."

"It is the answer you need."

Davin found a short piece of hard wood in his box and began shaving it down.

I watched.

"Leave the knife," he said.

"I'm not touching it."

"Good."

He went back to the replacement pin.

Rinna said, "The cart-yard story is real."

That pulled my attention away from Davin's knife.

"Real how?"

"I asked."

"Who?"

"No."

"Of course."

"The yard got the same approach. Small share. Wheel trouble. More
later."

"Same man?"

"Don't know."

"Bren?"

"Don't know."

"Glove?"

"Greg."

"That's a reasonable identifying detail."

"It would be if anyone I asked saw him."

"So no."

"No."

That was better.

Not good.

Better.

The method existed outside our hall.

Someone had asked another small business for money and named the same
kind of cheap damage.

Now our wheel pin was gone.

It still did not tell us who pulled it.

It did tell us that whoever had pulled it had chosen something we had
already been told to fear.

That was enough to make the rear lane feel narrower.

Rinna said, "We're opening."

"Good."

"You sound surprised."

"I wasn't."

"You looked at the cart."

"The cart is currently missing a wheel."

"It has a wheel."

"Not functionally."

Davin held up the new pin.

"It will."

Rinna pointed inside.

"Board."

I went.

The board said:

BORROWED HOUSE: VEYA / SELLEN / GREG

KING: MAREK / HARA / EVIN

SHORT: PELL / NESSA

GREG: TENANT / SET

I read it.

"Tenant?"

Teren was moving the narrow wall panel.

"Six lines."

"Do I own anything?"

"No."

"Accurate."

Borrowed House had enough versions that I had stopped pretending there
was one correct arrangement.

Today Veya was the owner.

Sellen was her brother.

I was the tenant who had been living in a room long enough to believe
the broken shutter belonged to someone else.

This was not difficult acting.

Teren handed me a key.

"Door."

"Which door?"

"The one we don't have."

"Excellent."

"You give it to Veya after she asks why you didn't lock it."

"What am I locking?"

"Your room."

"With a door we don't have."

"Yes."

"Do I have the room?"

"No."

"Very efficient property."

Teren left.

I put the key on the correct prop shelf.

Then moved it when Nessa told me that shelf was for King.

No lesson.

The cart came inside twenty minutes later.

Both wheels attached.

Davin had fitted the replacement pin and then fitted a second small
wedge because he did not like the first one.

Jori rolled the cart once across the rear floor.

Davin crouched and watched the wheel.

"Again."

Jori rolled it back.

"Good."

I said, "Can I touch it now?"

"No."

"At what point does this become personal?"

"Earlier."

Rinna had the cart pushed farther inside than usual.

It blocked part of the rear path.

This meant the large painted panel had to come through front.

This meant Pell and I carried it around the benches.

This meant a woman arriving early found two men carrying a wall toward
her.

She stepped aside.

"Renovating?"

"No," I said.

Pell said, "Constantly."

We set it.

Rinna made us move it.

Then Teren made us move it back half the distance.

Davin needed the rear floor clear long enough to test the cart again, so
the Widow table also went somewhere it did not belong. Nessa came
through carrying two folded shawls, stopped, and looked at it.

"No."

"Temporary," I said.

"Still no."

"Where?"

She pointed to a space beside the costume pegs.

I moved it.

Pell came through with a basket.

"No."

"Why?"

"That's where the basket goes."

"It has never gone there."

"It does today."

I looked at Nessa.

She had already left.

I put the table against the wall.

Teren came back.

"Why is Widow there?"

"Pell."

Pell said, "Greg."

"Shared responsibility."

Teren pointed to the other side.

We moved it again.

For several minutes, the extortionists lost to a painted wall and a
table nobody could agree where to put.

Davin rolled the cart forward once more. The chalk line stayed straight.

Jori leaned over the opposite wheel.

"Other pin's good."

Pell said, "Unless that's what they want us to think."

Rinna looked at him.

"Who?"

"Them."

"Who are them?"

Pell opened his mouth.

Nothing useful came out.

"Good," Rinna said. "Keep it that way."

That quieted him for almost a minute.

A delivery boy knocked at the front with a bundle of lamp wicks for
Jori. Greg the Guild man was apparently not required for this
transaction. Jori paid, counted the bundle, complained that two were
shorter than last time, and made the boy stand there while he compared
them.

The boy cared about none of our problems.

I liked him immediately.

House opened.

I worked front until the first piece.

I counted nine.

Then heard something scrape in the rear.

I looked.

Nothing visible.

I counted again.

Ten.

Rinna said, "Again."

"Why?"

"You said nine."

"I revised."

"Again."

I counted.

Ten.

A woman had come in while I was listening to the rear.

"Ten."

"Good."

"Was this a test?"

"No."

"Then why did I fail?"

"You were counting."

"Apparently not."

She took the tickets.

I went backstage.

The missing pin sat on the corner of Rinna's table.

Not the original pin.

There was no original pin.

Davin's replacement was in the cart.

The empty space was what mattered.

I disliked that.

Breaking the cart would have cost someone effort.

Making us inspect it every morning was cheaper.

They did not need the wheel to come off.

They needed Rinna to imagine it coming off while loaded with lamps.

I did not say this.

Rinna was already checking the rear latch.

She did not need philosophy.

Borrowed House began.

My Tenant coat was brown and too warm.

The key was in my pocket.

I entered after Veya called for me.

"How long have you lived here?" she asked.

"Long enough to know the shutter was broken before I arrived."

"You arrived last month."

"Then it has been broken at least a month."

Small laugh.

Sellen stepped into the room that did not exist.

"You said the roof leaked."

"It does."

"It has not rained."

"I am planning ahead."

Better laugh.

Veya held out her hand.

"Why didn't you lock the room?"

I reached for the key.

Wrong pocket.

I had put it in the inner pocket because the outer one had a hole.

That had been sensible before I was onstage.

Now I had to find it.

Veya watched.

Sellen watched.

The audience watched.

I found the key.

Veya said, "Were you hiding it?"

"From the door."

A laugh.

Not huge.

Enough.

I gave it to her.

She held it up.

"There is no lock."

"Then I have been extremely careful for nothing."

That was the written line.

It got the normal laugh.

My next line got nothing.

The final two happened.

I left.

Backstage, Pell said, "Door feared you."

"Correct."

"No lock."

"That's why."

King followed.

The cart did not matter.

The rear latch did not matter.

Bren might have mattered somewhere in Carrow, but Marek had the hat and
Hara had a petition and Evin had managed to sit in the King's chair
before Marek entered.

Marek stopped.

Evin looked up.

"You're early."

"This is my chair."

"I was keeping it."

"For whom?"

"You."

Marek held out his hand.

Evin stood.

"You're welcome."

Marek sat.

The audience laughed.

Later, Hara handed Marek the petition upside down.

He turned it.

She turned her head to match.

He turned it again.

She matched again.

Marek finally held it sideways.

Hara stared.

"Now neither of us can read it."

"Good."

"Why?"

"I was losing."

That got the biggest laugh of the afternoon.

The audience had no idea a cart pin was missing.

That was their right.

Between pieces I went rear for water.

The cart sat against the wall with the chalk mark still unbroken.

I checked it without touching.

Rinna saw me.

"Don't."

"I didn't."

"You were about to."

"I was observing."

"Observe the stool."

The stool for the short was still on the wrong side because I had left
it there after King.

I moved it.

That was less satisfying than checking the cart and more useful.

The short belonged to Pell and Nessa.

Pell was trying to return a borrowed coat.

Nessa kept identifying things wrong with it.

"That button isn't mine."

"It was attached when I got it."

"Then it was wrong earlier."

"The sleeve is shorter."

"Your arm may have grown."

"One?"

"Possibly."

Pell looked at his arms.

The audience laughed.

Nothing threatened anyone.

Nothing implied anything.

A coat was a coat.

This was comforting.

Afterward, the hall returned to itself.

The painted wall went back.

The Tenant key returned to the shelf.

I checked the pocket before hanging the coat and found a seed shell.

I did not investigate.

Rinna paid me one copper.

I took it.

No one mentioned danger pay.

Good.

The cart was stored fully inside.

Jori put a chalk mark across the replacement pin and axle.

I watched.

"What's that?"

"If the pin moves, line breaks."

"That's clever."

"It's a line."

"Still."

Davin said, "Don't touch it."

"I am developing a reputation."

"You already have one."

"Unfortunately."

The word landed differently than Rinna's joke had days ago.

Nobody followed it.

Rinna latched the rear.

"Tomorrow third bell."

"Still?"

She looked at me.

"Do you want them to close us?"

"No."

"Then third bell."

Pell said, "What if they take another pin?"

Rinna said, "Then Davin makes another pin."

Davin looked offended.

"I have other work."

"Then they owe you."

"Who?"

"Exactly."

That ended it.

We left in groups again.

Not military groups.

Not assigned pairs.

People simply waited an extra minute when their roads overlapped.

I walked with Nessa for one street.

She had another bundle.

"Three coats?" I asked.

"Two."

"Business declining."

"Different house."

"How much?"

"No."

She turned north.

I went east.

Nothing happened.

At home, Lyssa had one onion cut in half and a heel of bread on the
table.

She looked at me.

"They come back?"

"No."

"What happened?"

"Someone pulled the cart pin."

Her knife stopped.

"Did you see them?"

"No."

"Anyone?"

"No."

"Davin says it was pulled, not broken."

"Who?"

"Unknown."

She resumed cutting.

"The men said wheel."

"Yes."

"So maybe."

"Yes."

"What else?"

"The other cart yard really did get the same demand."

"From him?"

"Unknown."

"Bren?"

"Still maybe."

She nodded.

That was all.

No dramatic realization.

No demand that I stop going.

No plan to flee Carrow.

She put the onion in a pan with a little fat.

"Rinna opening tomorrow?"

"Third bell."

"Good."

I looked at her.

"You use that word too much."

"What word?"

"Good."

"Theatre is open."

"Yes."

"You are not hurt."

"Yes."

"The cart is fixed."

"Yes."

"Then good."

That was difficult to argue with.

I checked the cup.

Nothing.

Still no Hessa note.

The lamp burned steadily while onion softened in the pan.

Someone had taken a piece of wood from a cart.

Davin had put another piece back.

Rinna had opened the doors.

We had performed.

The threat had cost time.

Attention.

A little fear.

It had made Jori check two wheels instead of one and Rinna ask questions
she would rather spend on tickets. It had put chalk on a cart pin and
changed where the cart slept.

That was the arithmetic I recognized.

They did not have to smash the wheel.

If they could make us spend ten minutes every day proving the wheel
still existed, they had already taken something.

Not enough to close the hall.

Not enough to pay them.

Not enough to stop Lyssa cutting an onion or Nessa fitting coats or Pell
arguing with furniture.

But something.

It had not yet cost the day.

That felt temporary.

It also felt true.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 211

## THE NEPHEW

There was still nothing under the cup.

I checked.

Lyssa watched me from the table.

"Anything?"

"No."

"Good."

"You've started doing that deliberately."

"Doing what?"

"Calling nothing good."

She pulled thread through a dark cuff.

"Nothing exploded."

"Nothing usually explodes."

"Then most mornings are good."

This was unreasonable optimism.

I ate bread.

The bread was yesterday's. The edge had gone hard enough that dipping it
in water became less an option and more a structural requirement.

Lyssa had two cuffs today.

Same dark cloth.

Different stitching.

"Same customer?"

"No."

I waited.

She did not continue.

I had become excellent at not asking about cloth.

I was less excellent at not thinking about a missing cart pin.

The men had threatened a wheel.

Someone had pulled a wheel pin.

Another cart yard had heard the same demand.

Bren might be Bren.

That was everything.

Anything beyond that belonged to the part of my brain that liked turning
three stones into a fortress.

I finished the bread.

"Third bell," Lyssa said.

"Yes."

"You're going."

"That is generally how employment works."

"Sometimes you go before they ask."

"Initiative."

"Annoying."

"Also true."

She tied the cuffs into a bundle.

We left within a few minutes of each other, then separated at the second
crossing because her work went south and mine went west.

Carrow was busy enough to be insulting.

A fish seller argued with a woman over whether a fish had been larger
before it died.

Two boys carried a chair between them and nearly took out a basket of
onions.

A cart passed with both wheel pins visibly present.

I noticed.

Then got annoyed that I noticed.

By third bell, the theatre cart was still inside.

The chalk line Jori had drawn across the replacement pin and axle
remained straight.

I looked once.

Then stopped.

Progress.

Rinna was at the front table with a woman I did not know.

The woman was perhaps forty, maybe older, with broad hands, sun-dark
skin, and a leather apron rolled under one arm. Dust had settled into
the seams of her boots. Not pale Mason's Cut grit. Darker road dirt and
wood dust.

She looked like she had somewhere else to be.

Good.

People with nowhere else to be were dangerous to conversations.

Rinna saw me.

"Greg."

The woman looked at my crutches.

Then my leg.

Then my face.

"That's him?"

I stopped.

"Depends."

Rinna said, "Come here."

That was less encouraging.

I crossed the hall.

Pell was carrying a stool behind them and slowed down enough to become
furniture.

Rinna pointed.

"Out."

Pell kept walking.

The woman put her apron on the table.

"Name's Talla."

"Greg."

"I know."

"Apparently."

She looked at Rinna.

"This the Guild one?"

Rinna said, "No."

I said, "Technically complicated."

Rinna looked at me.

"Not complicated."

"I go there."

"Not helping."

Talla's mouth moved slightly.

Not quite a smile.

"Thought you had Guild."

"We don't," Rinna said.

There.

Clean.

No gray coat.

No paper.

No shadow.

No broom wearing royal clothing.

Talla nodded.

"Fine."

Rinna said, "Talla works at the cart yard."

"The one near Mason's Cut?"

"Yes."

Talla looked at me more carefully.

"You know it?"

"I know the road."

"Bad road."

"Yes."

"Good."

Apparently road criticism was trust.

Rinna said, "Tell him what you told me."

Talla looked at her.

"I already came here."

"You said you were passing."

"I am."

"Then pass information."

Talla sighed.

"Man came last month. Said yard was doing well. Said good runs meant
good coin. Wanted a small share."

Exact enough that my shoulders tightened.

"What happened?" I asked.

"We paid."

Rinna said, "Once?"

"Once."

"And then?"

Talla looked at me.

"More."

There it was.

Not sophisticated.

Not hidden.

The first payment did not buy safety.

It bought the next invoice.

"How much more?" I asked.

Rinna said, "Doesn't matter."

Talla said, "Enough."

Also true.

"Same threat?" I asked.

"Wheel first. Then axle. Then somebody might start finding our customers
before we did."

That was new.

Not a gang map.

Not a boss.

Just pressure moving from objects to business.

Rinna's face did not change.

"Did you see him?"

"Yes."

"Description."

Talla shrugged.

"Big enough. Heavy coat. Hair cut close."

My attention narrowed.

"Glove?"

Talla looked at me.

"What?"

"Did he wear a glove?"

She thought.

"One."

"Which hand?"

"Left."

Nobody spoke for a moment.

Pell dropped something backstage.

A wooden knock.

Ordinary.

Talla glanced toward it.

I did not.

That felt strange now.

"Left," I repeated.

"Pretty sure."

"Pretty sure?"

"He kept that hand close. Other one did the talking."

"Did you see what was wrong with it?"

"No."

Good.

Not good.

Useful.

I had not known anything about his hand.

I still did not.

The glove was real.

The reaction had been real.

Whatever conclusion he had drawn from my sentence remained his.

Rinna said, "Name?"

Talla scratched the side of her nose.

"Other man called him Bren."

"Called him?"

"Once."

"Bren what?"

"Didn't ask."

"Could be nickname?"

"Could be his mother's name for all I know."

I liked Talla.

Rinna said, "That is all."

"Do we know where he stays?"

"No."

Talla looked between us.

"You always do that?"

"Apparently."

"Yes," Rinna said.

Talla picked up her apron.

I asked, "Did the same lookout come back the second time?"

Rinna's expression sharpened.

One question too many.

Talla answered anyway.

"Didn't notice."

Good.

Unknown.

"How many men?"

"Two first time. Three second."

That mattered less than my brain wanted it to.

Different day.

Different purpose.

Maybe same people.

Maybe not.

I kept my mouth shut.

Rinna said, "You paid the first time. What happened after the second?"

"We didn't."

"And?"

Talla tied the apron around her waist.

"Nothing for four days. Then one of our drivers got offered the same run
by another yard for less. Could be them. Could be business."

"Wheel?"

"One cracked spoke two weeks later."

"Cut?"

"No."

"Broken?"

"Broken."

"Then not evidence."

Talla looked at me.

"You're annoying."

"Yes."

"Good."

Rinna's mouth moved.

Definitely a smile this time.

Talla continued.

"We started bringing the carts in. Told the drivers not to talk coin in
taverns. Told the next yard over what happened."

"Did it stop?"

"Don't know yet."

There.

No solution.

No heroic resistance.

No secret militia.

A yard paid once, refused once, changed some habits, and kept working.

Talla had probably come because Rinna asked through somebody ordinary
and because small businesses survived partly by warning other small
businesses when somebody started charging for air.

She picked up her apron.

"I've got a wheel waiting."

"Broken?" I asked.

"Actually broken."

"Good."

She stared at me.

"Sorry."

"You're strange."

"I've been told."

At the door she stopped.

"Rinna."

"Yes?"

"If he comes back, don't tell him I came."

Rinna nodded.

"Wasn't planning to."

Talla left.

The door closed.

I looked at Rinna.

"Bren."

"Probably."

That was an upgrade.

"Same man."

"Probably."

"Left glove."

"Probably."

"He was at the yard."

"Probably."

"He asked them for a small share."

"Yes."

"They paid once."

"Yes."

"He came back for more."

"Yes."

I nodded.

Rinna said, "You're enjoying this."

"No."

"You look interested."

"That's different."

"Make it less different."

I looked toward the door.

"Where does he stay?"

"No."

"I knew you'd say that."

"Then why ask?"

"Hope."

"No."

"Do we know who he works for?"

"No."

"How many?"

"No."

"Territory?"

"No."

"Why Havel?"

"No."

"Whether Havel mattered specifically or we were just visible?"

"No."

"Whether he has an injured hand?"

"No."

Rinna waited.

I sighed.

"Fine."

"Good."

"Everybody uses that word too much."

She pointed at the board.

"Work."

The board said:

DEBTOR'S UNCLE: SELLEN / VEYA / EVIN / GREG

WIDOW: VEYA / NESSA / HARA

SHORT: MAREK / PELL

GREG: NEPHEW / SET

I stared.

"Nephew."

Teren was behind me.

"Five lines."

"Whose nephew?"

"Evin's."

"Is he the debtor?"

"No."

"Am I?"

"No."

"Then why am I here?"

"Family."

"Terrible reason."

"That's the piece."

He handed me a folded cap.

The Nephew existed mostly so the Uncle could try to transfer
responsibility for a debt to someone younger and less responsible.

I understood the social structure immediately.

Teren walked me through the entrance.

Left side today.

Not right.

"Why?"

"Table."

I looked.

The table was on the other side.

"That seems like the table's fault."

"Left."

"Fine."

My first line was:

"I was told my uncle required family."

Second:

"He usually requires money."

Third:

"I have less family than money."

That line did not make sense to me.

Sellen said, "It makes sense in the scene."

"It does not make sense in language."

"Then say it convincingly."

I hated actors.

Before the house opened, I bought a small onion from the woman two doors
down.

Not our onion seller.

A different onion seller.

Carrow had enough onions to support competing institutions.

I put it in my coat pocket and immediately regretted the shape.

Pell saw it.

"Lunch?"

"Dinner."

"You carry dinner?"

"I transport assets."

"Guild assets?"

I looked at him.

He grinned.

"No."

"Good."

The onion stayed in my pocket through setup and made sitting
uncomfortable. This was entirely my own fault and therefore one of the
day's better problems.

House opened with the cart still inside.

Jori checked the chalk line once.

Straight.

No missing pin.

No Bren.

No lookout.

No mysterious glove.

A man bought two tickets and complained that the bench nearest the aisle
was already occupied.

A child asked if there would be a ghost.

I said no.

Nessa said, "Maybe."

The child was happier with Nessa.

We had twenty-three by the time Debtor's Uncle started.

I was backstage with the folded cap on.

The cart-yard conversation had settled into a smaller shape.

Bren was probably Bren.

Probably the same man.

Left glove probably real across both encounters.

He had used the same language.

The yard paid once.

He asked for more.

That was enough.

I did not need his address before my entrance.

This was obvious.

I still almost entered from the right.

Veya caught the back of my coat.

"Other."

I stopped.

Looked.

Left.

"Yes."

I crossed behind the panel and entered correctly.

Small failure.

No one died.

Sellen, as the creditor, was already arguing with Evin.

"Your brother owes twelve."

"My brother owes everyone twelve."

"Then he is consistent."

Veya said, "Consistency is a virtue."

"Not in debt."

That got a laugh.

My cue came.

I stepped in.

"I was told my uncle required family."

Evin looked at me.

"I require assistance."

"He usually requires money."

Laugh.

Sellen turned.

"Are you the nephew?"

"Unfortunately."

"Can you pay?"

"I have less family than money."

Nothing.

Of course.

The line remained nonsense.

I kept going.

Veya said, "How much money?"

"Also less."

That got the laugh the written line had failed to earn.

Sellen looked at Evin.

"Your family is insolvent in several directions."

"That is why we stay close."

Better laugh.

The piece moved.

My final line was just:

"I'll come back tomorrow."

Sellen said, "Bring money."

I said, "Then I won't."

Normal laugh.

Exit.

Done.

Backstage, Teren said nothing.

Good.

Widow followed.

Veya and Nessa had a bowl, a shawl, two chairs, and a letter.

Hara entered carrying the wrong basket.

I knew it was wrong because the correct basket had a broken handle.

Hara knew too.

She looked down at it.

Then at Veya.

Veya said, "You brought everything?"

Hara looked inside.

"No."

"What did you forget?"

"The basket."

Audience laughed.

Nessa looked at the basket in Hara's hands.

"That is a basket."

"Not ours."

"Whose is it?"

Hara looked toward the wings.

I stepped backward.

"Apparently his."

The audience laughed harder.

I had not said anything.

This was good.

Hara put the wrong basket down carefully.

Veya opened it.

Inside were Pell's gloves for the short.

She lifted one.

"Your husband left these?"

Hara said, "He had private interests."

Nessa covered her mouth.

The audience broke.

That was the strongest moment.

Pell, beside me, whispered, "Those are mine."

"I know."

"Get them."

"No."

"I need them."

"After."

"What if she throws them?"

"Then adapt."

"You've changed."

"No. I'm enjoying this."

He glared.

The gloves survived.

The short happened.

Pell had his gloves.

Marek had both shoes.

Nobody stole anything.

Nobody threatened anyone.

The audience laughed at a man pretending not to recognize his own hat.

For almost twenty minutes, Bren ceased to exist.

Afterward, he returned only because Rinna checked the rear lane before
we put the cart away.

Empty.

Jori checked the chalk.

Straight.

Davin was not there today, which meant nobody was allowed to improve the
pin.

I returned the Nephew cap.

Nessa took it from me.

"Inside out."

I looked.

It was.

"I didn't do that."

"You wore it."

"That is not the same thing."

She fixed it.

Rinna paid me one copper.

I took it.

No hazard pay.

No information pay.

No reward for correctly identifying a glove.

Employment remained stubbornly local.

Talla had been gone for hours.

Nothing else happened.

At close, Rinna said, "Third bell tomorrow."

I nodded.

Then she handed me a folded scrap.

"For you."

I looked at it.

"From?"

"Boy brought it while you were on."

I opened it.

Hessa.

TWO DAYS.

AFTER MIDDAY.

INTERMEDIATE / FAR.

AWAY ONLY.

SAME APPARATUS.

SAME RESTRICTIONS.

I read it twice.

There.

The other life.

Not separate.

Not connected either.

Just another appointment.

"Guild?" Rinna asked.

"Two days. After midday."

"Tell me tomorrow."

"I'm telling you now."

"Tell me tomorrow when I have tomorrow's board."

"Efficient."

"Go home."

We left.

I walked partway with Pell and Evin.

Pell said, "Bren."

"Probably."

"Left glove."

"Probably."

"Same man."

"Probably."

"You've become boring."

"Evidence."

He looked at me.

"Don't."

Good.

At home, Lyssa had beans again.

Different beans.

I could tell because these were darker and slightly less disappointing.

"Trade?" I asked.

"Part."

"For cloth?"

"For work."

I sat.

She looked at me.

"They come back?"

"No."

"Cart?"

"Still has both wheels."

"Good."

I let it go.

"We know more."

"Do you?"

"Probably."

She waited.

I told her about Talla.

Not every line.

The useful parts.

Cart yard paid once.

Bren returned for more.

Heavy coat.

Close hair.

Left glove.

Another man called him Bren.

"Same man?" Lyssa asked.

"Probably."

"Probably Bren?"

"Yes."

"Hand?"

"Still don't know."

"Who he works for?"

"No."

"Where he stays?"

"No."

"What happens next?"

"No."

She put beans in my bowl.

"So you know his name."

"Probably."

"And he has a glove."

"Probably."

"And he asks for more after people pay."

"Yes."

"That's useful."

"Yes."

"Not much."

"No."

She nodded.

That was becoming our preferred amount of certainty.

I put Hessa's note on the table.

Lyssa read it.

"Far."

"Intermediate and far."

"Two days."

"Yes."

"Rinna know?"

"Yes."

"Good."

I pointed at her.

"There."

"What?"

"Again."

She ate a bean.

I ate mine.

Bren was probably Bren.

The glove was probably the same glove.

The cart-yard payment had bought another demand.

Two days from now, Hessa wanted paper at two positions.

Tomorrow, Rinna wanted me at third bell.

None of those things explained the others.

For once, I let them remain separate.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 212

## THE USHER

Antonius Vale found me before breakfast.

Not personally.

That would have been alarming.

A boy in a brown vest knocked while Lyssa was scraping the last beans
from last night's pot into a smaller bowl.

I opened the door.

He looked at me.

"Greg?"

"Usually."

He handed me a folded note.

"Vale."

Of course.

I stared at the note.

The boy held out his hand.

"For reply."

"I haven't read it."

"I can wait."

This was apparently a threat.

I opened it.

GREG,

YOUR DEBT REMAINS ACTIVE.

TABLE WORK.

DELIVERY ACCOUNTS.

TOMORROW, FIRST BELL.

ONE HOUR.

TRANSPORT FROM VALE OFFICE IF REQUIRED.

A. VALE

I read it again.

The debt had not changed while I was busy becoming a servant, nephew,
paper problem, and possible Guild criminal.

Rude.

Lyssa leaned against the table.

"Vale?"

"Yes."

"Debt?"

"Apparently."

"You knew."

"I knew quietly."

The boy was still waiting.

I looked at him.

"What reply does he need?"

"Yes or no."

"What happens if I say no?"

The boy shrugged.

"Then I bring back no."

Not a threat.

Worse.

Administration.

I turned the note over and wrote YES with Lyssa's charcoal.

Then added:

FIRST BELL IS OFFENSIVE.

I considered it.

Crossed that out.

Then wrote:

YES.

The boy took it.

"Tomorrow."

"Apparently."

He left.

I closed the door.

Lyssa put the smaller bowl in front of me.

"You thought he'd forgotten."

"I hoped his records had suffered."

"He's a lender."

"Records can suffer anywhere."

"Not there."

Probably not.

I ate cold beans.

The old arrangement had not survived my leg.

That part had been obvious.

Antonius had stopped treating me like a body he could send wherever a
body needed to go, which was considerate mostly because a missing leg
had made the alternative ridiculous.

The debt had survived.

So had the newer arrangement.

Table work.

Some room work.

No roads.

No pretending I could carry a warehouse because a ledger said I owed
somebody money.

I had done invoice comparisons for him before.

Two copper of debt credit.

Then nothing for a while.

Apparently Antonius did not require regular proof that a debt existed.

He had a ledger.

I hated that I could hear him saying it.

"Delivery accounts," Lyssa said.

"Yes."

"Carts?"

"Probably."

"Vale has carts."

"So does half Carrow."

"That is what makes it interesting."

"That is what makes it carts."

I ate another bean.

She went back to her work.

Nothing sat under the cup.

No Hessa note.

That one had already arrived.

Tomorrow had become crowded without asking me.

First bell, Antonius.

After midday, Hessa.

Theatre had not yet voted.

I left the Vale note on the table long enough to remember the time, then
folded it into my coat.

By third bell, the theatre cart still had its wheel.

The chalk mark still crossed pin and axle in one unbroken line.

Jori was crouched beside it anyway.

"Moved?"

"No."

"Then why are you there?"

"Checking."

"Didn't you just check?"

He looked at me.

I moved on.

Rinna had today's board in one hand and a short list in the other.

The short list was not theatrical.

BREN?

LEFT GLOVE.

CART YARD.

PAID ONCE. MORE.

That was all.

No strings.

No map.

No dramatic circle around the name.

Good.

She folded it before Pell could see.

I said, "Vale found me."

Rinna looked up.

"Who?"

"Antonius Vale."

That got recognition.

Not surprise.

"Creditor?"

"That is an ugly way to describe our relationship."

"Accurate?"

"Unfortunately."

"When?"

"Tomorrow, first bell. One hour."

She looked at the board.

I added, "Guild after midday."

"I remember."

"You told me to tell you."

"Now I remember twice."

"Efficient."

She looked at the board again.

"Tomorrow, Vale. Guild. Come after Guild if you're still useful."

"That's a very open condition."

"Then don't make it worse."

"So no morning theatre."

"No."

"Will you survive?"

"We did yesterday."

I had no answer to that.

Rinna pointed at today's board.

KING: MAREK / HARA / EVIN

LETTER BOX: VEYA / SELLEN / NESSA

SHORT: PELL / EVIN

GREG: HOUSE / SET

"No role?"

"No."

"Again?"

"You've had several."

"I've developed expectations."

"Kill them."

She handed me the ticket box.

Apparently today I was the usher.

I stood at the front door while the hall warmed behind me.

Pell and Jori argued over whether the rear cart being farther inside
made the passage safer or merely more annoying.

Nessa had three costumes hanging from the side rail.

Davin was repairing a lamp hook that had nothing to do with Havel.

Marek was eating something that smelled like garlic.

Normal.

A woman arrived first and paid for one.

Then two men together.

Then an older couple.

Then a broad man in a dark coat came up the street and looked directly
at the door.

My body noticed before my brain did.

Shoulders tight.

Good foot planted.

Crutch angle changed.

He stopped in front of me.

Large.

Dark coat.

No close-cut hair.

No glove.

He looked at the board beside the door.

"How much?"

I stared.

"For what?"

He stared back.

"The show."

Right.

"One copper."

"For both?"

"Each."

"That's robbery."

I almost laughed.

Did not.

"Then you may remain financially secure outside."

He frowned.

Then paid two copper.

His wife came up behind him carrying a wrapped bundle.

He had been waiting for her.

That was all.

I put the coins in the box.

The man said, "King first?"

"Yes."

"Good."

He went inside.

I watched his hands.

Both ordinary.

I disliked myself a little.

Rinna appeared behind me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Then sell tickets."

"That's what I'm doing."

"Do it less suspiciously."

"Apparently I have a face now."

"You've always had one."

Unfortunate.

Before the house opened fully, a familiar produce woman stopped at the
door with a basket on one hip.

She did not buy a ticket.

She pointed past me at Rinna.

"Her."

Rinna came over.

The woman said, "Your cart still inside?"

"Yes."

"Good."

Rinna waited.

The woman waited too.

Apparently Carrow had developed competitive waiting.

Finally the woman said, "Heard about Talla."

Rinna's face stayed flat.

"From?"

"Not your business."

"Then neither is mine."

The woman snorted.

"Fair."

She shifted the basket.

"No one came to me."

Rinna nodded. "Good."

I liked her.

She looked at me.

"Guild man?"

"No," Rinna and I said together.

The woman blinked.

"Fine."

She left without buying a ticket.

Rinna looked at me.

"That rumor is annoying."

"I agree."

"Do not improve it."

"I haven't."

"Keep doing that."

"Finally, a task suited to me."

She went back inside.

That was the entire threat for the afternoon.

A woman with turnips had heard something.

No new victim.

No new man.

No broken object.

The rumor remained small enough to fit in a produce basket.

I preferred it that way.

More people came.

A carpenter with sawdust on one sleeve.

Three young women who argued over who had paid last time.

A boy who tried to give me half a copper and confidence.

An old woman who said she would sit near the back because Marek
projected spit.

I did not verify this.

By the time the King started, we had twenty-six.

I counted twice.

Not because of scraping noises.

Because Rinna had made me count twice.

Progress could also be externally imposed.

I closed the door and carried the box back.

The King needed one platform, two chairs, the narrow table, and the red
cloth.

I brought the red cloth.

Wrong red cloth.

There were apparently two.

Nessa took it from my hand.

"Other."

"How many colors are secretly plural?"

"All of them."

I found the darker red cloth.

The first one belonged to Short.

No lesson.

The King started.

Marek was clean today.

Hara had a petition that was not upside down.

Evin had both shoes.

For several minutes, nothing improvised.

Then a fly landed on Marek's forehead.

He ignored it.

The fly remained.

Hara watched it.

The audience began watching Hara watching it.

Marek kept speaking.

Finally Hara said, "Your Majesty."

"What?"

"You have an adviser."

Marek's eyes narrowed.

"Where?"

Hara pointed at his forehead.

The audience laughed.

Marek held perfectly still.

"Is he wise?"

"He has stayed through the entire petition."

"Then wiser than most."

Bigger laugh.

Evin stepped closer, examined the fly, and said, "He disagrees."

"With what?"

"Everything."

The fly left.

Marek watched it go.

"Dismissed."

That was the strongest moment.

No chair moved.

No paper fell.

No one needed me.

Good.

Between pieces, I changed the table and nearly carried the wrong red
cloth back again.

Nessa made a sound.

I stopped.

"Right."

"Which?"

I looked at both.

"Darker stays."

"Good."

I carried the other.

Competence.

Letter Box played normally.

Veya found the key.

Sellen lied about a letter.

Nessa made the audience laugh by sitting down before Sellen finished
inviting her to sit.

The hall forgot about Bren because the hall did not know Bren existed.

I forgot for most of it too.

That was better.

At the rear, Pell whispered, "Vale?"

I looked at him.

"How do you know?"

"You said it near Rinna."

"I regret your hearing."

"Antonius Vale?"

"Yes."

"You owe him money?"

"Debt."

"That's money."

"Not currently."

"How much?"

"No."

He looked offended.

"Nessa gets to do that."

"Do what?"

"Say no when I ask how much."

"Learn from her."

"I refuse."

Short was next.

I moved the correct red cloth.

This was my contribution to art.

After the show, the broad man in the dark coat came back to the door.

My shoulders tightened again.

He held out a small wooden token.

"My wife dropped this."

I looked at the lost-things cup.

The matching token sat inside.

I handed it to him.

He nodded.

"Good show."

"Thank you."

"King was better last month."

"I'll tell him."

"Don't."

He left.

Ordinary people were exhausting.

Cleanup took longer because one bench had shifted enough to wobble on
the floor.

Davin fixed it with a thin scrap under one foot.

I did not ask whether this was another secret system.

I knew.

Jori checked the cart.

Chalk straight.

Rinna counted tickets.

One copper came to me.

Normal local pay.

I took it.

She said, "Tomorrow."

"Vale first bell. Hessa after midday. Then here if useful."

"Correct."

"You want me after Guild?"

"If Hessa says you can work."

"She'll say no lifting if I look tired."

"Then don't lift."

"What do I do?"

"We'll discover whether you have other skills."

"Harsh."

"Go."

I did.

At the pay table, I unfolded Vale's note again.

Rinna noticed.

"You're reading the same words."

"They may improve."

"They won't."

"Antonius writes efficiently."

"Then why read twice?"

"Hostility."

She took the note before I could stop her.

Not snatched.

Simply removed from my hand with the confidence of somebody accustomed
to props being in the wrong place.

"Delivery accounts," she read. "One hour."

"Private correspondence."

"You showed me the time."

"I showed you the time."

"Now I've seen the rest."

She handed it back.

"Debt credit?"

"Yes."

"How much?"

"Whatever the work earns against the debt."

"That's not an answer."

"It's the arrangement."

"How much debt?"

"No."

Rinna raised one eyebrow.

I had apparently learned Nessa's technique after all.

"Does Vale know about the men?" she asked.

"No."

"Are you going to tell him?"

"I don't know."

That answer bothered me because it was true.

Rinna folded today's ticket count.

"Don't tell him for us."

"I wouldn't."

"If it comes up, it comes up. But the hall didn't hire Vale."

"Vale would probably charge the hall for being rescued."

"Then definitely don't."

I put the note away.

That settled one thing.

If Antonius heard about Bren, it would not be because I marched into his
office and offered him the theatre's problem like an unpaid invoice.

On the way home I passed a Vale warehouse.

Not one I had worked in recently.

Two men rolled a barrel through the wide door while a clerk stood beside
a board and argued about a number.

A cart waited outside.

Its driver had one foot on the wheel hub.

I looked at the pin.

Present.

Of course.

Antonius's world was full of carts.

Warehouses.

Deliveries.

People who owed money.

People waiting to be paid.

People arguing over whether three jars had arrived broken or two.

Bren's world, as far as I knew, was two men at a theatre door and a
missing pin.

The overlap was not a connection.

It was Carrow.

That mattered.

I kept walking.

Lyssa was home before me.

She had changed out of the dark work wrap and was cutting the bad edge
off an onion.

Not my onion.

We had become onion-rich by accident.

"Vale?" she asked.

"Tomorrow."

"You didn't go?"

"Note."

"Ah."

I put the note on the table.

She read it.

"Delivery accounts."

"Yes."

"One hour."

"Yes."

"Transport covered."

"I can walk."

"That's not what it says."

"I know."

She gave me a look.

My leg had made certain arguments less philosophical.

"If he sends transport, use it," she said.

"I enjoy arriving under my own power."

"You enjoy being difficult."

"Also true."

She put onion into a pan with a little fat.

"What happened at theatre?"

"Nothing."

She waited.

"Actual nothing," I said. "Cart pin still there. Bren did not arrive.
Nobody threatened anyone."

"Good."

"There was a suspicious man."

Her hand stopped.

"He wanted tickets."

She resumed cutting.

"Greg."

"I discovered that."

"Before or after you stared at him?"

"During."

"Good."

"Why does everyone keep saying that?"

She ignored me.

I told her about the fly.

That got more interest than Vale.

Correctly.

Then I told her tomorrow's schedule.

"First bell Vale. After midday Hessa. Theatre after if I'm useful."

"That's three."

"Two and a possible."

"Still three places."

"Only if the third place wants me."

She put the knife down.

"Vale debt still alive."

"Yes."

"You knew."

"People keep emphasizing that."

"Because you keep acting surprised."

"I was surprised by timing."

"Why?"

"I've been busy."

"So has he."

That was fair.

Antonius Vale had not disappeared because I had joined a theatre.

My debt had not dissolved because Bren had walked through a door.

Hessa's paper did not care about either of them.

Tomorrow, I would spend an hour looking at somebody else's delivery
accounts, then sit behind glass and ask folded paper to move away from
me at two distances.

After that, if my arm still belonged to me, Rinna might find something
for me to carry without carrying it.

Lyssa put food down.

Onion.

Bread.

A little fat.

No beans.

"Luxury," I said.

"Eat."

I did.

The Vale note stayed beside Hessa's note.

One debt.

One experiment.

Neither had anything to do with Bren.

Probably.

I left it there.

The lamp burned steadily while we ate.

That was my household scale now: not wealth, not safety, just enough oil
that the room did not flicker every time the wick reconsidered its
profession.

I looked at the two notes again.

Lyssa said, "Stop."

"I wasn't touching them."

"You were thinking loudly."

I ate the rest of the bread.

Tomorrow could arrive without further preparation.

This was apparently allowed.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 213

## THE COUNTER

Antonius sent a cart.

Not a carriage.

That distinction mattered because one had cushions and the other had a
sack of nails under the bench.

The boy from yesterday sat beside the driver.

He looked at my crutches.

"Vale said transport."

"I can walk."

"He said transport."

Lyssa stood behind me in the doorway.

"Get in."

I looked at her.

She looked at the cart.

I got in.

The sack of nails shifted under my good foot as we started.

Luxury.

Carrow at first bell belonged to people who had already decided the day
was late. Bakers had doors open. Carts occupied corners as if streets
had been built around them. A man carried three empty baskets on his
head and insulted somebody I could not see.

Vale's office was attached to one of the smaller warehouses, which meant
it was still larger than our room and contained fewer useful onions.

The driver stopped near the side door.

The boy said, "One hour."

"I remember."

He shrugged.

Apparently he was not responsible for whether I did.

Inside, a clerk pointed me toward the back table.

Antonius Vale sat there with three narrow stacks of paper and a cup he
was not drinking from.

He looked almost exactly as he had the last time I had seen him.

This was unreasonable.

I had lost a leg, joined a theatre, moved paper with mana, acquired
several kinds of employment, and accidentally convinced an extortionist
I might be dangerous.

Antonius had changed coats.

"I thought you'd forgotten me," I said.

"I have a ledger."

There it was.

I sat.

He looked at my crutches leaning against the chair, then at me.

"You move better."

"I've practiced."

"Walking?"

"Mostly falling less."

He slid the first stack toward me.

"You are harder to schedule."

"I have employment."

"You have several fragments of employment."

"That's still plural."

"Unfortunately."

The work was exactly what the note had promised.

Delivery accounts.

No hidden message.

No criminal code.

No secret list of men named Bren.

Three warehouse receiving sheets. Four cart departure tallies. Six
invoices. Two repair charges. One complaint from a customer who had
apparently believed a late barrel of lamp oil had ruined a wedding.

Antonius tapped the piles.

"These should agree."

"They don't?"

"If they agreed, I would not need you."

"Maybe you enjoy my company."

"No."

Good.

I started with dates.

Then cart marks.

Then loads.

The first disagreement was boring.

A clerk had copied the previous day's cart mark onto the next line. The
receiving record had the correct one because the driver had signed
beside it.

I put the two papers together.

"This is copied."

Antonius looked.

"Why?"

"Same mark two days. Different driver. The second receiving sheet has
the other cart."

"Which means?"

"Clerk copied yesterday before writing today."

"Probably."

"You don't sound impressed."

"I pay clerks to create fewer problems than they solve."

"I could create more."

"I know."

He made a small mark in his ledger.

The second problem took longer.

Three delivery invoices had deductions for late arrival.

Two matched the receiving times.

The third did not.

The invoice said the load arrived after second bell because of a wheel
repair.

The warehouse receiving sheet had it before first bell.

The repair charge existed.

Wrong day.

I spread the papers.

"This repair belongs to the next run."

Antonius looked at the repair slip.

"Why?"

"Cart mark."

"Same cart."

"Yes. But the repairman wrote the warehouse destination in the margin.
This one went south. The delayed invoice went west."

He took the slip.

The margin was faint.

"Useful."

"I prefer praise with more emotion."

"No."

He moved the corrected papers aside.

Most of the rest was ordinary mess.

A receiving clerk counted nine jars where the invoice said ten. A note
attached to the invoice said one jar had cracked while loading, which
solved the mystery unless the cracked jar had developed a commercial
afterlife.

A driver had written his own name differently on two sheets.

A repair charge for a wheel rim was real and correctly placed.

One late delivery was simply late because the driver had left late.

I found no conspiracy.

This was comforting.

Then I reached a receiving sheet marked for a yard near Mason's Cut.

I stopped.

Only for a moment.

Antonius said, "What?"

"Nothing."

"That was not nothing."

I looked at the sheet again.

It was not Talla's yard.

At least not the name she had given Rinna.

It was a receiving point two streets off Mason's Cut.

Ordinary.

"Place," I said.

"What place?"

"Mason's Cut."

Antonius waited.

I considered Rinna's instruction.

Do not tell Vale for us.

Fair.

"I am not bringing you a problem from the theatre."

"I did not ask for one."

"Good."

"Now tell me why you stopped."

I hated efficient people.

"Two men came to the hall."

Antonius's expression did not change.

"They wanted a small share."

"Of what?"

"Us."

"That is not an amount."

"Apparently it becomes one after you agree."

That got the smallest pause.

I continued.

"They mentioned Havel work. Said carts lose wheels. Lamps disappear.
Doors stop opening. Rinna refused."

"And?"

"Later somebody pulled the retaining pin from our handcart."

"Did you see them do it?"

"No."

"Then somebody pulled a pin."

"Yes."

He waited.

"Another cart yard near Mason's Cut got the same kind of visit," I said.
"They paid once."

"And?"

"More."

Antonius leaned back slightly.

"Of course."

"You say that like it's obvious."

"It is obvious after it happens."

That was annoyingly close to my opinion of plans.

"The man is probably called Bren," I said.

"Probably?"

"Someone at the other yard heard another man call him that."

"Bren what?"

"No surname."

"Then you have half a name."

"He has one glove."

Antonius looked at me.

"That is less than half a name."

"Left."

"Still."

"He may have something wrong with the hand."

"May?"

"I made him think I knew."

That finally changed Antonius's face.

Not much.

His eyes stayed on me a little longer.

"Why?"

"Because I didn't want him to break anything."

"Did it work?"

"He left."

"Then what?"

"He said he'd come back."

Antonius looked down at the delivery sheet.

"That is not the same as working."

"I know."

I did not tell him about the broom shadow.

Or Sellen's coat.

Or paper.

Especially paper.

He tapped the Mason's Cut sheet once.

"Did Rinna send you to tell me this?"

"No."

"Did she ask for help?"

"No."

"Do you?"

"No."

The answer came quickly enough that he looked at me again.

"I don't know what you would do," I added.

"Good."

"That sounded insulting."

"It was practical."

He stacked the Mason's Cut sheet with the others.

I said, "You don't care?"

"I care when people interfere with repayment."

I stared.

Antonius continued, "If the theatre pays you, and you pay me in labor or
coin, damage to the theatre can affect me."

"That is the nicest thing you've said to me."

"It was not intended kindly."

"Still counts."

"No."

I smiled despite myself.

He did not.

"Small share," Antonius said.

"Yes."

"After visible better work."

"Havel."

"And the other yard?"

"Good runs. Good coin."

He nodded once.

"The first amount tests whether payment is easier than refusal."

"And if they pay?"

"They have answered the question."

That was all.

No lecture.

No criminal map.

No revelation that Bren had once borrowed money from Antonius's cousin's
warehouse keeper.

Just arithmetic.

I looked at the papers.

"The cart yard paid once."

"And was asked for more."

"Yes."

"Then they answered clearly."

The clerk near the door called that first bell had passed.

Antonius checked the small sand timer on the table.

My hour was nearly gone.

He pointed at the corrected stacks.

"Two copper credit."

"Against the debt."

"Obviously."

"Cash would be more emotionally satisfying."

"You owe me."

"People keep remembering."

"I have a ledger."

"Yes."

He recorded the credit.

I did not see the remaining total.

I did not ask.

That felt like maturity or cowardice.

Possibly both.

As I stood, Antonius said, "If you get the other half of the name, tell
me."

"Why?"

"Because I asked."

"That isn't a reason."

"It is from this side of the table."

I put my crutches under me.

"Are you going to do something?"

"I have half a name and a story from my debtor about somebody else's
theatre."

"When you say it like that, it sounds weak."

"It is weak."

"Good."

"If it becomes relevant to my business, it may stop being weak."

There he was.

Not future Antonius.

Not yet.

But I could see the shape of the man who would eventually understand
that money moved through people before it moved through ledgers.

Or maybe I was cheating because I already knew.

"Do not make it relevant before it is," Antonius said.

I blinked.

"What?"

"Half a name. One story. Leave it there."

"That sounds like advice."

"It is accounting."

I left.

The Vale cart took me only as far as the central market because I told
the driver I had time.

He did not care.

Hessa's appointment was after midday.

I bought nothing.

This felt financially sophisticated.

At the Guild, the gray-coated woman saw me in the corridor.

"Paper Man."

"Comparative Paper Man."

She stopped.

"That is worse."

"I've been told."

Hessa had the same room prepared.

Same pale board.

Same chair mark.

Same glass.

Near remained marked.

Intermediate remained marked.

Far remained marked.

No one had improved the paper.

I sat.

Hessa looked at my hands.

"What did you do this morning?"

"Accounts."

"Physical."

"Sat."

"Before that?"

"Cart."

She looked up.

"Rode in one."

"Good."

"Lyssa made me."

"Also good."

"I dislike consensus."

She checked wrist rotation.

Fine.

Arm lift.

Fine.

Forearm.

Boring.

Shoulder.

Boring.

"Water?"

"Some."

She gave me more.

"This experiment has a lot of water."

"Your body continues to have a lot of water."

Apparently that joke had survived peer review.

She showed me today's page.

INTERMEDIATE / FAR.

AWAY ONLY.

SAME APPARATUS.

"We are not testing farther," she said.

"We are literally testing far."

"We are comparing the established intermediate position with the
existing far position."

"Which is farther."

"Do you want to go home?"

"No."

"Then be precise."

I drank water precisely.

She had already written the order on a folded slip and placed it face
down.

"You chose before I came?"

"Before you attempt."

"Why?"

"So I do not choose based on what happens."

That seemed unfair to outcomes.

She turned the slip.

INTERMEDIATE.

FAR.

FAR.

INTERMEDIATE.

"Four?"

"If your body stays boring."

"My body is deeply committed to boring."

"We will see."

Baseline first.

The thirtieth successful supervised minimal draw came without surprise.

The gathering was familiar now.

Small heat behind the sternum.

An inward pull that still felt less like grabbing something and more
like noticing a current already moving.

The wrist-side unevenness remained.

No increase.

No sudden reservoir.

No hidden second Greg.

Hessa watched.

"Enough."

I released.

She marked it.

Then the twenty-sixth supervised deliberate shaping attempt.

The gathered mana crowded toward the wrist side.

I tried to give it the rough deliberate form we had been using.

The shoulder wanted to join.

I lowered it.

The shape softened.

I did not chase.

It held badly for two breaths.

Then less badly for one.

Then it was gone.

Hessa wrote.

"Better?" I asked.

"Comparable."

"I miss praise."

"You have theatre."

Cruel.

External attempt thirty-one.

Intermediate.

Fresh matched fins.

Target and control.

Glass down.

Same chair.

Same foot.

Same hands.

Same request.

AWAY FROM GREG.

I gathered.

Shaped.

Kept it small.

Requested away.

Nothing.

Then the lower edge moved.

Tiny.

Clean.

Less than the width of my smallest fingernail.

Away.

Control unchanged.

Hessa checked.

"Response."

She wrote:

31. INTERMEDIATE.

TARGET DISPLACED AWAY.

SMALL.

CONTROL UNCHANGED.

I looked at Far.

She said, "Don't."

"I am allowed to look."

"Apparently not productively."

Fresh pair.

Attempt thirty-two.

Far.

The extra distance was not dramatic.

That was the annoying part.

The paper was still right there.

Reachable if I leaned.

Not reachable under the rules.

I gathered.

The shape was rougher.

I backed off.

Requested away.

Nothing.

I waited.

Nothing.

A shadow shifted.

I kept my head still this time.

Still nothing.

Hessa checked both fins.

"No observed response."

She wrote it.

I exhaled.

"Far remains rude."

"Far remains far."

"That was almost poetry."

"No."

Rest.

Water.

Hessa read another page while I sat with my hands open.

Someone outside asked for a frame.

Someone else said Soren had it.

A third person said Soren did not have it.

The frame developed a social life without us.

My forearm felt used.

Not tight.

Wrist fine.

Shoulder quiet.

Hessa checked anyway.

Then attempt thirty-three.

Far again.

Fresh pair.

Same everything.

I gathered.

The shape came cleaner at first.

Then tightened.

I let it become smaller.

Requested away.

For a breath, nothing.

Then the top edge turned.

Not much.

The lower corner shifted.

I stopped breathing.

Hessa leaned closer.

"Do not add."

I didn't.

The fin settled.

She checked from one side.

Then the other.

The target had moved.

Barely.

But the far corner now sat farther from me than it had.

The control had not changed.

Hessa looked at the marks.

Then at the paper.

"Observed."

My chest tightened for an entirely different reason.

"Far."

"One far response."

"Still far."

"Yes."

"At far."

"Yes."

I smiled.

She pointed at me.

"Do not make it larger."

"I haven't said anything."

"You are about to."

I was.

She wrote:

33. FAR.

SMALL ROTATIONAL RESPONSE.

NET DISPLACEMENT AWAY OBSERVED.

CONTROL UNCHANGED.

ONE OBSERVATION.

I read the last two words.

"You wrote that aggressively."

"I wrote it legibly."

Attempt thirty-four.

Intermediate.

Fresh pair.

My forearm was more obviously used now.

Still no pain.

Hessa made me rotate the wrist.

Fine.

Lift.

Fine.

"Last."

"I know."

"Say it."

"Last."

Same position.

Same request.

The gathering came.

Shape came rough.

I let it stay rough.

Requested away.

Nothing happened immediately.

Then the target slipped.

Small.

Straight enough that even I did not need to negotiate with the angle.

Away.

Control unchanged.

Hessa checked.

Then stopped the session.

No fifth attempt.

I did not ask.

This was growth.

Possibly.

She wrote for a while.

I drank water and tried not to turn one far movement into a kingdom.

Eventually she turned the page toward me.
