30TH SUCCESSFUL SUPERVISED MINIMAL DRAW.

26TH SUPERVISED DELIBERATE SHAPING ATTEMPT.

INTERMEDIATE / FAR.

AWAY ONLY.

ATTEMPT 31, INTERMEDIATE: AWAY RESPONSE OBSERVED. CONTROL UNCHANGED.

ATTEMPT 32, FAR: NO OBSERVED RESPONSE. CONTROL UNCHANGED.

ATTEMPT 33, FAR: SMALL ROTATIONAL RESPONSE WITH NET DISPLACEMENT AWAY
OBSERVED. CONTROL UNCHANGED.

ATTEMPT 34, INTERMEDIATE: AWAY RESPONSE OBSERVED. CONTROL UNCHANGED.

Then:

INTERMEDIATE RESPONSE REPEATED.

FAR RESPONSE OBSERVED ONCE TODAY.

FAR RESPONSE NOT ESTABLISHED.

INTERMEDIATE REMAINS MORE RELIABLE THAN FAR UNDER CURRENT CONDITIONS.

DISTANCE DEPENDENCE REMAINS SUPPORTED.

NO RANGE ESTABLISHED.

NOT GENERALIZED.

I pointed.

"So it reaches far."

"No."

"It moved at far."

"Once."

"That is a response at far."

"Yes."

"So it reaches far."

"No."

I stared at her.

She stared back.

"Your problem," I said, "is language."

"My problem is you."

Fair.

"What can I say?"

"That a response was observed once at the far position in the current
apparatus."

"That sentence needs food."

"It is accurate."

"Paper moved farther away than before."

"Paper at the far position responded once today."

"Somewhat less close to me."

She considered.

"Still bad."

"Progress."

"No."

She repeated the restrictions.

All of them.

No independent draw.

No independent shaping.

No external testing.

No paper.

No coins.

No thread.

No larger objects.

No practice.

No loopholes.

No date for next session.

No next question yet.

"Body?"

"Forearm used."

"Wrist?"

"Fine."

"Shoulder?"

"Fine."

She checked.

"Non-lifting work is fine if it stays non-lifting."

"Theatre has discovered chairs."

"Then avoid discovering them."

I left with counts that had become:

thirty successful supervised minimal draws.

Twenty-six supervised deliberate shaping attempts.

Thirty-four supervised external-effect attempts.

One far response.

Not far established.

Annoying.

Useful.

Both.

The afternoon house had already started when I reached the hall.

I checked the cart once.

Chalk straight.

Rinna saw me.

"Body?"

"Fine. No lifting."

She pointed at a stool beside the front table.

"Sit."

"I have become management."

"No."

There were six late arrivals during the next piece.

I took their copper.

One tried to argue the show was already partly over and therefore should
cost less.

I told him the remaining actors were still full size.

He paid.

Rinna heard.

She did not praise me.

I worked the front until the house ended.

No carrying.

No set.

No acting.

No Bren.

No broken pin.

No suspicious glove.

At close, Rinna gave me one copper.

I looked at it.

"For sitting?"

"For tickets."

"Vale gave me two copper credit for sitting."

"Go work for Vale."

"I owe him money."

"Then this seems difficult."

I took the copper.

At home, Lyssa had bread and a small bowl of cooked greens.

She looked at me.

"Vale?"

"Two copper off the debt."

"Good."

"Paper?"

"Four tries."

"How many?"

"Three responses."

She waited.

"Two intermediate. One far. One far did nothing."

"Far moved?"

"Once."

"Does that mean far works?"

I pointed at her.

"You've been talking to Hessa."

"No."

"It means a response was observed once at far."

Lyssa smiled.

I hated everyone.

"Theatre?"

"One copper."

"Bren?"

"No."

That was the whole day.

Debt moved.

Paper moved.

Tickets moved.

Bren did not.

For once, I was willing to count all four.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 214

## THE BROTHER

My forearm had become boring again.

This was good.

It was also rude after yesterday.

One piece of paper had moved at the far mark. Once. Hessa had spent
several lines making sure that one movement remained exactly one
movement, and my body had responded by becoming completely ordinary
overnight.

No ache in the wrist.

No shoulder complaint.

No mysterious new ability.

Nothing under the cup.

I checked once.

Lyssa was tying a narrow roll of pale cloth with blue thread.

"Nothing?" she asked.

"Nothing."

"You sound disappointed."

"I enjoy receiving instructions."

"No, you don't."

"Sometimes they prove people remember me."

She looked at me.

"Vale remembered you."

"That was debt."

"Still remembered."

I considered this.

"I preferred Hessa."

"Because she doesn't charge you?"

"She charges me dignity."

Lyssa put the cloth roll into her bag.

She had work near the west market, then somewhere beyond it. She did not
explain where. I did not ask.

This was becoming a skill.

She tore the last heel of bread in half and gave me one piece.

"Third bell?"

"Third bell."

"Bren?"

"Not scheduled."

"Good."

That was all.

She left.

I washed the cup.

I did not touch the paper under it.

At third bell, the theatre cart still had both wheels.

This was becoming how I greeted the building.

The cart sat farther inside than it used to, between the rear wall and a
stack of painted flats. Jori's chalk line across the replacement pin and
axle remained straight.

I looked.

I did not touch.

Pell was already beside me.

"Still there."

"Yes."

"I checked before you."

"Congratulations."

"I also checked the other wheel."

"Did it become dangerous?"

"No."

"Good work."

He nodded as if I meant it.

Marek came past carrying his King hat.

He held it with both hands.

Pell watched.

Marek stopped.

"Leave the hat alone."

"I didn't touch it."

"Keep that record."

Marek carried it backstage.

The King hat had apparently survived crime and become more valuable.

Rinna's board had three pieces.

EMPTY TABLE: VEYA / SELLEN / EVIN / GREG

WIDOW: HARA / NESSA / VEYA

SHORT: MAREK / PELL

GREG: BROTHER / SET

I pointed at Brother.

"Whose?"

Rinna looked up from a list.

"Veya's."

"In life?"

"In the play."

"Good. I wasn't prepared."

"You have six lines."

"Less prepared."

The Brother belonged to The Empty Table.

I had seen the role before, though not recently. He came in late,
complained that Veya's character had sent for him without saying why,
discovered that the family argument had already moved beyond him, and
left with somebody else's scarf.

The scarf was important.

Not emotionally.

It was simply the thing I had to remember.

Teren walked me through the entrance once.

"Door side. Not kitchen side."

"We don't have a kitchen side here."

"You still tried it last time."

"Memory is powerful."

"Use it on the correct entrance."

Nessa handed me a dull brown coat with a loose right cuff.

"This one."

I checked the label.

GREG.

"Mine."

"For today."

"Still counts."

She gave me the scarf.

Blue with a thin cream edge.

"Veya's chair. Then your hand. Then off."

"Why does he steal it?"

"He thinks it's his."

"Is it?"

"No."

"Good. Character depth."

Nessa left me.

The hall was normal.

Davin was checking a lamp bracket.

Jori was cutting two wicks to the same length and becoming angry that
one was not the same length.

Sellen and Evin were arguing over whether a written pause was actually
written.

Hara had brought a small paper packet of roasted seeds and was refusing
Pell access.

Nobody had been stabbed.

Nobody had burned anything.

Nobody had returned.

I was starting to believe the day might have poor dramatic instincts.

The knock came while I was moving the Empty Table table.

Not hard.

Three quick taps.

Rinna looked at the door.

So did everyone else.

That was new.

Before Bren, a knock had meant somebody wanted the door.

Now a knock had acquired options.

Rinna went herself.

She opened it only far enough to see out.

A boy stood there.

Maybe fourteen.

Maybe younger.

Thin arms. Dusty knees. A cord around one wrist. He held a folded piece
of rough cloth in one hand.

He looked past Rinna.

"Greg?"

Nobody moved.

The boy looked around again.

"One leg?"

Pell said, "That's him."

Rinna turned her head.

Pell looked offended.

"What? It is."

The boy held out the bundle.

"Man paid me to bring this."

Rinna did not take it.

"What man?"

"Said Bren."

The room got quieter.

Not silent.

Jori's knife still scraped once against wick.

Then stopped.

Rinna said, "You know him?"

The boy shook his head.

"Where did he find you?"

"South market."

"What did he look like?"

The boy shrugged.

"Man."

"Large?"

"Large enough."

"Coat?"

"Had one."

"Glove?"

The boy looked annoyed now.

"I didn't count his clothes."

Fair.

Rinna pointed at the bundle.

"What is it?"

"He didn't tell me."

"Did you open it?"

"No."

"Why not?"

"He paid me not to."

This was the first thing about the boy that made complete sense.

"How much?" Pell asked.

Rinna looked at him.

"What?"

Pell said, "Information."

The boy said, "One copper."

Pell looked impressed.

I was too.

The boy extended the bundle again.

"For Greg."

Rinna said, "No."

The boy stared at her.

"I have to give it."

"You can put it down."

"That's giving it."

"Fine."

He crouched and set it on the floor outside the door.

Then he remembered something.

"Bren says the share is still small."

Nobody answered.

The boy waited.

Rinna said, "Anything else?"

"No."

"Did he tell you to bring an answer?"

"No."

"Good."

She closed the door.

The boy's footsteps went away.

Pell moved first.

"One copper."

"The boy earned it," Rinna said.

Pell sighed.

Rinna waited a few breaths before opening the door again.

The bundle remained on the ground.

The boy was gone.

No one else stood immediately outside.

Rinna picked it up.

She did not hand it to me.

This seemed correct.

She put it on the front table and unfolded the cloth.

The iron pin inside was dirty.

Small.

Ordinary.

I knew what it was before Davin came over.

So did Jori.

Pell said, "That's ours."

"Probably," I said.

"He kept it."

"Apparently."

"That's rude."

Davin picked up the pin.

He turned it once.

There was a flattened mark along one side.

Jori leaned over.

"Nick."

Davin nodded.

I asked, "Certain?"

Davin looked at me.

"I made it."

"The first one?"

"Yes."

He set it beside the edge of the table.

Jori pointed to the nick.

"From the old axle edge. Before I cleaned it."

"So ours," Rinna said.

Davin nodded.

"Ours."

That changed the shape of things.

Not everything.

The boy had said a man calling himself Bren had paid him.

The boy had brought our missing pin.

The message had been about the share.

That was considerably less maybe than yesterday.

It was still not a surname.

It was not an organization.

It was not proof that Bren himself had pulled the pin with his own left
hand.

But somebody who wanted us to believe the message came from Bren had
possession of the pin.

That was enough to make my stomach feel colder.

Pell looked at the pin.

"He could have kept it."

"Yes," I said.

"Why give it back?"

Because the pin was not the point.

The point was that he had touched the cart.

The point was that he could prove it.

The point was that returning a cheap piece of iron cost him almost
nothing and made us imagine the next thing he might choose not to
return.

I did not say all of that.

Rinna folded the cloth over the pin.

"What do we send back?" I asked.

"Nothing."

I looked at her.

"Nothing?"

"Nothing."

"He addressed it to me."

"I noticed."

"That feels like it deserves something."

"No."

Pell said, "We could send his copper back."

"We don't have his copper," Rinna said.

"The boy does."

"Then especially no."

She carried the bundle to the small shelf near her papers.

The threat note was still there.

BREN?

LEFT GLOVE.

CART YARD.

PAID ONCE. MORE.

Rinna added one line beneath it.

PIN RETURNED.

MESSAGE IN BREN'S NAME.

She did not write BREN PULLED PIN.

Good.

I said, "Vale told me to tell him if I got the other half of the name."

Rinna looked at me.

"Did you?"

"No."

"Then don't."

"Bren returned a pin."

"Vale asked for a name."

"Yes."

"Good."

I disliked when everyone became Hessa.

Rinna turned away.

Then turned back.

"You made yourself part of this."

"I was already standing there."

"You made him think you were more than standing there."

I had no useful answer.

She was right.

The bluff had bought us time.

It had also apparently changed who Bren thought he was negotiating with.

I had wanted one man to hesitate.

I had not intended to receive mail.

Rinna pointed toward the stage.

"Table."

Right.

Theatre.

The Empty Table table was still halfway between the wall and where it
belonged.

I moved it.

Not alone.

Davin took the heavier end because Hessa had said no lifting yesterday
and because apparently I was now surrounded by people who listened when
told things.

We set it.

Teren checked the spacing.

"Too far left."

Of course.

We moved it six inches.

Crime could not protect me from furniture.

By the time the doors opened, the returned pin was off the front table.

The replacement pin remained in the cart.

The chalk remained straight.

The audience knew none of this.

They came in wanting seats.

One woman wanted to know whether Widow was playing.

A man with a red nose wanted the aisle.

Two boys argued over who had paid last time.

An older woman brought her own cushion and treated our bench as a
hostile surface she had prepared to defeat.

I worked front until Rinna sent me backstage.

My brown Brother coat waited on the hook.

The blue scarf waited on Veya's chair.

The pin waited somewhere near Rinna's papers.

I tried not to think about any of them.

The Empty Table began.

Veya and Sellen had the first exchange.

Evin came in with the letter.

The house was not large, but it was awake.

That mattered.

I stood in the wing.

Door side.

Correct side.

Teren touched two fingers together.

My cue.

I entered.

"You sent for me."

Veya looked at me.

"I sent for family."

"You should have been more specific."

A small laugh.

Good.

Sellen turned.

"Are you her brother?"

"Only when required."

Another laugh.

The written line after that was about the table.

I knew it.

I had known it ten minutes earlier.

Then I looked toward Veya's chair.

The scarf was there.

Blue.

Cream edge.

And for half a second the folded shape reminded me of the cloth around
the pin.

My line disappeared.

Not dramatically.

It simply ceased to exist.

Veya waited exactly long enough to make the audience think the pause
belonged to us.

Then she said, "My brother has always needed instructions."

There it was.

"Only the useful ones," I said.

Not the line.

Close enough to the scene.

Sellen said, "Then sit."

That got me back.

I sat.

The next written line arrived where it belonged.

No disaster.

No breakthrough.

Backstage, Teren did not say anything.

That was worse.

I remembered the scarf.

At least I did that.

Near the end, Veya threw it over the chair while arguing with Evin.

I picked it up.

"This is mine."

Veya said, "It is not."

"Then why do I recognize it?"

"Because you've stolen it before."

That was written.

I left with the scarf.

The audience laughed.

Backstage, Nessa took it from me.

"You missed."

"One line."

"I know."

"Teren knows."

"I know."

"Everyone knows."

"Probably."

Comforting.

Widow went next.

The strongest moment had nothing to do with us.

A little girl in the second row did it.

Hara stood near the front and asked Veya, "Do you expect me to believe
that?"

The line was rhetorical.

The girl answered.

"No."

The audience laughed before any actor did.

Hara stopped.

Looked at the girl.

Then back at Veya.

"Good. We have found one sensible person."

Veya turned toward the child.

"She's too young to understand."

The girl said, "No."

That was worse.

Hara put a hand to her chest.

Veya stared at the ceiling.

Nessa, who was supposed to cross behind them with a folded shawl,
stopped halfway and said, "Do you want her removed?"

The girl's mother was already laughing too hard to object.

Veya said, "No. She has earned her seat."

The whole house went.

The girl sat straighter.

Hara nearly lost the next line.

She got it back.

The scene continued.

Nobody backstage could do anything except wait until the laughter came
down.

Pell whispered, "Hire her."

Marek said, "No."

"Why?"

"She'll take my hat."

Pell considered this a serious concern.

Widow finished strong.

Short followed.

Marek and Pell did their usual work without a child taking over the
government.

Between pieces I passed Rinna.

"What was the line?" she asked.

I told her.

"Why'd you lose it?"

I looked at the shelf where the pin was no longer visible.

"Thinking."

"Do less."

"People keep recommending that."

"Listen."

Then she was gone.

Short got its laughs.

Pell missed one of his own cues because he was looking at the wrong side
of the room.

This improved my mood.

At close, the audience left with no idea that a piece of cart hardware
had occupied more of my attention than six written lines.

We swept.

We stacked.

Jori checked the replacement pin.

Chalk straight.

Davin checked the rear door.

Normal.

Nobody had taken a lamp.

Nobody had blocked the entrance.

No boy returned.

No Bren.

Rinna paid me one copper.

I looked at it.

"Still one."

"You forgot a line."

"I recovered."

"Veya recovered."

"Teamwork."

"One."

I took it.

Pell was still thinking about the returned pin.

"He paid a copper to return our own property."

"Yes."

"That's bad business."

"It wasn't a sale."

"He could have just brought it himself."

"No," Rinna said.

Pell looked at her.

She tied the small bundle closed again.

"He could have."

That landed differently.

Bren had chosen not to come through the door.

Maybe because he believed something about me.

Maybe because he doubted it.

Maybe because he wanted to see what we did.

Maybe because he had something else to do.

Those were all stories.

The only fact was that he had sent a boy.

Rinna put the pin away.

"We leave."

We did.

Not in one frightened knot.

Not alone either.

The company naturally broke into overlapping directions.

Marek and Pell went east for part of the route.

Veya and Nessa south.

I went west with Sellen until his turn.

No one followed that I could see.

I did not keep looking after every corner.

That was how you supplied your own extra men.

At home, Lyssa was later than me.

I had time to cut the remaining onion before she arrived.

This was dangerous because I was not good at cutting onions evenly.

She came in carrying an empty cloth bag and looked at the pieces.

"What happened?"

"To the onion?"

"To you."

Apparently my face remained a security failure.

"Bren sent the cart pin back."

She stopped.

"The missing one?"

"Davin says yes."

"Did Bren bring it?"

"No."

"Who did?"

"Boy. Runner, I think."

"He knows Bren?"

"Says a man called Bren paid him one copper in the south market."

"What did he say?"

"'The share is still small.'"

Lyssa put the bag down.

"To Rinna?"

"To me."

Her eyes came back to my face.

"Why you?"

"I don't know."

"You know some possibilities."

"Yes."

"Those aren't the same."

Everyone had become Hessa.

I put the onion in the pot.

"Rinna sent nothing back."

"Good."

"I asked."

"Of course you did."

"I thought maybe silence looked weak."

"Does it?"

"I don't know."

That was the problem.

I knew what I had said to Bren the first time.

I knew what Pell had put behind the panel.

I knew what Bren had seen.

I did not know what he had built from it.

Now he had sent something specifically to Greg.

Not the Guild man.

Not the gray coat.

Not Rinna.

Greg.

Maybe that meant he had learned enough to doubt the rest.

Maybe it meant he believed it more.

Maybe he wanted me to wonder.

Lyssa added beans to the onion.

"Vale?"

"No."

"You didn't tell him?"

"He asked for the other half of Bren's name."

"And?"

"I have a pin."

"Not a name."

"Exactly."

She nodded.

No Hessa note waited under the cup.

I did not check twice.

The counts remained thirty, twenty-six, thirty-four.

One far response remained one far response.

The theatre had both wheels.

The door still opened.

The lamps were still ours.

Bren had spent one copper to return a piece of iron worth less than the
message attached to it.

I had made him uncertain.

Now he was making sure I knew he was still there.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 215

## THE BAILIFF

Nothing happened in the morning.

This was becoming suspicious.

No runner.

No cart pin.

No note under the cup.

No one knocked on the door and asked whether I had reconsidered paying
criminals.

Lyssa had already left by the time I finished washing because a woman
near the west market had decided two repaired cuffs were not the same
length.

"They were the same length yesterday," Lyssa had said.

"Are they the same length today?"

"Yes."

"Then this seems easy."

She had looked at me for a long moment.

"You're very good with customers you don't have."

Then she left with the cuffs.

My body was ordinary.

Hessa had not summoned me.

Vale had not summoned me.

Bren had not summoned me.

For a few hours, nobody wanted anything.

I almost enjoyed it.

At third bell, the replacement cart pin remained exactly where Jori had
left it.

The chalk line was straight.

I looked once.

Pell came up beside me.

"You know we can both look."

"I know."

"You're making it feel competitive."

"Then lose."

He checked the other wheel.

I did not.

Growth.

The original pin was no longer on Rinna's shelf in plain sight. I knew
she had put it away somewhere with the little cloth bundle. I did not
ask where.

If Bren wanted us to think about it, making him work harder seemed
polite.

The hall smelled like dust, old paint, lamp oil, and something Hara was
eating out of a paper twist.

Nessa had two costume boxes open.

Jori had the rear door propped while Davin carried in a narrow painted
post.

Marek was wearing the King hat.

Pell looked at it.

Marek looked at Pell.

"No."

"I didn't move."

"You leaned."

"That isn't illegal."

"It is now."

Rinna was at the front table with a woman I had not seen before.

The woman wore a dark green overshirt over work clothes and had a narrow
leather purse hanging crosswise under one arm. Not rich. Not poor enough
to pretend money did not matter. She had ink on two fingers and a little
flour on one sleeve.

Rinna saw me.

"Greg."

I went over.

"This is Sera Venn."

Not Havel Sera.

Different Sera.

Carrow had apparently begun recycling names to save money.

The woman nodded.

"Venn is fine."

"Greg."

"I gathered."

She looked at my leg.

At least she did not say Guild.

Rinna said, "Venn keeps accounts for the Copper Kettle."

I knew the place.

Not well.

A tavern and eating room north of the market, wide front, covered side
yard, cheap stew, respectable beer according to people who drank more
beer than I did.

Venn said, "Owner's daughter is marrying a cooper."

"Congratulations to the cooper," I said.

Venn's mouth moved slightly.

"Maybe."

Rinna ignored me.

"They want entertainment after supper. Two pieces. Short. Covered yard
if weather holds, main room if not."

"When?" I asked.

"Tomorrow," Rinna said.

That got my attention.

Venn added, "Second bell after midday. Guests eat first. You play
after."

"How many?"

"Forty. Maybe fifty if cousins become real."

"How much room?" Rinna asked.

Venn described it with her hands.

Longer than our stage.

Narrower.

One support post in the middle of the covered yard.

Three tables that could move.

Two that could not.

No raised stage.

One side against a wall.

One side open to the lane.

Rinna asked about lamps.

Venn said they had six wall lamps and two hanging.

"Oil?"

"Ours."

"Benches?"

"Ours."

"Food?"

"Company eats after first piece."

That improved Pell's posture from across the room.

Rinna asked, "Payment?"

Venn looked at me.

Then at Rinna.

Rinna did not move.

Venn leaned closer and told her quietly.

I could not hear.

That was new.

Not secret enough to require another room.

Just not spoken across the hall.

Rinna asked two questions about when and who paid.

Venn answered.

Then Rinna said, "We'll do it."

I looked at her.

She looked back.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Say it."

"Are we taking the cart?"

Venn's eyes moved between us.

Rinna said, "We haven't decided."

Venn said, "You need it?"

"Maybe."

"We have stools."

"Not the same thing."

"Nothing is."

Venn gave Rinna a small folded paper with the tavern name, tomorrow's
time, and the owner's mark.

Then she left because she had actual work.

No ominous pause.

No strange glance toward the cart.

No secret Bren tattoo.

She just left.

Pell came over before the door finished closing.

"Food?"

"After first piece," I said.

"I heard."

"Then why ask?"

"I wanted confirmation."

Rinna unfolded the paper again.

Teren joined us.

So did Nessa.

Marek remained farther back because he was already dressed and had
discovered responsibility.

Rinna said, "Two pieces. Tomorrow. Copper Kettle."

Nessa asked, "Which?"

"King and Widow."

"Not Empty Table?"

"No."

Pell looked wounded.

"Short?"

"No."

"Why are we going?"

"Money."

That answered most theatre questions.

Teren asked about space.

Rinna repeated Venn's description.

Nessa asked about changing.

Small back room near kitchen.

Davin asked what had to travel.

That was when the conversation changed.

Not much.

Just enough.

Before Bren, the answer would have been a list.

Now Rinna looked toward the cart.

"Minimum."

Davin nodded.

"King platform?"

"No."

Marek said, "Good."

Pell said, "Coward."

"The floor exists," Rinna said. "Use it."

Nessa said, "Two costume boxes."

"One."

"I need two."

"One."

"Then someone wears their own trousers."

Pell looked down at his trousers.

"They're theatrical."

"No," Nessa said.

Jori asked, "Painted wall?"

Teren shook his head.

"Too much."

"Door?"

"No."

"Table?"

"The Kettle has tables."

Veya had come in without me noticing.

"Do their tables know the lines?"

"They'll learn."

Hara arrived behind her.

"Are we traveling?"

"Tomorrow," Pell said. "Food."

Rinna said, "Work."

"Food at work."

"Yes."

Hara accepted this.

I looked at the cart.

One costume box.

A prop basket.

Maybe lamps if the tavern's lighting proved bad, but Venn said they had
lamps.

No platform.

No painted wall.

No door.

They could probably hand-carry everything if enough people went.

Rinna was already counting.

"Davin?"

"Carry."

"Jori?"

"Carry."

"Nessa?"

"Costumes."

"Teren?"

"Front and space."

She kept going.

No guards.

No weapons.

No one proposed hiring six large men with matching sticks.

Just fewer things.

I said, "No cart?"

Rinna looked at Davin.

Davin said, "Don't need it."

Jori nodded.

"Not for that."

Rinna said, "Then no cart."

That solved one problem by leaving it inside.

"What about pay?" Pell asked.

Rinna looked at him.

"You'll know your share."

"When?"

"When I pay you."

"Can I know the company amount?"

"No."

"Could I guess?"

"You can do anything quietly."

Pell thought.

Marek said, "Don't."

"I haven't guessed yet."

"You're leaning."

Apparently leaning remained illegal.

Rinna tapped Venn's paper.

"We go together. We leave together. Nobody tells the street what they're
paying."

Hara opened her paper twist.

"Can we tell the street they're feeding us?"

"No."

"That's cruel."

"It's stew."

"You don't know."

"I know the Kettle."

That settled it.

Nobody said Bren.

Nobody needed to.

The cart stayed home.

The payment stayed between Rinna and the client.

The company would move together.

All of those choices could have been ordinary.

Yesterday made them less ordinary.

Rinna turned to today's board.

"Now stop doing tomorrow."

That was fair.

Today's board read:

DEBTOR'S UNCLE: EVIN / VEYA / SELLEN / GREG

KING: MAREK / HARA / EVIN

SHORT: NESSA / PELL

GREG: BAILIFF / SET

I stared at Bailiff.

"Am I collecting money?"

"No," Rinna said.

"Good."

"Why?"

"No reason."

Teren said, "You bring the notice."

"What notice?"

"The debt notice."

I looked at him.

He looked at me.

Neither of us said anything.

"That's unfortunate," I said.

"Five lines."

"More unfortunate."

The Bailiff entered halfway through Debtor's Uncle with a folded notice,
asked which man was responsible for the debt, was pointed toward three
different people, became increasingly irritated, and finally left the
paper with Veya because she was the only person who would take it.

I had seen Pell do it once.

Pell had played the Bailiff like a man personally offended by
handwriting.

Teren gave me a plain dark vest.

"No coat."

"Authority is shrinking."

"Good."

The notice was a folded sheet with meaningless marks.

Real paper.

No mana.

I held it normally.

This was not difficult.

We walked the entrance twice because today's stool sat where I wanted my
crutch.

The first time I clipped it.

The second time I moved around it.

Teren moved the stool two inches anyway.

"That was my solution."

"It was a bad solution."

"It worked."

"It made noise."

"Temporary applause."

"No."

The house came in normally.

No one asked for Guild.

No boy came back.

A Vale cart passed the open front while I was taking one copper from an
older man.

I recognized the painted side mark.

It continued down the street.

Nobody inside it noticed me.

Antonius existed without participating.

This felt healthy.

Debtor's Uncle started.

I waited in the wing with the notice.

Evin complained about the debt.

Sellen denied owing it.

Veya said the house owed everyone.

That got a laugh.

My cue came.

I entered without hitting the stool.

Professional.

"I have a notice."

Evin pointed at Sellen.

"For him."

Sellen pointed at Evin.

"For him."

I looked between them.

"Which of you is poorer?"

Veya said, "Yes."

Good laugh.

That was written.

I opened the notice.

There was nothing useful on it.

Also written.

"Someone owes twelve copper."

Evin said, "Then it cannot be me."

"Why?"

"I owe more."

That landed.

Sellen looked offended.

"Braggart."

I turned to him.

"Your name?"

He gave the wrong name.

I looked at the paper.

"That is unfortunate."

"Why?"

"I can't read this one either."

Small laugh.

The next part was simple.

I was supposed to hand the notice to Veya.

Instead, I handed it to Evin.

No Bren.

No threat.

No traumatic folded cloth.

I simply gave it to the wrong person.

Evin looked down at it.

Then at me.

Veya held out her hand.

I took it back from Evin and gave it to her.

"Administrative correction."

The audience laughed more than the line deserved.

Veya said, "Do I owe it now?"

"Apparently."

"Efficient."

We moved on.

Backstage, Teren said, "Veya."

"I found her."

"Eventually."

"Administrative process."

He walked away.

Normal failure.

Beautiful.

King went next.

The strongest moment belonged to a leaking cup.

Not a magical cup.

Not a prepared effect.

A bad cup.

Hara poured water into it before going on.

Nobody noticed the thin crack near the bottom.

Marek sat in the King chair.

Evin entered with the cup.

Set it beside him.

Water began creeping across the table.

Slowly.

Marek kept talking.

Hara saw it first.

She was supposed to accuse Evin of stealing a ribbon.

Instead, while Marek spoke, she moved the cup three inches toward him.

The water followed.

Marek noticed.

He moved it back.

Still speaking.

Hara moved it toward him again.

The audience started laughing before Evin understood why.

Evin looked at the cup.

Then at Marek.

"Your Majesty leaks."

Marek looked down at himself.

"I do not."

Hara pointed.

"Your table disagrees."

Marek lifted the cup.

Water dripped onto his sleeve.

He stared at it.

Then handed it to Evin.

"Arrest the cup."

Evin held it away from his body.

"On what charge?"

"Treason."

That broke the room.

Hara had to turn away.

Marek kept his face straight.

Mostly.

The cup continued leaking onto the floor until Davin reached from the
wing and took it during a crossing.

No one died.

The King survived.

The table required a cloth.

Short followed with Nessa and Pell.

Pell had spent the entire interval asking whether the cup would be
traveling tomorrow.

Nessa told him no.

He asked if the child from yesterday would be traveling tomorrow.

No.

King hat?

Marek said no from across the room.

Pell had become very easy to manage.

At close, Rinna paid me one copper.

Normal local day.

I put it away.

No danger pay.

No private-job money yet.

Tomorrow's work did not pay today.

This also felt healthy.

Then tomorrow invaded again.

Nessa packed the costume box.

One.

She was unhappy about it.

"Veya's second dress stays."

Veya said, "Good."

"You say that now."

"I hate that dress."

"You look good in it."

"I hate it attractively."

Nessa accepted this as possible.

Davin and Jori put together the prop basket.

No cart.

Teren wrote the two-piece order.

KING.

WIDOW.

Rinna checked the client paper again.

"Meet here before midday," she said. "We walk from here."

Pell asked, "How long?"

"Long enough."

"Food?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"Do not tell anyone what they're paying."

"I don't know what they're paying."

"Excellent."

The cart remained inside.

Jori checked the replacement pin anyway.

Chalk straight.

He caught me watching.

"Still there."

"I know."

"You want to check?"

"No."

He looked disappointed.

Apparently my restraint was ruining community.

We left in the usual pieces.

Nothing happened.

No runner.

No lookout I could prove.

No man in a heavy coat waiting at the corner.

No one asked where we were playing tomorrow.

I passed a produce stall and bought two small carrots because they were
cheap and because Lyssa had recently demonstrated that food could
contain colors other than brown.

At home, she had not returned yet.

I cut the carrots more evenly than yesterday's onion.

This was progress nobody would record.

Lyssa came in after dark enough that I had started the beans.

She put her bag down harder than usual.

"Same length?" I asked.

"Yes."

"Did she agree?"

"Eventually."

"What changed?"

"I measured them in front of her."

"Were they the same?"

"Yes."

"So the measuring changed nothing."

"It changed her."

That sounded familiar.

She washed her hands.

"Your day?"

"New job tomorrow."

She looked over.

"Outside?"

"Copper Kettle. Wedding. Two pieces."

"Late?"

"Probably not very. We go after midday. Food."

"Cart?"

"No."

The question came immediately.

Before Bren, I was not sure it would have.

"Don't need it," I said. "One costume box. Prop basket. Tavern has
tables and lamps."

"Pay?"

"Rinna knows."

"You don't?"

"No."

"Good."

I looked at her.

"Why is that good?"

"Because you talk."

"Only when necessary."

She looked at me.

"Sometimes while asleep."

"Unverified."

She tore one carrot piece from the board.

"Bren?"

"Nothing."

"Pin?"

"Still with Rinna. Replacement still in cart."

"Vale?"

"Passed one of his carts."

"Did it rescue you?"

"No."

"Bad cart."

"Hessa?"

"Nothing."

She ate the carrot.

"Then wedding."

"Wedding."

It was good work.

That was the problem.

Not because work was dangerous.

Because good work was visible.

Havel had been visible.

The cart yard's good runs had been visible.

Tomorrow we would leave the hall carrying costumes and props and come
back after feeding fifty people laughter for money.

The easiest answer would have been not to go.

Rinna had not even seriously considered it.

Neither had I.

We needed the work.

More than that, the company wanted it.

Bren had asked for a share of something we had not earned yet.

Tomorrow we were going to earn it anyway.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 216

## THE RELATIVE

I counted my money before breakfast.

Not all of it.

That would have required knowing exactly where all of it was, which was
a level of organization I had not achieved.

I counted what I had in the little cloth fold Lyssa had made me stop
calling a purse because, according to her, it was not a purse.

"It holds money."

"So does a mouth."

"Not well."

"Exactly."

She was tying back her hair while I put yesterday's copper with the
others.

The two copper Antonius had credited against my debt were not there.

That was the point.

I had worked for an hour and become two copper less poor without
becoming one copper richer.

In my old life, I had spent years thinking about money as a number that
got larger if I did things correctly.

Here it had become more physical.

Bread.

Oil.

Carrots.

A repaired grip.

A theatre copper.

A private-job copper.

A debt copper that never touched my hand.

And somewhere in Antonius Vale's ledger was a number I still did not
know exactly, becoming smaller slowly enough to remain important.

I folded the cloth.

Lyssa watched me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You were making the money face."

"I don't have a money face."

"You do."

"What does it look like?"

"Like you're trying to argue with arithmetic."

"I usually win."

"How much today?"

"I don't know."

"You don't know what they're paying?"

"Rinna knows."

"Good."

"You said that yesterday."

"It stayed good."

I put the money away.

The Copper Kettle job might pay me two copper.

It might pay one.

It might pay three, although that seemed optimistic enough to become
religion.

Whatever it paid, some part of me had already started dividing it.

Food.

House.

Keep.

Debt.

Except the debt did not work like that. Antonius did not want me
arriving with random coppers whenever guilt became numerically
inconvenient. He called labor when he had labor that fit me, recorded
credit, and continued owning the problem.

It was an unpleasantly efficient relationship.

Lyssa picked up her work bag.

"You meeting them before midday?"

"Yes."

"Then don't be late."

"I'm never late."

She looked at me.

"I have been late in several very specific ways."

"That is still late."

She left.

There was nothing under the cup.

I checked.

No note.

No Hessa.

No magic.

Today I was going to a wedding.

That felt safer until I remembered weddings involved families.

At the theatre, everyone had arrived early enough to make the room look
as if it were being robbed by professionals.

One costume box sat near the front.

One prop basket sat beside it.

Nothing else was allowed to travel.

Nessa stood over the costume box with the expression of a woman being
forced to choose which children survived winter.

"I need the blue wrap."

Rinna said, "Then take out something else."

"I need everything else."

"Then you don't need the blue wrap."

"This is why art dies."

Pell carried the prop basket.

"Art is heavy."

"Put that down."

"I was helping."

"You were bending the handle."

He put it down.

The cart remained farther inside.

Jori's chalk line was straight.

I looked once.

He saw me.

"Good?"

"Good."

That was all.

Rinna gave assignments.

Davin and Jori took the costume box between them for the longer
stretches.

Pell and I shared the prop basket, which meant Pell carried it whenever
the street was smooth and handed it to me whenever he wanted to point at
food.

Teren carried rolled cloth.

Nessa carried a smaller personal bundle she had somehow produced after
Rinna said one costume box.

Rinna stared at it.

"Personal."

"That word does work," Rinna said.

We left together.

Not in formation.

Not as a defensive column.

A theatre company walking through Carrow with too much cloth.

The north market was busy enough that we became ordinary almost
immediately.

A fish seller shouted at a cart driver.

Two boys ran between us and nearly took Pell with them.

Someone was unloading sacks of grain.

A Vale cart crossed the next street ahead of us, brown side mark visible
for a moment before it turned.

I watched it go.

Antonius had two copper more of me than he had three days ago.

Or two copper less of my debt.

Both were true.

Neither helped carry the basket.

Pell handed it to me.

"Important question."

"No."

"You don't know it."

"Still no."

"Do you think wedding food is better than funeral food?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

"People are happier."

"That doesn't affect stew."

"It affects portions."

He considered this.

"That's good."

The Copper Kettle had copper-colored paint on its hanging sign and no
actual copper kettle visible from the street.

Fraud.

The covered yard was already crowded.

Venn had underestimated the number of cousins who became real.

Tables filled the long space. One fixed table ran along the wall. Two
others had been shifted toward the open side. The support post stood
almost exactly where any sensible actor would want to stand.

Children occupied the floor as if they had been assigned to prevent
movement.

The bride wore a pale green dress with stitched yellow flowers at the
cuffs. The groom was broad through the shoulders and had the forearms of
a man who made barrels and expected them to remain barrels.

Neither looked miserable.

Promising.

Venn found Rinna.

"Fifty-three."

Rinna looked at the room.

"That post moved?"

"No."

"Shame."

"We tried asking."

"Didn't listen?"

"Old timber."

Teren walked the space.

Not metaphorically.

He actually walked it.

From the wall.

Around the post.

To the open lane.

Back.

He pointed at one table.

"That moves."

Venn said, "After the bread."

"Why?"

"Grandmother."

I looked.

An elderly woman sat at the table with both hands around a cup.

She looked capable of defeating us.

Teren nodded.

"After the bread."

We had no wings.

We had a kitchen passage, a narrow strip behind the wall-side table, and
the open lane.

The lane could work as an entrance if no cart came through at the wrong
time.

The kitchen passage could work if servers did not need it.

They did.

Constantly.

Nessa said, "We're changing there?"

Venn pointed to a back room.

Nessa inspected it.

"Fine."

This was high praise.

King went first.

I had a small Relative role.

Apparently weddings had infected casting.

The Relative belonged to Hara's side of the court, entered once to
complain that the King had ignored a family obligation, stayed for six
lines, and returned near the end to discover the obligation had been
assigned to someone else.

I had seen Sellen do it.

Teren had given me the role because Sellen was needed elsewhere around
the room.

"Entrance from lane," Teren said.

"Through actual outside?"

"Yes."

"What if someone is there?"

"Go around them."

Advanced stagecraft.

We tested it.

I came in from the lane.

Half the audience area could see me.

The other half got the support post.

Teren moved me two steps farther before my first line.

"Here."

"That's almost in the audience."

"Yes."

"Good."

"No."

I had become comfortable enough with theatre to know that "stage" meant
whatever patch of floor everyone had agreed not to eat on.

The problem was getting everyone else to agree.

Before the first piece, Venn's people cleared two tables.

Grandmother remained.

Teren built the action around her.

The King chair was a tavern chair with a folded cloth over the back.

Marek sat in it.

Pell whispered, "Reduced throne."

Marek heard him.

"Reduced Pell."

Then we started.

Wedding audiences were terrible.

They were also excellent.

They talked.

They drank.

They recognized one another inside jokes that were not about them.

A man near the wall repeated one of Marek's lines a breath before Marek
said it because he had apparently seen King before.

Marek stopped.

Looked at him.

The man grinned.

Marek said, "Would you like the chair?"

The man shook his head.

"Then allow me the burden."

That got the room.

The man stopped helping.

Hara came in hard enough to pull the attention back.

Evin worked wider than he did at the hall, turning his body around the
support post so each side of the yard got pieces of him.

I watched.

My cue came.

I went through the lane.

A server carrying three cups came through at the same time.

We both stopped.

She moved left.

I moved left.

She moved right.

I moved right.

The nearest table laughed.

I said, "Family first."

She said, "Beer first."

The whole near side laughed.

I stepped back.

She passed.

Then I entered.

"Your Majesty."

Marek looked at me.

"Late."

"Delayed by a stronger authority."

Another laugh.

Not huge.

Mine.

I liked it.

Then I made the mistake.

I planted where Teren had told me during the empty-space test.

Except the guests had shifted.

The bride's uncle had moved his chair.

A child sat on the floor.

The support post now hid me from almost an entire table.

I delivered two lines to people who could hear a Relative but could not
see one.

Hara crossed behind me and touched my shoulder as part of the scene.

She kept the hand there half a beat longer.

Pressure.

Move.

I moved two steps.

Faces appeared around the post.

Right.

I finished.

No one died.

The strongest moment came later.

A guest raised his cup during Hara's accusation.

Not to interrupt.

He was trying to toast the bride from the side of the yard.

"To Lessa!"

Half the room answered.

The play stopped because fifty-three people had decided it did.

Hara looked at the bride.

Then at the groom.

Then at Marek.

Marek lifted the King's cup.

"Apparently the court recognizes a higher marriage."

The groom laughed.

Hara took the cup from Marek.

"Then the court can pay for it."

She handed it to the groom.

The groom said, "I already did."

That destroyed the room.

Even Rinna laughed.

Marek leaned back in his tavern throne and waited until they returned
the play to him.

Experienced people made interruption look planned.

I made it look survivable.

Different skill levels.

After King, we ate.

Pell had been correct.

Wedding stew was better.

There was pork in it.

Actual pieces.

Bread still warm enough to tear instead of negotiate with.

Pickled greens.

A little dish of onions with vinegar.

We sat against the wall and ate quickly while guests argued about
whether the groom's brother had cheated at some game I did not
understand.

I took a second piece of bread.

Then stopped.

Not because I was full.

Because I had started thinking about what bread cost.

This was irritating.

Two copper debt credit.

One copper yesterday.

Whatever today paid.

Bren wanted a share.

Antonius already had one, except his was legal, older, and attached to a
ledger I had agreed to while having more legs.

The comparison was unfair to Antonius.

Which was a sentence I disliked.

He had loaned me value.

I owed him value back.

Bren had loaned us nothing.

That difference mattered.

The amount could be small and still be theft.

Maybe especially then.

A small demand was designed to make refusal feel more expensive than
surrender.

Antonius had said something close to that.

I ate the bread.

Pell looked at me.

"You done?"

"No."

"Good."

"Why?"

"I was going to ask for it."

Widow started after the tables were pushed back again.

I had no role.

I helped Nessa with the change, carried the prop basket behind the wall
table, then stayed near the open side where I could move a stool if
someone blocked Veya's path.

The wedding crowd was louder now.

Fed.

Some drinking.

Children more certain they owned the building.

Veya handled them like weather.

Nessa came in with the widow's things.

Hara followed.

A boy crawled under the wall table during a quiet line.

Veya saw him.

She did not look down.

She shifted one foot so he could pass.

Nessa nearly stepped on him, saw him at the last instant, and turned the
near-stumble into a sharp little spin toward Hara.

The audience laughed.

Hara had no idea why for half a second.

Then the boy emerged behind her.

She looked down.

He froze.

Hara said, "Are you mine?"

The boy shook his head.

"Excellent."

She pointed toward the audience.

"Return to whoever failed to keep you."

The boy ran.

His mother laughed harder than anyone.

The rest of Widow went clean.

Not quiet.

Clean enough.

When it ended, the bride hugged Hara.

The groom shook Marek's hand.

Pell accepted congratulations despite not having been in either piece.

We packed.

One costume box.

One prop basket.

The missing blue wrap had not killed art.

Venn found Rinna near the front room.

The owner came with her.

Payment changed hands discreetly.

I did not see how much.

Good.

I was getting better at not knowing things.

Then Venn said, "I thought you'd have a cart."

Rinna stopped folding the client paper.

"Why?"

Venn shrugged.

"Man asked this morning."

The room around us remained a wedding.

Someone shouted for more beer.

A child cried because another child had taken something.

Pell was trying to acquire wrapped bread.

Rinna said, "What did he ask?"

"If the theatre people were coming by cart."

"Those words?"

"Near enough."

"Did he give a name?"

"No."

"Know him?"

"No."

"What did he look like?"

Venn thought.

"Man."

Rinna waited.

"Brown coat. Maybe. Hair."

"Helpful."

"I keep accounts. I don't paint portraits."

"Anything else?"

"He asked. I said I didn't know."

"Then?"

"He bought beer."

That complicated criminality.

"Which hand?" I asked.

Rinna looked at me.

Venn looked at me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

I stopped.

I had promised myself not to stare at everyone's hands.

Now I was interviewing beverages.

Rinna said, "Did he say why?"

"No."

That was all.

Unknown man.

Asked about cart.

Bought beer.

Could be Bren.

Could be someone who liked carts.

Could be a man whose entire morning had been destroyed by curiosity.

We did not know.

Rinna said, "All right."

Venn looked at her.

"Is that bad?"

"Don't know."

"Good."

"Why is that good?"

"I have a wedding."

Fair.

We left together.

The walk back felt different because now I knew someone had asked.

That did not make the streets different.

A man with a sack remained a man with a sack.

Two women arguing over cloth remained two women arguing over cloth.

A brown coat became irritating for half a block until the person wearing
it turned and proved to be an old woman carrying radishes.

I stopped trying.

At the theatre, the cart was where we had left it.

Jori went to it first.

Replacement pin installed.

Chalk straight.

Other wheel fine.

Rear door fine.

Nothing missing.

The original pin remained wherever Rinna had stored it.

Nobody celebrated.

Davin put away the prop basket.

Nessa unpacked the costume box because apparently cloth suffocated
overnight if left folded incorrectly.

Rinna paid us.

My share was two copper.

I held the two coins.

Two.

Not debt credit.

Mine.

That distinction felt larger than it should have.

Yesterday I had earned one copper locally.

Three days ago Antonius had written two copper against debt.

Today I had two copper in my hand because fifty-three people had wanted
a King, a Widow, and enough distraction to make a wedding louder.

Pell looked over.

"Two?"

"Yes."

"Me too."

"Then stop looking at mine."

"I was checking fairness."

"Check yours."

I put one copper into the inner fold of my coat.

The other I kept in my fingers for a moment.

Bread.

Oil.

Meat if I was stupid.

Debt if Antonius asked for labor, which was not how this coin worked.

That was the strange part.

I could not throw it at the ledger from across Carrow and make the
number smaller.

Money had lanes here.

Theatre money fed us.

Vale labor reduced Vale.

Guild work mostly cost me time and gave me something stranger than
money.

Bren wanted to cut a lane into all of it and call the cut small.

I closed my hand.

Rinna said, "Greg."

"Yes?"

"You're making a face."

"Apparently I have one."

"Go home."

We left.

No one followed us that I could establish.

No one stopped us.

No Bren.

No runner.

No threat.

At home, Lyssa had eaten.

She had left beans covered for me.

I put the two copper on the table before I sat.

Her eyes went to them.

"Two?"

"Two."

"Good."

"Food too."

"Better."

I ate.

She asked about the show.

I told her about the support post.

The server.

The groom accidentally improving Marek's scene.

The boy under the table.

Pell trying to steal my bread through anticipated charity.

Then I told her about Venn.

"Someone asked if we were bringing the cart."

"Who?"

"Unknown man."

"Did you see him?"

"No."

"Did Venn know him?"

"No."

"Name?"

"No."

"Description?"

"Man."

Lyssa waited.

"Possibly brown."

"Terrifying."

"He bought beer."

"Monster."

"So we don't know it was Bren."

"No."

"Cart?"

"Fine when we got back. Pin there. Chalk straight."

She nodded.

I ate another spoonful.

Then she touched one of the copper pieces.

"Keeping both?"

The question should not have been difficult.

"I owe Vale."

"Yes."

"He doesn't take random payment."

"I know."

"I could ask."

"You could."

I looked at the coin.

Antonius had a ledger.

Bren had a share.

Rinna had wages.

Hessa had counts.

Everyone in Carrow seemed determined to turn my life into arithmetic.

"I think I remembered the debt more when I wasn't earning," I said.

Lyssa looked at me.

"Why?"

"Because it was impossible."

"And now?"

"Now it looks payable."

Not soon.

Not easily.

Not by throwing two copper at Antonius after a wedding.

But payable was different from impossible.

That changed the shape of it.

It also changed the shape of Bren.

A copper was not just a copper anymore.

It was bread I did not need to borrow.

Oil.

A little meat.

A piece of debt if Antonius had work.

A day where Lyssa did not have to cover something because I could not.

Bren's share could stay small forever and still take pieces out of those
things.

I put the two coins away.

Nothing under the cup.

No note.

No magic.

Thirty draws.

Twenty-six shaping attempts.

Thirty-four external attempts.

One far response that did not yet mean far worked.

Tomorrow had not been assigned yet.

For once, neither had my money.

I liked that.

I also understood it better than I had yesterday.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 217

## THE DEBTOR

I moved one copper.

That was the entire financial revolution.

It went from the cloth fold with the other money into a smaller fold
Lyssa used for buttons.

The button fold had no buttons in it.

This made it available for promotion.

Lyssa watched from the other side of the table while eating the end of
yesterday's bread.

"What is that?"

"Debt."

"That is a copper."

"It can become debt."

"It is already money."

"You're making this difficult."

"No. Antonius did."

Fair.

I had woken with the Copper Kettle coins still feeling different from
ordinary theatre pay.

Two copper was not wealth.

It was also not nothing.

Yesterday it had been stew, fifty-three wedding guests, a support post,
two plays, and several hours of carrying cloth through Carrow. Now it
was two small pieces of metal on a table.

I had decided one of them belonged to Future Greg.

Future Greg had debts.

Present Greg had breakfast.

This seemed balanced.

Lyssa tapped the little fold.

"Did Vale ask for it?"

"No."

"Can you pay him cash?"

"I don't know."

"Then it's still yours."

"I know."

"You look offended."

"I preferred impossible debt."

She chewed.

"Why?"

"It required less administration."

That got a small smile.

I picked up a scrap of paper.

The question took longer to write than it should have.

ANTONIUS,

WHAT DO I CURRENTLY OWE?

CAN I PAY CASH AGAINST IT, OR DO YOU PREFER WORK CREDIT?

GREG

I read it.

Lyssa held out her hand.

I gave it to her.

She read it too.

"That's almost normal."

"I can make it worse."

"Don't."

"Should I mention the copper?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Because you don't know if he wants it."

I took the note back.

There was another reason not to mention Bren.

Antonius had asked for the other half of Bren's name.

I did not have it.

I had an iron pin, a verbal message, and a man in a possibly brown coat
asking a tavern about a cart.

None of those were surnames.

This note was mine.

My debt.

My question.

That distinction felt worth preserving.

I folded it.

Nothing waited under the cup.

No Hessa note.

Thirty, twenty-six, thirty-four remained exactly where they had been.

One far response remained one far response.

Paper could wait.

Debt had apparently become interesting.

I left with Lyssa because she was headed toward the market again. Her
cuff customer had become satisfied enough to recommend another customer,
which meant yesterday's argument had somehow reproduced.

"Good?"

"Maybe."

"More cuffs?"

"Shoulder seam."

"Same length?"

She looked at me.

"I'll go now."

We separated two streets later.

I went toward Vale.

Not the main office.

That would have been dramatic for a note containing two questions.

One of Antonius's commercial yards had a receiving clerk near the east
market. I knew it because I had once spent half a morning waiting there
while three men disagreed about whether wet rope counted as damaged
rope.

The clerk was not one of those men.

She was older, narrow-faced, and busy making marks beside six stacked
crates.

"Vale?"

She looked at the side of the building.

"Usually."

"I have a note for Antonius."

"Name?"

"Greg."

"His or yours?"

"Mine."

That earned me a look.

"Gregory?"

"Unfortunately."

She held out her hand.

I gave her the note.

"Does this need an answer today?"

"No."

"Good."

"That sounded personal."

"It sounded like work."

Also fair.

I left.

That was it.

I had taken action on my debt and nothing exploded.

Disappointing.

By third bell the theatre smelled like damp wood, lamp oil, and
yesterday's costumes.

Nessa had already found a crease in one of the Copper Kettle dresses and
blamed travel.

"We walked."

"Exactly."

I did not ask.

The cart sat inside.

Replacement pin installed.

Chalk straight.

I checked once.

Pell was holding the King hat.

Marek entered from the rear.

Pell put it down.

Marek stopped.

"You touched it."

"I moved it."

"With what?"

Pell considered lying.

"Hands."

"Those are touching tools."

Pell looked at me.

"He's become unreasonable."

"You put it on a broom during an extortion attempt."

"One time."

"That's a sentence that doesn't improve with repetition."

Rinna came from the front with a folded page.

"Greg."

"Yes?"

"House and set today."

"No role?"

"No role."

I waited.

She waited.

"Good," I said.

"That sounded injured."

"I contain multitudes."

"You contain one leg and too many opinions. Move the front bench."

I moved the front bench.

Not far.

The house was smaller today.

Normal smaller.

People came in ones and twos. An older couple sat near the wall. Three
workers arrived together and argued about which one had promised to pay.
A woman brought a boy old enough to insist he did not need to sit beside
her, then sat beside her.

We were setting Debtor's Uncle and the Short.

I had just finished moving a stool when someone knocked.

Not three careful knocks.

Two fast ones.

Every head still changed direction.

That was the problem now.

Rinna opened the door.

A Copper Kettle apron stood outside.

Not Venn.

A woman I recognized from carrying bowls yesterday.

She held no package.

No pin.

No weapon.

Just irritation.

"Venn sent me."

Rinna opened the door farther.

"Problem?"

"Maybe. She said tell you."

The woman looked past Rinna at all of us.

Rinna said, "Tell me."

"That man came back."

The room got quieter.

I did not ask which man.

Rinna did.

"The one who asked about the cart?"

"Yes."

"You know it was the same one?"

"I served him both times."

Better than coat color.

Rinna nodded.

"What did he ask?"

The woman frowned, remembering.

"Asked if you got paid."

Nobody moved.

"Those words?"

"Asked if the players got their money."

"What did Venn say?"

"That wasn't his business."

I liked Venn more.

The woman continued.

"Then he asked how much."

"Answer?"

"No."

"Then?"

"He asked if you took it back here."

Rinna's face did not change much.

"Here?"

"The theatre. Said, 'They carry it back to their hall?' Something like
that."

"Did Venn answer?"

"No."

"Name?"

"No."

"Did he say why he wanted to know?"

"No."

"Did he threaten anyone?"

"No."

"Touch anyone?"

"No."

"Follow you here?"

The woman turned and looked into the street.

"I don't think so."

Rinna looked too.

Nobody useful appeared.

"What did he look like?"

"Brown coat. Thin face. Dark hair. Not old."

That was more than Venn had remembered.

Not enough.

"Gloves?" I asked.

Rinna looked at me.

The woman thought.

"Don't know."

I nodded.

Good.

I had asked once.

The world had survived.

Rinna said, "Anything else?"

"He drank another beer."

"Same table?"

"No. Front."

"Did he ask who paid us?"

"No."

"Did he ask where we live?"

"No."

"Any names?"

"No."

That mattered too.

The woman shifted her weight.

"Venn said you'd want the exact questions."

"We do."

"She also said if he comes back she's charging him more for beer."

Pell said from behind me, "That's good policy."

Rinna ignored him.

"Thank her. And you."

The woman left.

Rinna shut the door.

Nobody spoke for a moment.

Then Pell said, "Money?"

Rinna turned.

I said, "That's not just the cart."

"No," Rinna said.

"He wanted the money."

"He asked about the money."

"Fine."

Rinna pointed at me.

"Keep that difference."

I leaned against the end of the bench.

Unknown man.

Same unknown man, according to someone who had served him twice.

Cart.

Payment.

Amount.

Whether the money returned to the hall.

That was a pattern.

It was not a name.

It was not Bren.

It was also getting harder to call curiosity.

"We can't keep waiting for him to decide what happens," I said.

Rinna looked at me.

"Which him?"

Annoying.

"Whoever is asking."

"Good correction."

"That wasn't an answer."

"No."

"What do we do?"

"We stop carrying private-job money back here when we don't need to."

I looked around.

"Did we?"

"No."

Right.

Rinna had paid us after we returned, but that did not mean the client's
payment had traveled in one obvious purse. I had not watched.

Apparently not knowing things remained useful.

She continued.

"Next outside job, shares get handled before we leave or somewhere else
if that works. Client payment stays quiet. We don't tell strangers what
work pays. Which was already the rule."

"So our response is more accounting."

"Our response is not helping a stranger count our money."

"What if he isn't Bren?"

"Then we've inconvenienced a nosy man."

"What if he is?"

"Then we've inconvenienced a nosy man."

I hated how often Rinna's plans were boring.

They kept working.

"We could ask around the Copper Kettle."

"No."

"I didn't say I would."

"You were thinking it with your face."

Apparently I had several.

"I don't like waiting."

"Neither do I."

That surprised me.

Rinna picked up the folded page she had been carrying before the knock.

"I also don't like sending one-legged actors into markets to ask thin
men in brown coats whether they're extortionists."

"I'm not an actor today."

"Excellent. Then you're even less qualified."

Pell laughed.

I threw a rag at him.

Teren called from the rear, "If everyone is finished solving crime, the
stool is wrong."

The stool was wrong.

Work resumed.

That was not emotionally satisfying.

It was physically useful.

I moved the stool.

Davin adjusted the table.

Nessa changed a cuff.

The audience did not know that someone had been asking where our money
went.

They had paid to watch Veya insult Evin.

That was more reasonable.

Debtor's Uncle started.

I stayed offstage.

House work had its own timing.

Close the door after late arrivals.

Move along the wall without becoming scenery.

Keep the boy from touching the lamp stand.

Fail to keep the boy from touching the lamp stand.

Move him with words instead.

"Hot."

He pulled his hand back.

It was not hot yet.

Truth adjacent.

I felt mildly guilty.

Then the lamp was lit.

Future truth.

The strongest moment came from Sellen.

A woman arrived late exactly as Evin said, "Anyone who arrives after the
debt is due deserves the consequence."

The door opened.

Everyone looked.

Sellen looked at the woman.

The woman froze.

Sellen said, "Apparently consequences have arrived."

She bowed.

"I was looking for a seat."

Veya pointed toward the empty chair beside her.

"Sit near him. He owes more."

The woman sat.

The audience laughed.

Evin stared at Sellen.

"You've invited the consequence inside."

Sellen said, "It paid."

That got them.

I shut the door.

No one outside was watching that I could prove.

The Short followed.

Pell forgot where Nessa had hidden the wooden cup.

Nessa did not.

She made him search three wrong places while the audience watched his
confidence die.

When he finally found it under his own folded cloth, the laugh was
better than the written one.

Backstage he accused her of moving it.

"I put it there before house."

"You knew I'd forget."

"Yes."

"Entrapment."

"Repertory."

Normal.

Good.

Afterward, Rinna paid me one copper for house and set.

I put it with the money I had not assigned to Vale.

That was immediately confusing.

Was the morning copper still the Vale copper?

Yes.

Could I change which physical copper represented the debt?

Obviously.

Did that matter?

No.

Did my brain care?

Apparently.

I was standing near the front trying to determine whether a copper had
an identity when a Vale runner walked in.

Not Antonius.

The young woman with the narrow ledger case.

I knew her.

She knew me.

"Gregory."

"Greg."

"Still."

"Mr. Vale has an answer."

Rinna looked over from the benches.

I almost said this was personal.

Then remembered debt was not private if a creditor sent a clerk into
your workplace.

The runner opened her case.

"Your request was received."

"Excellent system."

"He says the current balance requires the ledger."

"Doesn't he have the ledger?"

"He has several."

Of course he did.

"He wants you at the west office tomorrow, first bell after midday, if
available. Twenty minutes. No labor credit for reviewing your own debt."

"Cruel."

"Accurate."

"Can I pay cash?"

"Yes."

That stopped me.

"Just yes?"

"Cash reduces the debt at recorded value."

"Any minimum?"

She checked the page.

"Mr. Vale says he does not want a runner carrying one copper across
Carrow every time you feel responsible."

Rinna made a noise that might have been a laugh.

I looked at her.

She became very interested in a bench.

The runner continued.

"You may pay at a Vale office against the account. It will be recorded.
He recommends you know the balance before developing a repayment
strategy."

"That sounds almost sensible."

"It was written more rudely."

"Can I see that version?"

"No."

I liked her less.

"Tomorrow. First bell after midday."

"Are you available?"

I looked at Rinna.

She said, "Third bell tomorrow."

There was enough space.

"Yes."

The runner marked it.

"Transport?"

"No."

She looked at my leg.

Not pity.

Calculation.

"Mr. Vale's note says transport is provided for requested debt labor.
This is your requested account review."

"Then no."

"Correct."

Commercial consistency.

She closed the case.

"Anything else?"

I thought about Bren.

Half a name.

No surname.

Unknown man at the Kettle.

No.

"Nothing."

She left.

Rinna watched the door close.

"You didn't tell her."

"I noticed."

"Good."

"I can learn."

"Slowly."

We finished putting the room away.

Jori checked the cart.

Straight chalk.

Replacement pin.

Nothing missing.

Again.

At the market I spent one copper.

Not the Vale copper.

I decided that before I handed it over.

Beans and a small piece of hard cheese.

The cheese was unnecessary.

This made it important.

I carried both home.

Lyssa was not there.

Her work bag was gone from its hook, which meant her day had continued
beyond whatever I had imagined for it.

Good.

I put the food away.

The little fold marked by no actual mark sat where I had left it.

One copper inside.

Vale.

Not paid.

Not gone.

Mine until I chose otherwise.

Lyssa came in later with thread stuck to her sleeve and irritation in
her mouth.

"Bad seam?"

"Customer's daughter changed the dress after I measured it."

"That seems unfair."

"It is."

"Did you tell them?"

"Yes."

"Did they become reasonable?"

"No."

"Good. I was worried the world changed."

She saw the cheese.

"You bought cheese."

"Financial planning."

"That's not what that means."

"I planned to eat cheese."

She sat.

I told her about the Copper Kettle woman first.

Same unknown man had returned.

Asked if we got paid.

Asked how much.

Asked whether we carried the money back to the hall.

Lyssa stopped unwinding thread.

"Same man?"

"The worker says yes. She served him both times."

"Do you know it was Bren?"

"No."

"Do you think it was?"

"More than yesterday."

"That's not the same."

"No."

I told her Rinna's response.

No private pay talk.

No obvious money movement when avoidable.

No investigating.

Lyssa nodded.

"What did you want to do?"

"Something."

"Specific."

"That was the problem."

"Good."

"Everyone keeps saying that."

"Maybe listen."

Then I told her about Vale.

Tomorrow.

Twenty minutes.

Actual ledger.

Cash accepted.

No random one-copper courier confessions.

She looked toward the little fold.

"You paying it tomorrow?"

"I don't know."

"Good."

I stared at her.

She smiled.

"You hate that word now."

"I have developed associations."

She tore the cheese in half.

"You still eat while owing money."

"I know."

"You still need oil."

"I know."

"You still need things that break."

"I know."

"So find out the number first."

That had been my idea.

It sounded better when she said it, which was offensive.

We ate beans with cheese.

One copper waited in the fold.

Tomorrow I would learn what Antonius's ledger said I owed.

Today I knew someone had asked where the theatre's money went.

Those were separate problems.

I kept them separate.

That took more effort than it should have.

Outside, a cart went past.

I listened until the wheel noise faded.

Then I ate the rest of my cheese.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# CHAPTER 218

## THE PAYER

The copper was still in the fold.

I checked.

This was unnecessary.

I checked anyway.

Lyssa was standing with one arm inside a dark green sleeve that did not
belong to her. The rest of the dress lay across the table.

"You think it escaped?"

"No."

"Then why are you checking?"

"I have an appointment."

"With the copper?"

"Antonius."

"Does Antonius know?"

"About the appointment?"

"The copper."

"No."

She pulled the sleeve through and inspected the seam.

"Then the copper is having a more stressful morning than Antonius."

Probably.

Nothing waited under the cup.

No Hessa note.

My forearm was boring. My shoulder was boring. My wrist was boring.

Thirty, twenty-six, thirty-four.

Paper remained paper.

Money had become the strange thing.

I ate bread.

Lyssa left before me because the shoulder-seam problem had turned into a
second fitting. I had no idea whether this meant the first fitting had
failed or succeeded.

"Which?"

"Both."

"Useful."

"Customer work."

She left.

I waited until first bell after midday because arriving early to inspect
my own debt felt too much like enthusiasm.

The west Vale office occupied the front corner of a warehouse that
smelled of dust, rope, old wood, and whatever had leaked into the floor
over several years and become part of the building.

Two carts stood outside.

One Vale side mark.

One without.

Men were moving sacks through the larger door. A clerk argued with a
driver about six barrels that were physically present and apparently
administratively absent.

Commerce.

I went through the smaller door.

The young woman with the narrow ledger case sat at a side desk.

"Gregory."

"Greg."

"Still."

"Apparently."

She pointed toward the inner room.

"He's waiting."

"That sounds threatening."

"He has another appointment."

Worse.

Antonius sat behind a table with three ledgers open.

Not one.

Three.

I stopped in the doorway.

"You've made my debt plural."

"No."

"Then why are there three books?"

"Because you made your debt inconvenient."

"That sounds like blame."

"It is accounting."

He pointed to the chair.

I sat.

The chair had a straight back and one leg slightly shorter than the
others. I discovered this immediately.

Antonius watched me rock once.

"Don't."

"Fix your chair."

"It's a guest chair."

"That explains your business relationships."

He slid the first ledger toward himself.

I could see my name.

GREGORY.

"Greg."

"The debt is Gregory's."

"I am Gregory."

"Then we've resolved something."

I disliked him.

Good.

The page was older than I expected.

Not ancient.

Just handled.

Marks had been added in different inks. Dates. Credits. Charges.
Cross-references to other pages. Several entries pointed into the second
ledger.

I leaned closer.

"How much?"

Antonius looked at me.

"That was fast."

"I have theatre."

"Of course you do."

He put one finger on the first line.

"Your original loan."

The amount was there.

I stared.

Then the next line.

Additional financing.

Then another.

A fee.

Another advance.

A correction.

Gambling stake.

Materials.

Something I had apparently agreed to while believing future income would
solve present arithmetic.

That version of me had been very optimistic.

"Why isn't there one number?"

"There is."

"Where?"

"At the end."

"Then why are we doing this?"

"Because you asked what you owe."

"I meant the number."

"No. You asked what you currently owe. If you only wanted the number, my
clerk could have copied it."

I looked at him.

He was right in the most annoying possible way.

I went back to the page.

Thirty-five percent.

Eight days.

The original terms were still there.

Then later financing with different conditions attached.

First refusal language.

Labor conversion after default.

Credits for work.

Charges I remembered.

Charges I did not.

I pointed at one.

"What's that?"

"Additional advance."

"For?"

"You were short."

"That describes a period."

Antonius opened the second ledger.

There.

A date.

A note.

I remembered.

"Oh."

"Useful?"

"No."

"It cost money."

"Apparently."

The problem became clear after several minutes.

The ledger was real.

The debt was real.

The exact current balance was also in the ledger.

But the manuscript of my own life had been financially incompetent.

Not literally.

The problem was that several old advances and gambling periods had been
recorded through linked entries, and I did not remember the numbers well
enough to pretend I had always carried the total in my head.

Antonius did.

Of course.

He tapped the current balance.

I looked at it.

Large.

Not impossible.

Those were different things.

I could not reduce it to normal theatre days cleanly because it crossed
denominations and my world had never bothered to give me a comforting
conversion table.

That was probably healthier.

I could still understand scale.

One copper for an ordinary theatre day.

Two copper from the Copper Kettle.

Two copper of debt credit for an hour of useful Vale account work.

Silver had mattered enough that six of them once covered two days of
Pell's time.

Gold belonged to another altitude entirely.

My debt had pieces from the period when I had treated future success
like a currency.

Now I was earning copper.

Regularly.

That was better than earning theories.

It was also slower.

"Well?" Antonius asked.

"I liked it better when it was abstract."

"Most debtors do."

"Do they start theatre companies?"

"You did not start the theatre company."

"Good. I was worried the ledger had added that."

He leaned back.

"You've begun earning."

"Some."

"Regularly."

"Mostly."

"And now the debt bothers you."

"It bothered me before."

"No. Before, it offended you."

I looked at him.

He continued.

"You treated it as an obstacle."

"It is."

"Now you're treating it as an account."

I hated that more because it sounded accurate.

"Do you rehearse these?"

"No."

"That was too neat."

"You are not complicated."

"Fuck you."

"There."

Normal.

I looked down again.

There were the two recent credits.

The earlier post-injury invoice comparison.

Two copper.

The delivery-account reconciliation.

Two copper.

Separate lines.

Small.

Real.

I touched the second one.

"You actually wrote it."

Antonius stared at me.

"What did you think credit meant?"

"I knew."

"Apparently not."

"I knew conceptually."

"Excellent. My favorite kind of repayment."

I took the little cloth fold from inside my shirt.

Antonius looked at it.

I put one copper on the table.

He looked at the copper.

Then at me.

"One."

"Yes."

"This is not a strategy."

"No. It's a copper."

His mouth moved slightly.

Not a smile.

Accounting amusement.

"Do you intend to do this every day?"

"No."

"Good."

"Can I?"

"Yes."

"Do you want me to?"

"I want the debt repaid."

"Helpful."

"I do not care whether a given copper came from theatre, a private
performance, or you selling your remaining leg, provided the transaction
is lawful and you remain capable of producing more value."

I looked at him.

"That became dark."

"Don't sell the leg."

"Thank you."

"It would damage repayment."

There he was.

He pulled the copper toward the ledger.

The clerk entered without knocking, carrying a small ink pot and a
narrow receipt strip.

Apparently one-copper payments had infrastructure.

Antonius made the entry.

Cash.

One copper.

Date.

Account.

The clerk copied it.

Antonius signed.

She handed me the strip.

I stared at it.

"That's it?"

"What were you expecting?"

"Nothing."

"Then you're satisfied."

I folded the receipt.

The current balance had changed by one copper.

Not emotionally.

Mathematically.

That was enough.

"What should I pay?" I asked.

Antonius closed one ledger.

"Whatever does not make you useless."

"Very precise."

"You have variable income."

"Yes."

"You have household expenses."

"Yes."

"Your theatre work may disappear."

"Optimistic."

"Private work may increase."

"Maybe."

"Your Guild work currently produces no meaningful cash."

"Correct."

"Your labor for me can reduce the account when I have work that suits
you."

"Yes."

"Then why would I invent a fixed payment that either becomes trivial
when you earn more or cripples you when you earn less?"

I stared at him.

"You're refusing to budget for me."

"I would rather die."

"Good."

"Pay when you have surplus. Work when I have suitable work. Do not
starve yourself to improve my ledger."

"Lyssa said that."

"She is protecting you."

"You're not?"

"I'm protecting the ledger."

Distinct.

Useful.

I folded the receipt smaller.

"Anything else?"

Antonius looked at the clock on the wall.

"You have theatre."

"That's not an answer."

"It is twenty-two minutes."

"You scheduled twenty."

"You spent two arguing with a chair."

"The chair started it."

He stood.

Meeting over.

I stood too.

At the door he said, "Greg."

I turned.

"If you want the figure again, ask for a copy of the balance. Do not
schedule me to discover that numbers remain numbers."

"You're welcome."

"For?"

"The copper."

"Get out."

I did.

I walked to the theatre with a receipt in my pocket.

The debt had not become small.

It had become finite.

That was worse in one way.

Better in another.

Impossible things did not require plans.

Finite things did.

Third bell was already approaching when I reached the hall.

The cart sat inside.

Chalk straight.

Replacement pin installed.

I checked once.

Rinna was moving names on the board.

"Vale?"

"Still rich."

"You?"

"Less indebted."

"How much?"

"One copper."

She stopped.

"You went there to pay one copper?"

"I went there to learn the account."

"And paid one."

"Yes."

"Did he laugh?"

"Not visibly."

"Strong restraint."

I looked at the board.

KING.

EMPTY TABLE.

SHORT.

GREG: SERVANT / SET.

I pointed.

"I've been Servant."

"So has Evin."

"That isn't how titles work."

"What titles?"

"Nothing."

Nessa threw the dull brown coat at me.

I caught it badly.

"Servant."

"Apparently."

The role was the same small role from before.

Four lines.
